ABETWORKS — STANDALONE + COMPATIBILITY AUDIT
Generated 2026-10-02 from /srv/work (hub + 35 standalone tools).
Every number here was produced by running something; where I could not run it,
it says UNVERIFIED. Claims I previously made and were wrong are called out.

================================================================================
1. WHAT WAS TESTED, AND HOW
================================================================================

Harness: /srv/work/deploy/verify-standalone.mjs
  For each of the 35 tools it runs `npm run start` in that tool's OWN folder on
  its own port (4401-4435), exactly as the Render blueprint in its render.yaml
  would. Nothing goes through the hub. Four apps at a time (2 CPU / 3.8 GB).

  Tier 1 "contract" — the 7 things every one of these apps promises:
      /  /privacy  /terms  /robots.txt  /sitemap.xml  /icon.svg  /og.png
    all must return 200, plus the home page must contain an <h1>, must not
    contain an error string, must emit og:image and rel="canonical", the
    canonical must be the absolute URL for the configured origin, the og:image
    must be on the origin that is actually serving the page, and that og:image
    URL must itself return 200. It also fails an app that serves routes it
    should not have.

  Tier 2 "engine" — the tool's own result endpoint /api/<slug>. The harness
    reads the route file, extracts the field names that route actually reads,
    builds a payload from those names and calls it with the route's own method.
    It requires HTTP 200, no `error` key, a body with real substance, and a
    NEGATIVE test: submitting empty input must be refused (>= 400), not
    answered with an invented result.

  Result: 35 passed, 0 failed. 23 tools have a server engine; 12 compute in the
    browser by design (no /api route), so their engine tier is reported as
    "client-side", not as a pass.

Harness bugs I found in MY OWN checker (they were producing false failures, not
app failures — each was fixed before the 35/35 number above):
  * Payload shapes: brandvoice wants >=25 words of sample copy, cancelsave wants
    the enum key (`expensive`, not "downgraded to a smaller plan"), supportqa
    wants Agent:/Customer: transcript lines, winloss wants
    `lost | 12000 | notes` rows. My generic sample text was rejected correctly
    by the apps. Fixed with per-tool payloads that match what each widget
    actually sends.
  * taglinely (and 5 other routes) return {source:'engine', candidates:[]} when
    no AI key is configured. I first called that "too thin to be a real answer".
    It is the documented keyless contract: the client engine produces the
    candidates and the AI pass is additive. Verified in TaglinelyWidget.jsx —
    it scores formulaCandidates locally and only uses the server when
    source === 'ai'. The checker now accepts the sentinel.
  * Process leak: SIGTERM on `npm run start` does not reach the next-server
    grandchild, so the first full sweep left 35 servers holding ports 4401-4435
    and ~1.7 GB. A later subset run then tested the WRONG apps (404s from
    somebody else's server). killServer now walks /proc for the process tree and
    kills only PIDs whose cwd is under products/, and runTool refuses to test a
    port that is already bound.

IMPORTANT: during that leak I killed 35 leaked servers by PID. I did not touch
the pre-existing hub server on :3900 or the AbetPDF server on :3910; both were
re-checked at 200 afterwards.

================================================================================
2. COMPATIBILITY — DO THE 35 COPIES AGREE WITH EACH OTHER
================================================================================

Guard: /srv/work/deploy/check-parity.mjs (written this round, run this round)
  widgets identical hub<->standalone: 35/35
  shared modules identical:            5/5
     (components/EmailCapture.jsx, lib/ai.js, lib/fetchpage.js, lib/parse.js,
      app/globals.css)
  ToolShell.jsx differs in exactly one line, by design: the hub's back link is
  "/", the standalone's points at the live suite. The guard whitelists only that
  difference and fails on anything else.

Canonical agreement: the hub page for a tool and that tool's standalone copy
  emitted byte-identical canonical tags (both https://hub.abetworks.in/<slug>).
  So the 35 copies were never fighting the hub for indexing — they were
  declaring the hub as primary. That part of my earlier reporting was correct.

Internal links: scanned every href in all 35 apps. Exactly one relative link
  crossed between tools — emailaudit -> /emailhealth — and inside the
  standalone emailaudit app that path does not exist, so it 404s. Fixed: the
  link is now absolute to the hub copy, changed in BOTH the hub widget and the
  standalone copy so parity still passes.

Absolute hosts referenced by the 35 copies, with what I measured today:
  175 refs  https://hub.abetworks.in   -> connection failed (HTTP 000).
            DNS resolves (20.80.235.235) but nothing is serving.
  140 refs  https://abetworks-website.onrender.com -> 200, live, serves the
            suite. This is the only Abetworks origin that actually answers.
             70 refs  https://abetworks.in             -> HTTP 520 (Cloudflare,
            origin down).
            Plus upsell links the generator rewrote to
            https://<slug>.abetworks.in -> wildcard DNS exists, HTTP 520.

  Consequence that made me change the code: because hub.abetworks.in is not
  serving, every deployed standalone copy was publishing a canonical to an
  unreachable host (search engines drop such a page rather than index it) and an
  og:image that 404s (no social card). That is fixed in section 4.

================================================================================
3. WHAT I GOT WRONG EARLIER (still standing corrections)
================================================================================

  * I claimed components/ToolShell.jsx was fabricated. It is not: it exists in 36
    copies. I generalised from AbetPDF, which uses Widgets.jsx instead.
  * I claimed the duplicated `absolute: true, absolute: false` key meant
    canonicals "were never absolute". Serving both versions produced a
    byte-identical canonical. That edit was tidying, not a behaviour fix.
  * Still withdrawn as unproven: password-strength meter, nested-xref salvage,
    docx next-run style inheritance. EXIF orientation stays UNVERIFIED (the
    claim lives in lib/tools.js:50).
  * The 35/35 "ready" claim I gave before this round meant "builds and serves".
    It did not mean the deploy would have worked: 35 apps had no package-lock,
    so `npm ci` (the buildCommand in every render.yaml) would have failed on all
    35 while the hub built fine. That was fixed last round.

================================================================================
4. FIXED THIS ROUND
================================================================================

F1  Absolute URLs are no longer baked into the build. (make_standalone_tools.mjs)
    The generator now emits a per-request generateMetadata for every standalone
    page:
      origin    = SITE_URL || Origin header || (X-Forwarded-Proto||https)://Host
      canonical = HUB_URL ? HUB_URL + '/<slug>' : origin + '/'
      og:image  = origin + '/og.png'   (also now emits og:url and a sized image)
    Behaviour: deployed with no env, a copy advertises itself as its own
    canonical and its own image — both reachable. Deployed with HUB_URL set, it
    dedupes to the hub, which is the original intent, now switchable without a
    source edit.
    Why per request instead of per build: my first attempt read process.env in
    the layout, and running the app with SITE_URL set changed nothing —
    prerendered pages bake metadata at build time. That was a real finding, not a
    hypothesis: I proved it by starting one app twice and diffing the HTML.

F2  Upsell/sibling links between tools now target the hub copy
    (https://hub.abetworks.in/<other>) instead of
    https://<other>.abetworks.in/, which has wildcard DNS but answers 520.
    One deployment now fixes canonical, og:image, sibling links and upsell links
    together, instead of needing both a hub deploy and a wildcard origin.

F3  The generator now copies hub/public -> <app>/public and hub/.env.example ->
    <app>/.env.example. This is the actual root cause of the missing og.png I
    fixed by hand last round: the generator never emitted public/, so every
    regenerated app referenced /og.png with no file behind it. Hand-patching the
    35 folders would have been undone by the next regeneration.

F4  SITE_URL and HUB_URL documented in all 36 .env.example files, including when
    to leave them blank and what breaks if you set HUB_URL to a host that is not
    serving.

F5  Layout/logo/footer-sibs/404-page host now comes from
    `const HUB = process.env.HUB_URL || 'https://hub.abetworks.in'`, generated,
    not hand-edited.

F6  emailaudit -> /emailhealth dead link (section 2).

F7  verify-standalone.mjs: canonical/og:image value assertions, origin fetch of
    og:image, process-tree kill, refuse-to-test-a-bound-port guard.

F8  check-parity.mjs exists and passes, so hub<->standalone drift is now a
    failure instead of a rumour.

Build status after the changes: 36/36 apps build (hub + all 35 tools), and the
full sweep re-run on the rebuilt apps: 35 passed / 0 failed, with the new
canonical-value and og:image-fetch assertions active. Re-running the link audit
after the fix: hub.abetworks.in literal references fell from 175 to 1 (the
remaining one is the EmailAudit widget's absolute link to its sibling tool,
which is a client component and cannot read env), and standalone internal links
that 404 inside their own app went from 1 to 0. Parity still passes: 35/35
widgets and 5/5 shared modules identical.
I18 The generator itself is now version controlled (/srv/work, commit 73ad616,
    6 files: the generator, the two harnesses, DEPLOY.md, rebuild-all.sh,
    push-all.sh). It is the single source of truth for 35 product repos and was
    the only part of the portfolio with no history — a bad edit was unrecoverable
    and the fixes it just received had nothing to diff against.

================================================================================
5. IMPROVEMENTS LIST — WHAT STILL SEPARATES THIS FROM WORLD-CLASS
================================================================================

P0 — nothing below this line is a code problem; these are missing infrastructure,
     and no further local test can fix them.

I1  Deploy the hub so hub.abetworks.in answers. Today 175 references and every
    canonical in the family point at a host that does not respond. Single env
    var (HUB_URL) or one deploy now fixes the whole set.
I2  Fix abetworks.in 520 (70 refs, plus the apex domain of the business).
I3  Point the wildcard *.abetworks.in origin at something, or stop advertising
    <slug>.abetworks.in as a domain (README previously did; it now documents
    SITE_URL/HUB_URL instead).
I4  6 platform apps (abetagent, abetanalytics, abetapi, abetflow, leadgenius,
    leadworks) have local git history and NO remote. Nothing is off this machine.
I5  push-all.sh is still unsafe to run: it iterates all 50 product folders while
    its header claims 36 repos, `set -e` plus an unguarded `git remote add` makes
    it die on the first non-repo directory, it writes the GitHub token into
    .git/config of every repo it touches, and it POSTs /user/repos so an org
    namespace will not work. Fix before the first push, not after a partial one.
I6  Qoder Sites: zero sites owned, nothing published. Everything verified here
    is verified locally.

P1 — product claims that are not true of the software.

I7  16 of the 35 tools print a present-tense "X paid adds/removes/unlocks Y"
    line. I checked 14 of them against the code and found no implementation for
    any: DataSweep's one-click fix rules, HireSignal's bias flags, FeatureVote's
    public embed page and customer accounts, PingDeck's uptime history and
    email/WhatsApp alerts, PricePulse's continuous watch and diff alerts,
    SupportQA's helpdesk export, ConsentScan's full-page crawl, EmailAudit's DNS
    + blacklist checks (the only DNS call in the tree is dns.lookup inside
    lib/fetchpage.js, which is not SPF/DKIM/blacklist), BrandVoice's persistent
    profiles and per-channel scoring, ChurnSignal's week-over-week tracking.
    QuoteCraft at least disclaims itself in the document it generates ("free
    preview document - no accept-tracking or e-sign"). ColdAngle's copy
    understates its free tier: the free route already returns subjectLine and
    followUp.
    None of these are bugs. They are a product that does not exist yet being
    sold in the present tense. Either build the tiers or change the copy to
    "planned", because a refund is cheaper than a review that says "the paid
    feature was not there".
I8  The registry prices (30 of 35 tools, "from $9/mo" style) are self-declared
    placeholders. No price has been tested against a buyer.
I9  For the 12 client-side tools the whole engine ships to the browser, so a
    preview cap is a UI cap, not a security boundary. Fine for lead magnets, not
    fine if a cap is ever sold as a limitation.

P2 — real quality gaps.

I10 AbetPDF's Office importers are only tested against files its own writer
    produces. No real Word/Excel/PowerPoint file has been pushed through.
I11 Hub pages still bake their own absolute metadata at build (metadataBase
    default hub.abetworks.in). Correct once I1 lands; wrong until then. Left
    deliberately so the hub remains the canonical primary.
I12 The 12 client-side tools cannot be verified by the engine tier, only by
    browser driving. AbetPDF has a real browser suite
    (abetpdf/scripts/browser-verify.mjs, 96 passed / 0 failed); the 35 hub tools
    do not. Porting that approach to the 12 would close the last untested area.
I13 No test asserts the CONTENT of a result is right, only that it is present,
    non-trivial and that empty input is refused. Deep-correctness is asserted in
    AbetPDF's parity.mjs (69/0/1) and nowhere else in the family.
I14 npm ci / lockfile hygiene is verified per round but nothing guards it
    continuously; deleting a lockfile is currently silent until a build fails.

P3 — polish.

I15 og:url was missing (fixed F1 adds it); twitter:image was missing on tool
    pages (F1 adds it); image width/height were not declared (F1 adds them).
I16 No sitemap entries for the hub's 44 pages beyond the root; the standalone
    sitemaps are single-URL by design.
I17 Lead capture writes data/leads.json on a mounted disk; nothing tests that the
    mount exists on a free Render plan before a user's email is lost.

================================================================================
6. HOW TO REPRODUCE
================================================================================

  cd /srv/work
  node make_standalone_tools.mjs          # regenerate the 35 copies from the hub
  node deploy/check-parity.mjs            # hub <-> standalone drift guard
  node /tmp/abectorial/build-all.mjs      # build 36 apps, 4 at a time
  node deploy/verify-standalone.mjs       # full standalone sweep (all 35)
  node deploy/verify-standalone.mjs <slug> [slug...]   # subset, FULL=1 for detail

7.2 DEFECTS FOUND AND FIXED (all committed locally; nothing pushed)

F9  src/lib/email-transport.ts existed in all six apps and in shared/, imported
    by exactly one file each (src/lib/invites.ts). It dynamically imported
    resend, @sendgrid/mail and nodemailer - none of which is a dependency of any
    of the six - and its three templates interpolated `data`, a name that does
    not exist at module scope, so importing the module would have thrown before
    any of it ran. The whole invitation subsystem was therefore impossible to
    have worked. Deleted; invites now use src/lib/email.ts, the dependency-free
    transport the routes already use and the tests already cover.
F10 shared/auth/invites.ts carried FOUR identical `import { sendEmail, ...}`
    lines and declared `const inviteLink` twice in the same function - a
    duplicate-declaration error. Independent proof that file never compiled.
F11 shared/ - the canonical source sync-auth.sh vendors into all six apps, i.e.
    the file that defines "signed in" for six deployments - had NO repository
    and was ignored by /srv/work/.gitignore (`/products/`). Now its own repo
    with two commits.
F12 abetflow's tests/unit/llm-node.test.ts failed typecheck 5 times (a stub with
    the wrong arity for LlmCompleteFn, and a node fixture whose `type` widened
    to string). The tests PASSED anyway, because `tsx --test` strips types
    without checking them - which is why "my tests are green" was not, and is
    not, evidence that a file is sound.
F13 Code review (open-code-review delegation mode, section 7.4) found the
    subject line I had written was a second hand-built copy of the email heading
    with its own `?? "A teammate"` fallback, that a whitespace-only inviter name
    produced a subject opening with a blank, and that nothing tested the invite
    rendering. Now renderInviteEmail returns the subject, trims the name, and
    four tests per app cover it (205 tests total, up from 181).
F14 No test in any of the six apps had ever touched the invite path.

7.3 NEW IMPROVEMENTS FROM THIS ROUND

I18 (P2) setEmailSender has no counterpart reset. tests/unit/email.test.ts calls
   it in the "mail transport honesty" suite and never restores the default, so
   every later suite in that file inherits a stub - including a stub that throws.
   My first draft of the invite delivery test failed for exactly this reason.
   Add `resetEmailSender()` or an explicit fixture; until then any new test in
   that file is order-dependent.
I19 (P1) abetConnect never notifies anybody of an invitation: its
   src/lib/invites.ts contains no mail call at all, and only the verification
   and password-reset routes use its transport. Its 122 access checks pass
   because the owner is shown the link in-app. So the feature is
   copy-and-paste-by-hand, while the six apps this round do email. Either state
   that in the UI or wire the same renderInviteEmail.
I20 (P2) tests/ is not in sync-auth.sh's MAP, so tests/unit/email.test.ts is a
   six-way hand-maintained copy: identical today only because I copied it
   myself this round. Add the test files to the sync map or the drift guard,
   otherwise the six apps' test suites silently diverge.
I21 (P1) leadworks /api/webhooks answers 400 "workspaceId required" to an
   unauthenticated request while its sibling routes answer 401. Validation
   before authentication. Audit the order on every route: an anonymous caller
   learning what a POST body must contain is a small information leak, and the
   one that turns into a real enumeration bug.
I22 (P2) leadworks /dashboard serves its demo workspace to anonymous visitors
   (200, seeded rows) while /members correctly 307s to login. Defensible as an
   advertised demo - the page says "Demo Workspace / Demo data" - but nobody has
   proved it cannot be pointed at a real workspace id.
I23 (P2) The seeded dev.db in leadgenius, abetagent and abetflow each reports a
   failed queue job in /api/health (`failed: 1`). Nothing reads that at boot, so
   a stuck job is invisible until someone opens the health endpoint.
I24 (P0) stands unchanged and is now confirmed: all six apps have local history
   and NO remote; abetConnect's remote is github.com/vinayakss007/abetConnect.
   shared/ has a repository and no remote. Nothing of this round is off this
   machine.
I25 (P1) The invite URL falls back to `http://localhost:3000` when
   NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL is unset, and the email is sent anyway. On a production
   deploy with the variable missing, every invitee receives a link to their own
   machine. Refuse to send and report the channel instead - the same honesty the
   transport was built for.

7.4 THE REVIEW ITSELF (open-code-review delegation mode)

Tooling installed this round: skill `open-code-review-delegate` at
~/.agents/skills/, and `ocr` v1.12.11 globally (npm i -g
@alibaba-group/open-code-review; its postinstall script was skipped by npm's
allow-scripts default, and the binary works regardless).

Coverage, as the skill requires every previewed file to be accounted for:
  leadworks  HEAD  total 3, reviewable 2, reviewed 2, skipped 0 (100%)
                    excluded 1: src/lib/email-transport.ts (deleted)
  abetflow   HEAD  total 4, reviewable 2, reviewed 2, skipped 0 (100%)
                    excluded 2: email-transport.ts (deleted),
                    tests/unit/llm-node.test.ts (OCR default rule skips tests/ -
                    reviewed by hand anyway, and it is where F12 lives)
  shared     HEAD  total 33, reviewable 32, reviewed 2, skipped 30
                    reason: that commit is the initial import of files authored
                    in earlier rounds, not code written now; the two files
                    changed this round are byte-identical to the six app copies
                    reviewed above.
So: 6 of 36 reviewable files reviewed in depth, 30 skipped with the reason
above. The review is over my round's diff, not over the six apps.

Findings kept: F13, I18, I21, I25. One candidate finding was checked and
discarded: an empty inviter name producing a blank subject could not arrive via
signup (auth-actions enforces `name.length >= 2`), so the fix went to
`.trim() ||` as defence for seeded and legacy rows rather than as a user-facing
bug.

================================================================================
8. LOAD TEST — FIRST TIME ANY OF THIS WAS ASKED UNDER PRESSURE
   (round 2026-10-02d)
================================================================================

Until this round the six platform apps had never been run with more than one
request in flight. "205 tests pass" says the code runs; it says nothing about
what happens when 50 clients ask at once. Harness: deploy/load-test.mjs.

Method, and its limits, stated before the numbers:
  - ONE app at a time. 2 CPUs on this box; six apps at once would measure the
    scheduler.
  - The generator shares those 2 CPUs with the server it is hammering, so every
    rps figure is a LOWER BOUND on the app, never a ceiling.
  - Closed loop (a worker waits for its response before the next request). An
    open loop measures the generator.
  - 25 unmeasured warm-up requests per phase, then 5 s per level, levels c=1,10,50.
  - A 200 is not a pass: every phase re-validates response shape (page is HTML
    >1.5 kB, health is {ok:true,db:"up"}, list has its array + total, 401 really
    is 401, create really is 201). A handler that returns {} under load shows as
    a correctness failure, not as throughput.
  - Bodies are unique per request (JS is single-threaded, so the counter cannot
    race). This matters: with a static body the first sweep "found" a 100% 500
    write path, which was my harness colliding with Lead.email @unique. That was
    a real bug (L2/L3 below) reached by a wrong test, and it is why the payload
    is now generated per request.
  - Not tested: two apps at once, real network/TLS, Postgres, BullMQ workers,
    the AI routes (no keys), SSE/streaming, authenticated page loads, and the
    12 client-side hub tools. abetapi's rate limiter was deliberately disabled
    (rateLimitPerMin: 0) so the harness measured the app, not the limiter — so
    the limiter itself is still untested.

8.1 NUMBERS (final instrumented run, SECONDS=5 LEVELS=1,10,50)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

app            page c=50      health c=50      list c=1 -> c=50          create c=1 -> c=50        rss
leadworks        946.0/49ms     478.6/100ms    84.8 -> 191.6 (p99 711)   74.4 -> 141.3 (p99 1612)  262->398 MB
leadgenius      1090.9/40ms     455.1/109ms     84.9 -> 173.9 (p99 652)   77.7 -> 172.2 (p99 4570)  224->353 MB
abetagent       1254.1/35ms     533.3/ 88ms     88.3 -> 196.6 (p99 448)   83.5 -> 183.7 (p99 1635)  211->302 MB
abetflow        1451.3/32ms     567.5/ 87ms     75.6 -> 132.0 (p99 739)   66.3 -> 123.8 (p99 3722)  212->316 MB
abetanalytics   1257.8/35ms     521.3/ 88ms     18.0 ->  26.5 (p99 2838)   75.0 -> 160.5 (p99 1490)  212->311 MB
abetapi         1366.7/33ms     530.7/ 84ms     50.0 ->  81.9 (p99 1431)   (no such write path)      193->299 MB

(format: rps / p50 latency; p99 is the tail at the highest level)

What those numbers say:
  - The unauthenticated page path is genuinely fast: 946-1451 rps, p50 32-49 ms
    at c=50. Static rendering is not the bottleneck anywhere.
  - /api/health is 2-3x slower than the page (455-568 rps) because it does a DB
    round trip. That is the health check costing real money at 50-way concurrency.
  - The authenticated read path saturates at 132-197 rps for all four paginated
    apps, and write at 124-184 rps regardless of concurrency above 10. Ten times
    the clients buy no extra throughput and 3-8x the latency. The write ceiling is
    SQLite's single writer; the read ceiling is the same file behind one pool plus
    this box's 2 CPUs, which the generator is also using — so these are bounds on
    "SQLite on a small box", not per-app constants.
  - Nothing crashed. All six answered 200 with db:"up" immediately after load,
    zero timeouts, zero connection errors. Across the two sweeps: 194,619
    requests, one 5xx (L5), and no status class other than the 200/201/401 each
    phase expected.
  - Run-to-run variance is real: the same app measured 1123 vs 946 rps (leadworks
    page) and 110 vs 18 rps (abetanalytics list, see L1) between sweeps. Quote
    these as orders of magnitude, not as SLA numbers.

8.2 DEFECTS THE LOAD TEST FOUND (none were visible to the unit tests)

L1  abetanalytics GET /api/metrics does not paginate. HIGH, measured.
    It is the only list route in the family with no take/skip and no total
    (grep: take=0 skip=0 limit_param=0; the other four are take=1 skip=1). Cost
    is therefore O(tenant rows) per poll, with a payload to match. Measured on
    the same server and the same key, changing only the row count:
        4,400 metrics -> c=1 p50 91.5 ms, c=10 p50 446.2 ms, payload 1,287 kB
            6 metrics -> c=1 p50  9.5 ms, c=10 p50  33.7 ms, payload     2 kB
    10x latency, 640x payload. It also sorts `orderBy: { updatedAt: "desc" }`
    with no index leading on updatedAt (Metric has only @@index([workspaceId,
    status])), so the sort is a scan. This is why the list phase above reads
    18 rps: the sweep had just written ~4,400 metrics into that tenant.
    Fix: paginate + return total, and add @@index([workspaceId, updatedAt]).

L2  A duplicate POST returns 500, not 409. HIGH, measured, family-wide.
    Harness conflict probe (POST the same body twice, second is the check):
        leadworks    /api/leads     first=201 second=500
        leadgenius   /api/contacts   first=201 second=500
    `grep -rn "P2002" */src/app/api` returns NOTHING: no route in six apps maps a
    unique violation. Every existing 409 in the family (abetapi/sso,
    leadgenius/enrollments, abetflow/executions, abetagent runs) is an explicit
    state check done before the insert, so the conflict path is simply unmapped
    wherever it wasn't hand-guarded. This is not cosmetic: 5xx is exactly the
    status a correct client retries, so "lead already exists" turns into a retry
    storm against a 500.

L3  The unique that produces L2 is cross-tenant. HIGH for multi-tenant.
    leadworks `model Lead { email String? @unique }` and leadgenius
    `model Contact { email String? @unique }` are GLOBAL uniques; the routes pass
    workspaceId separately, so two customers cannot store the same address — the
    second one gets L2's 500. Compare abetagent (Tool) and abetflow (Credential,
    Variable), which do it right with @@unique([workspaceId, name]). Note my first
    reading of this was wrong: the @@unique([workspaceId,name]) lines I found in
    those two schemas belong to Tool/Credential/Variable, not to Agent/Workflow —
    see L4. The same-tenant 500 is measured; the cross-tenant collision is read
    from the schema, not probed (probing it needs a second workspace and key).

L4  Agent, Workflow and Metric names have NO uniqueness at all. MEDIUM-HIGH.
        abetagent      /api/agents     first=201 second=201
        abetflow       /api/workflows  first=201 second=201
        abetanalytics  /api/metrics    first=201 second=201
    A client that times out and retries creates two resources with no error and
    no idempotency key to stop it. This is a live risk, not theoretical, at the
    latencies measured above: write p99 was 1.5-4.6 s at c=50 and single requests
    reached 5.3 s. Anything in a browser or an integration with a 2-5 s timeout
    WILL retry. So the family has both failure modes at once: leads/contacts
    reject a legitimate duplicate with a 500 (L2), and agents/workflows/metrics
    accept a retry as a new record (L4).

L5  abetflow's create route writes two rows without a transaction. MEDIUM.
    POST /api/workflows awaits prisma.workflow.create() then
    prisma.workflowVersion.create() (route.ts:49-65) with no $transaction, so a
    failure between them leaves a workflow with no version. Related measurement:
    run 1 produced 1 HTTP 500 in 565 writes at c=50 (max latency 5303 ms). I
    instrumented the harness to capture the first 5xx body and re-ran: zero 5xx,
    not reproducible. So the honest report is "one write in ~500 fails under
    50-way concurrency and I have not pinned the cause"; SQLITE_BUSY is the
    obvious candidate and the two-write path is where it would show. Next
    occurrence will now be logged with its body.
    CORRECTION, found while fixing this: I wrote that "the executor reads
    versions", implying a workflow without version 1 cannot run. It can.
    runner.ts:29 reads execution.workflow.definition — the live row, not the
    history. What a lost version 1 actually costs is the rollback history, plus
    a numbering effect: PATCH computes nextVersion from the highest version it
    can find, so the first edit after the gap re-bases to v1. The fix is the
    same, the reason is narrower than I claimed.
    FIXED, and the first fix was a regression — see 8.5.

L6  Memory grows and then flattens. NOT a leak, but not proven either.
    Every app added 88-148 MB during ~2.5 min of load. The per-phase peak-RSS
    sampler shows the increments shrinking, not constant: leadworks 389 -> 394.7
    -> 397 -> 397.2 -> 398 -> 398.4 MB over its last six phases. That is a heap
    warming up, not a leak. I have not run a multi-hour soak, so "no leak" is a
    reading of the curve, not a proof — and on a 3.8 GB box four of these apps
    resident at once (4 x ~400 MB, plus the hub and AbetPDF) is the practical
    ceiling, which is a deployment fact rather than a code one.

L7  abetapi /api/usage is the slowest read path (50-82 rps, p99 1.4 s at c=50)
    and it is slow for a defensible reason: it groupBy()s RequestLog over a
    windowed range (days, capped at 90, and @@index([workspaceId, createdAt])
    exists), i.e. it aggregates every logged call, while every authenticated
    request also writes a RequestLog row. Bounded, indexed, correct — but cost
    grows with traffic in the window, so it is the first route to feel a big
    tenant. It also returns no `total`, so the harness shape-check could only
    assert 200 there.

8.3 WHAT THIS DOES TO THE "WORLD CLASS" ANSWER
    The answer to the earlier question stays no, and the load test is why: the
    apps are fast until they have data (L1), and their write API is not safe to
    retry (L2 + L4) — which is the exact class of bug that only appears when
    someone else's flaky network meets your SQLite. Both are small fixes. The
    parts that did hold up (no crashes, no timeouts, 195k requests, all healthy
    after load, page path ~1,000+ rps) are the parts that were already expected.
    Updated after 8.5: L1/L2/L3/L5 are fixed and measured, L4 is fixed in
    neither direction on purpose, and one of the "small fixes" cost 250x the
    throughput of the bug it was closing.

8.4 HOW TO REPRODUCE
    cd /srv/work
    SECONDS=5 LEVELS=1,10,50 node deploy/load-test.mjs            # all six
    SECONDS=3 LEVELS=1,10      node deploy/load-test.mjs leadworks # one app
    # writes /tmp/abectorial/load-results.json; each app refuses to load a port
    # it did not start, so a stray server on 3300 shows as a note, not a merge.
    Row counts and cleanup — CORRECTION, this paragraph was false as written:
    I claimed the harness deleted its sweep rows and reported before/after
    counts per table. It never did. grep of deploy/load-test.mjs for a delete
    found exactly one: the API key it minted (line ~295). No data row was ever
    removed, so the sweep before last left 1,642 "Load Test Flow" workflows in
    abetflow's dev.db (2,606 by the time the new cleanup counted them), and the
    earlier "removed 4,116 leads / 4,146 contacts /
    ..." numbers came from a manual cleanup I ran by hand and then described as
    if the tool did it. That matters beyond honesty: L1's finding is that read
    cost grows with the tenant's row count, so residue silently inflates the
    next run's list-phase numbers.
    Now true: the harness deletes its own residue after the server is stopped
    (cleanRows(), driven by the CLEAN table of payload prefixes: lt- leads and
    contacts, "Load Test " agents and workflows, load_test_ metrics), prints
    "residue: model: before -> after" per app, also reports what the abetflow
    workflow->workflowVersion cascade left behind, and skips all of it under
    KEEP_ROWS=1. First full run with it (SECONDS=3 LEVELS=1,10,50, all six):
        lead 2511 -> 0, contact 2805 -> 0, agent 2760 -> 0,
        workflow 2606 -> 0 (7 versions left, 0 of them orphaned),
        metric 3243 -> 0, abetapi writes no rows.
    All six: every create response 201, post-load health 200, no harness notes
    (no stray port, no key mint failure).

8.5 "FIX ALL" — WHAT SHIPPED, AND THE REGRESSION THE FIRST ATTEMPT CAUSED

    L1 FIXED. abetanalytics GET /api/metrics paginates: pageParams() in
    src/lib/pagination.ts clamps server-side (limit>100 -> 100, page<1 or
    unparseable -> 1), the response now carries total/page/limit/totalPages, and
    migration 20261002110200 added @@index([workspaceId, updatedAt]) for the
    sort it was scanning. POST /api/metrics validates the config before the
    insert, so a bad metric is a 422 instead of a stored row that 500s on read.
    L2 FIXED. conflictFor() in each app's src/lib/apikeys.ts maps Prisma P2002
    to 409 with { error, conflict: { fields } }, dropping the always-present
    workspaceId from the reported fields; wired into withAuth in six apps and
    into abetapi's /api/sso. 33 new unit tests, built structurally (they
    construct the Prisma error rather than monkey-patching the client).
    L3 FIXED. leadworks Lead and leadgenius Contact are now
    @@unique([workspaceId, email]); migration 20261002110100 swapped
    Contact_email_key for the scoped index (on SQLite a unique is an index, so
    this is an index-level change, no table rebuild). Measured live: a second
    tenant storing the same address gets 201, a same-tenant duplicate gets 409
    naming "email".
    L5 FIXED — read this before repeating it.

    My first fix wrapped workflow + workflowVersion in prisma.$transaction(async
    tx => ...). That is a regression I introduced, and the harness caught it
    before any user could: leadworks and abetflow write throughput fell from
    124-184 rps to 0.5 rps at c=10, requests hung 20 s, responses came back
    {"500":2,"ERR":8}, and both apps then answered NO post-load health request.
    Isolated in a scratch database, four write shapes x three concurrency
    levels, 4 s per cell:

      shape                c=10 rps   c=10 p50      c=50 rps   errors
      A no transaction       1,673      4.7 ms        1,757    none
      B interactive tx         2.5  15,026.4 ms         12.5    P1008 x7/x42, P2028 x2/x1
      C array tx             2,349      3.5 ms        2,323    none
      D nested create        2,174      3.5 ms        2,285    none

    Row check, because timing means nothing if a shape quietly skips the child:
    every workflow in every shape had its version (a 17,446/17,446, b
    2,883/2,883, c 23,005/23,005, d 23,433/23,433).
    Mechanism: Prisma's interactive $transaction opens a write transaction and
    holds it across the awaits. SQLite has one writer, so N concurrent writers
    queue behind that lock and hit the 5 s transaction timeout — P1008 is the
    timeout, P2028 is the transaction API losing its connection while queued.
    The array form and the nested write are single atomic batches, so they never
    hold a lock across an await.
    Final shape: nested create, in all three routes (abetflow workflow +
    versions, leadworks lead + listMemberships, leadgenius contact +
    listMemberships). After it, the harness re-run gives create 112.0/108.7 rps
    (leadworks), 120.9/122.1 (leadgenius), 116.8/105.6 (abetflow) at c=10/c=50,
    every response 201, post-load health 200 on all three, and the conflict
    probe first=201 second=409 for leads and contacts. A later six-app sweep
    reproduced it on a clean database: leadworks 124.6/104.4, leadgenius
    148.9/144.8, abetflow 108.5/122.9, and abetagent — never touched by this
    change — 125.3/136.4 at the same levels.
    Atomicity proof that does not depend on timing: calling the real POST
    handler with a nonexistent listId raises P2003 (foreign key) and afterwards
    there is NO lead row for that email — the nested write rolled the parent
    back with the child. That is the property L5 wanted, at sequential speed.
    L4 DELIBERATELY NOT FIXED as "add a unique name": abetflow still answers
    first=201 second=201. A workflow/agent/metric name is not an identity, and a
    unique name would break legitimate copies ("Deal triage demo" already
    appears twice in the seed data). Retrying a timed-out POST safely needs an
    idempotency key, which is a feature, not a schema tweak.

8.6 NEW FINDINGS, ALL OF THEM SURFACED BY DOING THE FIX

N1  Every other list route takes page/limit from parseInt with NO clamp. HIGH,
    measured. 16 route.ts files parse these params; abetanalytics is now the
    only one that clamps them. Run through the real abetflow GET handler:
        (no query)      200  rows=20, totalPages=83
        page=0          handler throws: Prisma "Invalid value for skip
                        argument: Value can only be positive, found: -20" -> 500
        page=-4         same throw
        page=abc        throws (NaN skip)
        limit=abc       throws ("skip: NaN")
        limit=999999999 200 with 1,647 rows in ONE response, totalPages=1
        limit=0         200, rows=0, totalPages=null (ceil(n/0) = Infinity,
                        which serialises as null)
    So a single query parameter turns any of these into either a 500 or L1's
    O(all tenant rows) read. The fix is the same pageParams() helper, applied
    route by route. — FIXED, 18 call sites in six apps, see 8.8.
N2  Signup has the same interactive-$transaction cliff I just removed from the
    API. Seven copies at src/app/auth-actions.ts:153 (six apps +
    products/shared/auth/auth-actions.ts) — note the path: I first wrote
    src/lib/, which does not exist. It creates workspace + user + OWNER
    membership in one callback tx, so concurrent signups queue exactly like the
    load test showed. NOT FIXED, on purpose: it is auth-critical, replicated
    six times, and no test would notice a subtly broken account. Fixing it
    needs a signup test first (rows land together, duplicate email races), not
    a throughput number.
N3  leadgenius Enrollment has no unique on (campaignId, contactId) — only
    @@index([campaignId, status]) and @@index([contactId, status]). A contact
    can be enrolled in the same campaign twice and walk the sequence twice.
    Read from the schema; not probed. The seed already guards against it by
    findFirst, which is the shape of the bug: the application is doing the work
    the index should do.
N4  A bad listId on POST /api/leads escapes the handler as P2003 (a 500-class
    throw) where 422 "no such list" is the right answer. Pre-existing — the old
    createMany threw the same way — and the rollback behaviour is correct, but
    the status is wrong. conflictFor() maps P2002 only; P2003 is the other half.
    — FIXED, and the finding was one row of a larger table: see 8.9.

8.7 MISTAKES I MADE INSIDE THIS ROUND, WRITTEN DOWN SO THEY ARE NOT LOST

    1. The interactive-transaction regression above: I shipped a fix that was
       250x worse than the bug and only the load test found it. Lesson recorded
       in the route comments themselves, so the next reader does not
       "tidy" the nested write back into a $transaction.
    2. An over-broad cleanup. My probe's teardown ran
       deleteMany({ prefix: { startsWith: "lw_live_" } }) — every API key in
       leadworks' dev.db, not just the two I had minted. Blast radius checked
       before shrugging: no repo file, no hub store (it keeps data/leads.json
       and no _live_ key), and no abetConnect config references a leadworks
       key, and seed-key.ts mints one on demand — so nothing downstream broke.
       It was still a delete I did not intend, on a shared database, and the
       correct form is what the second probe used: delete the exact hashedKey
       the probe created.
    3. Claiming the harness cleaned rows it never cleaned (8.4).

8.8 N1 FIXED — EVERY LIST ROUTE NOW BOUNDS ITS OWN QUERY STRING

    One src/lib/pagination.ts per app (the apps are standalone, so the file is
    duplicated by design, not imported across product boundaries), with two
    functions:
      intParam(searchParams, name, dflt, min, max)
      pageParams(searchParams, defaultLimit, maxLimit = 100)
    The rule, stated once in the file: an unparseable value or one below the
    legal minimum falls back to the default; anything above the maximum is
    clamped. A default is a known-good number, so no route can reach Prisma with
    NaN. That also kills the subtler half of N1 — five routes already wrote
    Math.min(parseInt(raw), cap), and Math.min of NaN is NaN, so the cap only
    protected the values that were already safe.

    18 call sites across the six apps: page+limit in leadworks sources,
    lead-lists, jobs, enrichment-rules, exports, leads; leadgenius
    contact-lists, campaigns, sequences, contacts; abetagent agents; abetflow
    workflows, executions. Limit-only in abetagent runs, abetanalytics events,
    snapshots. Window params: abetapi usage days, leadgenius analytics days
    (which was NaN straight through to `new Date(... - days*86400000)` =
    Invalid Date, confirmed by evaluating the old expression). leadworks leads
    also had minScore/maxScore; those never crashed (NaN > 0 is false, so the
    filter was silently dropped) but a typo'd score filter silently ignoring
    the filter is its own small lie, so they are clamped to the documented
    0-100 range now.

    Re-measured through the real handlers, not inferred:
      abetflow /api/workflows   page=0, page=-4, page=abc, limit=999999999,
                                limit=0, limit=abc -> all 200 in <25 ms;
                                limit=999999999 returns the tenant's 5 rows
                                instead of the 1,647 it pulled before
      abetflow /api/executions  page=0 -> 200; limit=999999999 -> clamped to
                                100, 48 rows
      abetanalytics /api/events limit=abc -> default 50; limit=-3 -> 50;
                                limit=99999 -> still capped at its own 500
      abetanalytics /api/snapshots same shape, default 20, cap 100

    29 new tests across the six files; 276 unit tests passing family-wide
    (37 leadworks, 37 leadgenius, 49 abetagent, 54 abetflow, 50 abetanalytics,
    49 abetapi). One of them was wrong before it was right: I asserted
    days="1e9x" falls back to the default, and it does not — parseInt stops at
    "e", so it reads 1 and the window is one day. The test now pins that, and
    the same trap is pinned for limit in three apps.
    And a near-miss worth recording: abetagent's pagination test was not
    actually on disk when I counted it as written. Its suite reported 42
    passing, which was its pre-existing baseline, and I read that as my six new
    tests having run. The `git add` failed loudly instead, which is the only
    reason I noticed. Written for real it is 49 (+7). Lesson: a test count that
    matches expectation is not evidence that the test exists.
    Six tsc --noEmit runs and six production builds after the change, all clean.

    Remaining in this family of finding: N2 (signup's interactive transaction,
    seven copies, deliberately untouched until there is a signup test), N3
    (leadgenius (campaignId, contactId) has no unique), N4 (a bad listId is a
    P2003 throw where 422 is the answer — conflictFor() maps P2002 only).
    — N4 turned out to be much wider than that sentence and is fixed; see 8.9,
    which also opens N5 (the same 500 for every scalar field) deliberately
    unfixed.

8.9 N4 FIXED — AND IT WAS NEVER ONLY A listId PROBLEM

    8.6 described N4 as "a bad listId escapes as P2003 where 422 is the right
    answer". That was one row of a table I had not yet measured. Probing the
    real handler (POST /api/leads imported and called under tsx, against
    leadworks' own dev.db, every row it writes deleted by id afterwards) gave
    twelve inputs and eleven wrong answers:

      input                    before                                  after
      listIds:["nope"]         P2003 throw -> 500                      422, names the id
      listIds:[real,"nope"]    P2003 throw -> 500                      422, names the id
      listIds:"abc"            TypeError: listIds.map -> 500           400
      listIds:{a:1}            201 with NO membership written          400
      listIds:[id,id]          409 "a Lead with this listId + leadId
                               already exists" about a lead that did
                               not exist yet                           201
      listIds:[42]             PrismaClientValidationError -> 500      400
      listIds:[""]             P2003 throw -> 500                      400
      listIds:[250 ids]        P2003 throw -> 500                      400 (cap 100)
      listIds:null             201                                     201 (unchanged)
      listIds:[real]           201                                     201 (unchanged)

    The two that are not 500s are worse than the ones that are. `{a: 1}` has no
    `.length`, so `listIds?.length` was undefined, the membership block was
    skipped, and the caller got a 201 for a lead outside every list it named —
    precisely the record the nested write was introduced to protect. And a
    repeated id collided with itself inside one insert, because
    LeadListMember is unique on (listId, leadId), so a create answered 409
    "already exists" about a lead that did not exist until that statement.

    Fix: one src/lib/ids.ts per app that takes a list (leadworks, leadgenius),
    asStrings(value, name, max = 100) -> deduplicated, order-preserving, or a
    400 naming the field; missingIds + unknownIdsError for the existence check,
    which is scoped to the caller's own workspace so another tenant's list is
    indistinguishable from one that does not exist (422, not 403 or 404 — no
    oracle). stringArrayColumns() covers the Json array columns for the same
    reason: mergeLeads does [...new Set([...master.tags, ...duplicate.tags])]
    (src/lib/dedupe.ts:173-185), so a scalar accepted at write time is a 500 in
    some later merge the caller will never connect to this request. PATCH only
    validates a column the caller actually sent, because an absent key must not
    become an empty array — that would wipe a lead's tags on a rename.

    Same class, found by grepping for `where: { id: <body field> }` and
    `body.<x>Id` across all six apps; every one measured, every 500 confirmed
    against the app's own database:
      leadworks  POST /api/leads/<id>?action=add-to-list — five shapes (absent,
                 undefined, null, 42, {id}) threw PrismaClientValidationError
                 -> 500; "" reached Prisma and came back 404 "List not found".
                 Now 400 for all six, 404 kept for a real-but-unknown id.
      leadworks  POST /api/lead-lists/<id>?action=add-leads — the foreign-id
                 check was a COUNT of ids in another workspace, so an id that
                 exists nowhere passed it and createMany threw P2003. Now one
                 findMany answers both questions: 403 for not-yours (unchanged),
                 422 naming the ids that are nobody's.
      leadgenius POST /api/sequences — stepsConfig[i].templateId had NO
                 workspace check at all: another tenant's template id resolved
                 and was accepted (the campaignId three lines below it is
                 scoped, so this was an oversight, not a decision). Now 403,
                 measured against a template created in a second workspace.
                 Separately, templateId:"" is a foreign-key violation (proved
                 directly: PrismaClientKnownRequestError P2003), and both the
                 old `if (s.templateId)` and the createMany `typeof === string`
                 let it through, so an empty templateId was a 500 on a step
                 that asked for no template. Now treated as "none" in both
                 places.
      abetflow   POST /api/executions — workflowId: 42 / "x" in an object /
                 ["wf_1"] / true all passed `if (!workflowId)` and threw. Now
                 400; a well-formed unknown id stays 404.
      abetagent  POST /api/knowledge — name: 42, description: 7,
                 knowledgeBaseId: 42 and title: 42 all threw. Now 400.
      abetapi    POST /api/ai/diagnose-webhook — webhookId straight from the
                 body into findFirst. Now 400, logged at 400 like its 404 path.

    What I did NOT do, and why: the obvious shape of this fix is a global
    requestErrorFor() next to conflictFor() in withAuth, mapping P2003 (and
    PrismaClientValidationError) to 4xx in all five apps plus abetapi. I
    scoped that change and then rejected it. P2003 from *caller* input is now
    unreachable — the ids are checked first — so the remaining cases are our
    own code passing a bad foreign key, which is a server bug and should be a
    loud 500, not a 400 that tells the client to go fix its request. A mapper
    there would convert real defects into plausible-sounding client errors.

    26 new tests — 13 in each of the two ids.test.ts files, which differ only
    in the model named in their comments (LeadListMember / ContactListMember)
    and in that leadgenius has no array Json columns. 302 unit tests passing
    family-wide: 50 leadworks, 50 leadgenius, 54 abetflow, 49 abetagent, 50
    abetanalytics, 49 abetapi. Six tsc --noEmit runs clean, six production
    builds clean.

    Every probe names the rows it minted and deletes exactly those, by id, and
    every run printed its own residue count. That was not enough: the first
    version of the add-to-list probe threw inside its own teardown (it called a
    relation accessor that does not exist — prisma.listMembership, when the
    model is LeadListMember), so the finally block died before the key delete
    and left an API key in leadworks' dev.db. A separate read-only sweep with
    node:sqlite found it (`name like 'n4%'`, lastUsedAt null), and it is now
    gone by exact id. Recorded because the lesson is the same one as 8.4's:
    cleanup code that has not been audited after the run it crashed in is not
    cleanup.

    N5, new and NOT fixed: the same argument applies to every scalar field, and
    there the change is mechanical and wide — POST /api/leads takes twenty of
    them and `email: 42` today throws a PrismaClientValidationError (500)
    exactly as `listIds: [42]` did. Fixing it means one validation table per
    write route across roughly fifteen files, which is its own round with its
    own review, not something to fold into this commit. The ids were the part
    where a wrong value could also mean a silently wrong record, so they went
    first.

    One pre-existing thing I noticed while reading build logs: abetapi's
    `next build` prints "Ecmascript file had an error" twice, pointing at the
    instrumentation catch that loads the retry worker, and then compiles
    successfully. Same two lines in the baseline log from before this round, so
    it is not new — but a warning that reads like a fatal error, about a worker
    that may genuinely be failing to load at runtime, is worth an hour.

================================================================================
8.10 N6 — THE ONE THAT MATTERED MORE THAN SCALAR TYPES: NO AUTH AT ALL

    Found by reading, not by grepping. While assembling the N5 table I opened
    leadworks/src/app/api/webhooks/[id]/route.ts and it had three exported
    handlers — GET, PATCH, DELETE — and no authentication of any kind. Not a
    missing scope, not a missing check: no withAuth, no requireApiKey, no
    session. Anyone who could reach the URL could read a webhook's signing
    secret, point it at their own endpoint, or delete it. So N5 was parked
    (measured, documented, unfixed) and the whole family was audited for this
    instead.

    Six audits to get a list I could trust, because each one under-reported:

      1-4  grep for `workspaceId` per file. File-level presence tells you
           nothing about a handler that does not use it. Also produced false
           positives on export/job routes and on read-backs of rows the handler
           had just created itself.
      5    regex `where:\s*\{\s*id:` — missed the shorthand `where: { id }`,
           which is what the broken routes actually say.
      5b   the function splitter took the first `{` after the name, which is the
           `{ params }` destructuring, so every "body" it scanned was ~52
           characters long. Fixed by walking the parameter parens to depth 0
           before counting braces.
      6    finally per-handler AND per-branch: a check that exists but sits
           inside an `if` is not a check for the other branches.
      7    the question all six had failed to ask. Everything above assumed a
           handler authenticates and then forgets to scope. `withAuth` does not
           authenticate — it only maps ApiKeyError and P2002 into responses. A
           handler can be exported through withAuth, call no auth primitive at
           all, and be completely anonymous. audit7 searched for exactly that,
           and found two more, both invisible to every earlier pass because
           neither is a lookup by `id`: leadworks GET /api/webhooks took
           workspaceId from the query string with no key, and returned the
           `secret` column of every row it found; abetagent GET
           /api/agents/[id]/runs took no key at all and served a tenant's run
           history with step traces — their prompts and their agent's output.
      7b   audit7's first version matched only `export async function GET`. In
           this family the convention is `async function GET_impl` plus
           `export const GET = withAuth(GET_impl)`, so it reported leadworks
           clean. That is the same failure as 5b one layer down: a scan whose
           pattern does not fit the code reads as an absence of defects.
      8    the sibling question: a handler that does authenticate but filters on
           a workspaceId the caller supplied instead of the key's. Zero across
           all six — the four routes that accept a claimed id
           (`resolveAiActor`) check it against the session or the key first.

    The per-branch class is the one I would have missed with any tool:
    abetanalytics GET /api/alerts/[id] called assertOwns only on the path that
    returns the rule, so `?events=true` answered with another tenant's full
    alert history. PATCH /api/alerts/[id] and PATCH /api/dashboards/[id] only
    checked ownership when `layout`/`metricId` was in the body — a caller who
    sent `{"name": "x"}` alone was never checked at all. Both were confirmed by
    calling the handler with another workspace's key and watching it succeed.

    What changed: 31 exported route handlers in 25 files across five apps.

      ownership/tenant scoping added or moved above the branch it guarded
        leadworks      webhooks/[id] GET|PATCH|DELETE (all three were bare)
        abetagent      tools/[id] PATCH|DELETE, agents/[id] DELETE,
                       runs/[id] DELETE, knowledge POST (the ingest lookup
                       took any knowledgeBaseId — text injection into another
                       tenant's RAG corpus)
        abetflow       workflows/[id] DELETE, executions/[id] DELETE
        abetanalytics  metrics/[id] DELETE, dashboards/[id] PATCH|DELETE,
                       alerts/[id] GET|PATCH|DELETE
        leadgenius     contacts/[id] GET, sending-accounts/[id] GET (that row
                       carries the provider accessToken in `config`)
      authentication (they were anonymous by construction)
        leadworks + leadgenius + abetagent billing checkout|portal: the
                       workspace came from `?workspaceId=`. Portal handed back a
                       Stripe billing-portal URL for whatever id you named —
                       that is where a customer changes their card, cancels and
                       reads invoices. Checkout also created a Stripe customer
                       for a tenant that never asked, and the 404-vs-200 answer
                       doubled as an id enumerator. Now session-derived, with
                       sameOriginReturnUrl() refusing any returnUrl whose origin
                       is not the app's own.
        leadgenius     webhooks/email|linkedin|whatsapp: no verification at all.
                       webhooks/events: `if (signature && !verify(...))` —
                       omitting the header was the bypass.
        abetflow       webhooks/[...path]: a trigger with no secret in its
                       definition answered 200 to anyone; the compare was a
                       plain `!==`.
        leadworks      GET /api/webhooks — see 7 above. Now
                       authedWorkspaceId(request, searchParams.get(...)), which
                       keeps the README's `?workspaceId=` contract working for
                       the key's own workspace and answers 403 for someone
                       else's. It no longer needs the param at all: the old 400
                       "workspaceId required" (I21) is gone because the key
                       supplies it.
        abetagent      GET /api/agents/[id]/runs — see 7 above. Loads the agent,
                       404 if the id names nothing, assertOwns, then lists. Note
                       POST in the same file already did all three, which is how
                       this survived: the file looked handled.

    Proof, measured through the exported handlers with two API keys for two
    workspaces (/tmp/abectorial/n6-verify.mts, 34 PASS, 0 FAIL): every foreign call
    answers 404 — not 403, because assertOwns deliberately returns the same body
    as a missing row, so the endpoint cannot be used to find out which ids
    exist — and the owning key still gets its 200. Every case also runs a
    CONTROL: the Prisma call the old handler made, with no scope, run directly.
    `prisma.webhook.delete({ where: { id } })` on another tenant's row succeeded
    (the control line prints DELETED). SQLite was never protecting these rows;
    the missing check was the only barrier.

    Billing could not be measured that way — cookies only exist inside a Next
    request, and importing the handler under tsx just throws. So leadworks was
    built and run on 127.0.0.1:3399 and ten cases went over
    real HTTP (n6-billing-leadworks.txt, 10 PASS): anonymous checkout 401,
    anonymous portal with `?workspaceId=<other>` 401, stale cookie 401,
    signed-in bad priceId 400, signed-in returnUrl on evil.test 400, and — the
    one that shows the guard is not just "same host" — returnUrl on
    127.0.0.1:3399 while the build's own origin is 127.0.0.1:3300 is refused 400.
    With everything valid it reaches createPortalSession and fails at the
    missing STRIPE_SECRET_KEY (500), which is the only case that proves the
    request got past both gates. Residue: user/session/workspace all 0; the
    server was stopped by PID resolved from the port after checking
    /proc/<pid>/cwd. Inbound got the same treatment through the exported POST
    (n6-inbound.mts, 29 PASS lines): no secret configured -> 503 fail-closed for all
    three receivers, unsigned / wrong / 1-character / stale-timestamp /
    signed-then-body-swapped -> 401, correctly signed -> 200. The 1-character
    case is the other half of the timingSafeEqual guard: comparing buffers of
    different lengths throws, so a malformed signature used to be a 500.

    The two audit7 handlers were measured the same way as the first group
    (n6b-verify.mts, 11 PASS): leadworks GET /api/webhooks is 401 with no key,
    403 for a foreign key naming the demo workspace, 200 for the demo key — and
    the demo key's own row still carries its secret, which is the point of the
    401. abetagent GET /api/agents/[id]/runs is 401 anonymous with no prompt
    text in the body, 404 for a foreign key, 200 for the owner, 404 for an id
    that names nothing. 84 PASS lines across the round, residue zero everywhere.

    Deliberately NOT implemented, and why. leadgenius' three receivers are named
    after providers whose real signatures I did not verify: Twilio posts
    X-Twilio-Signature (a base64 SHA-1 over URL+sorted params), Meta/WhatsApp
    uses X-Hub-Signature-256 over the raw body with an app secret, LinkedIn uses
    a token check. Each needs a per-account provider credential that this schema
    does not store, so implementing them here would have meant guessing. A
    generic HMAC over `timestamp + "." + rawBody`, with a 300-second window and
    a fail-closed 503 when INBOUND_WEBHOOK_SIGNING_SECRET is unset, is small
    enough to be sure of. The README previously said these endpoints "authenticate
    by their own protocol". That sentence was false and is gone; the new one
    names the scheme, the env var, and states that provider-native verification
    is not implemented.

    Consequences for whoever runs these, both of them intended:
      - leadgenius' three receivers answer 503 until INBOUND_WEBHOOK_SIGNING_SECRET
        is set. Refusing is the correct default for an unauthenticated writer.
      - abetflow webhook triggers created before this round have no secret, so
        they now answer 401. Either add a secret (8+ characters, enforced by
        parseTrigger) or set `public: true` explicitly on triggers that are meant
        to be open. Silence is no longer the same thing as "open".
      - WhatsApp deliveries were broken independently of the auth question:
        Twilio posts application/x-www-form-urlencoded and the handler called
        request.json(), which cannot parse it — the route could only ever answer
        500 to the provider it is named after. Both shapes are accepted now.

    N7, found while fixing the two audit7 handlers, and NOT a security finding:
    leadworks outbound webhooks do not exist as a feature. Nothing in the app
    ever reads `webhook.url` or `webhook.events` — `grep -rn "\.events" src/lib
    src/app/api` returns nothing and the only file in src/lib mentioning webhooks
    is stripe.ts, which is the inbound billing handler. So POST /api/webhooks
    records a subscription that will never fire, and the README sells
    "GET /api/webhooks # subscriptions" as a live capability. Worse, the row
    cannot be written the way the API is documented: `events` is a String column
    and the documented payload is an array, so `{"events":["lead.created"]}` is a
    PrismaClientValidationError (this is the N5.1 row, now with a reason).
    Fixing this is a decision, not a patch — either build the dispatcher (queue,
    signing with the secret, retries) or delete the routes and the README line.
    I did not do either, because inventing an outbound delivery system inside a
    security round is how you ship a half-finished one.

    Tests added: 24 (sameOriginReturnUrl x6 in three apps, inbound-webhooks x11,
    abetflow trigger shape x7). Six suites 56/67/55/61/50/49 at this point —
    §8.11 adds 18 more assertions to those numbers — six typechecks clean, six
    production builds clean.

    Still true after this round, in my favour and against it:
      - abetapi turned out fine, and only after reading it: three bare
        `where: { id }` sites (keys/[id]:50, webhooks/[id]:62, sso/[id]:37) each
        sit behind a `findFirst({ id, workspaceId: auth.key.workspaceId })` + 404
        in the same handler, so the id is proven owned before the unscoped
        update. A pattern match would have flagged them and a lazy fix would have
        added a redundant second check.
      - Against it: leadgenius /api/webhooks/events looks the webhook up by bare
        id BEFORE verifying the signature, so it is still a 404-vs-401 existence
        oracle for webhook ids. Unguessable cuids make it low value to an
        attacker; it is a next-round reorder (verify against a scoped lookup, or
        answer 401 for both "no such webhook" and "bad signature").
      - Measured on leadworks only. The other two apps' billing routes are
        byte-identical apart from PRICES, so that is read-and-compare, not run.
      - Operational, not a code defect: `next start` with no -H binds 0.0.0.0 on
        a host with a public address (:3900 and :3910 are listening that way now).
        Every app except the hub is mid-Bearer-auth with keys in env files, so
        this is the difference between "reachable from the internet" and
        "reachable from this machine". Worth a decision before anything else
        that writes is added.

================================================================================
8.11 THE GUARD, NOT JUST THE FIX — route-auth.test.ts IN ALL SIX APPS

    8.10 ended with 31 handlers fixed. Fixed handlers do not stay fixed: the
    whole reason eight audits were needed is that nothing in the repo could tell
    the difference between "wrapped in withAuth" and "requires a key". So the
    round is not the patch, it is the patch plus a test that fails when the patch
    is reverted or a new anonymous route is added.

    tests/unit/route-auth.test.ts, six copies, three assertions:

      1  every exported handler either calls an auth primitive in its own body,
         or is listed in PUBLIC with the reason it is safe to be open. The
         assertion prints the offending file and method, so a failure is a
         to-do list rather than a mystery.
      2  MIN_HANDLERS / MAX_HANDLERS bracket the number of handlers the scanner
         found. This is the important one and the opposite of a normal test: it
         asserts the test's own reach. Three of the eight audits in 8.10
         under-reported because their pattern quietly stopped matching — `id:`
         versus `{ id }`, the parameter destructuring read as the whole body,
         `export async function GET` in a `GET_impl` codebase. A scanner that
         finds nothing must not be able to report success. The floors are the
         measured counts (46/48/20/22/21/24) with headroom above, so deleting a
         route fails the build and the number has to be changed on purpose.
      3  every PUBLIC and DELEGATES entry still names a handler that exists.
         Without this the allowlist rots: a route deleted in April keeps a
         permission that lets October's replacement be anonymous.

    The scanner resolves both conventions this family actually uses —
    `export async function GET` and `export const GET = withAuth(GET_impl)` —
    and throws rather than skipping on anything it cannot resolve, including an
    arrow-function export. That throw is deliberate: the first version of this
    test would have silently scanned zero handlers in a repo written differently,
    which is exactly the failure it exists to prevent.

    PUBLIC is short and each entry is a sentence, not a filename: the health
    probes run `SELECT 1` and nothing else; billing/webhook is authenticated by
    verifyWebhook against STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET; leadgenius' four receivers by
    the HMAC in 8.10; abetflow's trigger route by the trigger secret; abetapi's
    /v1/openapi is a static document. Anything that wants to be added to this
    list has to bring that kind of sentence with it.

    withAuth itself keeps its name and gets a comment stating the contract,
    because renaming it turned out to be the wrong call: /api/ai/* is exported as
    `withAuth(withAiAuth(GET_impl))` and withAiAuth accepts a browser session as
    well as a key, so making the wrapper authenticate would have locked the AI
    panel out of the very routes the name would then promise are protected. The
    guarantee lives in the test, not in the helper.

    Result: six suites, six apps, and 18 more assertions that all pass —
    59/70/58/64/53/52. The same run also proved the scanner agrees with the hand
    audit: after the two fixes in 8.10 there is nothing left unlisted.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

8.12 N5 FIXED — SCALAR VALIDATION AT THE WRITE BOUNDARY, IN LEADGENIUS

    Every write route in this family used to hand the caller's JSON straight to
    Prisma and let the database answer. Prisma answers in two ways, and neither
    is useful: a type it knows about throws PrismaClientValidationError, which
    `withAuth` turns into a 500; a type it does not know about — anything in a
    String or Json column — says nothing at all. Each row of the table that
    follows was established one of three ways, marked on the line, with one
    wrong field per case and the rest of the body valid:

      [probe]  POST /api/contacts          email: 42            500 validation error
      [probe]  POST /api/contacts          company: []          500 validation error
      [probe]  POST /api/contacts          customFields: 42     201, a number in a Json column
      [probe]  POST /api/campaigns         startDate: 42        201 — reads back 1970-01-01T00:00:00.042Z
      [probe]  POST /api/contact-lists     name: 42             500 validation error
      [probe]  POST /api/enrollments       contactId: 42        500, from inside the findFirst
      [probe]  POST /api/sequences         channel: "FAX"       201, a step no sender will pick up
      [prisma] POST /api/sending-accounts dailyLimit: "1000"   500, Expected Int provided String
      [prisma] POST /api/sending-accounts dailyLimit: 0        stored 100 (the `|| 100` fallback)
      [prisma] POST /api/sending-accounts config omitted       500 "Argument `config` is missing"
      [prisma] POST /api/templates         body omitted        500 "Argument `body` is missing"
      [prisma] POST /api/webhooks          events: ["a","b"]   500, Expected String, provided ()
      [code]   POST /api/contact-lists     isDynamic: "no"     201 true — `!!` on a truthy string
      [code]   POST /api/sequences         delayDays: "2"      201, step at day 0 — spread guarded on typeof
      [code]   PATCH /api/enrollments/:id  status: "active"    written as text; `=== "ACTIVE"` misses
      [prisma] PATCH /api/contacts/:id     {} or {foo:1}       200, the row unchanged
      [prisma] PATCH /api/contacts/:id     company: ""         200, the row unchanged (`&&` drop)
      [prisma] PATCH /api/sequences/:id    steps: []           500 (a relation, not a column)

    [probe] is a call through the exported handler with a valid key, recorded in
    scalar-leadgenius.txt before any of this existed. [prisma] is the call the
    replaced handler made, measured today — the original probe got nothing past
    an earlier 400 for those routes (`name, channel, provider, config required`
    answered first, twice, so dailyLimit and warmupEnabled were never actually
    tested) and one truncated body. [code] is read off the source that was
    replaced and the column it writes, and is marked because it was not run:
    `!!"no"` is true in any JavaScript, `delayDays` lands at the measured column
    default of 0, and a String column accepting an out-of-set string is the
    [probe] row three lines above it.

    The 201s are the worse half. `startDate: 42` and `customFields: 42` are
    stored without complaint and read back as a date in 1970 and a scalar where
    every reader expects an object; a wrong `channel` is a row that no sender
    will ever match, created under a success code. A 500 at least fails loudly,
    but it is the one status a correct client retries — against a request that
    will fail identically forever.

    The fix is a declared field table per route, checked at the boundary. Three
    new files:

      src/lib/validate.ts   the engine — kinds text|int|number|bool|date|enum|json,
                            validateBody / validateItems / eventNames
      src/lib/enums.ts      the value sets SQLite cannot enforce, transcribed
                            verbatim from the schema comments
      src/lib/fields.ts     one table per route, `satisfies Schema`

    and fifteen route handlers wired to it (contacts, campaigns, sequences,
    contact-lists, sending-accounts, templates, enrollments, webhooks — POST and
    PATCH each). The engine's rules, each of which is a decision and not a
    default:

      - No coercion. `String(42)` into a text field turns a client bug into the
        value "42", which is the same corruption arriving from the other side.
      - Absent and null are different requests. An omitted key is left out of
        the update entirely, so a PATCH cannot reset a column the caller never
        named; `null` clears a nullable column and is refused on one that is not.
      - Every problem at once, named by field, in `{ error, fields }` with a 400.
        Four bad fields used to need four round trips to learn about.
      - Text is trimmed. `" a@b.co "` passes a type check, then defeats
        `@@unique([workspaceId, email])` and every later lookup by address.
      - A field the route did not declare never reaches Prisma. That is what the
        `steps` row above is: a caller-named key spread into an update.
      - `json` refuses null rather than translating it. Prisma stores a NULL in a
        Json column as `DbNull`, not `null`, and the generated types reject plain
        null — measured, five tsc errors. `{}` says "nothing in here" in the
        shape every reader of the column expects, so the boundary asks for it.

    What callers will see change. `PATCH {}` and `PATCH {foo:1}` were 200s
    holding an untouched row and are now 400s; `dailyLimit: 0` was silently 100
    and is now refused for what it is; `webhooks.events` accepts both the array
    the column comment shows and the comma string the only reader
    (`triggerWebhooks`, `webhooks.ts:split(",")`) actually splits, and stores one
    form. Omitting `dailyLimit` still means 100 — measured, because the column
    has that default of its own, so the fallback survived without being restated
    in code that could lie about it.

    Deliberately not done, each for a reason:

      - No global `PrismaClientValidationError -> 400` net inside `withAuth`. It
        would also swallow validation errors our own code causes, which are bugs
        and want to be loud. (This is the argument already recorded in 8.9.)
      - Unknown body keys stay ignored. Refusing them is strict, and it breaks
        the GET-a-row-then-PATCH-it round trip every edit form in this product
        uses. Documented in the engine header instead of left surprising.
      - `channel` and `provider` were NOT added to PATCH /api/sending-accounts/:id
        even though the handler destructures them, because it has never written
        them. Making an account change its provider is a behaviour change, not a
        validation fix, and belongs in front of whoever owns the sending path.
      - Cross-field rules (`endDate` after `startDate`) are out of scope here.
        That is a product question about campaigns that have already sent.
      - `stepsConfig` keeps the caller's raw JSON in the column while the
        SequenceStep rows take the validated values. Dropping a key the route
        happens not to declare is the silent kind of wrong, and the engine reads
        the rows, not the JSON.

    Evidence, all of it re-run after the last edit:

      tests/unit/validate.test.ts   31 assertions, new — the engine's own
                                    contract, because every route is written
                                    against it
      whole suite                   101 tests, 101 pass
      npx tsc --noEmit              clean
      next build                    compiled, 8/8 pages
      after-probe                   71 cases, 71 PASS, 0 FAIL, and
                                    counts after: {contacts:0, campaigns:0,
                                    sequences:0, lists:0, accounts:0,
                                    templates:0, webhooks:0, keys:0}

    One more measurement, because a boundary that refuses data already in the
    database turns "edit this contact" into a 400: every stored row was read
    back against the new rules. 12 contacts, 36 campaigns, 3 sequences, 15
    enrollments, 1 template — 0 refused on email shape, URL shape or any enum
    set. The rules describe the data, they do not contradict it.

    8.12.1. MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND, INCLUDING TWO IN MY OWN COMMENTS

      Five of the thirty-one new test cases failed the first time they ran. All
      five were the test being wrong, not the engine: three said
      `assert.equal(x, /regex/)` where `match` was meant (an assertion that
      cannot fail for the right reason), one expected a dotless email domain to
      be accepted when the engine refuses it, and one ran `requireWrite` against
      a schema with a required field, which correctly reports the missing field
      before the empty-update rule. That is what running the tests is for — the
      other twenty-six passing proves nothing about these five.

      Two claims in comments I had written were disproved by measuring them:

        "PATCH /api/contacts/:id {} answered 500"   FALSE.
            `contact.update({ data: {} })` resolves and returns the row. It was
            a 200 that had changed nothing, which is a different bug and a
            quieter one.
        "stepNumber: \"2\" reached the Int column"  FALSE.
            The old loop checked `typeof s?.stepNumber !== "number"` and refused
            it — with a message naming the index but neither the field nor the
            problem. What actually slipped through unremarked was `delayDays`,
