            guarded by a spread that dropped a non-number, so the step took the
            column default of 0. Measured: a step created without delayDays
            reads back delayDays=0, delayHours=0, and sequence-engine.ts:134
            adds that to the send time.

      A third error was in the probe itself, and it survived a full green run:
      the note that "measured" `sequence.update({ data: {} })` passed
      `where: { id: "none" }`, so it threw P2025 — no record found — and told me
      nothing about the empty update it was there to test. Every note now runs
      against a real control row created earlier in the same script. A green
      number is not evidence until something has checked what it measured.

      The probe was also wrong about its own subject twice over before this: its
      enrollment cases used fabricated ids, so they were measuring the 404 that
      precedes validation rather than validation. One of those cases is reframed
      as the ordering fact it is ("unknown id: 404 before field rules") and the
      accept-cases were dropped rather than fixed, because pushing a real row
      onto the sequence queue to test a type check trades one problem for a
      noisier one.

      The fourth mistake was in this document rather than in the code, and it is
      the one worth noticing: the first draft of the table above presented every
      row as "measured through the exported handlers", including six rows that
      were not. Two of those — `webhooks.events` as an array, and what
      `startDate: 42` actually stored — were written down as facts before
      anything had checked them, and only became measured when the Prisma calls
      were made today (an array is refused as `Expected String, provided ()`;
      the date reads back `1970-01-01T00:00:00.042Z`, so that one happened to be
      right). A summary carried forward across a compaction is a claim that has
      been re-stated, not one that has been re-checked, and the three kinds of
      evidence here now say which they are.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

8.13 N5, LEADWORKS — THE SAME BOUNDARY, AND THE FIRST TIME IT REFUSED DATA THAT
     WAS ALREADY IN THE DATABASE

    leadgenius' engine (§8.12) is in leadworks as `538a152`: `src/lib/validate.ts`
    (578 lines), `src/lib/fields.ts` (236 lines, fourteen tables), `src/lib/enums.ts`
    (the seven sets SQLite cannot enforce, transcribed from the schema comments).
    26 files changed, +1780/-313. Every row of the before-table in the engine's
    header is marked [probe], [code] or [probe+code]; the [probe] rows are 71 cases
    sent through the exported handlers with a valid key and an otherwise-valid body,
    recorded in `/tmp/abectorial/n5-lw-before.txt` as `71 PASS, 0 FAIL` before
    anything was wired.

    Two things leadworks added that leadgenius did not need:

      - `src/lib/ids.ts` gained `unknownIdError`/`unknownIdsError`, so a scalar
        foreign key with no row in this workspace is a 422 naming it. Measured,
        `POST /api/jobs {sourceId:"nope"}` was a `PrismaClientKnownRequestError`,
        i.e. a 500, and `{profileId:"nope"}` the same.
      - `queue.ts` now has a runner table written as a `Record` over the `Job.type`
        set. Two actions in this app returned `{"jobId": …}` for a row they had only
        inserted: `?action=retry` and `?action=run`, because the file imported
        `queues` and never called `enqueue`. A new job type with no worker is now a
        compile error instead of a row that sits PENDING forever.

    THE LIMB. §8.12 closed with "the rules describe the data, they do not contradict
    it", measured on leadgenius' rows. leadworks is the first app in the family where
    that sentence is false, so it was measured rather than hoped:

      stored: leads=45 sources=37 jobs=135 rules=5 exports=67
      refused by the new tables: score 0, Lead.status 0, Lead.seniority 0,
                                 Job.type 0, Job.status 0, Rule.trigger 0,
                                 Source.type 32, Export.format 1

    32 `Source` rows hold `type: "manual"` against a declared `MANUAL`, and one
    `Export` holds `format: "csv"` against `CSV`. Both predate this work: the backup
    of this database taken before the campaign (`/tmp/abectorial/db-backup/leadworks.db`)
    has the same 3 WEB_SCRAPER + 32 manual and the same 64 CSV + 1 csv. All 32 are in
    one workspace (`cmu99qk5y0000ni35n530i6ld`), all named `Sim <timestamp>`, all
    dated 2026-09-24/25 — a load simulator's rows, not route writes, and no seed or
    script in the repo produces them (`prisma/seed.ts` writes `WEB_SCRAPER`; `grep`
    across `src` finds `Source.type` read by nothing but its own enum declaration,
    and no `.tsx` calls `/api/sources` at all).

    The consequence, measured rather than argued — against a COPY of dev.db, because
    the first time I asked this question I wrote to the live file (see 8.13.1):

      PATCH {name:"…"}                        200, the row keeps its "manual"
      PATCH the row back exactly as it was read  400 "type must be one of … got \"manual\""
      PATCH {type:"MANUAL"}                   200, and the row is normalised
      the one lowercase-csv Export            status FAILED, and exports have no PATCH

    So "edit this source" still works; what breaks is the round trip that re-sends
    `type`, and it breaks with a message that names the field and offers the set. The
    three options were case-folding `Source.type` the way `format` is folded,
    rewriting the 32 rows, or recording the limb. Folding was refused: `format` is
    folded because the route has upper-cased it since before this file existed, and
    `Source.type` never folded anything, so making it fold is a data migration
    dressed up as a type check. Rewriting the rows was refused for the same class of
    reason — it is a change to a tenant's data, made by a commit about request
    parsing. The boundary stays strict, and the 32 rows are named here.

    A gap in the guard, found while writing this section.
    `tests/unit/route-validation.test.ts` asked "does this handler call
    `readJson()`" — matching the helper rather than the act — so
    `POST /api/billing/checkout` and `POST /api/billing/portal`, which both read a
    body with `await request.json().catch(() => null)`, were invisible to a rule that
    is supposed to see every body. The rule now matches both spellings and the two
    handlers are in its `UNVALIDATED` allowlist with the reason: session-authed, no
    Prisma write, `priceId` refused unless it is a key of `PRICES`, `returnUrl`
    through `sameOriginReturnUrl` (a same-origin test, not a field shape), and a
    non-JSON body caught into a 400. Those four claims were read off the handlers
    before they were written down. The allowlist has its own test that fails if an
    entry stops matching a real handler, so an excuse cannot outlive its subject.

    Evidence, re-run after the last edit:

      n5-lw-before.mts              71 cases, 71 PASS, 0 FAIL (pre-fix)
      n5-lw-after.mts               89 cases, 89 PASS, 0 FAIL
      tests/unit/*.test.ts          97 tests, 97 pass
      npx tsc --noEmit              clean
      next build                    exit 0
      dev.db after all of it        leads 42, sources 35, jobs 130, rules 3,
                                    exports 65, webhooks 0, lists 0, one key
                                    — identical to the pre-campaign backup

    8.13.1. MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND

      Three of the after-probe's 89 cases failed, and all three were the probe
      being wrong, not the app: I expected `"score is 9999; at most 100"` where the
      int-range rule says `"score must be at most 100"`; I expected
      `fields holds "nope"` in a JSON body that escapes its own quotes; and one
      `report()` call was handed a field value where a status belongs. The app
      answered correctly in all three, which is exactly why the number in the table
      above is 89/0 and not 86/3 remembered from an earlier run.

      The second one is mine and the worse one. To find out whether a `manual`
      source is still editable I ran the PATCH probes against leadworks' live
      dev.db, and one of them — `PATCH {type:"MANUAL"}` — succeeded, renaming a
      simulator's row and normalising its type. It was restored from the backup
      (`name`, `type` and `updatedAt`, the only three columns that differed) and the
      difference is now zero, but the answer to the question did not need to be
      bought that way: every measurement of a stored row since has run against a
      copy of the file. A probe that writes to the data it is auditing is a probe
      that changes what the next one sees.

      The third is in this document's own shadow. `tests/unit/validate.test.ts`
      cites "the after-table in improvements §8.13" — a section that did not exist
      when that line was committed. It exists now, and the citation points at the
      89/0 above, but a forward reference to a promise is not evidence, and the
      same class of mistake is what §8.12.1 already warned about.

      Recorded and not fixed: a `describe(undefined)` in the engine renders as
      "a undefined", so one webhook message reads "…got a undefined". Cosmetic, in
      a 400 whose field name and set are both right; it wants a one-word fix in the
      engine rather than a special case at the call site.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

8.14 N5, ABETAGENT — VALUES QUIETLY NOT STORED, AND AN OWNERSHIP CHECK BURIED IN
     A BRANCH

    Same engine, third app, and a different shape of failure. leadgenius and
    leadworks mostly stored the wrong thing; abetagent often stored nothing at
    all. Its before-table's 25 recorded rows (`n5-aa-before.mts`, sent over HTTP to
    this app's own dev server on :3400 with a minted key) are full of successes that
    dropped a field on the floor:

      POST /api/agents     model: 42              201, model "" — the value dropped
      POST /api/agents     tools: "kb_search"     201, tools [] — filtered away
      POST /api/tools      isActive: "no"         201, isActive true, via Boolean()
      POST …/:id/runs      subject: 42            202, subject null — dropped
      POST /api/agents     status: "PAUSEDX"      201, and every later run 409s

    `typeof model === "string" ? model : ""` is not a check, it is a default that
    fires on a wrong type: a number where a model name belongs moved an agent to
    heuristic-only and told nobody. `.filter(t => typeof t === "string")` on `tools`
    makes a scalar vanish, so an agent gets no tools with a 201. `Boolean("no")` is
    true. And `status` is a String column whose legal values live in a comment, so
    "PAUSEDX" indexes like any other value while every `status !== "ACTIVE"` guard in
    the app refuses it — the agent exists and runs nothing.

    The N6 regression, found here rather than by the guard. Wiring
    `PATCH /api/agents/[id]` meant reading where its ownership check actually sat:

      if (tools !== undefined) {
        if (unknown.length > 0) {            // assertOwns was in HERE
          await assertOwns(request, agent.workspaceId);

    So any PATCH that did not mention `tools` — every rename, every pause — reached
    `prisma.agent.update({ where: { id } })` with no key check of any kind. Measured
    before the fix: the same request with no Authorization header at all answered
    200 and renamed another workspace's agent. Measured after: 401 with no key, and
    404 with a valid key from a different workspace, which is the same answer a
    non-existent id gets. The ownership check now sits at the top of the handler,
    before the body is read for anything. Task #19 still owes the scanner rule that
    fails a handler whose auth check is buried in a branch, and this commit does not
    pretend to have paid it.

    What abetagent's engine dropped, each with the reason in its header rather than
    carried "for parity": `int`, `number` (no write route here takes a caller's
    number — `Chunk.position` and `Step.position` are the worker's, and
    `guardrails.maxSteps` lives inside a Json column), `date`, `validateItems`,
    `eventNames` and the exported `stringList` (there is no `ids.ts` to call it; the
    array rule is still here as the `strings` kind, which is what `Agent.tools`
    needs). Five kinds remain. One helper is this app's own: `boolFlag(value, name)`,
    for `sync` — a route-control flag that is not a column, so it cannot go in a
    field table, and `sync === true` is the only thing the old handler honoured.

    Six of the eight handlers that read a body are wired. The two that are not are
    `POST /api/billing/checkout` and `POST /api/billing/portal`, excused by name in
    `route-validation.test.ts` for the same verified reasons as §8.13's pair, and the
    test keeps both excuses tied to handlers that exist.

    Evidence, all of it re-run after the last edit:

      n5-aa-before.mts / .txt       25 cases through the running app, pre-fix
      n5-aa-after.mts               47 probes, 0 FAIL, over HTTP against :3400
      tests/unit/*.test.ts          90 tests, 90 pass (27 suites)
      npm run typecheck             clean
      npm run build                 compiled
      n5-aa-stored.mts              stored read-back: 2 agents, 2 bases, 4 documents,
                                    70 runs — nothing refused
      n5-aa-roundtrip.mts           whole-row PATCH round trip, against a COPY of
                                    dev.db: both stored agents 200

    The read-back needed a caveat and gets one. `Tool` has 0 rows in this database,
    so the TOOL tables have no read-back evidence at all — the rules for
    `POST /api/tools` and `PATCH /api/tools/:id` describe a population this app has
    never had. And the round trip was the interesting case: both stored agents have
    `model: ""`, which is the documented "heuristic only, never call an LLM" value
    (`schema.prisma:59`), and the text rule turns a blank into `null` for a nullable
    field while the handler turns `null` back into `""`. That is a translation at the
    boundary, so it was measured rather than reasoned about — the whole row sent back
    is a 200, and the agent is still heuristic-only.

    8.14.1. MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND

      The header of `src/lib/validate.ts` claimed "Every row was measured against
      this app's own dev.db, through the exported handler". Going back to the saved
      run today — rather than trusting the sentence, which is what §8.12.1 says to do
      — it was wrong in two ways. The mechanism is not the exported handler: the
      before-probe spoke HTTP to the dev server, which is a stronger thing to have
      done and a different thing to have said. And four rows were never in that run's
      output at all: `sync: "yes"`, `body "prose"`, `body (nothing)` and `body []`.
      The output file ends at `subject: 42`. Those four are true, and what makes them
      true is one line each of the handler this engine replaced — `if (sync === true)`
      and `await request.json()` on a body that is not JSON — but that is [code]
      evidence, and the table now marks them so. The rule this keeps teaching: a
      claim about how something was established has to be checked against the
      artifact, not against the memory of making it.

      A wrong `DATABASE_URL` (`file:./dev.db` resolves against `prisma/`, not the
      project root) pointed a probe at an empty path, so Prisma created a 0-byte
      dev.db in abetagent's repo root and died on the first query before writing
      anything. The stray file is deleted; the run was repeated against the real
      database, which is where the 47/0 above comes from.

      The two probe scripts were also left in the repo as `n5-before.scratch.ts` and
      `n5-after.scratch.ts`. They belong in `/tmp/abectorial`, and they are there now
      (`n5-aa-before.mts`, `n5-aa-after.mts`) with their assertions unchanged and only
      their imports made absolute; the tree is clean.

8.15 N5, ABETFLOW — A WRONG TYPE THAT REACHES A COLUMN THE SCHEMA ONLY
     DESCRIBES, AND A FILE NAME SOMETHING ELSE ALREADY HAD

    Fourth app on the same engine, and the first edit was a rename. abetflow
    already owned `src/lib/validate.ts`, and it was the workflow *graph*
    validator: node ids, edge targets, cron expressions, whether a set of nodes
    is a runnable flow. The family name for the request boundary belongs to
    `validate.ts` in leadgenius, leadworks and abetagent, so the old file moved
    to `definition.ts` and the engine took the name (task #22). Two files called
    `validate.ts` answering "is this valid?" about two different objects is how a
    reviewer ends up trusting the wrong one, and each now says in its header
    which one it is.

    abetflow's failure is neither leadworks' (a wrong value stored) nor
    abetagent's (a value quietly not stored). It is a third shape: a wrong type
    reaches a column that Prisma and SQLite only *describe*. Its before-table —
    75 cases in `n5-af-before.mts`, sent over HTTP to this app's own dev server
    on :3510 with a minted key, one wrong field per case in an otherwise-valid
    body — is full of successes that were honest about nothing:

      POST /api/workflows    status: "BOGUS"    201, and stored
      POST /api/workflows    status: "archived" 201, stored lowercase: `status` is
                                                a String column whose legal words
                                                live in a comment, so this row is
                                                neither of the two things every
                                                `!== "ACTIVE"` guard compares it to,
                                                and it still appears in every list
      POST /api/workflows    name: "   "        201, a row named with three spaces
      POST /api/workflows    name: 42 / []      500 PrismaClientValidationError
      POST /api/workflows    body "prose"       500 — `await request.json()` throws
                                                and withAuth has no mapping for it
      PATCH /api/workflows/:id  {}              200, AND a new WorkflowVersion row.
                                                Counted on the copy: 1 version
                                                became 3 across an empty PATCH and
                                                a `note: 42` whose value was then
                                                dropped on the floor
      POST …/:id/versions  version: 1.5         200 — it rolled back to v1, and the
                                                snapshot it wrote is titled
                                                "Rollback to v1.5"
      POST /api/variables    name: ["ok"]       500, *after* the route's own regex
                                                called it a valid identifier:
                                                `/^[a-zA-Z_]\w*$/.test(name)` runs
                                                `String(name)` first and
                                                `String(["ok"])` is `"ok"`
      POST /api/variables    name 5000 chars    201, stored (no length limit exists)
      POST /api/credentials  name: 42           500, same coercion accepting "42"
      POST /api/executions   input: 42          202, and 42 *is* the run's input; a
                                                later `{{input.name}}` resolves to
                                                nothing rather than saying so
      POST /api/executions   sync: "yes"        202, queued instead of run inline

    `version: 1.5` is the row worth staring at, and the layer that caused it took
    a second experiment to find. The route's only check was
    `typeof version !== "number"`, which a float passes, and the lookup then
    found *something*: the workflow rewound to v1 and the snapshot it wrote is
    titled "Rollback to v1.5". I first wrote that off as SQLite's integer
    affinity, which is the obvious story and is wrong — measured against a copy,
    the same value through `$queryRawUnsafe` matched nothing at all, while
    Prisma's `findUnique` on the composite `(workflowId, version)` key returned
    the row for 9001 when given 9001.5 *and* 9001.4. The truncation is Prisma's
    binding of an `Int` field: it sends the integer part. So a caller asking for
    a version that does not exist gets a different version restored, silently,
    with a 200. (`affinity.mts` is that experiment; the `int` kind turns it into
    a 400 that says what the field wants, and refuses the text case with
    "send it unquoted".)

    And the empty PATCH is not cosmetic. `WorkflowVersion` is the rollback
    history: every write snapshots the workflow under the next number, and a
    rollback restores the newest. A request that changed nothing appended a
    version anyway, so the history fills with identical entries and the top of
    it is what the next rollback lands on. The engine cannot see this case,
    because `note` labels the *snapshot* rather than the row: "did the caller
    name anything I would write?" is a question only the handler can ask. So
    `NO_FIELD_TO_UPDATE` is exported once (the wording is API), the handler
    re-checks after the merge, and `validate.test.ts` pins the half-truth —
    `validateBody({ note }, WORKFLOW_PATCH, { requireWrite: true })` is `ok`, by
    design, which is exactly why the route must not trust it.

    What the engine dropped here, each reasoned in its header rather than carried
    for parity: `number` (no column takes a non-whole number — `version` and
    `position` are integers and anything else lives inside Json), `bool` (no
    boolean column; `sync` is a route-control flag, which is what `boolFlag` is
    for), `date` (`nextRunAt` is the scheduler's, computed from a cron
    expression, never taken from a caller) and `format` (no email or URL column
    at this boundary). Five kinds remain: `text`, `int`, `enum`, `json`,
    `strings`. Two Json columns keep their *contents* validator rather than this
    one — `trigger` and `definition` are shape-and-byte-checked here and
    graph-checked by `definition.ts`, which is the split the UI already relies
    on: a browser action can save an empty DRAFT, and `POST /api/workflows`
    refuses one.

    Every handler that reads a body is wired, counted from the scanner rather
    than from memory: 22 exported handlers in this app, 7 of them read a body,
    and all 7 validate. The two methods of `webhooks/[...path]` read one only
    once the delegation is resolved (below), and are the two entries on the
    allowlist. That route takes the caller's payload *as* the run's input —
    refusing a bare string there would break the endpoint's whole purpose — and
    is safe to leave rather than merely convenient: the path must match an
    ACTIVE workflow's trigger, `parseTrigger` requires a signing secret of 8+
    characters or an explicit `public: true` (definition.ts:47), the secret is
    compared with `timingSafeEqual`, and an unparseable body stores `null`
    instead of throwing.

    A shipped bug the type system could not see, found by probing the real
    payload. `AiPanel` posts its free-text box under a hard-coded key `reply`,
    because `/api/ai/draft-reply` reads `reply`. abetflow's AI page reuses that
    panel against `/api/ai/try-node`, whose node works on any text and calls the
    field `text`. So the playground sent `{ task, reply }` and was told
    "Paste some text to work on." — an answer that was true of the route and
    useless about the cause: the panel *had* sent text, under a key this route
    never reads, and nothing on screen could tell the person who built the page
    which of the two was wrong. `AiPanelConfig` gains `inputField` (defaulting
    to `reply`, so any panel that says nothing behaves exactly as before) and the
    playground page sets `inputField: "text"`. Both payload shapes are in the
    after-probe: the old one 400s naming `text`, the new one reaches the node.
    Worth stating plainly: `ai-panel.tsx` is copied into four of these apps
    rather than shared, so this fix lands in abetflow's copy only, and the same
    key mismatch is waiting for anyone who wires a new route to another app's
    copy.

    Evidence, re-run after the last edit:

      n5-af-before.mts / .txt     75 cases through the running app, pre-fix
      n5-af-after.mts / .txt      83 expected answers, 83 matched, 0 failed — the
                                  75 plus top-level `[]` and `null` bodies, a
                                  201-character name, a note-only PATCH, the
                                  identifier pattern, and both playground payloads
      stored read-back            no `n5-*` row with a refused status, none with a
                                  blank name, `PATCH {}` and `PATCH {note}` each
                                  leaving the version count alone (1 → 1), no
                                  fractional `WorkflowVersion.version`, and the
                                  5003-character variable / 4003-character
                                  credential the before-run stored never written
      affinity.mts                  which layer truncates a float sent to an Int
      tests/unit/*.test.ts        103 tests, 103 pass (25 suites)
      npm run typecheck           clean
      npm run build               exit 0

    The live database is a claim worth measuring rather than asserting:
    `prisma/dev.db` is 1929216 bytes with mtime 05:02, and both probe copies are
    1929216 bytes made at 09:31 and 10:15. Nothing this round wrote to the
    product's own database — §8.13's rule, "every measurement of a stored row
    since has run against a copy of the file", held without an exception this
    time.

    8.15.1. MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND, AND WHAT IS STILL OWED

      Four rows in the engine header claimed to be measurements and were
      readings of code. `POST /api/ai/try-node` with `task: 42` and with
      `labels: "a,b"` did answer 200 over HTTP, but the probe server had no
      `LLM_API_KEY`, so it returned `{configured: false}` before the node ran:
      what those values *would have done* — `readTask` falling back to summarize,
      `readStringList` reading a non-array as no labels at all — comes from
      `llm-node.ts`, not from the run. Both rows now say `[code]`. Same habit as
      §8.14.1: the check is against the artifact, not against the memory of
      making it.

      The mechanism I got wrong first was the interesting one. Having written
      "SQLite matched the fraction against the stored integer" in
      `fields.ts` — a sentence that explained the measurement and was
      unsupported by it — I tested the claim instead of keeping it, and the
      explanation was a different layer's. That comment now describes what was
      run. It is the same rule as the four rows above, aimed at a claim about
      *why* something happens rather than *whether* it does.

      The first after-probe run was 7 failures out of 83, and only one of those
      classes was my test being wrong. Two expectations asserted unescaped quotes
      against a JSON response, which escapes them; one matched a substring of a
      summary that is truncated at 160 characters. The other four were a real
      defect in the engine I had just written: `describe()` said `got a object`.
      That word is the client's entire answer, and "got a object" reads like a
      typo in someone else's code and sends the caller looking for the bug in
      their own request. Fixed, and pinned by a unit test on the grammar rather
      than only by the probe.

      My first allowlist entry for the webhook route was malformed — a key of
      `"…route.ts GET"` mapped to an empty list, which can never match a handler
      and would have flagged rot on a route that is correctly excused. Corrected
      to one file key listing both methods, with GET excused on its own terms:
      after resolving the delegation, the GET body does contain
      `await request.json()`, and that read sits inside
      `if (request.method === "POST")` (route.ts:50), so a GET reads no body and
      stores `input: null`.

      One guard-test assertion was written against a cap that does not exist:
      `VARIABLE_PUT.name` is 100 characters, and I had asserted 200. Caught by
      running the suite before shipping it, which is the only reason it is cheap.

      Still owed, recorded rather than fixed:

        `DELETE /api/variables` and `DELETE /api/credentials` call
        `prisma.variable.delete` / `prisma.credential.delete` on the composite
        key. `conflictFor` recognises P2002 and nothing else (apikeys.ts:107-108)
        and `withAuth` rethrows whatever it does not recognise (apikeys.ts:136),
        so a name that is not there is a P2025 and a 500. [code]: read from
        `apikeys.ts` and the two routes; not probed this round, and out of N5's
        scope because it is a missing row rather than a wrong type.

        The `describe()` article bug is in the other three engines too — read
        from them, not remembered: `function describe` in leadgenius
        (validate.ts:400), leadworks (:533) and abetagent (:375) all end in
        ``return `a ${kind}` `` and so say "got a object" as abetflow did.
        Porting the article is a one-line change each, and their probes would
        have to be re-run to say the fix holds.

        `npm run lint` cannot run in any of the six apps. Measured, not
        remembered: `ls eslint.config.*` finds nothing in leadgenius, leadworks,
        abetagent, abetflow, abetanalytics or abetapi, and running it in abetflow
        says `ESLint couldn't find an eslint.config.(js|mjs|cjs) file` — ESLint 9
        requires the flat config. So for every N5/N6 guard in this family the
        typecheck and `node --test` suite are the whole gate, and there is no
        lint layer beneath them.

      The scanner's blind spot, which this round hit rather than avoided:
      `webhooks/[...path]` exports `GET` and `POST` as
      `return trigger(request, ctx)`, so the handler body a brace-slice scanner
      sees neither authenticates nor reads a body — it would pass both guards for
      the wrong reason. `tests/unit/route-handlers.ts` now exports
      `functionBody(src, name)`, and the validation test appends the delegated
      helper's block before scanning, with a second test that fails if a listed
      file stops delegating. That is one case handled explicitly, not the
      general rule: task #19 still owns the scanner change that resolves
      delegations and the brace-depth rule that fails an ownership check buried
      in a branch, which is what found abetagent's anonymous PATCH (§8.14).

8.16 N5, ABETANALYTICS — TWENTY-ONE 500s, AND THE FIRST AFTER-RUN THAT MADE A
     COLUMN WORSE THAN THE ROUTE IT REPLACED

    Fifth app on the same engine, no rename needed (`src/lib/validate.ts` was
    free), and the failure shape is the one the previous four circled without
    quite landing: a wrong-typed scalar reaches Prisma, Prisma's client throws,
    `withAuth` has no mapping for a `PrismaClientValidationError`, and the caller
    gets a 500 with no field named. `n5-an-before.mts` sent 99 cases and 21 of
    them were 500s — one per route on an unparseable body, and then one per
    scalar a caller can mistype.

    The before-table also has the other half, which is harder to total and worse
    to read: 11 × 200 and 22 × 201 that answered successfully about a row nobody
    wanted.

      POST /api/metrics       body "prose" / (nothing)   500 — `request.json()` throws
      POST /api/metrics       name: 42 / []              500
      POST /api/metrics       name: "   "               201, and `name` came back as " "
      POST /api/metrics       status: "BOGUS"           201, stored
      POST /api/metrics       status: "archived"        201, stored lowercase — every
                                                        read in the app filters
                                                        `status: "ACTIVE"`, so this
                                                        metric vanished from the
                                                        dashboard while still being
                                                        listed by id
      POST /api/metrics       status: ""                201, and the empty string was
                                                        not stored either: the route
                                                        spread on truthiness, so the
                                                        column default answered for a
                                                        value the caller did send
      POST /api/metrics       config: 42                422 "count needs an event" —
                                                        `asRecord(42)` is `{}`, so a
                                                        type error arrived as a
                                                        complaint about contents
      POST /api/metrics       kind: 42                  422, with the list of legal
                                                        words. This route's own
                                                        check was the only one in the
                                                        app that had ever named the
                                                        enum, and it answered 422 for
                                                        what is a 400
      PATCH /api/metrics/:id  {}                        200 — and the same empty PATCH
                                                        with another workspace's key
                                                        was 200 too (see below)
      POST /api/alerts        isActive: "no"            201, stored **true**.
                                                        `Boolean("no") === true`, and
                                                        so is `Boolean([])`; three of
                                                        the four spellings a caller
                                                        might use for "off" mean "on"
      POST /api/snapshots     sync: "no"                201 — the route honoured only
                                                        `=== false`, so "no" ran
                                                        inline, which is the answer
                                                        the caller wanted, by accident
      POST /api/events        value: "5" / NaN          201 `{"ingested":1,"skipped":0}`
                                                        with `value` stored null
      POST /api/events        occurredAt: 42            201, stored 2042-01-01 —
                                                        `new Date(String(42))` reads
                                                        "42" as a year, so the event
                                                        is in the future and in no
                                                        window anyone queries
      POST /api/events        1002 events               201
                                                        `{"ingested":1000,"skipped":0}`
                                                        — two events vanished and the
                                                        answer said none did, because
                                                        the cap sliced 1000 and the
                                                        subtraction used a different
                                                        total
      POST …/explain-metric   metricId: 42              500, in Prisma's client
      POST …/explain-metric   dimension: 42             200, accepted then ignored

    Three things came out right, and they are in the table because a boundary is
    only worth replacing if you know what it already protected: `NO KEY` 401s, a
    demo key against another workspace's metric / dashboard / rule 404s, and
    `workspaceId: 42` 403s rather than 404s. `PATCH /metrics/:id {}` with the
    *victim* workspace's own key answered 200, which looked like a cross-tenant
    write until the read-back: `victim metric` was renamed by nobody. It is the
    empty-PATCH bug in §8.12's Prisma reading (`update` whose `data` holds nothing
    returns the row untouched), reached with a legitimate key.

    `isActive` is abetagent's bug verbatim (§8.14), and it is worth saying why it
    keeps recurring: `Boolean(x)` at a boundary is not a conversion someone got
    wrong, it is a check that accepts by converting. Same family, `new
    Date(String(v))`, `asRecord(v)`, and the truthiness spread that turns `""`
    into a column default. The engine's rule is one line and it is the whole
    round: no coercion.

    Kinds, measured across the five copies rather than assumed: leadgenius has
    `text | int | number | bool | date | enum | json`, leadworks adds `strings`,
    abetagent keeps five (`text | bool | enum | json | strings`) and abetflow six
    (`text | int | enum | json | strings`). abetanalytics landed on
    `text | enum | json | number | bool | date` — `number`, `bool` and `date`
    re-adopted from leadworks because this schema has the columns they are for
    (`AlertRule.threshold` and `Event.value` as two `Float`s, `AlertRule.isActive`,
    and three caller-namable `DateTime`s including `Event.occurredAt`, whose
    number-read-as-a-year is the 2042 row above), and `int` and `strings` dropped:
    no caller-facing `Int` column here (`AuthThrottle.count` is the engine's own,
    and the snapshot counters live inside Json) and no column holds a list of
    names. One genuinely new thing: `objectOnly`.

    `validateArray(value, schema, name, { max })` is new, and it is the ingestion
    contract rather than a helper. `POST /api/events` no longer skips. One table
    applied per element with the position in the field name
    (`events[7].value must be a finite number, got "5" as text — send it
    unquoted`), and any wrong element refuses the whole batch. That is a
    deliberate trade: a caller told the index can fix one field and resend,
    whereas a caller told `ingested: 999` has to diff their own batch against the
    database to find the row that never arrived. For a metrics feed a silently
    missing event is invisible in the number it should have moved, which is the
    bug this whole family has been chasing. Over the cap the batch is refused
    naming both numbers, and the response lost `skipped`:
    `{ ingested: rows.length }` is now the truth, so a caller reading `skipped`
    gets `undefined` instead of a number that was a lie. No caller of it exists
    in this app (below).

    `objectOnly` on the `json` kind is the flag this round's own after-run
    forced, and it is a regression I introduced. The old `parseEvent` guarded
    dimensions with `typeof e.dimensions === "object" && !Array.isArray(…)` — so
    `dimensions: []` was a 201 whose key was quietly not included, stored null.
    A Json column holds an object *or* an array, so the engine's `json` kind
    accepts both, and the first after-run measured `dimensions: []` as a 201 that
    **stored `[]`** — a value that now survives the write and disappears at every
    read instead, because `asRecord` answers an array with `{}` and
    `metrics.ts:38` spreads it into an object, where `[1,2]` would become keys
    `"0"` and `"1"`. That is worse than the bug being replaced. Fixed by naming
    the shape in the table rather than by making the kind stricter for everybody
    (`Metric.config` takes an array through the kind and `validateMetric` still
    refuses it on contents — measured `422 sum needs an event`, unchanged). The
    second after-run measured `dimensions: []` and `dimensions: [1,2]` as 400s.

    Two more message defects the after-run found in my own wiring, both in
    `POST /api/events`: `events: "prose"` and `events: null` were labelled
    `body must be an array`, which points a caller at the wrong line — the key
    was there and misspelled in type, so the name now follows the key's presence
    rather than its arrayness. And `{"events": []}` is a 201 `{"ingested":0}`:
    nothing arrived, nothing was ingested, and the route is honest rather than
    guessing that an empty batch is a mistake.

    The ownership check buried in a `catch` (the §8.14 pattern) is here too, in
    the opposite direction: `POST /api/snapshots` wrapped the whole handler, and
    `assertOwns`'s 404 for a foreign `metricId` was re-mapped to
    `422 "Not found."`. The rewrite reads the body, then `authedWorkspaceId`,
    then one `findUnique` + `assertOwns` before either branch, with
    `if (error instanceof ApiKeyError) throw error` so the error boundary
    (`apikeys.ts`, still an error boundary and not an authentication — §8.10)
    sees what it maps. Now 404. Its range check kept the 422 it had:
    `rangeEnd must be after rangeStart` is a statement about the data, not about
    the caller's types, which is the split every app in this family has landed on.

    Two contract changes deliberate enough to name: `workspaceId: 42` in a body
    was a 403 from `authedWorkspaceId` and is now a 400 that says
    `workspaceId must be text.` — a type error is not a statement about the key,
    and the 403 sent people checking their credentials for a request that had a
    number in it. `GET ?workspaceId=42` still 403s, measured, because a query
    parameter is always a string and that path never sees a non-string. `layout:
    "x"` on a dashboard moved from 422 to 400 for the same reason.

    Length caps were measured here rather than carried. `TEXT_NAME_MAX = 200` in
    `enums.ts` first said "the cap the three rounds before this one declared",
    which is §8.12's habit of restating (8.16.1). So `n5-an-len.mts` measured
    this app: a 5000-character metric name, dashboard name and event `type` were
    all 201s that stored 5000 characters, and a PATCH to 5000 was a 200. It also
    found that the alerts length case had *never* been tested — the route
    requires `webhookUrl`, and the probe's body omitted it, so it answered
    `400 metricId, name, webhookUrl required` for a different reason than the
    case asked about. Closed on the after-run: same 5000-character name with
    `webhookUrl` present, `400 name is 5000 characters; at most 200`.

    `webhookUrl` is recorded, not half-fixed. Any http(s) URL is accepted and
    `alerts.ts:43` POSTs to it from the server, so a caller with a key can point
    a rule's notifications at an internal address — an SSRF surface, [code] (read
    from `alerts.ts`, not probed: probing it means making the app send a request
    somewhere, which is a different authorisation than writing a row to a copy).
    A `text` field with a `max` is not a fix for that and pretending otherwise
    would be the kind of partial change this ledger has been catching itself for.

    No UI writes to these routes, measured rather than assumed: no `fetch` and no
    `method: "POST"` anywhere under `src/app` or `src/components`, and the only
    server actions in the app are three unrelated ones. So abetflow's
    `AiPanel`-posts-`reply`-against-a-`text`-field bug (§8.15) has no analogue
    here: there is no browser caller whose key could disagree with a table. The
    API is the product, which is also why every contract change above is written
    down instead of slipped in.

    Guards, from the scanner (`tests/unit/route-handlers.ts`, ported verbatim
    from abetflow) rather than from memory: 21 exported handlers across 10 route
    files — 9 GET, 6 POST, 3 PATCH, 3 DELETE — and 9 of them read a body. All 9
    validate, `UNVALIDATED` is empty, and `route-auth.test.ts` re-run on this
    app's real handler set keeps `PUBLIC` at exactly one entry
    (`/api/health` GET). `MIN_HANDLERS = 21 / MAX_HANDLERS = 51`, measured.

    Evidence, re-run after the last edit:

      n5-an-before.mts / .txt   99 cases, exit 0, against a dev server whose

    8.16.1. MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND

      The one that mattered happened before this session and is the reason the
      probe scripts now check their own environment. The first attempt at the
      before-probe inherited `.env`'s `file:./dev.db` in the *script* process
      while the server read the copy, so it minted API keys and wrote probe rows
      into the product's own database and got 401s back. Cleaned up the same
      session, and every probe since opens with a guard that exits 2 unless its
      own `DATABASE_URL` is the copy it names. The rows are gone
      (`an-livecheck2.mts`, above), and the habit is now in the script rather
      than in my attention.

      A restated number in my own comment, caught the same way as §8.12's.
      `enums.ts` wrote that abetanalytics' before-table "measured a 5000-character
      metric name" — that is abetflow's measurement (§8.15), carried into a
      sentence that claims this app ran it. It had not. Measured properly with
      `n5-an-len.mts`, the comment now holds this app's numbers, and the same
      pass produced a fact I had not expected: the alerts length case was never
      reached at all, because the route requires `webhookUrl`.

      My first after-run's read-back reported `events dated outside 2026` with
      every seed event in it. The SQL was mine and the bug was mine: Prisma
      stores these `DateTime`s as integers, and `occurredAt > '2027-01-01'`
      compares an integer against text, which in SQLite's type ordering makes
      every row true. Replaced with a Prisma `where` on real `Date`s, so the
      empty result means what it says. A check that passes for the wrong reason
      is worse than no check, and this one nearly became a ledger row about the
      app's seed data.

      Same category, one line below it: the read-back ended with
      `prisma.snapshot.groupBy({ by: ["status"] })`, and `Snapshot` has no
      `status` column. That was an assumption about a schema I had read earlier
      and not re-read, and it aborted the run *after* every row had already been
      written to the copy — so the recorded table came from a half-finished
      database. Fixed by re-copying the file and re-running the whole probe, not
      by patching the output, which is why the after-table above is one clean run
      of 118 cases.

      Three of my own new tests asserted things the engine does not do, and all
      three were my tests being wrong rather than the code's: `validateBody` on
      `"prose"` does not say "not valid JSON" (that is `readJson`, which has its
      own test); a `null` on a required field is answered by the null branch's
      own wording (`… is required and cannot be null`), not by
      `describe()`; and `asDate` does not copy a `Date`, it narrows one. The
      third I could have made true by writing `new Date(value.getTime())`, and
      did not: nothing in this app mutates a validated date, and inventing a
      guarantee to satisfy a sentence I liked is the same habit as the `status`
      column above. The tests now say what is actually promised.

      Two files I damaged or misplaced, both cheap to fix and both from the same
      cause — editing with a stale picture of the file: an `Edit` whose
      `old_string` reached one line too far deleted `collect()`'s signature and
      left the next declaration glued to a comment; and a second `Edit` re-added
      the `jstr` helper that a first one had already put in, which esbuild
      reported as a duplicate symbol. A draft of the field tables also went to
      `src/lib/anfields.ts` because I started writing them before porting the
      engine, and was deleted once the real file existed. `npx tsc --noEmit`
      after each is why none of it survived; the lesson is to re-read before
      editing, not to trust the shape from memory.

      Still owed, recorded rather than fixed:

        `webhookUrl` → server-side POST to any http(s) target (`alerts.ts:43`),
        the SSRF surface above. Needs a decision about egress policy, not a
        length cap, and belongs to a round that can also test it.

        The `describe()` article fix (§8.15's `got a object`) is in this copy
        from the start and still missing from leadgenius, leadworks and
        abetagent — task #28. This copy has pulled further ahead than the
        article: measured, `validateArray`, `asDate` and `objectOnly` exist in
        abetanalytics' `validate.ts` and in nobody else's, while `boolFlag` is in
        abetagent, abetflow and here. Five apps now hold five engines that are not
        the same file, and a port between any two of them is no longer a one-line
        change. That is the trade §8.15 named for `ai-panel.tsx` — copied, not
        shared, and drifting by exactly the amount each round needed.

        `POST /api/events` with a non-object body that is not an array answers
        `body[0] …` (`{"type": …}` is accepted as a single event, so a bare
        scalar is read as event zero). It is a 400 and it names a field that does
        not exist. Left alone because the alternative — special-casing the
        single-event form — costs a branch at the busiest boundary in the app.

        `page.tsx:65` tells the demo visitor "102 events waiting" while the live
        file holds 237 (`an-livecheck2.mts`, above). A stale number in a landing
        page, not a defect this round touches.

        `DELETE` on a route whose Prisma delete matches nothing (P2025 → 500) is
        task #29 and still applies here: the three DELETE handlers in this app
        were scanned for authentication, not for a missing row.


8.17 N5, ABETAPI — THE SIXTH APP, AND THE ROUND A PUBLISHED CHANGELOG POLICY
     REVERSED HALF WAY THROUGH

    Sixth and last app on the same boundary. `n5-api-before.mts` sent 165 cases
    over HTTP to this app's own dev server, one wrong field per case in an
    otherwise-valid body, each authenticated with a minted key, against a COPY of
    `prisma/dev.db`: 18 of them were 500s, 85 answered 2xx, and the rest — 39 ×
    422, 9 × 400, 10 × 404, 2 × 401, 1 × 403, 1 × 409 — were mostly right answers
    with the wrong reason ("name required" for `name: 42`, which is present). [probe]

    The 19th 500 was the one the run could not ask. `PATCH /api/sso/:id` renaming
    a connection onto another connection's name hits the `(workspaceId, name)`
    unique index, and the before-run's own row for that shape renamed a connection
    to the name it *already had*, which a unique index allows. So it passed as a
    200 and the real duplicate went unasked until `n5-api-ssoconf.mts` measured it
    afterwards, still on unwired code: 500. [probe]

    What this app added to the family is not another copy of the same argument. It
    is the only one of the six with a published contract: `/api/v1/openapi` is
    served by the app, `src/app/docs/page.tsx` promises "breaking changes get a new
    version prefix and 30 days' notice in the changelog — v1 keeps working", and
    `src/app/changelog/page.tsx:15` says the same in its own header. Four rounds of
    this campaign changed behaviour and wrote it up afterwards. Here, the document
    is a caller, so the shape of every refusal had to be decided before the
    refusal was written:

      • A field that is the wrong shape answers 422 with every problem at once,
        `error.message` joined for a human and `error.fields` keyed by the body's
        own names (`scopes[1]`, `events[3]`) for a client.
      • Bytes that are not a JSON object answer 400 and carry no `fields`, because
        there is no field to name. That split is this app's divergence from the
        other five, which answer one status for both; it is what `validate.ts`
        exists separately for, and it is documented in `openapi.ts` rather than
        discovered in a response. [code]
      • Nothing is coerced. `isActive: "no"` stored **true**, `url: ["https://…"]`
        became that address through `String()`, `rateLimitPerMin: "1000"` fell to
        the 600 default behind a `typeof === "number"` test, `1.9` became 1, `1e21`
        became a Prisma `Int` overflow and a 500. Every one of them was already
        against the document, which says boolean, string, integer. [probe] +
        [prisma]
      • A `PATCH` that names no writable field is refused. Three of them answered
        200 with the row untouched, which is an answer about a write that did not
        happen.

    The stored read-backs are the half that decides whether a refusal is real, and
    they moved: 21 keys created by the probe → 11, longest stored webhook URL
    5004 → 29 characters, longest stored SSO name 5001 → 17, no event filter over
    128 characters, no stored name with surrounding whitespace, every
    `SsoConnection` still `UNCHECKED` after two callers tried to set `status` in
    the create body, and the workspace the cross-tenant cases attacked still
    holding exactly the one key minted for it. [prisma]

8.17.1. THE DECISION THAT GOT REVERSED, AND THE HALF THAT WAS LEFT OPEN

    The first cut of `KEY_POST` made `scopes` required. That is the correct
    security answer and it is a breaking change: a caller who omitted `scopes` and
    got a working key now gets a 422. `changelog/page.tsx:15` is the rule this app
    wrote for itself, and it says a change of that kind gets 30 days' notice before
    it ships — so shipping it as a patch, in the same release as the bug fixes, was
    the app breaking its own published policy. It was reverted to inheritance
    before the after-run: omitting `scopes` now gives the new key the scopes of the
    key that minted it, which is what closed the escalation measured in the
    before-table (`POST /api/keys` `scopes` omitted, from a key holding only
    `keys:write`, answered 201 with `["*"]`) without refusing a request that used
    to work. [probe] + [code]

    Then the route comment claimed more than the code did. It said a key may only
    hand out what it holds. Inheritance covers the case where no scopes are named;
    `scopes: ["*"]` from a `keys:write` holder was still a 201, still measured on
    the pre-change route. That gap is closed with the same predicate the auth layer
    uses — `scopesCover(auth.key.scopes, [scope])` per element — on both routes,
    because re-scoping an existing key is the same grant reached from the other
    side, and answered 403 naming the scope rather than 422: the body's shape was
    fine, the caller's authority was not. The grant check sits before the
    20-live-key count so a refused request cannot learn the workspace's key
    population. [code]

    The 403 is itself a request that used to succeed, and this time the honest
    framing is on the record rather than in a comment: it is written into the
    changelog bullet as an exception to the 30-day rule, on the argument that the
    behaviour withdrawn is privilege escalation and the published document never
    described it. Whether that argument is sound is a product question with an
    answer that is now at least auditable.

8.17.2. REFUSALS THAT A PERSON CAN SEE AFTERWARDS

    `requireKey` logs its own 401/403/429 into `RequestLog`, and `/api/usage` reads
    that table. Nothing else this round added was logged: a 422 that never reached
    Prisma left no row, so the app's own audit trail said nothing about the 116
    refusals it had just answered. Every handler that reads a body now returns
    through a `refused()` wrapper that writes the status first — measured in the
    after-run's read-back, which finds 422×116, 400×15, 403×4, 409×2 next to
    200×8, 201×20 and 202×2 for the keys the probe minted. The scanner agrees with
    the count: 24 exported handlers in 15 route files, 9 of them read a body, 9
    validate, and all 9 answer through the wrapper. [probe] + [code]

    That read-back also puts a number on the gap §8.16 left vague. Nine 404s were
    asked; two are in the log. `[code]` says which two: `ai/diagnose-webhook` and
    the replay route each call `logSuccess` with 404 spelled out, while
    `keys/[id]`, `webhooks/[id]`, `sso/[id]` and replay's own webhook lookup return
    a `not_found` without logging. A route answering "no such row" is invisible to
    the endpoint that counts a key's traffic. Not a validation refusal — there is
    no body in those cases to have gotten wrong — so it is recorded as owed rather
    than fixed beside this work.

8.17.3. GUARDS, AND THE ONE FALSE POSITIVE THEY COST

    `tests/unit/route-handlers.ts` is ported from abetflow's, verbatim, and both
    `route-auth.test.ts` and the new `route-validation.test.ts` read the same
    source through it. `route-auth` pins `MIN_HANDLERS = 24 / MAX_HANDLERS = 54`
    and the scan returns exactly 24 (11 GET, 7 POST, 3 PATCH, 3 DELETE) — the floor
    is the real number, so a scanner that stops seeing a handler fails rather than
    reporting a smaller passing set. [probe]

    The validation guard is seven tests: every body-reading handler calls
    `validateBody`/`boolFlag`, no handler calls `request.json()` directly any more
    (that is the call that threw 500), every `validateBody` argument resolves to a
    table in `lib/fields`, no table or cap is unused, no `PATCH` can answer 200 for
    an empty write, no foreign key is taken on faith — a `*Id` field that is
    validated as text must be looked up, or be excused by name — and one test whose
    whole job is to distrust the other six: it asserts that every handler the guard
    exempts by name still exists, so an excuse cannot outlive the code it was
    written for.

    The excuse list is where the guard was wrong, once. `SsoConnection.clientId`
    matched the `*Id` shape and no lookup exists for it: `[prisma]`
    `schema.prisma:159` says `clientId String?` with no relation — it is the id the
    *provider* issued, and what checks it is `runSsoCheck`, which asks the issuer
    over the network. The rule was kept and the field was excused with that reason
    written next to it, plus a stale check (`a field excused that no longer ends in
    `Id` fails`) so the excuse cannot outlive the field. Weakening a guard to make
    it green is the failure mode this family has caught itself in three times.

8.17.4. MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND

    The 129-character label that was not 129 characters. `validate.ts` recorded
    that the before-run measured "type … 129 chars" and stored it. The value the
    script builds is `"n5." + "e".repeat(125)`, which is 128: exactly the cap this
    file sets. So the row is legal on both sides, the comment had quietly credited
    this round with refusing a request it never sent, and the after-run shows the
    case unchanged at 201. Fixed in the comment, and a real 129-character case was
    added to the AFTER-ONLY block for both webhook routes (422) plus a 128-character
    boundary control (201). §8.12's mistake, again: a number restated from a label
    instead of read from the value.

    A breaking change shipped against this app's own notice policy, described
    above in 8.17.1. The lesson is not "ask before defaulting" — it is that a
    product with a published changelog policy has to be read before the table is
    written, and §8.12 through §8.16 never had to because none of those apps
    published one.

    A comment that claimed a property the code did not enforce — "a key may only
    hand out what it holds" while `scopes: ["*"]` from a `keys:write` holder was a
    201. It is one thing to leave an escalation open and another to write, in the
    route that guards it, that it is closed. Found by re-reading my own comment
    against the before-table, not by the probe.

    Refusals that no one could see afterwards: 116 422s absent from the app's own
    audit table, because `logSuccess` had only ever been called on successes.
    Caught when the changelog bullet claimed the refusals were auditable.

    A probe that crashed on its own truncation. `n5-api-ssoconf.mts` read a
    response body it had clipped to 200 characters and `JSON.parse`d it, which
    threw `Unterminated string in JSON at position 200` partway through and left
    rows in the copy. Fixed by returning the untruncated text and by having the
    script delete its own leftovers at the start of the run, so a crash is
    recoverable instead of poisoning the next measurement.

    An `Edit` that landed on the wrong occurrence, in `validate.test.ts`, leaving
    `payload.error.fields` as a possibly-undefined union at the assertion site:
    `tsc` reported TS2769 on a test that looked perfectly reasonable in the diff.
    Same cause as §8.16's stale-picture edits — editing before re-reading.

    The field tables were drafted as `const KEY_POST: Schema = { … }` instead of
    the family's `} as const satisfies Schema;`. An annotation checks the table
    against `Schema` and widens the literals back out; `satisfies` checks it and
    keeps them, which is what `describe()` and the enum member lists in a caller's
    hands depend on. Converted before wiring anything. Nothing in the suite pins
    the form, so nothing prevents it from coming back in the seventh port — that
    is the guard I did not write.

    And the `clientId` false positive above, which was the guard being wrong about
    the schema rather than the schema being wrong about the guard.

8.17.5. STILL OWED

    `Webhook.url` accepts any http(s) URL, including `http://127.0.0.1:6379/`,
    which the before-run measured as a 201 that stored, and the after-run measures
    as a 201 that stores — deliberately, and pinned by a line in
    `tests/unit/validate.test.ts` that says so out loud. The gateway POSTs signed
    payloads to whatever that column holds, so a key holder can point the app's
    egress at an internal address. A length cap or a `format: "url"` is not a fix
    for that and pretending it was would be the partial change this ledger keeps
    catching. Task #32, and it wants a round that can also test egress policy
    rather than guess it.

    The 7 unlogged 404s above: a lookup miss on an `:id` route is invisible in
    `RequestLog`, which `/api/usage` reads.

    `DELETE /api/keys/{id}` requires `keys:write` (`route.ts:98`) and
    `openapi.ts` documents 401 and 404 for it but no 403. `[code]` for the handler,
    unprobed for the response, so neither the probe nor the document moved this
    round. Same class as §8.16's route-level logging gap: a documented response
    that is a promise nobody has asked the app to keep.

    `package.json` says `0.1.0` and `openapi.info.version` says `0.1.0` while the
    changelog this app publishes says `v1.0.0` and `v1.0.1`. Three version numbers,
    two stories, and the one in the served document is the one a client reads.

    Task #28 is now six engines, not five. This copy adds `itemMax` on a `strings`
    field (a subscription could carry 101 event filters but no filter could be
    longer than 128 characters until this round), `fieldProblem` for a rule about
    two fields at once, `NO_FIELD_TO_UPDATE`, `int` and `strings` kinds — and it
    answers 400 where the other five answer 422. A port from here to leadgenius is
    no longer a copy and a rename.

    Tasks #21 (the guard has never been ported to leadgenius), #19 (abetagent's
    unauthenticated `PATCH /api/agents/[id]`), #27, #29 and #17 are untouched by
    this round.

8.17.6. EVIDENCE

                                 `DATABASE_URL=file:/tmp/abectorial/api-copy.db`;
                                 the script exits 2 unless its own URL is the copy.
                                 18 × 500, 85 × 2xx. Read-backs in the same file.
      n5-api-ssoconf.mts         the duplicate-name 500 the 165 could not ask, and
                                 its own start-of-run cleanup after the truncation
                                 crash above
      n5-api-after.mts / .txt    178 cases — the 165 under the same labels plus 13
                                 AFTER-ONLY (5 grant probes, the SSO sibling/dup/
