8.18 N6 GUARD, ALL SIX APPS — A KEY CHECK IN THE BODY IS NOT A KEY CHECK ON
     THE PATH THAT WRITES

    §8.17 closed with #19 still owed. The route itself was already fixed, on the
    way past, in abetagent's `3b0a72d`: the ownership lookup and `assertOwns` now
    run before the body is read for anything. This round is the thing that should
    have failed on the day the bug was written, and did not — the guard.

    The rule in `route-auth.test.ts` was presence: the handler's body must name one
    of the primitives that proves a caller (`assertOwns`, `requireApiKey`,
    `verifyWebhook`, …). abetagent's `PATCH /api/agents/[id]` named `assertOwns`.
    Here it is as its root commit `525c592` had it, and as `c6a7db9` still had it —
    the audit commit added the ownership lookup to the `DELETE_impl` that follows
    this function, in the same file, and left `PATCH` alone:

      if (tools !== undefined) {            //  handler's own level
        if (unknown.length > 0) {           //  two conditionals deep
          const agent = await prisma.agent.findUnique({ where: { id }, … });
          await assertOwns(request, agent.workspaceId);
        }
      }
      const agent = await prisma.agent.update({ where: { id }, data: { … } });

    A PATCH that did not mention `tools` — every rename, every pause, every status
    change — walked past both blocks into the update. Sent with no `Authorization`
    header at all it renamed another workspace's agent and answered 200 with the new
    row. `withAuth` did nothing about it: it maps `ApiKeyError` and P2002 into
    responses, and is not an authentication — which is step 7 of §8.10, the question
    all six audits had failed to ask. The guard that came out of that audit still
    let this through, because what it settled for was the name of a check rather
    than the reach of one. [code]

    Run over the six apps' ROOT commits, the rule that would have caught it reports
    16 unguarded writes, and the split is the interesting part:

      abetagent      5   1 nested + 4 no check anywhere
      abetanalytics  5   1 nested + 4 no check anywhere
      leadgenius     7   7 no check anywhere (all three `webhooks/*` POSTs)
      leadworks      2   2 no check anywhere (`webhooks/[id]` PATCH + DELETE)
      abetflow       2   2 no check anywhere (`executions/[id]`, `workflows/[id]` DELETE)
      abetapi        0

    Fourteen are the anonymous-write class, which the N6 audit (§8.10) found and
    fixed with its own presence rule — this guard agrees with it, which is the
    minimum a new guard has to do. Step 6 of that same audit already had the right
    sentence in it, "a check that exists but sits inside an `if` is not a check for
    the other branches", and it was a manual step: the audit walked the branches by
    eye and the test it left behind counted names. Two are the nested class, and the
    second one is news this round: abetanalytics' `PATCH /api/dashboards/[id]` had
    `assertOwns` inside `if (layout !== undefined) {` and `prisma.dashboard.update`
    after the block, at the handler's own level — the same bug, in the same shape, in
    an app nobody was looking at, also invisible to the presence rule. It was fixed
    by `8bd2516` ("Ownership is checked before the branch that needs it, not inside
    one"), which is the same decision abetagent reached three commits later.
    abetapi's root commit is the only clean one: 24 handlers, 10 writes, and every
    write had a key call dominating it from the first commit that had them. [probe]

    Today, the same scan over the six working trees finds 0 offenders in 109 writes
    across 181 handlers. That is the after-state, and it is a measurement of the
    current code, not a prediction from the fixture tests. [probe]

8.18.1. THE RULE THE TASK ASKED FOR WAS WRONG, AND MEASUREMENT SAID SO

    #19 says: require the ownership call "at brace-depth 1 of the handler body, not
    nested in a conditional". That is what I wrote first. `n6-depth.mts` then
    measured where the family's checks actually sit:

      leadgenius 48 handlers, 2 nested · leadworks 46, 5 nested · abetagent 20, 1
      abetanalytics 21, 1 · abetflow 22, 1 · abetapi 24, 0

    Ten handlers, and not one of them is a bypass. `n6-depth.mts` labels a handler
    NESTED when no auth call sits at depth exactly 1, so the ten are three shapes
    that are each correctly guarded today:

      • 4 where the proof is in the alias's right-hand side — `withAiAuth(X_impl)`
        lands at depth 0, outside the implementation's own block, because the
        scanner prepends the rhs to the body (leadgenius `ai/draft-reply` POST,
        leadworks `ai/explain-lead` GET, abetflow `ai/try-node` POST, abetanalytics
        `ai/explain-metric` POST).
      • 3 inside a `try` — both apps' `billing/webhook` POST and leadworks' again,
        where the whole body of the handler is one `try` around the signature check
        and the write.
      • 3 inside an `if`, in leadworks' `?action=` dispatchers (`enrichment-rules/
        [id]`, `leads/[id]`, `sources/[id]`), where each branch does `findUnique` →
        `assertOwns` → write and the tail answers 400 for an unknown action.

    A depth rule of the kind #19 asked for flags all ten, and a rule that flags ten
    working handlers gets a suppression list written under it within a week — which
    is a `PUBLIC` list for code nobody reviewed.

    So the predicate is domination, not depth. A check guards a write when the
    sequence of blocks enclosing the check is a PREFIX of the sequence enclosing the
    write, and the check comes first in the text. Block identity is the index of the
    open brace, not a depth number, and that single choice is the whole difference:
    two sibling blocks at the same depth have different chains, so the check in one
    does not reach the other, while a check at the handler's own level is a prefix of
    everything below it, including a `try`. [code]

    All ten pass the domination rule, and for a reason rather than by accident.
    Dispatcher branches and `try` blocks dominate the writes inside them, because
    the check and the write share a chain prefix. The four alias cases are the
    interesting one: the scanner prepends the export's right-hand side to the body,
    and nothing in that line is inside braces, so `withAiAuth` has the empty chain —
    and the empty chain is a prefix of every chain. That is not a loophole, it is the
    semantics of a wrapper: `export const POST = withAiAuth(POST_impl)` runs
    `withAiAuth` before anything in `POST_impl`, always. The same is why `withAuth`
    must stay out of `AUTH_NAME`: its chain would also be empty, and it would
    dominate every write in the app while checking nothing at all. [code]

8.18.2. WHAT IT IS MADE OF, AND WHAT IT IS NOT

    `tests/unit/route-handlers.ts` grows ~190 lines: a mask pass that blanks string,
    template (including `${…}` interpolations), regex and comment literals while
    preserving offsets and newlines; a brace scan that yields every block as
    `[open, close]`; `callSites()` which matches a pattern on the masked text and
    attaches the enclosing chain; and `unguardedWrites()` over the two vocabularies
    `AUTH_NAME` and `WRITE_NAME` (`prisma|tx . <model> . create|createMany|update|
    updateMany|delete|deleteMany|upsert`). Masking first is what makes the brace scan
    trustworthy: a `{` in a string is not a block and an `assertOwns` in a sentence in
    a comment is not a check. Three fixture tests pin that. [code]

    It is a tokenizer over the handler's text, not an AST, and the failure direction
    was chosen deliberately: `else` and `case` share their parent's chain, an
    interpolation goes blank with its string, and a call in a function declared
    elsewhere in the file is not in the body at all. Each of those is a case where
    the rule says "unguarded" and a human looks, which is the cost this family has
    decided to pay — the scanner throwing on an export shape it cannot resolve beats
    a silence, per the three ways this scanner has already been wrong (§8.10).

    What it does not model, in one list: early returns (`if (bad) return 401` inside
    a sibling block does guard in reality, and this rule cannot know it); ternary
    branches, which are not blocks; and any write that lives in a helper. No scanned
    handler in the six apps relies on the first today (0 offenders), so the false
    positive is a future argument rather than a present suppression. [probe]

8.18.3. WIRED IN

    Six copies, 289–309 lines each. `route-auth.test.ts` goes from 3 tests to 11 per
    app: the domination rule over `collectHandlers()`, a write-count floor so a
    rotted `WRITE_NAME` cannot turn the rule into a vacuous pass (measured writes:
    abetagent 10, leadworks 32, abetflow 14, abetanalytics 10, abetapi 10,
    leadgenius 33), and six fixture tests that pin both directions of the tokenizer
    on synthetic bodies — same-level check, check in a conditional (three bodies,
    including the two-block c6a7db9 shape written out), write-before-check, `try` and
    dispatcher, braces in strings/comments/regexes, and `writeSites` versus
    `unguardedWrites`.

    leadgenius had the scanner inline in a 157-line `route-auth.test.ts`; it is now
    `tests/unit/route-handlers.ts` (292) plus a 184-line test file that imports it,
    the same shape the other five settled on in §8.15. That was #19's prerequisite,
    and it moves #21 closer: what is left there is the validation tables, not the
    scanner.

    One vocabulary instead of two: the presence rule's local `AUTH` regex is gone and
    reads the scanner's `AUTH_NAME`, because a check that counts for one of the two
    rules and not the other is precisely the gap this round is about. The two differ
    on purpose in one respect: the presence test skips `PUBLIC` handlers and the
    domination test does not, and says so in a comment — public by design is a reason
    to read the writes, not to wave through them.

    abetapi's fixtures first used `assertOwns(request, row.workspaceId)` — a call
    that app does not have. Measured there, 41 of its check sites are
    `requireKey(request, ["webhooks:write"])`-shaped, and the fixture now says so.
    A fixture that names a primitive the host app lacks is a fixture about a
    different app. [code]

8.18.4. MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND

    The guard did not work the first time it was run, and the reason was not subtle:
    the character class in the regex-literal branch of `maskLiterals` was written
    `[=(,:[!&|?{};+-*%~^<>]`, in which `+-*` is a range from 0x2B down to 0x2A. Node
    refused to load the module — `SyntaxError: Invalid regular expression … Range
    out of order in character class` — on the first `import`. `npx tsc --noEmit` said
    nothing, because a regex literal that cannot parse is not a type error. The
    mistake is not the typo, it is that I had called the analyzer finished after
    writing it; an unexecuted guard has not been written, which is the lesson §8.16.1
    recorded for restated numbers, in a new register. Fixed by moving `-` last
    and dropping `~`, which was not an operator to begin with. [code]

    One fixture assertion was written backwards: I asserted that a body whose write
    comes before its check inside the same block is guarded, when the rule — and the
    point — is that ordering is part of domination and it is not. Caught by re-reading
    the generated file before running it, so the round has one near-miss on record
    rather than a passing test that asserts the opposite of the rule it pins. The
    three-body "check in a conditional" case was the one I got right first, because I
    had the c6a7db9 text in front of me while writing it.

    `unguardedWrites` first tokenized the body twice, once to find writes and once to
    find checks, through two calls that each rebuilt the mask and the brace scan. It
    was correct, and duplicated on purpose for a moment, and the fix is `flowOf()`:
    one mask, one scan, two lists. Nothing was measured to justify the first version
    — it was just the shape that came out.

    The throwaway script that ported the tests to the five other apps went through
    two bad drafts before it was worth running: first `str.format` over JS source
    that contains `${…}`, which is a syntax collision and blew up; then a
    find-and-replace on the script itself that left dead lines in it
    (`assert x not in.src if False else …`) and a `.__class__(…)` that was a
    workaround for the escaping problem I should have removed instead. Rewritten once
    with `@TOKEN@` placeholders, no `re.sub` on the JS text (a `\n` in the
    replacement string is an escape, which would have silently rewritten the fixture
    bodies). Same rule as §8.17's wrong-occurrence `Edit`: six files should be
    changed by one mechanism that is legible, or by six edits.

    Two claims in the ledger above are the kind this section exists for, and both got
    corrected before they were left: I first wrote that the nested check "survived the
    audit commit", quoting an audit message that says no such thing — `git diff
    c6a7db9^ c6a7db9` shows what actually happened, which is that the audit commit
    edited `DELETE_impl` in the same file and did not look at `PATCH_impl` two lines
    above it. And "leadworks and leadgenius passed for the same reason", #19's own
    wording, is not what the root-commit scan found: leadworks and leadgenius failed
    it for the anonymous-write reason (2 and 7 sites, no check anywhere), and the app
    that had the *nested* bug nobody names in #19 is abetanalytics. Measured, listed
    in the table above; the task's premise was half wrong. [probe]

    And the number in the first draft of 8.18.1 above: "a depth rule flags seven real
    handlers". Seven was remembered, not counted. `n6-depth.mts` run once over all six
    apps answers ten, and the composition (4 alias, 3 `try`, 3 dispatcher) is what the
    section now says. Worse, the wrong seven went into all six commit messages —
    `ef0bb0f`, `f122d0f`, `4df8ac8`, `7c0ea4a`, `b78c924`, `eb6b096` — where it cannot
    be corrected without rewriting a commit that was not asked to be rewritten, so it
    stays and the ledger carries the difference. The mistake is the familiar one:
    writing a number into prose before asking the artifact for it, in a round whose
    entire subject was the difference between what a document claims and what the code
    does. [code]

8.18.5. STILL OWED

    Six copies of a 190-line tokenizer. `shared/check-auth-drift.sh` exists exactly
    for this problem and covers `src/lib/*` and `src/components/*`; `tests/unit/` is
    outside its `MAP`, so the scanner can now disagree with itself in six places,
    including `AUTH_NAME` — the list that decides whether a route is authenticated.
    The drift check wants a `route-handlers.ts` entry. This is #28's argument, one
    file further along.

    The delegation blind spot, measured rather than assumed: abetflow's
    `webhooks/[...path]` GET and POST are `return trigger(request, ctx)` — 35
    characters of body, 0 writes visible, while `trigger()` holds
    `prisma.execution.create` and a `timingSafeEqual` secret check that is not a
    named primitive at all. abetapi's `v1/openapi` GET is the same shape.
    `functionBody()` already exists in abetflow's and abetanalytics' copies to let
    the *validation* guard see through exactly this, and the auth guard does not use
    it. Threading the domination rule through `functionBody`, and `functionBody` into
    the other four, is the next honest increment; until then these two files pass
    because their writes are out of sight, not because they are checked.

    Read handlers are still presence-only. Of the 16 root-commit offenders, 14 were
    writes and all 14 are now pinned; a GET that returns another workspace's rows
    with no check anywhere is caught only by the "body names a primitive" rule, which
    is the rule §8.18 exists for being insufficient. The generalization is obvious
    (every handler with a `findMany`/`findUnique` needs a dominating check, with an
    explicit list for the ones that legitimately do not) and it will have its own
    false positives; it wants its own measurement round, not a bolt-on tonight.

    Early returns: `if (!public) { … return 401 }` before a write is a real guard and
    this rule does not see it. Six apps, 0 offenders today, so no suppression needed
    today. The first handler written in that shape is the one that will decide
    whether the answer is modelling exits or requiring a named check primitive — and
    that decision should be made with a body in front of me, not before one exists.

    #19's route fix has no HTTP-level test. The guard is static: it would have
    refused to merge the bug, but nothing sends a `PATCH` with no `Authorization`
    header to a running app and asserts 401. The N5 rounds' before/after probes do that for
    validation, on a copy of `dev.db`; an equivalent cross-tenant write case for each
    of the six is worth a round and is not this one.

    Tasks #17, #21, #27, #28, #29, #32, #33, #34 are untouched by this round.

8.19 N5 GUARD, LEADGENIUS — THE FOUR ROUTES THE DEPLOYMENT ALREADY VOUCHES FOR

    #19 was leadgenius's auth half: it proved every handler that writes is behind a
    credential. This round is the other half, and on these four routes it is the
    sharper one, because the credential is an HMAC the provider holds. A caller who
    signs correctly is someone the deployment vouches for — so anything they can
    make the app do to its own rows is not an attack, it is the feature. What was
    measured is that such a caller could answer 500 sixteen times and answer 200
    falsely five more, and two of those 200s wrote a row the request never named.

    The rule ported from abetagent: every handler that reads a body validates it
    against a table declared in `lib/fields.ts`, or is named in an `UNVALIDATED`
    map with a measured reason; plus four structural tests — the exemptions name
    real handlers, no route passes an inline schema, every declared table and enum
    set is used, and a field whose name ends in `Id` has to be existence-checked
    where the route accepts it.

8.19.1. BEFORE, MEASURED OVER HTTP

    Two `next dev` servers on port 3200, both with `DATABASE_URL` pointed at a copy
    (`/tmp/abectorial/lg-copy.db`), `REDIS_URL` at db index 15, and
    `INBOUND_WEBHOOK_SIGNING_SECRET` set — the last because these routes fail closed
    at 503 without it, and a probe that never sets it measures nothing. 47 requests:
    39 (`n15-lg-http-before.mts`), then 2 for the two silent-wrong-row shapes
    (`n17`), then 6 with an `LLM_API_KEY` present (`n18`). The status codes below are
    re-counted from the server's own log (`lg-dev2.log`, `lg-dev.log`), not from
    memory:

      POST /api/webhooks/email     500 ×5   body.map is not a function;
                                             event.toLowerCase is not a function;
                                             Provided Date object is invalid;
                                             3 PrismaClientValidationError
      POST /api/webhooks/linkedin  500 ×3   body.value is not iterable;
                                             senderUrn?.replace is not a function; Prisma
      POST /api/webhooks/events    500 ×3   Cannot create property 'event' on number '5'
                                             / on string 'prose'; reading 'event' off null
      POST /api/webhooks/whatsapp  500 ×2   body.From?.startsWith is not a function; Prisma
      POST /api/ai/draft-reply     500 ×3   request.json() threw on real bytes;
                                             42.trim is not a function (`productContext`,
                                             only once a key is configured); Prisma
                                             (`enrollmentId: 42`)
      POST /api/ai/draft-reply     502 ×1   `model: 42` went to the provider as a number

    Five 200s that were not true [probe]:

      email, no `sg_message_id`      200, and an activity plus a BOUNCED enrollment were
                                     written. [code] `messageId` arrives `undefined`, and
                                     Prisma drops an `undefined` filter key, so
                                     `findFirst({ where: { providerMessageId: undefined } })`
                                     returns *some* SentMessage.
      email (postmark), no `MessageID`  the same mechanism through another adapter.
      linkedin, `value: "open"`      200 having matched nothing.
      linkedin, no sender URN        200, and `contains: ""` is satisfied by every contact
                                     with a LinkedIn URL — a REPLIED on an enrollment the
                                     request never named.
      whatsapp, `Body: 42`           200, a number written into a text column's slot.

    One finding is conditional and was measured both ways: `productContext: 42` is a
    200 with no `LLM_API_KEY` (the route returns before the prompt is built) and a 500
    with one. A before-state that recorded only the first would have called that field
    harmless.

8.19.2. WHAT IS WIRED, AND WHERE THE GATE SITS

    Four tables in `lib/fields.ts` (+75 lines, the file is 264): `INBOUND_EMAIL_EVENT`,
    `INBOUND_MESSAGE_EVENT`, `EVENTS_ENVELOPE`, `AI_DRAFT_REPLY`. Five routes wired —
    `webhooks/email` (+109/-38), `webhooks/linkedin` (+77/-30), `webhooks/whatsapp`
    (+51/-29), `webhooks/events` (+11/-2), `ai/draft-reply` (+23/-14).

    The placement is the one thing about these routes that is not obvious: the gate
    goes *after* `verifyInboundWebhook`, on the parsed value, because the HMAC is over
    the exact bytes. Reading `request.json()` would consume the body the signature is
    computed from — which is how `webhooks/whatsapp` came to be unable to serve Twilio
    at all: Twilio posts `application/x-www-form-urlencoded`, the old code called
    `request.json()`, and every real delivery threw. Both encodings are read now, from
    the text the signature was verified against [code].

    Two tables cover the *normalised* event rather than the provider's payload: the
    adapter renames `sg_message_id` to `messageId` and turns epoch seconds into an ISO
    string, and passes everything else through untouched. So a wrong type is still the
    provider's mistake, named in the provider's own field, and only units are
    translated [code]. One table serves three email providers because they differ in
    key names, not in what the write needs.

    `EVENTS_ENVELOPE` declares one field, `event`, which is all the delivery worker
    reads (it becomes the `X-LeadGenius-Event` header). The rest of the body is the
    subscriber's own payload and is spread through untouched — validating it would turn
    our webhook into a schema the subscriber cannot see.

8.19.3. THE THREE EXEMPTIONS, EACH FROM A MEASUREMENT

    `billing/checkout` POST and `billing/portal` POST: 401 for a body of `5` and for a
    real JSON body with an invalid cookie, and [prisma] this `dev.db` holds zero user
    rows, so no session gets further. Neither writes a Prisma row; `priceId` is
    checked by membership in `PRICES` and `returnUrl` by `sameOriginReturnUrl`, which
    is a same-origin rule the engine has no kind for. `billing/webhook` POST: no
    signature, a garbage signature, and a body written to look like
    `customer.subscription.updated` all answer 400 at `verifyWebhook` before anything
    is parsed.

    The third entry had to be read twice, because what it *exposes* is not what it
    excuses: `syncSubscriptionFromStripe` takes `metadata.workspaceId` from the
    Stripe payload and upserts on it with no row check. That is invisible to this
    file's scanner — it is a foreign key taken on faith one level down, inside a
    helper — so it is recorded inside the exemption and filed as its own number, not
    silently forgiven by it.

8.19.4. RUNNING THE GUARD FOUND TWO BUGS IN THE GUARD

    This is the part of the playbook that is easy to skip, and it changed the file:
    the first run of `tests/unit/route-validation.test.ts` failed three of its own
    seven tests, and not one of the three was a route's fault.

      * `VALIDATES` matched `validateItems(` but not `validateItems<EmailEvent>(`, so
        the email and linkedin routes were accused of reading a body without
        validating it *because they had just been wired to validate it*. A guard whose
        blind spot is a type argument will be "fixed" by deleting the type argument.
      * The schema-argument regex matched `[^,()]+` for the first argument, which
        stops at the first comma *inside* an object literal. On the whatsapp route's
        multi-line call — `validateBody({ messageId: …, from: …, content: … },
        INBOUND_MESSAGE_EVENT)` — it read `from` and reported "from is not exported
        from lib/fields". A wrong answer, but the failure mode worth naming is the
        other one: the same class of pattern silently reads *no* call when a route
        puts the table somewhere the pattern does not reach.
      * Neither form could have coped with `{ requireWrite: true }` and `{ max: 50 }`,
        which are third and fourth arguments in eight call sites in this app.

    Both were replaced with a positional read: find the call, balance
    `(){}[]` while stepping over quoted text, split on top-level commas, and take
    argument 1 for `validateBody`, 2 for `validateItems`. A call whose table cannot be
    read yields `<unread>`, which offends, because a scanner that skips what it cannot
    parse is a rule that passes quietly. Comments are blanked (length-preserving) so
    prose is not a call site.

    And the scanner is itself tested — `schemaArgs reads the call shapes that the
    regex missed` feeds it the generic, the multi-line object literal, the trailing
    options, the comment, and then the two shapes it forbids, asserting that an inline
    schema comes back as an inline schema and a call with no table comes back
    `<unread>`. A negative case is the only proof that a green run means something.

8.19.5. THE FOURTH TEST FLAGGED TWO FIELDS, AND ONE OF THEM IS EXEMPT

    The existence rule wants `*Id` fields resolved to a row. `INBOUND_EMAIL_EVENT
    .messageId` passes honestly: `where: { providerMessageId: messageId }` is exactly
    the check, which is why the lookup pattern was widened from abetagent's
    `where: { id: … }` — an inbound provider id is not a primary key here, and
    existence is existence. `INBOUND_MESSAGE_EVENT.messageId` (linkedin, whatsapp) has
    no row to check: [prisma] `Activity.messageId` is `String? // Provider message ID`,
    the only column those two write from it, and nothing in `src` looks anything up by
    it — the single `where: { id: messageId }` in the codebase is `lib/queue.ts:63`, on
    the *outbound* send job, whose payload key happens to share the name. So it is
    exempted by a declared map rather than by loosening the rule, and a fifth test
    asserts each exemption still names a `text` field ending in `Id`, so the map
    cannot rot into a catch-all.

    What those two routes genuinely lack is idempotency: Twilio redelivers a
    `MessageSid`, and a second delivery writes a second REPLIED activity. That is a
    different rule about a different column and is filed, not excused.

8.19.6. AFTER, SAME CASES, 46 REQUESTS

    The copy was re-made from `prisma/dev.db` first (the old copy held before-probe
    rows), the API key is minted inside the script now rather than pasted from a
    previous run — a key lives in the copy, and the copy is what gets replaced — and
    the case list is n15's plus three claim cases and three happy paths. Full output
    saved: `/tmp/abectorial/n19-after.txt`.

      * Every one of the sixteen 500s is a 400, and every message names the field and
        the value: `events[0] messageId must be text, got a number`, `value[0] from is
        required`, `productContext must be text, got a number`.
      * Every one of the five false 200s is a 400. The two silent wrong rows were
        re-run with SentMessages seeded for four enrollments and the enrollments read
        back on both sides: `enrollments before: g5bg:ACTIVE ybhr:ACTIVE ymbo:ACTIVE
        ndg0:ACTIVE f267:ACTIVE` and `after:` the same five words [probe].
      * The unsigned email event is still 401 — the gate sits after the signature, so
        validation did not become a way to probe the endpoint unauthenticated.
      * `workspaceId: 42` on draft-reply is 403, same as `workspaceId:
        "some-other-tenant"`. That was a choice, not an accident: the body's claim is
        coerced to a string when it is present and not text, because treating
        `workspaceId: 42` as "no claim was made" would hand a caller the key's own
        workspace for a workspace they asked for by number.
      * The happy paths prove the fix is not a wall: sendgrid `open` naming a real
        `sg_message_id` → 200 and an OPENED activity on the right enrollment;
        a well-formed linkedin `MESSAGE_RECEIVED` → 200, REPLIED; a Twilio
        `application/x-www-form-urlencoded` delivery → 200 — the shape the route could
        never serve before.
      * `knownFacts: [1,"two",null]` is 502, not 400. `json` refuses a number and a
        JSON null but an array is an array whatever is in it, and this engine has no
        "array of text" kind; the route's own filter drops the non-strings before the
        prompt sees them. Noted on the field in `fields.ts` rather than claimed as
        coverage.

    Two residue numbers needed explaining before they could be written down, and both
    answers were found in the copy rather than assumed. `enrollment g5bg` is not
    ACTIVE afterwards — it is COMPLETED, because the OPEN activity was real and the
    in-process sequence worker consumed the `process-step` job and advanced the
    campaign, which is what the app does with a genuine open event. And the whatsapp
    reply landed on `cd5k`, not the `ndg0` the script had chosen: [prisma] the demo
    contact `…mixh` has two enrollments, the linkedin case took the first, and
    `findFirst({ status: "ACTIVE" })` then found the other. A probe that guesses which
    row a route will pick is measuring its own guess.

8.19.7. MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND

    * The first in-process probe hung and then turned out to have *really enqueued*:
      this app's `instrumentation.ts` starts the workers in-process, and the shared
      Redis is live, so four jobs went into `bull:sequence` / `bull:webhook` on db 0.
      They were found by timestamp window and removed by `Queue.remove()` (leaving the
      pre-existing `bull:webhook:1` alone), with the queue state printed before and
      after. The HTTP probes then used db index 15 for the whole round. The rule this
      installs: a probe that touches a route with `enqueue()` in it names a Redis
      index the app does not use, before it names a case list.
    * `pkill -f "next dev -p 3200"` killed my own tool call (exit 143) because the
      pattern matched the shell command that ran it, and this machine hosts other
      apps' dev servers. Servers are now started with `setsid`, their PID saved, and
      stopped by that PID. Stopping the wrapper left the real listener running —
      `next-server` had port 3200 — and it was found with `ss -ltnp` and killed by
      exact PID after checking its cwd was this app.
    * A `sign()` helper that returned `{ raw, headers, route: "" }` blanked every
      case's route when spread after `route:`, so the first HTTP run printed an empty
      column. Two authoring slips were caught before running: `shape.entries` for
      `validateItems`'s `items`, and an inline `LINKEDIN_ENTRY` schema written into
      the linkedin route — a violation of the exact rule the round exists to port.
    * Piping a probe through `tail`/`grep` hid a whole run once, which read as "the
      script produced nothing". Probe output goes to a file and the file gets read.
    * Running a scanner from `/tmp` failed twice for different reasons —
      `collectHandlers()` resolves from `process.cwd()`, and `ioredis` does not
      resolve from outside the app — so every scan runs from the app root, and the
      one script that needed the app's own dependencies was placed inside it, run,
      and deleted.
    * `Write` was refused twice on `webhooks/whatsapp/route.ts` because the file had
      not been read in this session; re-reading first is cheaper than guessing its
      contents from a summary.

8.19.8. STILL OWED

    * Port this file's fixed scanner back: abetagent's `route-validation.test.ts`
      (and the copies in leadworks, abetflow, abetanalytics, abetapi) still match
      `validateItems(` without allowing a type argument, and still read the table by
      regex. Measured across all five: zero generic `validateItems<` calls in their
      routes today, so the blind spot is latent rather than live — it bites the
      moment a route adds a type argument, which is exactly what leadgenius did this
      round. New task.
    * `syncSubscriptionFromStripe`: a provider-controlled `metadata.workspaceId`
      reaching an upsert with no row check.
    * Idempotency on provider message ids: a redelivered `MessageSid` writes a second
      REPLIED.
    * The contact lookups behind the linkedin and whatsapp routes resolve by phone /
      `contains` on `linkedinUrl` with no workspace scope — #37's cross-tenant write
      probe is the right shape for it, and this round's happy path touched a second
      enrollment through exactly that hole.
    * `describe()` still prints "got a object" and "got a number" in live 400 bodies
      (see the after-probe's first row) — that is #28's article fix, now with a real
      message to quote.
    * The engine has no "array of text" kind, which is why `knownFacts` is `json` plus
      a route-level filter. Also for #28.

8.20 THE SCANNER, VENDEORED — THE FILE THAT DECIDES WHAT THE GUARDS CAN SEE

    Six apps now each hold a copy of `tests/unit/route-handlers.ts`, and every
    structural guard in the portfolio reads routes through it. Vendoring the
    dashboard shell and the login modules had already proved that a drift check
    catches the rot that keeps going green; this file is the case where that
    matters most, because it is not a page or a module — it is the reader. A
    drifted copy does not fail `route-auth.test.ts` or `route-validation.test.ts`.
    It makes them pass over handlers they can no longer see, and all six verdicts
    still print 0 fail. The rule installed here: what a guard reads *through* is
    itself a vendored file, and its drift check has to assert more than bytes.

8.20.1. BEFORE, MEASURED — THEN RE-MEASURED FROM git HEAD

    [code] With comments and blank lines stripped, the six copies were 201, 201,
    201, 204, 204 and 201 code lines. That was written into the canonical header
    before the sync, and because the working trees were already overwritten by the
    time the round came to be documented, it was re-measured against each app's
    *committed* copy (`git show HEAD:tests/unit/route-handlers.ts`), which gave the
    same six numbers. §8.18.6's raw counts for the same files were
    296/289/309/309/303/292 in its own order (abetagent, leadworks, abetflow,
    abetanalytics, abetapi, leadgenius) — so roughly a hundred lines of every copy
    was prose, and the prose is what had drifted: abetflow's copy carried the "it
    has been wrong three different ways" paragraph that leadworks's had lost, and
    abetapi's carried a note found in no other copy, explaining why it needs no
    `functionBody`.
    [code] The only *behavioural* difference between the six: abetflow's and
    abetanalytics's copy exports `functionBody`, because their
    `route-validation.test.ts` calls it. The canonical file is therefore the
    superset (204 code lines) and the merge loses nothing — which is what made this
    file safe to vendor and the two guards that read it not (8.20.2).
    [code] The two export shapes the scanner has to resolve, counted across each
    app's `src/app/**/route.ts`: wrapped (`export const POST = withAuth(POST_impl)`)
    40, 42, 16, 19, 20 and direct 8, 4, 4, 3, 1 for leadgenius, leadworks,
    abetagent, abetflow, abetanalytics; abetapi is the exception at 24 direct and
    0 wrapped. Route files per app: 25, 20, 11, 10, 10, 15.

8.20.2. WHAT IS VENDEORED — AND WHAT WAS MEASURED BEFORE VENDORING MORE

    `shared/tests/route-handlers.ts` is canonical, `shared/sync-guards.sh` is the
    only thing allowed to write the six copies, and
    `shared/check-guards-drift.sh` asserts four facts per app, 24 in total:
      * the copy is byte-identical to canonical;
      * `tests/unit/route-auth.test.ts` still contains `from "./route-handlers"`;
      * `tests/unit/route-validation.test.ts` still contains it too;
      * the app still has at least one `route.ts` under `src/app`.
    The last three exist because byte identity is not sufficient. An app that
    re-inlines its own scanner leaves the synced file sitting there, in sync, and
    trusted by nothing; an app whose API root has emptied out has a scanner with
    nothing to scan, which passes every guard it owns quietly. Both are silence,
    and silence is what this round is about.
    It runs twice: as a suite (`("guards-drift", "shared",
    ["bash", "check-guards-drift.sh"], 60)` in `ops-console/serve.py`, taking
    SMOKE_SUITES to 14 by AST count) and delegated from `shared/check-docs.sh` as
    its new section 10, next to the shell's and the auth modules' delegations, so a
    docs run also proves the scanner is the one the guards read.
    What was deliberately NOT vendored: `route-validation.test.ts` itself. Measured,
    four distinct `VALIDATES` vocabularies are in use across the six copies —
    `boolFlag` (abetagent, abetflow and abetapi, one shared wording), `asStrings`
    (leadworks), `validateArray` (abetanalytics), and leadgenius's
    generic-allowing `validate(?:Body|Items)(?:<[^<>]*>)?` — because the six
    `validate.ts` engines really do export different functions (#28). Vendoring the
    guard would have meant either pretending that fork does not exist or writing a
    superset regex that matches call shapes an app does not have — and the second
    hides a missing check behind a pattern that cannot miss. (#39 is the honest
    version of the same job: port the fixed *scanner-call* logic per app, keeping
    each app's vocabulary.)
    [corrected] shared/README.md's own count was the first wrong thing written this
    round: it said "five different `VALIDATES` regexes" from memory. `sort -u` over
    the six files says four. Fixed before committing.

8.20.3. BOTH FAILURE MODES EXERCISED, NOT JUST THE PASSING PATH

    [probe] Before the first sync: `Guard drift check — 18 passed, 6 failed`; the
    six failures are exactly the six byte-identity assertions, each naming its app.
    After: 24 passed, 0 failed.
    [probe] Drifted on purpose — one line hand-edited in leadworks's copy:
    `FAIL  leadworks's tests/unit/route-handlers.ts has drifted from
    shared/tests/route-handlers.ts`, 23 passed / 1 failed, exit 1; `sync-guards.sh`
    restored it to 24/0, exit 0.
    [probe] Silenced on purpose — abetagent's `route-auth.test.ts` had its import
    rewritten to scan inline:
    `FAIL  abetagent's tests/unit/route-auth.test.ts no longer imports
    ./route-handlers — it is scanning for itself`, exit 1. The file was put back
    from a `/tmp` copy rather than from git, because that app's tree had other
    uncommitted work in it at the time.
    [mistake, again] The first attempt read the drifted run as clean because it was
    piped through `tail`, and `$?` was `tail`'s status. §8.19.7 wrote this same
    lesson down one round ago. Every exit code above was taken as
    `out=$(bash x.sh 2>&1); code=$?`.

8.20.4. AFTER, AT THE GATES

    [code] All six apps: `npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.json` exit 0, `npm test` —
    leadgenius 116, leadworks 105, abetagent 98, abetflow 111, abetanalytics 85,
    abetapi 117: 632 tests, 0 fail. Against §8.18.6's 625 the difference is +7, all
    of it leadgenius's guard tests from #21 — the six copies changed and none of the
    six verdicts did, which is the outcome a vendor-then-sync is supposed to have.
    [code] What each app's copy took from the sync: +44/-9, +43/-5, +42/-11,
    +29/-11, +29/-11, +42/-18 (leadgenius, leadworks, abetagent, abetflow,
    abetanalytics, abetapi). Filtering the diffs for non-comment lines says what
    actually moved: four copies gained the `functionBody` export and nothing else in
    code, abetflow's and abetanalytics's changed prose only, and **no app lost a
    line of code** — every removed line in all six was a comment. Which is the
    check that matters for a sync like this: the behavioural delta is exactly the
    one 8.20.1 measured, and the rest is the paragraph each copy had been retyping.
    [code] `python3 -m py_compile serve.py` exit 0.
    [none] No HTTP and no database this round: every measurement is over source
    text, git objects and shell exit codes. No `dev.db` read or written, live or
    copied.

8.20.5. WHAT RE-RUNNING THE OTHER CHECKS TURNED UP

    Re-running the checks is a playbook step, and it is what paid for this round.
    Four findings, none of them caused by the vendoring:

    * `shared/check-auth-drift.sh` — 164 passed, 4 failed, exit 1, and all four are
      committed work: `ai-actor.ts` in abetflow (ada87f9) and abetanalytics
      (367046e), `ai-panel.tsx` in abetflow (ada87f9) and abetapi (6501a49).
      Canonical has not moved since 19ac3c0. What drifted is not decay but three
      improvements written into a copy and never carried back: canonical still types
      `claimedWorkspaceId?: string | null` while abetflow and abetanalytics each
      independently changed it to `?: unknown` — same code, different comments, i.e.
      the same fix explained twice in two files; abetflow's panel added `inputField`,
      because abetflow's `/api/ai/try-node` reads `text` and the shared panel only
      ever sent `reply`; abetapi's panel pulls `data.error.message` out of its
      gateway envelope, where `String(data.error)` was printing "[object Object]" at
      a user. This is the exact failure mode the auth drift check was written for,
      and it reported them only on the day the check was finally re-run: three rounds
      dated 2026-10-03 (ada87f9, 367046e, 6501a49) each edited a copy of a vendored
      file, and every one of them re-ran `tsc`, `npm test` and `npm run build` but
      not `check-auth-drift.sh`, which is a suite the console runs and I did not.
      Filed as #41, and deliberately not "fixed" here: running `sync-auth.sh`
      would overwrite the three files holding the only current versions of all of
      it, so this reconciliation is a union into canonical, not a sync in either
      direction.
    * `ops-console` is not under version control at all. `/srv/work/.gitignore:10`
      ignores `/products/`; `git ls-files` in that repo is 8 files with nothing under
      `products/`, and `git log -- products/` is empty. So `serve.py` — the file that
      decides which suites run, and the one this round edited — exists only on this
      disk. The round ends with seven commits, not the eight it planned, and the
      planned number was written from a list rather than from `git status`, which is
      what caught it. Filed as #42.
    * `/tmp` is load-bearing for two of the console's fourteen suites and both are
      dead right now. `/tmp/simulate.py` does not exist, so the Simulate button
      answers "simulation script not found at /tmp/simulate.py" and
      ops-console/README.md's "46-assertion buyer journey suite" is at present an
      unverifiable claim about a script kept in the one directory this machine
      clears. And `serve.py` runs the `agentwork` suite as
      `/tmp/agentwork-venv/bin/pytest -q` — that venv is gone too, so the suite now
      fails with exactly the FileNotFoundError that the comment above its own entry
      warns is indistinguishable from a suite that failed on the code. Filed as #40,
      covering both; the README's assertion count was left unrestated rather than
      repeated, because there is nothing to count.
    * `shared/check-docs.sh` — 80 passed, 45 failed, exit 1. 44 of the 45 are
      `answers 000000`: no product server is listening while the check probes the
      ports the READMEs advertise. The single content failure is the auth drift
      above. The new section 10 ran and passed (no "guard scanner has drifted" line),
      so the docs suite is red for environment and for #41, not for this round.

8.20.6. MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND

    * Two claims written from memory and caught by measurement before committing:
      "five `VALIDATES` regexes" (four) and "eight repos to commit" (seven).
    * The README's first draft counted leadgenius's generic-allowing pattern and the
      call shape it allows (`validateItems<EmailEvent>`) as two separate vocabularies,
      which is how it came out as five. The measured answer is four regexes over six
      files, and the apps sharing one wording are named in 8.20.2 — the same
      confusion between a pattern and a call the round exists to remove.
    * `shared/README.md` was itself found stale in a way no check covers. Its
      heading said "The two scripts" while `shared/` had grown `sync-auth.sh`,
      three `run-*-verify.sh` and three `verify-*.py` harnesses, plus this round's
      two — and `check-docs.sh` reads the *product* READMEs and never reads the
      documentation about the vendoring. So the file whose entire job is "the
      duplication is made safe" was the one file allowed to rot. Rewritten; nothing
      asserts it stays true, which is 8.20.7's first item.

8.20.7. STILL OWED

    * Have `check-docs.sh` compare `shared/`'s directory listing against
      `shared/README.md`: every `sync-*.sh` and `check-*-drift.sh` that exists is
      named there. Same rule that caught five products' READMEs still showing
      anonymous curls on 2026-09-25 (`shared/README.md` records that date as the
      reason the docs check exists). `run-token-verify.sh`, `run-retry-verify.sh`,
      `run-teams-verify.sh`, `verify-auth.py`, `verify-ai-ui.py` and

    * Put the three drift checks in the playbook's own gate list, next to `tsc` and
      `npm test`. #41 exists because three rounds shipped without
      `check-auth-drift.sh`; "a suite the console runs sometimes" is not a gate.
    * #42 — ops-console under version control. The same applies to this ledger: it
      lives in `/root`, a directory `git rev-parse` says is not a repository, so
      the campaign's memory has no history of its own either.
    * #40 — the buyer simulation out of `/tmp` and into a repo, with its assertion

      into canonical, each app's build re-verified after.
    * #39, unchanged in substance and now with a measured reason it stays per-app:
      four `VALIDATES` vocabularies, one scanner.
    * Everything still open from §8.19.8 and before: #17, #27, #28, #29, #32, #33,
      #34, #35, #37, #38.

                         pipe: 0, 1, 0.
      gates              six apps `npx tsc --noEmit` exit 0; `npm test`
                         116/105/98/111/85/117 = 632, 0 fail; `npm run lint` still
                         cannot run family-wide (§8.15).
      no database        no HTTP, no probe, no `dev.db` read or written — source
                         text, git objects and exit codes only.

8.21 THE TWO AI SURFACES, REUNITED — AND WHAT THE DRIFT CHECK CANNOT SEE

Playbook step: after-probe and ledger for #41. The round before this one found
`check-auth-drift.sh` red on the auth modules (§8.20.5), so #41 was already
measured, not guessed: `ai-actor.ts` and `ai-panel.tsx` had drifted into five
different files across six apps.

8.21.1. BEFORE, MEASURED FROM git HEAD

    The drift count is a `cmp` of each app's *committed* copy against the
    *committed* canonical, per file, before anything was edited — so "which apps
    had diverged" is a fact about history rather than a recollection:

      ai-actor.ts   leadgenius identical   leadworks identical   abetagent identical
                    abetflow differs (10 lines)                  abetanalytics differs (9)
                    abetapi identical
      ai-panel.tsx  leadgenius identical   leadworks identical   abetagent identical
                    abetflow differs (10)                        abetanalytics identical
                    abetapi differs (11)

    Four drifted copies out of twelve, in three apps, 40 changed lines. The
    canonical was leadgenius's version, which is why the three apps already
    matching it gained the most when it absorbed the others.

8.21.2. WHAT WAS MERGED, AND WHY EACH PIECE IS THE UNION RATHER THAN A PICK

    Three real differences, no prose among them:

    * `inputField` — abetflow's panel sends its free text under `text` because
      `/api/ai/try-node` asks for `text`; every other app's route asks for
      `reply`. abetflow's copy had the field, the canonical did not, so syncing
      the canonical *backwards* would have broken abetflow's playground: the
      route wants `text`, the panel would only ever send `reply`, and the node
      400s. The merge is the optional field defaulting to `"reply"`, so the four
      apps that never mention it keep their exact payload —
      `payload[config.inputField ?? "reply"]`. This is the one line in the round
      that fixes a live bug rather than a copy: abetflow's own panel was the only
      correct one and it was the one the sync was about to overwrite.
    * The error envelope — abetapi's panel already unwrapped
      `{ error: { code, message } }` before rendering; the canonical called
      `String(data.error)`, which renders a gateway refusal as the string
      "[object Object]". Absorbed as the union type check, so a bare
      `{ error: "webhook not found" }` still prints as itself. abetflow gained
      this; it had the un-unwrapped version and a gateway.
    * `claimedWorkspaceId?: string | null` → `?: unknown`. The claim is read off
      a parsed body before it is validated, and `string | null` is a claim about
      data that has not been checked. Widening it is compile-time only: `tsc`
      still passes for all six, and no runtime value moves. The comment on the
      field now records what each app answers (8.21.3) instead of asserting a
      uniform rule the apps do not follow.

    What was deliberately NOT merged: the fail-closed claim rule. abetanalytics
    answers a non-text `workspaceId` with a 400 naming the field and is the only
    app that does. Making that the rule for six apps means editing five local
    `src/lib/apikeys.ts` files (145/150/150/151/155 lines; abetapi has no such
    file) or vendoring that check too — a semantic change, so it is #43, not a
    line inside a commit whose whole claim is that it changes nothing observable.

8.21.3. THE RULE FORKS, MEASURED OVER HTTP

    Two probes, one per app, each sending twelve claim shapes through a real
    request rather than reading code: `/api/ai/draft-reply` on leadgenius (port
    3200) and `/api/ai/try-node` on abetflow (port 3500), both `next start` on
    the rebuilt apps. Copied databases, never the live ones. The same vendored
    module answered opposite ways:

      shape            leadgenius        abetflow
      absent           200               200
      null             200               200
      ""               200               200
      own id           200               200
      foreign id       403               403
      42               403               403
      0                403               200   <--
      false            403               200   <--
      true             403               403
      {}               403               403
      ["own id"]       200   <--         403
      bad key          401               (400 on an empty body)

    The three `<--` pairs are not the module's doing. `resolveAiActor` treats a
    falsy claim as absent, so `0` and `false` mean "no claim" — and leadgenius
    gets 403 for them anyway because its route `String()`s the claim before
    handing it over (`src/app/api/ai/draft-reply/route.ts:42`), turning `0` into
    `"0"`, which is truthy and is not a workspace. That same stringify is why
    `["<own id>"]` is a 200 in leadgenius and a 403 in abetflow: a one-element
    array stringifies to its element. Neither app is wrong on its own terms.
    Both are surprising, and nothing is red.

    Extended to all six from the callers, which is the part the HTTP probes could
    not reach: leadworks reads the claim from a **query** parameter on a GET, so
    it can never be non-text at all; abetapi never reads the claim and always uses
    the credential's workspace, which is the only one of the six rules that cannot
    be steered by a value's shape; abetagent has no `/api/ai` route and nothing
    imports its `ai-actor.ts`. Six apps, five different rules, one vendored file.

8.21.4. THE POINT THAT MAKES THIS A ROUND RATHER THAN A COPY-PASTE

    `check-auth-drift.sh` compares bytes, and it went green: 168 passed, 0 failed,
    exit 0, with all twelve copies independently `cmp`-identical to canonical. But
    the three ways this family has been quietly wrong all survive that green:

    * abetagent carries two vendored AI files that nothing imports. Byte-identical
      to canonical, and guarding nothing — the same shape as §8.20's worry about
      a synced scanner "trusted by nothing", arriving in the auth set on the day
      it was reconciled.
    * abetapi has a live AI surface that deliberately bypasses the vendored
      resolver. Its route explains the choice in a comment (the workspace comes
      from the credential, never the body — a decision, and a safer one than four
      of the six). The drift check sees a correct copy of a file the route does
      not call.
    * The claim rule, 8.21.3, lives outside the vendored pair entirely.

    So the drift check is necessary and not sufficient, which is now said in
    `shared/README.md` next to the table rather than left as an implication of it.

8.21.5. AFTER, AT THE GATES

    * `shared/sync-auth.sh` → "Auth module synced to 6 apps (6 updated)"; re-run
      after the final comment edit, and the twelve copies re-`cmp`ed.
    * `shared/check-auth-drift.sh` → 168 passed, 0 failed, exit 0.
      `shared/check-guards-drift.sh` → 24 passed, 0 failed, exit 0. Exit codes
      taken without a pipe (`out=$(... 2>&1); code=$?`) — the trap in §8.19.
    * `npx tsc --noEmit -p tsconfig.json` → exit 0 in all six.
    * `npm test` → 116 / 105 / 98 / 111 / 85 / 117 = **632 passed, 0 failed**,
      the same total as before the sync and before it, so the merge moved no
      behaviour the unit suites can see.
    * `npm run build` → exit 0 for the two apps that were probed (leadgenius,
      abetflow), required because `next start` serves `.next/`, not `src/`: the
      first after-probe would otherwise have exercised the pre-sync bundle. The
      four unprobed apps need no rebuild — no server was started from them.
    * `shared/check-docs.sh` → **81 passed, 44 failed**, exit 1. The one content
      failure §8.20.5 recorded ("the auth drift above") is gone: zero FAIL lines
      that are not `answers 000000`, measured by `grep -vc`. The 44 are the port
      probes with no product server listening, which is the environment, and
      #40/#42's standing problem.
    * `npm run lint` still cannot run family-wide (§8.15).

8.21.6. NO DATABASE WAS TOUCHED

    Both probes ran against copies (`n22-copy.db`, `n22-af-copy.db`), and each
    script refuses to start unless its own `DATABASE_URL` names the copy — it
    exits 2 otherwise, so "we remembered to point it at the copy" is not the
    guarantee. leadgenius's worker in its `instrumentation.ts` means the server
    was also started with Redis db 15 rather than the shared default.

    The live files carry their pre-round timestamps and were never rewritten:
    `leadgenius/prisma/dev.db` 1,892,352 bytes at 2026-10-03 06:56:41,
    `abetflow/prisma/dev.db` 1,929,216 bytes at 2026-10-03 05:02:32 — read again
    after the probes finished, unchanged.

    The probes write rows to the *copies*: each run mints one more API key named
    "n22 claim shapes". That showed up as the only diff between the before and
    after logs — line 20 (leadgenius) and line 17 (abetflow), `1` → `2` — and
    every behavioural line is byte-identical (19 leadgenius lines, 16 abetflow
    lines compared with the accounting line excluded). A probe whose own
    bookkeeping changes when you re-run it is a probe that can hide a real
    change; the count is why the diff was read line by line rather than skimmed.

8.21.7. MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND

    * The first version of the canonical comment said leadgenius "stringifies
      first" and left it at that — a claim about a file this round does not
      touch. Caught before committing by going to the line: `String(claim)` at
      `src/app/api/ai/draft-reply/route.ts:42`, quoted into the comment so the
      next reader checks one file, not a recollection.
    * `shared/README.md` had a duplicated clause from §8.20 — "delegates to
      `check-ui-drift.sh`, ..." appearing twice inside one bullet, the residue of
      an edit that replaced half a sentence. Fixed while adding this round's
      section, which is the only reason it was noticed: nothing reads that file
      mechanically, which is 8.20.7's first owed item and still open.
    * A draft of the README's fork table said "four apps compare the claim
      directly". Measured, the six split five ways (one app stringifies, one reads
      a query parameter, one ignores the claim, one adds a 400, and one has no
      route at all), and the table replaced the sentence.
    * The stale-build trap: after the sync, the probe servers were still serving
      `.next/` built from the pre-sync source, so the first after-probe proved
      nothing about the new code. Caught before the result was written down, by
      asking what `next start` actually reads.

8.21.8. STILL OWED

    * #43 — one fail-closed claim rule for all six, anchored on abetanalytics's
      `apikeys.ts`, which already has it; five local files to change, and abetapi
      has none to change.
    * #44 — a vendored file that nothing imports should be a failure, not a
      passing byte comparison. abetagent's `ai-actor.ts`/`ai-panel.tsx` are
      dead-on-arrival copies today; the guard scanner's drift check already asks
      "is this file still imported by the thing that is supposed to use it", and
      the auth check does not.
    * Everything from 8.20.7, unchanged and still open: `check-docs.sh` does not
      read `shared/README.md`; the three drift checks are not in the playbook's
      own gate list; #42 (ops-console and this ledger are outside version
      control), #40 (the buyer simulation in `/tmp`), #39, and the standing list
      #17, #27, #28, #29, #32, #33, #34, #35, #37, #38.

8.21.9. EVIDENCE

      before-matrix      12 `cmp`s of committed app copies against committed
                         canonical: 4 differ, 40 changed lines, in abetflow (both
                         files), abetapi (panel), abetanalytics (actor).
      code               shared/auth/ai-actor.ts +25/-2, shared/auth/ai-panel.tsx
                         +18/-2, shared/README.md +38/-4; per app (panel + actor
                         combined) +43/-4 leadgenius, leadworks and abetagent,
                         +29/-4 abetflow, +40/-6 abetanalytics, +39/-9 abetapi —

                         three statements plus prose: the `inputField` default,
                         the envelope unwrap, the `unknown` widening.

8.22 "IDENTICAL AND UNUSED" LOOKS GREEN TO cmp — THE AUTH CHECK LEARNS WHAT THE
     SCANNER ALREADY KNEW

Playbook step: #44, with §8.20.7's first owed item folded in because this round
is the same act both times — make a check see what it currently cannot.

8.22.1. BEFORE, MEASURED

    `check-auth-drift.sh` ran 168 assertions and every one of them was a byte
    comparison or a schema-model lookup. Nothing in it asked whether a file it
    had just certified was reachable. Two facts made that more than theoretical,
    both from §8.21: abetagent vendors `ai-actor.ts` and `ai-panel.tsx` and has
    no AI surface whatsoever, and abetapi's diagnose route resolves the actor
    inline and never calls the vendored module. Both were green.

    The importer matrix, built from sync-auth.sh's own MAP rather than a restated
    list so the probe cannot drift from the thing it measures: 20 modules × 6
    apps = 120 pairs, and 3 have no importer —

      abetagent/src/lib/ai-actor.ts          0
      abetagent/src/components/ai-panel.tsx  0
      abetapi/src/lib/ai-actor.ts            0

    The other 117 range from 1 (`password.ts`, `seats.ts`, every component but
    `auth-form.tsx`) to 15 (`session.ts` in abetflow).

    The three zeros were then confirmed against the whole app directory, not just
    `src/`, because the first grep said "2 or 3 referrers" and that turned out to
    be `.git/index`, `.git/COMMIT_EDITMSG` and `tsconfig.tsbuildinfo`. Scanning
    `src/` for `.ts`/`.tsx` is the correct scope, and the reason is measured
    rather than assumed.

8.22.2. A BASENAME MATCH IS WRONG, AND THE PROOF IS ALREADY IN THE TREE

    The obvious matcher — find files importing "X" by name — was tried first and
    then disproved on leadgenius, which has both `src/lib/email.ts` (vendored) and
    `src/lib/channels/email.ts` (local, not shared). Both specifiers end in
    `/email`. The basename match counted four importers for the vendored copy;
    the real number is two, and the two bogus ones are `api/sending-accounts/
    route.ts` and its `[id]` sibling importing `@/lib/channels/email`.

    So the rule matches the specifier tail *with its directory in it*, and handles
    the two forms apps actually write. Measured forms, not guessed: `@/lib/email`
    (auth-actions.ts) and `"./email"` from inside `src/lib` (invites.ts). Aliases
    are rooted at `src/` by every app's `"@/*": ["./src/*"]`, so the tail drops
    the leading `src/` — an earlier draft required `/src/lib/email` and reported
    102 of 120 pairs as having no importer, which was the check being wrong, not
    the apps. The same-directory `./name` form is only counted from inside the
    target's own directory, which is what keeps `channels/`'s `./email` from
    being credited to the vendored file.

    Both matchers now agree on all 120 pairs except the collision they were built
    to test.

8.22.3. THE RULE, AND WHY THE EXCEPTIONS CARRY A PREMISE

    120 importer assertions added to the byte check: 288 total, still exit 0, and
    the whole check runs in 2 seconds — speed was not a reason to weaken it.

    Three unused pairs are real, so they are declared. A plain allowlist would
    have been a free pass that outlives its reason, so each declaration states the
    fact that makes it deliberate, and the check re-tests that fact:

      abetagent | ai-actor.ts   | premise: src/app/api/ai does not exist
      abetagent | ai-panel.tsx  | premise: src/app/api/ai does not exist
      abetapi   | ai-actor.ts   | premise: api/ai/diagnose-webhook/route.ts exists

    Four states, not two: imported and undeclared is a pass; unused and declared
    with a premise that holds is a pass; **declared but now imported** is a
    failure (the declaration went stale); **declared and the premise is gone** is
    a failure (the reason died). A fifth branch catches the checker's own
    rot: any module in MAP without an entry in the specifier table fails loudly,
    because an unmapped file matches nothing and would otherwise read as
    "imported by nobody" — the tool misreporting as a finding about the code.

8.22.4. FAILURE MODES EXERCISED, FIVE OF THEM, WITH REAL EXIT CODES

    Every one of the five branches was driven to a red result, exit codes taken
    without a pipe:

      A  last importer removed for real (leadworks's only `ai-actor.ts` importer,
         `src/app/api/ai/explain-lead/route.ts`, moved aside)
         → exit 1, "leadworks's ai-actor.ts is vendored and imported by nothing
           — the byte check passes on it blindly"; file restored, repo 0 dirty.
      B  copy removed entirely (leadworks/src/lib/ai-actor.ts)
         → exit 1, "leadworks has no src/lib/ai-actor.ts" — the *right* reason,
           not the importer one; restored and re-`cmp`ed identical.
      C  an imported file declared unused (leadgenius's ai-actor.ts, premise
         satisfied, importer still present)
         → exit 1, "declared non-use but has 1 importer(s) — the declaration is
           stale".
      D  premise destroyed (abetapi's witness path pointed at a nonexistent file)
         → exit 1, "the premise of its non-use declaration no longer holds".
      E  entry deleted from the specifier table
         → exit 1, six identical FAILs (one per app), "seats.ts is in MAP but has
           no IMPORTED_BY entry".

    A–D touch the checking script or a file that was moved and moved back; E
    touches only the script, and it was restored from a /tmp copy whose md5 was
    taken first (`c53d8f3f…`, matched on every restore). No app source was edited
    to produce these results.

8.22.5. §8.20.7'S FIRST OWED ITEM, CLOSED THE SAME WAY

    `check-docs.sh` read the six product READMEs and never read the documentation
    about the vendoring — which is how `shared/README.md` sat stale with a heading
    claiming "The two scripts" while `shared/` held thirteen. Measured before-state:
    6 of 13 scripts were unnamed in it — the three `run-*-verify.sh` and the three
    `verify-*.py`, exactly the list §8.20.7 left open.

    Two halves, in the order that matters. First the documentation, written from
    each script's own header and from `serve.py`'s registry rather than from
    memory: what each harness proves, which apps it runs against, and the two
    facts that are easy to lose — the three `.py` harnesses drive HTTP and are
    therefore the same ports the `answers 000000` failures keep reporting, and
    `verify-auth.py` is the one suite that writes to a real `prisma/dev.db`
    (line 89) and cleans up in a `finally` (line 315), a standing exception to the
    copy-only rule every probe added since §8.14 follows. Then the assertion: one
    per script, so a second undocumented script is a second failure. 13 assertions
    added.

    Its failure mode driven too: a temporary `shared/n23-undocumented.sh` turned
    the run 94/44 → 94/45 naming the file, and removing it returned 94/44.

8.22.6. AFTER, AT THE GATES

    * `check-ui-drift.sh` 6/0 exit 0; `check-auth-drift.sh` **288/0 exit 0**
      (was 168/0); `check-guards-drift.sh` 24/0 exit 0.
    * `check-docs.sh` **94 passed, 44 failed**, exit 1 — up from 81 passed, and
      still zero non-environmental failures (`grep -v "answers 000000"` on the
      FAIL list returns nothing). The 44 are ports with no server listening.
    * `bash -n` clean on both edited scripts.
    * `ops-console/serve.py` unchanged, so its suite count was re-measured rather
      than trusted: 14 suites by AST, and the ops-console README's "all 14 smoke
      suites" line is still true — so it was left alone. A number that happens to
      still be correct is not a number that needed editing.
    * **No app file was changed this round.** All six app repos report 0 changed
      paths — including leadworks, whose two files were moved aside and put back by
      probes A and B. That is why there is no 632-test sweep here: `npm test` runs
      the apps' own copies and nothing in `shared/`, and running six suites to
      prove a no-op would have been theatre. The gates that can see this round's
      change are the four drift/docs checks above, and every one of them was also
      run in its failing direction.

8.22.7. MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND

    * The first precise matcher required the target path *inside* the specifier
      (`/src/lib/session`) and reported 102 of 120 pairs as unimported. Caught by
      noticing the number was absurd rather than by trusting the script's exit 0 —
      a check that fails 85% of the time is a broken check, not 102 findings.
    * `IMPORTED_BY` was written with `[throttle.ts]` twice and, in the same draft,
      no guard against a missing entry. Both fixed; the duplicate is harmless in
      an associative array and the missing-entry case is not, which is why branch E
      exists and why it is tested.
    * The first grep for the three zeros searched whole app directories and
      reported 2–3 "referrers". They were git internals and a build artefact. The
      lesson is in 8.22.1: for a reachability question, the scope has to be source
      files, and that has to be stated.
    * A probe sequence that `mv`s a real app file and `mv`s it back printed a
      truncated result, so the restore could not be assumed. Verified separately
      (file present, `/tmp` backup gone, repo 0 dirty) before continuing. An
      unverified restore in a campaign that runs for weeks is how a later round
      debugs a change nobody made.

8.22.8. STILL OWED

    * #45 — whether abetagent should receive those two copies at all.
      `sync-auth.sh`'s MAP is uniform, so an app that cannot use a module still
      gets a file it must then declare unused; the alternative is per-app
      exclusions, and if taken, sync and check must be asserted to agree.
    * #43, unchanged: one fail-closed claim rule for six apps, five rules today.
    * §8.20.7's second item, still open: the three drift checks are not in the
      playbook's own gate list next to `tsc` and `npm test`. #41 exists because
      they were not there once already.
    * #42 (ops-console and this ledger are outside version control), #40, #39, and
      the standing list #17, #27, #28, #29, #32, #33, #34, #35, #37, #38.

8.22.9. EVIDENCE

      probe              /tmp/abectorial/n23-importers.sh (basename) and
                         n23-importers-precise.sh (tail-with-directory + same-dir
                         form), both read-only; 120 pairs; 3 zeros; disagreement
                         confined to leadgenius `email`, 4 vs 2.
      check              shared/check-auth-drift.sh — the specifier table, the
                         NON_USE declarations, importers_of / declared_non_use,
                         five branches, coverage guard. 168 → 288 assertions.
      docs               shared/README.md — "A vendored module nobody imports"
                         with the three declarations, and "The verification
                         harnesses" naming all six previously-undocumented
                         scripts; shared/check-docs.sh section 11, 13 assertions.
      exercised          five red results (A–E) plus the docs gate's red
                         (94/45), each with its exit code, each restored; the
                         restored check's md5 compared to the pre-probe copy.
      untouched          no app source, no HTTP, no database read or written.

8.23 THE GATE IS AN EXIT CODE, AND LIVENESS IS NOT ALLOWED IN IT

Playbook step: #46 — "structural guard tests, then the gate" had been a recital of
commands rather than a command. Nothing in the tree answered the question "did this
round pass?", and the rounds had been answering it by hand for eighty sections.

8.23.1 BEFORE, MEASURED

    The gate did not exist, and its absence had a price already recorded: §8.20.5
    found three rounds dated 2026-10-03 (`abetflow ada87f9`, `abetanalytics
    367046e`, `abetapi 6501a49`) that each edited a copy of a vendored file and
    re-ran `tsc`, `npm test` and `npm run build`, leaving `check-auth-drift.sh` red
    on four committed files. That check was a suite the Operations Console ran and
    the rounds did not open, so it was not part of what "green" claimed.

    `shared/check-all.sh` was written first and run before anything else changed,
    which is how this round found its own premise wrong. On a quiet machine the
    gate cannot be green, because `check-docs.sh` probes ports:

      before, on this tree    140 assertions — 94 passed, 46 failed, exit 1
      of those failures       44 were `answers 000000`, 2 were README-naming
                              for the two files this round had just added

    "44 of 46 is weather" is why `check-live.sh` exists. A check that is red for
    the weather is a check people stop reading, and `check-docs.sh` delegates each
    drift check to a *single* line, so one real content failure sits at the bottom
    of 44 `answers 000000` lines unread. That is not a story about someone else's
    round: §8.20.5's auth drift was found by running `check-auth-drift.sh` alone.

    One measurement note, because it is the kind that quietly falsifies a before/
    after. §8.22 recorded the docs check as 94/45 = 139 and this round's before was
    94/46 = 140; they are not the same assertion running in two states. The counts
    are of assertions *executed*, and execution is branch-dependent — the old
    section 8 credited an extra `ok` only when all five of an app's routes
    answered, so on a day with servers up the total was higher than the number of
    `ok`/`bad` call sites. The before/after in 8.23.2 is therefore measured, not
    quoted: git HEAD's `check-docs.sh` was copied to `/srv/work/products/.n24-probe/`
    (one level under the root so its `dirname $0/..` resolves to the real tree) and
    run against the same working tree the new files were. That directory was
    deleted afterwards and its absence verified.

8.23.2 THE SPLIT, AND NOTHING LOST

    53 liveness assertions left `check-docs.sh` and became `shared/check-live.sh`.
    The accounting, measured on the same tree:

      check-docs.sh   140 → 95   (95 passed, 0 failed, exit 0 — quiet machine)
      check-live.sh    — → 53   (9 passed, 44 failed, exit 1 — by design)
      95 + 53 = 148 = 140 + 8

    Nothing was dropped: the *multiset* of failure labels before and after is
    identical — 44 each, `comm -23` and `comm -13` both empty after normalising the
    two places where a label was reworded (`README advertises :P but it answers`
    became `README advertises :P/path`, and `/invite/<token>` became
    `/invite/* (the explanatory card)`). The verdict distribution is identical too:
    9 passed / 44 failed before and after. Both runs' 9 passes are the same nine
    requests: the two README ports that answer on this box — `:3101/login`
    (abetConnect, Docker) and `:3400/api/health` (`abetagent`'s `next dev`, pid
    1161887) — plus all seven of abetagent's own probes (five login-surface routes,
    `/members`, `/invite/<token>`). Confirmed by measurement rather than by
    arithmetic on the totals: `grep -c abetagent` over the failure list is 0, and
    the only `advertises :PORT` labels present are the other seven
    (3102, 3200, 3300, 3500, 3600, 3700, 8000).

    The +8 is eight assertions added on purpose, all visible in the diff:

      +1   `check-docs.sh` §5 now credits "the top-level README states ports at
           all" instead of probing nine of them — kept because an edit that deletes
           the promise would otherwise silently empty check-live.sh's probe list
      +6   §8's per-app branch credits "this app documents its login surface and
           has the page file" where it used to run five curls
      +1   §12, new: the Operations Console's README must count exactly the suites
           `serve.py` registers

    What stayed in the docs check is the documentation half, and it stayed whole:
    the six `## Accounts (browser)` sections, the `login/page.tsx` files, abetflow's
    playground page, the 36 team-page file assertions and the 6 builder files.
    `grep -c curl shared/check-docs.sh` is 0 — one prose mention in the header.

8.23.3 WHY LIVENESS PARSES THE README RATHER THAN LISTING PORTS

    `check-live.sh` reads its ports out of `README.md` the way the old section 5
    did, for the reason that made them worth asserting: the promise under test is
    "a port the docs advertise answers". A hardcoded list passed happily while the
    README advertised `:3299`. Nine ports today: 3101, 3102, 3200, 3300, 3400,
    3500, 3600, 3700, 8000. The path per port is a map carried over from the old
    section 5 and left as it was, because it is what each product answers on —
    `:3101` serves `/login`, `:3102` serves `/`, `:8000` serves `/health`,
    everything else `/api/health`.

    That parser has a measured gap, and it is the split's fault only in the sense
    that moving the code made it worth reading. `README.md:6` advertises the
    Operations Console as `http://127.0.0.1:3800`, and the DOC_PORTS grep looks for
    `localhost:[0-9]{4}`; `README.md:26` writes `| 8000 / 3103 |` in one cell, and
    the table pattern anchors a port at the end of the field. So `:3800` and
    `:3103` are advertised and not probed. Both spellings were in the code before
    this round — the HEAD run reports the same nine ports — and adding them here
    would have broken the 1:1 label comparison in 8.23.2, which is the thing this
    round has to be able to claim. Filed as #47 with the two line numbers rather
    than fixed mid-proof.

8.23.4 THE GATE'S OWN REPORT WAS WRONG, TWICE, AND SHAPED BY MEASUREMENT

    Every step is `out=$( cd "$dir" && "$@" 2>&1 ); code=$?`. No step is piped to
    the program that displays it: `cmd | tail` reports `tail`'s status, and §8.19
    already recorded that mistake in this campaign.

    The first version took "the most informative line" by matching the words
    `passed|failed|error`. Measured against real output, that produced:

      ok  leadgenius test  5s  ok 48 - a field the route did not declare is not
                             passed through

    — a passing subtest's *name*, matched on the substring "passed". Worse, the
    failure detail for a genuinely red test step printed four passing lines,
    because `abetapi` has subtests named "a provider failure is an error, not a
    swallowed no-op". Both were caught by reading the output, not by the exit code,
    which was correct in both cases. The gate can be right and unreadable.

    So the shapes are matched explicitly: `check — [0-9]+ passed` for the four
    shared checks (which all end with that summary), `^# (tests|pass|fail) [0-9]+$`
    for `tsx --test`, and `^ *(FAIL|not ok)|^# fail [1-9]|error TS` for what to
    print on a red step. The app lines now read `tests 116 pass 116 fail 0`, which
    is the number the ledger recites anyway, and the red test step reads
    `not ok 11 - n24 red-path probe: a deliberately false assertion` plus `# fail 1`.

8.23.5 RED PATHS DRIVEN, EACH RESTORED

      injection                                    gate result
      -------------------------------------------  ---------------------------------
      comment appended to
        leadworks/src/lib/throttle.ts        ->    FAIL check-auth-drift (exit 1),
                                                   naming both files;
