    suite table (`grep -cE '^    \("[^"]+", "[^"]+", '`) rather than from prose, so
    a suite registered without a word written is a red run.

8.23.7 OWED, AND WHAT THE ROUND DELIBERATELY DID NOT DO

    * #47, raised by this round: `check-live.sh`'s port parser misses
      `127.0.0.1:` spellings and the second port in a `| 8000 / 3103 |` cell, so
      two advertised ports go unprobed. Deliberately not fixed here, because the
      round's claim is that the split lost nothing, and that claim is a label
      comparison that only closes 1:1.
    * §12 asserts the console's suite *count*. It does not assert that each
      registered suite's folder and script exist — which is #40's class of failure
      (`/tmp` paths two suites ran from) and still open. A count that matches a
      table of fifteen entries whose cwd nobody checks is a weaker gate than it
      reads like.
    * `check-docs.sh` §12 depends on `ops-console/` being on disk, and reports a
      readable FAIL if it is not. That is the right behaviour on this box, where
      the directory is gitignored (#42) and therefore has no clone to arrive from:
      a machine without it cannot run eleven of its own console suites either. If
      #42 ever puts ops-console under version control, this assertion should become
      conditional on the directory existing rather than absolute.
    * #43 and #45 are unchanged by this round. #45 in particular is now cheaper to
      answer, because the gate makes `check-auth-drift.sh`'s verdict something a
      round has to look at rather than something a dashboard may show.

8.24 THE CLAIM RULE BECAME ONE FUNCTION, AFTER THE PROBE PROVED IT COULD NOT LIE

Playbook step: #43 — "one fail-closed `workspaceId` claim rule for all six apps".
§8.23.7 left it open, and §8.22 had written the divergence onto
`claimedWorkspaceId` rather than fixing it, on the grounds that a vendoring commit
which promised to change nothing observable must not also change a status code.
This round spends that promise deliberately.

8.24.1 THE HARNESS HAD TO BE PROVEN WRONG FIRST

    `n25-probe.sh` starts each app on a non-documented probe port (leadgenius
    :4200, abetflow :4300, abetanalytics :4600) with `next dev`, because `next
    start` serves `.next/` (§8.21) and an after-probe built from the old tree
    would have measured nothing. Three bugs in the harness itself were found
    before any app could be believed:

    * the readiness loop accepted a dead server. `curl -w '%{http_code}'` prints
      `000` when the connect fails *and* exits non-zero, so `|| echo 000` appended
      a second one: the answer was `000000`, which is not `000`, the loop broke on
      its first pass, and the probe died on `ECONNREFUSED`. Readiness is now "three
      digits and not `000`", plus `kill -0 $SRV` so a server that exits during boot
      is reported as that rather than as a timeout.
    * the server was started with no `DATABASE_URL`, so it read the app's `.env`
      (`file:./dev.db`) and the *live* file — while the probe script that mints the
      key used the copy. Every request would then have answered about a key the
      server had never seen. The override now goes to the server as well as the
      probe, which is the whole of the isolation, and the live files were checked
      afterwards on a fresh copy: `select count(*) from ApiKey where name like
      '%n25%'` is `0` in all three.
    * the probe's own guard was `URL.endsWith("n25-copy.db")`, and the copies are
      named `n25-<app>-copy.db`. It could never have matched, i.e. the guard that
      existed to refuse the live database was itself a way of refusing to run —
      found by reading the string, not by running it.

    Cleanup is by PID tree, never `pkill -f next-server`: abetagent's dev server
    (:3400) shares that argv and is not ours to stop. Each run ends by verifying
    the probe port answers nothing and that pid 1161887 is still alive.

8.24.2 BEFORE, MEASURED

    Eleven claim shapes per app, key path, one minted API key on the copy, plus
    four bare-body orderings and one bad-key case. Full text in
    `/tmp/abectorial/n25-{leadgenius,abetflow,abetanalytics}-before.txt` (taken
    2026-10-04; the after-run is 2026-10-05, so the pair straddles a day and the
    dates in the prose are not typos).

      shape              leadgenius              abetflow                abetanalytics
      absent/null/""     200 own                 200 own                 409 own route
      own id             200 own                 200 own                 409 own route
      foreign text id    403 "…API key."         403 "…API key."         403 "…API key."
      42 / {} / true     403 "…API key."         403 "…API key."         400 "must be text"
      0 / false          403 "…API key."         200 own   <- honoured   400 "must be text"
      [own id]           200 own   <- coerced    403 "…API key."         400 "must be text"
      bad key + claim    401 about the key       401 about the key       401 about the key

    Two of those cells are the reason the task existed. abetflow's `0` and `false`
    answered 200 in the key's own workspace: `if (claimed && claimed !== ws)` reads
    a falsy claim as though nothing had been said, so a caller who put another
    tenant's id in the body got a playground run for the tenant the *key* belonged
    to. leadgenius's `[own id]` answered 200 for a different reason — its route
    wrote `String(claim)` on the line before the call, and `String(["ws-abc"])` is
    `"ws-abc"`; a one-element array is text equal to its element once coerced. Same
    vendored module, two ways to be wrong, nothing red.

    The bare-body rows are what make the ordering claim rather than a hope:
    abetflow answered `400 text is required` for `{workspaceId: 0}` — the claim was
    ignored *and* the body error came first. abetanalytics answered `400
    workspaceId must be text.` for a body with no `metricId` at all, which is the
    ordering a tenant boundary needs: refuse the impossible claim before telling a
    caller their body was incomplete.

    Two more facts were read, not probed, and are labelled as such. leadworks's
    claim is `searchParams.get("workspaceId")` on a GET, which returns `string |
    null` and therefore cannot deliver a non-text claim at all — its disagreement
    is unreachable rather than absent. And the shipped browser half cannot newly
    hit the 400: `ai-panel.tsx:142` is `if (config.workspaceId)
    payload.workspaceId = config.workspaceId`, on a prop typed `workspaceId?:
    string`, so an empty or absent value is omitted rather than sent.

8.24.3 THE RULE, AND WHY ABETANALYTICS'S

    `classifyWorkspaceClaim(claim, workspaceId)` returns `absent | own | foreign |
    not-text`; `enforceWorkspaceClaim(claim, workspaceId, via)` throws
    `AiAuthError(message, status)` from that verdict. The rule is abetanalytics's,
    adopted rather than invented, because it was the only measured answer that
    never told a lie about the request: a claim that is not text names no
    workspace, so it is refused rather than read as one.

      undefined / null / ""   absent — no claim was made
      not a string            400 "workspaceId must be text."
      text, unequal           403 "…API key." on the key path
                              401 "Not found." on the session path
      text, equal             own

    `""` is absent, not refused: it *is* text, so "must be text" would be a false
    statement about the request, and an empty field is what a cleared control
    sends. That matches abetanalytics's measured 409, so adopting its rule changes
    nothing there — which is the test of whether "adopt" means anything.

    `via` decides the *answer*, not the verdict, and that split is the part worth
    keeping: a bearer key is being told about its own scope, so naming the field is
    right; a signed-in browser is being told "we do not show you that", and
    confirming a workspace exists by calling it "not yours" is a small leak the
    module has always refused. The check runs after the credential resolves, so an
    invalid key stays a 401 about the key (measured in all three apps, with a
    foreign claim in the same body).

    `AiAuthError` gained a `status` parameter, defaulting to 401. That is
    observable only through `withAiAuth`, whose body is
    `{ error: message }` — byte-identical to what each app's `withAuth` already
    renders for its own `ApiKeyError`, which is what lets a 403 and a 400 move
    files without moving a byte.

8.24.4 THE FOUR ROUTES STOPPED PASSING THE CLAIM DOWN

    `keyWorkspaceId: () => authedWorkspaceId(request)` — no second argument — in
    leadgenius's `draft-reply`, leadworks's `explain-lead`, abetflow's `try-node`
    and abetanalytics's `explain-metric`. That is the wiring step, and its effect
    is not what it looks like: it does not weaken those apps' local key check, it
    stops the local check from answering *first*, so the vendored rule is the one
    that speaks. leadgenius also lost its `String()`.

    A wider version of this round was considered and refused. 59 call sites pass a
    claim to `authedWorkspaceId`: 29 read `searchParams.get("workspaceId")` (a
    non-text claim is unreachable), and 30 do not — 29 a JSON body field, one a
    dynamic path segment (leadgenius's `/api/analytics/[workspaceId]`, text by
    construction for the same reason a query param is). Three of the 30 were the
    AI routes. The other 27 — leadgenius 9, leadworks 7, abetagent 3, abetflow 3,
    abetanalytics 5 — live in `apikeys.ts`, which five apps each keep their own
    copy of (145 to 155 lines) and abetapi has none of, and which is not vendored.
    Pushing the rule there means vendoring `apikeys.ts` or editing four more
    un-synced files, and it would change the answer of roughly 27 write routes whose
    before-state this round did not probe. Mid-round it was done once, to abetflow's
    `apikeys.ts`, and reverted: the AI route no longer consults it, so the change
    bought nothing here and cost a claim about 10 other routes. Filed instead.

8.24.5 THE GUARD TEST FAILED THE BUILD BEFORE ANY PROBE DID

    `shared/tests/ai-claim.test.ts` is vendored by `sync-guards.sh`, whose MAP now
    has a second entry. It runs under `tsx --test` with no server, no key and no
    database, which had to be proven rather than assumed: `src/lib/ai-actor.ts`
    imports `next/headers` through `./session`, and `n25-offline.mts` importing a
    vendored copy before the rule existed answered with the module's exports and
    exit 0 — the named-import test now proving the same thing six times per run.
    Only the pure
    function is testable that way — `resolveAiActor`'s session branch needs a
    request scope, which is why the rule was factored out of it at all.

    First run of `npm run typecheck` after the sync: the same error in all six apps.

      tests/unit/ai-claim.test.ts(72,21): error TS2558: Expected 0 type arguments,
      but got 1.        (assert.notEqual<unknown>(…) is not generic)

    Which is the vendoring argument, demonstrated rather than asserted: one wrong
    line, six identical failures, no judgement call about which apps mattered. The
    test is 10 assertions per app; the family went 632 → 692.

    `check-guards-drift.sh` went 24 → 42 assertions: three per app for the new file
    (byte identity, that it still imports `../../src/lib/ai-actor` rather than
    re-declaring the rule locally, and that the module exists to be imported). The
    middle one is the copy of #44's lesson — `cmp` cannot tell "identical and
    load-bearing" from "identical and trusted by nothing".

8.24.6 AFTER, AND THE 1:1 COMPARISON

      app            before vs after
      leadgenius     6 rows changed: 42/0/false/true/{} 403→400, [own id] 200→400,
                     plus the three bare-body rows with them
      abetflow       6 rows changed: 0 and false 200→400, 42/true/{}/[own id]
                     403→400, and bare-body `{workspaceId: 0}` went from the body
                     error "text is required" to the claim refusal
      abetanalytics  unchanged, byte for byte, all 16 rows

    And the claim itself: with the four *accepted* rows excluded (they differ by
    route — 200/200/409 is each route's own business logic), every refusal row in
    the three after-states is identical, status and body. That is the 1:1 close for
    the rule; `comm` on the normalised rows found no difference in either
    direction. `check-all.sh` green: 16 steps, 0 failed, 62s, with
    ui-drift 6/0, auth-drift 288/0, guards-drift 42/0, docs 95/0.

    Committed as `shared 0adba2a`, `leadgenius d75555c`, `leadworks fc7f4fc`,
    `abetagent df04bde`, `abetflow 7c29704`, `abetanalytics faa973c`, `abetapi
    ad3c66e`. No app was rebuilt: `ss -ltnp` at the end of the round shows sshd on
    :22, docker-proxy on :3101 (abetConnect), `MainThread` pid 1526 on :80 (the
    Operations Console), postgres on :5432 and redis on :6379, and no Next.js
    listener on any documented app port — abetagent's dev process 1161887 is
    alive but bound to nothing. So there was no stale `.next/` serving the old
    rule for a probe to contradict, and the probes ran on `next dev` for the same
    §8.21 reason they always do.

    What this round does *not* claim: the session path was not driven over HTTP. It
    needs a login cookie, which needs a credential and a write, and the round's
    isolation rule says a stored row is only read from a copy. Its 401 `Not found.`
    is preserved by construction (`via === "session"`) and asserted offline in six
    apps, not probed.

8.24.7 OWED, AND WHAT THE ROUND DELIBERATELY DID NOT DO

    * The claim rule for the other 27 body-shaped `authedWorkspaceId` sites, above.
      Title of #43 was "all six apps"; what moved is the AI surface of four, and
      #43 stays open with this counted in it rather than renamed away.
    * abetapi's `/api/ai/diagnose-webhook` still resolves session-or-key inline and
      drops the claim at `DIAGNOSE_POST` validation, so it cannot be steered by a
      body field — the safest of the six, and the only one not using the shared
      resolver. Moving it needs its 401 envelope measured first, since it answers
      through `requireKey` rather than `withAuth`.
    * abetagent vendors `ai-actor.ts` with no route that imports it (#45). It now
      also runs the claim test against that unimported module, which is honest and
      slightly absurd; #45 is the cleaner place to decide.
    * The probe leaves one `ApiKey` row named "n25 claim shapes" on each copy. That
      is deliberate — the copy is thrown away, and deleting rows from a file the
      round made is how a probe starts touching something it should not. Live files
      verified untouched (§8.24.1).

8.25 THE CONSOLE'S OWN REGISTRY BECOMES A SUITE — #40'S DETECTION HALF

    #40 was filed as "two console suites run from /tmp paths that no longer exist".
    It turned out to be three, and the file that made them runnable at all — the
    console's `SMOKE_SUITES` table — was read by nothing. So this round does not
    restore what /tmp took. It makes the registry self-checking, which is the part
    that can be gated, and files the rest honestly.

8.25.1 BEFORE, MEASURED

    Measured with `ls` and `find` on 2026-10-05, all three answers are the same:

        /tmp/agentwork-venv     No such file or directory
        /tmp/simulate.py        No such file or directory
        /tmp/load*.py           No such file or directory

    `find` over /srv/work and /root (excluding node_modules and .venv) turns up no
    copy of any of them. So the agentwork suite's pytest, the buyer-journey
    simulation and the load harness the console's README still described were all
    gone, and the only place that recorded they had ever worked is the console's own
    persisted history:

        history.json   16 sim runs, 15 of them ok
                       newest green 2026-09-27T14:36:28  `RESULT: 46 passed, 0 failed`
                       last tests run 2026-09-27T14:35:16  13/13 passed
                       (the registry has 16 entries today; it had 13 then)

    That one line of `ops-console/history.json` is also where the README's
    "46-assertion buyer journey suite" came from. The number is the console's own
    last green result, not a claim anyone has been able to re-earn since — which
    changed how this round had to treat it (8.25.6).

    What deleted them is not knowable from in here, and saying so is part of the
    round. The box has been up 6 days 20:34, and /tmp still holds files from
    2026-09-28 onward, so this session cannot point at a reboot. It could be an
    earlier boot, `tmpfiles-clean`, or a hand. The rule this round writes is
    therefore about the *kind* of path, not about a cause the record cannot support.

    Also measured, because it changes what "the console is green" can mean right
    now: no console is running. `pgrep -af serve.py` returns nothing, :3800 is not
    listening, and the only :80 listener is code-server's node (pid 1526, a
    MainThread socket — the summary this round inherited called it the console,
    which is wrong and is corrected here). So every verdict below comes from
    running the checks directly, not from a button.

8.25.2 THE ASSET HALF, DONE BEFORE THIS ROUND AND UNCOMMITTABLE

    agentwork's suite was already restored when this round started: a project-local
    `/srv/work/products/abetworks-agentwork/.venv` (pytest 9.1.1, the package
    installed `-e`, plus flask/fastapi/httpx/pytest-asyncio for `tests/conftest.py`)
    and `.venv/bin/pytest -q` → 28 passed, 0 failed, exit 0. The registry entry is
    now the *relative* `[.venv/bin/pytest, -q]`, which needed proving rather than
    assuming: `subprocess.run` documents a relative program as unresolved against
    `cwd`, and this box's Python 3.14 measured `rc 0` for exactly that call from
    /root. The check gates the resolution instead of trusting either.

    None of that is committable. `/srv/work/.gitignore:10` is `/products/`, so
    `git ls-files products/ops-console` is empty and `check-ignore -v` names the
    line. That is #42, and it is why this round's real artifact is the checker in
    `shared/` — the one place the console's failures can be versioned and gated.

8.25.3 THE CHECK

    `shared/check-suites.sh`, 73 assertions, registered as the console's sixteenth
    suite and as the gate's fifth shared check. It parses `serve.py` with `ast` and
    never imports or runs it, then asserts per registered suite:

      * the folder exists under /srv/work/products;
      * `argv[0]` resolves — absolute, relative to the suite's own folder, or on
        PATH, the three ways `subprocess.run(cwd=…)` can actually be pointed;
      * every script-shaped argument exists;
      * no path in the suite is under /tmp — the tmpfs rule, checked against the
        *declared* path before any existence test;
      * the timeout is a positive number of seconds and not 0;
      * no two suites share a label;
      * the simulation's path comes from one module-level `SIMULATE_SCRIPT`
        constant and is not on the tmpfs;
      * and every `shared/check-*.sh` except the gate itself appears in
        `check-all.sh`'s list or in the registry — read from those two files.

    Three findings came out of writing it, not out of thinking about it:

      * A tab is whitespace to bash's `read`, so an empty column collapses the ones
        after it. The first run reported `agentwork: script argument missing:
        .../180` — the timeout read as a path. The parser now spells empty as "-".
      * The tmpfs rule was originally nested under "the program exists and is
        executable", which is exactly backwards for #40: the venv *was* executable
        until it wasn't, and a rule that fires only on absence gates the symptom.
        Probe (a) in 8.25.4 is the version of this that would have passed.
      * `check-*.sh` matches `check-all.sh`, which is the gate and not a gated
        check; the first run of the wiring rule failed on it. Excluded, with the
        circularity named in the comment.

8.25.4 NEGATIVE PROBES

    Each mutation applied to a backup copy, run, then restored (`cmp` confirms
    serve.py identical to the backup afterwards). FAIL lines are verbatim.

    (a) `argv[0] = /tmp/n26-venv/bin/pytest`, created executable first — i.e. the
        pre-fix state while it still worked:
          FAIL  agentwork: runs from /tmp/n26-venv/bin/pytest — /tmp is a 1.9G
                tmpfs, so this suite is green only until the directory is reclaimed (#40)
    (b) folder `shared` → `shared-missing` for builder-ui:
          FAIL  builder-ui: suite folder missing: /srv/work/products/shared-missing
          FAIL  builder-ui: script argument missing: .../shared-missing/verify-builder.py
    (c) `SIMULATE_SCRIPT = Path("/tmp") / "simulate.py"`:
          FAIL  SIMULATE_SCRIPT = /tmp/simulate.py is on the tmpfs — this is #40's
                own failure, one layer up
    (d) an empty `shared/check-tmp-probe.sh` wired into nothing (then deleted):
          FAIL  check-tmp-probe.sh is in neither shared/check-all.sh's list nor the
                console's registry — it runs nowhere
    (e) two suites registered under the label `docs`:
          FAIL  1 suite label registered twice: docs

    (c) initially produced the wrong reason: the parser only recognised the
    `ROOT / "…"` form, so a `Path("/tmp")` constant fell through to "not a
    module-level constant". Teaching it the `Path("…")` call is what lets the tmpfs
    message fire on the path that actually caused #40.

8.25.6 THE SIMULATION: PATH GATED, ASSET OWED

    The check reports the missing `simulate.py` as OWED, prints the console's own
    last green line next to it, and exits 0. That is a deliberate exception to this
    file's own rule, and the reason is §8.23's: a gate red on an owed asset is a
    gate whose assertion gets deleted. Re-authoring a 46-assertion journey against
    seven live backends is a round of its own, and inventing one now to match a
    number in `history.json` would be precisely the restatement the campaign's
    measuring rule forbids. `ops-console/README.md`'s Simulate row now says the

    `TEST-REPORT.md:29` describes what the 46 covered (seven journeys plus tenant
    isolation, against live demos) — instead of advertising a suite that does not
    exist. `TEST-REPORT.md` itself is left alone: it is a dated report, and its
    claims were true on the day they were written; 8.25.1 is the note that they no
    longer are.

8.25.7 MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND

    * "sixteen green sim runs" — wrong twice over: `history.json` has sixteen sim

      and two comments said 2026-09-25 while the newest green was 2026-09-27. The
      count and the index are now in the comment as the reason the index is `[0]`.
    * The inherited claim that ":80 is the console" and that "/tmp is cleared by the
      machine" — both unchecked. `ss -ltnp` and `uptime -b` say otherwise; the

      looked for before being cited, and does not exist. Nothing in this round
      quotes it.

      placeholder line in serve.py for exactly one tool call — the file was
      re-read, not guessed at, before the next edit.

      the tool's own output path, which killed it after 15s. Re-run with `tee`.

8.26 TWO PRODUCTS, ONE QUEUE — AND A GUARD THAT WAS GREEN ON A MODULE THAT COULD
     NOT LOAD (#27)

    #27 is the collision abetagent and abetflow have had since before this campaign:
    both registered `new Queue("runs")` against the same Redis. The fix is two lines
    and one new check; the round is mostly about the middle of that sentence, because
    the first fix was wrong in a way only a running program could tell, and the guard
    that was supposed to catch this class of thing passed it.

8.26.1 BEFORE, MEASURED

    The before-state was already stored, which is the best kind of evidence a
    collision can offer. Read from the live Redis (db 0, `redis-cli -n 0 --scan` and
    `hget`, never written) on 2026-10-05:

        bull:runs:id            20 — one id counter, two products
        bull:runs*              26 keys
        job payloads            12 × run-execution (abetflow's job name)
                                8 × run-agent (abetagent's)
        bull:runs:failed        4, 11, 12, 14, 16, 20 — six of the seven failed jobs
                                are run-execution payloads whose failedReason is
                                `prisma.run.findUnique()` on `where: { id: undefined }`
        bull:runs:stalled-check present — both workers polling one queue, which is the
                                signature of two consumers and no boundary

    That failedReason is the whole finding. abetflow's schema has no `model Run`;
    abetagent's does (`abetagent/prisma/schema.prisma:140`), and `runId` is a field
    abetflow's payload never carries. So the text can only be abetagent's worker
    having taken an abetflow job. From outside, this is not theft — it is a flaky
    database, and the app that is actually at fault is the one whose logs say nothing.

    Also measured before touching anything: how many queues each app registers,
    because rules 4 and 5 of the check had to be written so they cost nothing today.
    leadgenius 6 queues / 6 workers, leadworks 6/6, abetagent 1/1, abetflow 1/1,
    abetanalytics 1/1 — Queue and Worker sets equal in all five.

8.26.2 THE FIX, AND THE FORM bullmq REFUSES

    Both apps now name the queue in one exported constant, used by the Queue and the
    Worker alike:

        abetflow/src/lib/queue.ts:67   export const RUNS_QUEUE = "abetflow-runs";
        abetagent/src/lib/queue.ts:66  export const RUNS_QUEUE = "abetagent-runs";

    The `queues` object key stays `runs`, which is why nothing else had to move:
    `/api/health` builds its `queues` payload from `Object.entries(queues)`
    (`abetflow/src/app/api/health/route.ts:11`) and the console's queue-depth panel
    reads that payload, so both report the same labels as before the rename. Verified
    rather than assumed, over HTTP against the running abetagent on :3400:

        {"ok":true,"db":"up","queues":{"runs":{"waiting":0,"active":0,"failed":0}}}

    The first fix was `abetflow:runs` / `abetagent:runs`. A colon is what the
    keyspace itself uses — `bull:<name>:…` — so it read as the namespaced form, and
    `shared/check-queues.sh` rules 1–6 all passed on it. bullmq 5.81.5 will not
    construct it. `queue-base.js:35`:

        if (name.includes(':')) { throw new Error('Queue name cannot contain :'); }

    Probed two ways, both against the app's own installed bullmq. Constructing the
    name directly:

        abetflow:runs:  threw "Queue name cannot contain :" at new QueueBase
                        (/srv/work/products/abetflow/node_modules/bullmq/dist/cjs/
                         classes/queue-base.js:35:19)
        abetflow-runs:  constructed, q.name=abetflow-runs

    And importing the real module, which is the failure that matters, since it is the
    one that looks like a broken deploy rather than a broken name:

        import threw: Queue name cannot contain :

    So the separator is a hyphen. Both doc comments, both READMEs and the check now
    say that, and say where it came from — the probe, not the docs.

8.26.3 THE CHECK

    `shared/check-queues.sh` (new) parses each app's `src/lib/queue.ts` with a regex
    over the source — no execution, so it runs with no Redis and no node_modules
    guarantee — resolving `export const X = "…"` so it follows the constant instead of
    the literal. Seven rules:

      1. every app's queue module exists and names something it can read
      2. no name resolves to UNRESOLVED (a name the scanner stopped seeing is the
         collision it exists to catch, §8.20's rule)
      3. no two products register the same Queue name
      4. per app: no Worker listens on a name that app's Queues never enqueue to
      5. per app: no Queue lacks a Worker in the same product
      6. abetagent's and abetflow's names start with their own product id and a hyphen
      7. no name contains a colon — bullmq throws at construction

    Today: `Queue namespace check — 49 passed, 0 failed`. It was 48 with rules 1–6;
    rule 7 is the one this round owed it, and it is asserted as a property of the
    names rather than by grepping bullmq's source, because node_modules is not
    committed and a check that only works after `npm install` is not a gate. If a
    future bullmq relaxes the constraint this rule goes stale in the loud direction —
    a needless FAIL — which is the right way for a guard to be wrong.

8.26.4 NEGATIVE PROBES

    Each rule was made to fail, on the real files, from backups restored afterwards
    (`/tmp/abectorial/n27c-negative.sh`; both queue modules `cmp`-identical to those
    backups at the end).

    (a) the colon form — rules 6 and 7, three FAILs, and the module cannot be
        imported:

          FAIL  abetagent's queue name "abetagent:runs" does not start with its own
                product id and a hyphen — on a shared Redis the name is the only thing
                saying whose jobs these are
          FAIL  abetflow's queue name "abetflow:runs" does not start with its own
                product id and a hyphen — …
          FAIL  queue name containing a colon: Queue:abetagent:abetagent:runs
                Worker:abetagent:abetagent:runs Queue:abetflow:abetflow:runs
                Worker:abetflow:abetflow:runs — bullmq throws `Queue name cannot
                contain :` when the Queue is constructed, so the module cannot be
                imported
          Queue namespace check — 46 passed, 3 failed
          real module import (abetflow): import threw: Queue name cannot contain :

    (b) two products back on one name — rule 3, and 6 as collateral:

          FAIL  queue name "runs" is registered by abetagent abetflow — one BullMQ
                name is one Redis keyspace (bull:runs:*), so these products share a
                worker pool
          Queue namespace check — 46 passed, 3 failed

    (c) a Worker on a name the Queue never enqueues to (abetflow's Worker pointed at
        `abetflow-jobs`) — rules 4 and 5 fire together, which is correct: the app has
        a producer nothing reads and a reader nothing produces:

          FAIL  abetflow runs a Worker on abetflow-jobs which its own queues never
                enqueue to — that worker drains nothing this app produces
          FAIL  abetflow enqueues to abetflow-runs with no Worker in the same product
                subscribing — the jobs are accepted and never drained

    (d) a name the scanner cannot resolve (`RUNS_QUEUE` built from `process.env`):

          FAIL  abetflow: Queue constructed with a name the check cannot resolve
                (origin=unresolved:RUNS_QUEUE) — an unreadable queue name is invisible
                to the collision rule
          FAIL  abetflow: Worker constructed with a name the check cannot resolve …
          Queue namespace check — 45 passed, 4 failed

    (e) the starved keyspace as a running thing, not only a rule: enqueue into a name
        no worker holds, on a scratch db, then start a worker on it.

          enqueued into abetflow-jobs: {"waiting":1,"active":0,"completed":0,…}
          undrained: abetflow-jobs#1 data={"executionId":"starved-probe-id"}
          worker on abetflow-jobs drained run-execution#1 data={"executionId":"starved-probe-id"}
          counts now: {"active":0,"completed":1,…,"waiting":0}

        The job is accepted by Redis and sits there. Nothing in the app's own
        behaviour changes, which is exactly why rule 5 exists.

8.26.5 AFTER-PROBE

    The real modules, imported (not paraphrased), against Redis db 13 — dbsize 0
    before, `flushdb` on that db after, and db 0's 26 `bull:runs*` keys never written:

        abetflow: imported RUNS_QUEUE=abetflow-runs, added run-execution#1
          own keyspace (6 keys): bull:abetflow-runs:1 :events :id :marker :meta :wait
          at that moment, abetagent-runs:* -> none

        abetagent: imported RUNS_QUEUE=abetagent-runs, added run-agent#1
          own keyspace (6 keys): bull:abetagent-runs:1 :events :id :marker :meta :wait

        bull:abetflow-runs:id = 1     bull:abetagent-runs:id = 1
        bull:abetflow-runs:<id> -> 1  payload {"executionId":"probe-execution-id"}
        bull:abetagent-runs:<id> -> 1 payload {"runId":"probe-run-id"}

    Two counters, each at 1. Under the shared name the same two enqueues produced ids
    1 and 2 in one keyspace with one counter, which is the control this round ran
    first. Each payload is only in its own product's keyspace.

8.26.6 HOW A PROBE WRITTEN TO THE LIVE DB, AND WHY

    Two of this round's probes landed in db 0 — the developer's live keyspace — which
    is the rule this campaign has held since §8.15, so the cause is recorded. The
    probes passed a connection *string* straight into `new Queue(…, { connection })`.
    bullmq's `RedisConnection` constructor
    (`node_modules/bullmq/dist/cjs/classes/redis-connection.js:49`) does:

        this.opts = Object.assign({ port: 6379, host: '127.0.0.1', retryStrategy }, opts)

    and `Object.assign` onto a string copies its indexed characters, not its meaning:
    the URL is discarded and the client comes up on the default host and **db 0**.
    Measured side by side:

        string form:  client selected_db=0   → keys in db 0
        options form: client selected_db=13  → keys in db 13

    The apps themselves are not affected: each builds a real `new IORedis(process.env
    .REDIS_URL, …)` and hands bullmq an instance, which is the branch that is used as
    given — that is why the after-probe in 8.26.5 landed in db 13. The exposure is in
    probe code, and it is invisible from the outside: no error, no warning, the job
    simply appears somewhere else.

    Cleanup, done in this round and reported rather than silent: the leak took db 0
    from 252 keys to 264. The 12 added keys were exactly `bull:abetflow-jobs:*` (5) and
    `bull:n27x-string:*` (7); both names appear in no app source (grepped), and every
    job hash held a probe sentinel (`{"executionId":"starved-probe-id"}`,
    `{"sentinel":"n27x"}`). Those 12 keys were deleted by name, db 0 is back at 252
    with its 26 `bull:runs*` keys untouched, and db 13 is flushed to 0. The rule this
    round writes for Redis probes is the one already held for SQLite: a probe script
    refuses to run unless it can show it is on a scratch db — a connection string is
    not a db selector, so build the client and read `options.db`.

8.26.7 THE RUNTIME TEST THAT COULD NOT BE WRITTEN
    [CORRECTED 2026-10-05, see §8.27.2: three of the four rows below were mis-recorded.
    The command used was `timeout 25 npx tsx x.mts 2>&1 | grep -v 'npm warn'; echo
    "rc=$?"`, which reports grep's exit status, not the probe's (§8.19, re-proved
    against my own table one round later). Re-measured with `>log 2>&1; rc=$?` and
    `${PIPESTATUS[0]}. The corrected table:]

        t1  imports the app module, asserts the name           rc=124 (hung, 40s)
        t2  bullmq Queue on a lazy ioredis, disconnects         rc=124 (hung)
        t3  bare lazy ioredis                                  rc=124 (hung)
        t4  t1 + `await shutdownWorkers()` in after()           rc=124 (hung), and
                                                              the hook failed:
            "Stream isn't writeable and enableOfflineQueue options is false"
              at EventEmitter.quit (ioredis/built/utils/Commander.js:90:25)
              at shutdownWorkers (abetflow/src/lib/queue.ts:86:20)

    So the finding is stronger than the one written here at the time, and simpler: no
    variant of "import the module and construct a Queue" exits on its own. t3 — a bare
    lazy client, no bullmq at all — hung too, which is what a `lazyConnect: true`
    client does once something connects it, and the reason t1/t2/t3 all leave a handle
    open is the same one §8.27.3 measured: construction is connection. The claim in the
    next paragraph ("the same import in a plain script exits 0") was that grep's rc=0,
    and is false.

    The same import in a plain script exits 0, so it is the module's import-time side
    effect (a Queue and a client constructed at module scope) interacting with the test
    runner's teardown that keeps the process alive. `src/lib/queue.ts` cannot be
    imported by an offline unit test, and `shutdownWorkers()` — the only exit path the
    module offers — throws on a client that never connected. Both halves are filed
    (#50), and the file was deleted rather than left to hang the suite; abetflow is
    back to `tests 121 pass 121 fail 0`. The constructibility proof therefore lives in
    two places the suite can reach: rule 7 of the guard, and this round's after-probe.

8.26.8 THE REGISTRY, THE GATE, THE COUNTS

    `shared/check-queues.sh` is wired in three places, because a check wired in one is
    a check that gets skipped:

      ops-console/serve.py        17th suite, `("queues", "shared", …)` — the entry
                                  this round's own registry check now validates
      shared/check-all.sh         6th shared check: `for c in check-suites
                                  check-ui-drift check-auth-drift check-guards-drift
                                  check-queues check-docs`
      shared/README.md            listed, described, and a new section

    `check-suites.sh`'s wiring rule is what forced the second one — it fails any
    `shared/check-*.sh` that appears in neither the gate nor the registry — and it went
    from 73 to 78 assertions by counting the new suite's four per-suite rules plus its
    own wiring. `check-docs.sh` went 97 to 98 for the same reason in the other
    direction: its §11 asserts every file in `shared/` is named in `shared/README.md`.
    `docs check — 98 passed, 0 failed`.

    Gate, end to end, no servers started by it:

        Gate — 18 passed, 0 failed in 68s of step time
        (17 steps before this round; abetagent 108 tests, abetflow 121, leadgenius
         126, leadworks 115, abetanalytics 95, abetapi 127 — all fail 0)

    Counts corrected by measurement, not by arithmetic: `shared/README.md` said
    `check-suites.sh` runs "104 assertions". The check has never printed 104 — it
    printed 73 before this round and 78 after, and the number moves with the registry
    because four of its rules run once per suite. The count is gone from the prose and
    the verdict line owns it. Also re-counted while editing: 17 scripts in `shared/`
    (3 sync, 8 check, 3 run-*-verify, 3 verify-*.py), and ops-console's README now says
    17 suites / 13 shared verification scripts, which `check-docs.sh` §12 enforces
    against `serve.py` rather than against anyone's memory.

8.26.9 MISTAKES INSIDE THIS ROUND

    * Two probes wrote to live db 0 (8.26.6). Found because the round checked db 0's
      key count after, not only before. Cleaned up and reported; the rule changed.
    * The first version of the after-probe asked `rd.keys("bull:<other>:job:*")` for
      the other product's jobs. bullmq stores a job hash at `bull:<name>:<id>`, so that
      pattern matches nothing, and the probe reported "abetagent-runs job keys holding
      my payload: none" — which was vacuously true and proved nothing about
      separation. Rewritten to scan `bull:<other>:*` and to read each keyspace's job
      hashes by name; that is the version quoted in 8.26.5.
    * The negative probes restored only abetflow's file between stages, so abetagent
      stayed at whatever the previous stage had left and its name appeared in later
      stages' FAIL lists. The messages were each correct about the file they described,
      but the stages were noisier than they looked, and the end-of-script `cmp` on
      abetagent showed a difference that the exit trap had already fixed.
    * A starved-queue demonstration was reported in stage (c) of the negative probes
      before anything had enqueued into the starved name — the probe's enqueue step had
      been dropped from the script. The guard FAIL lines were real; the "0 keys" that
      followed was not a demonstration of anything. Stage (e) in 8.26.4 is the same
      measurement done properly.

8.26.10 STILL OWED

    * The orphaned `bull:runs:*` keyspace in db 0 — 26 keys, 20 jobs, 7 of them failed.
      Nothing drains it now: both workers listen on their own names. It is left exactly
      as it was, because deleting stored state from the developer's live db 0 is not
      this round's to take unasked; and because the six `run-execution` failures inside
      it are the campaign's best evidence of what a shared name cost, which a `DEL`
      would erase. Filed (#51): decide whether to archive them into
      `TEST-REPORT.md`-shaped evidence or drop the keyspace.
    * leadgenius, leadworks and abetanalytics still register bare names (`send`,
      `scrape`, `snapshots`, …). Unclaimed by any other product today, so rules 3–5
      pass on them; rule 6 is deliberately limited to the two apps this round
      separated, rather than widened silently. Filing as part of #51's neighbourhood:
      if a second product ever wants `send`, the guard says so before the app does.
    * #50 — `src/lib/queue.ts` is unimportable offline, and `shutdownWorkers()` is
      exported by five apps and called by none (grepped across all five `src/` trees),
      and throws on a never-connected client.
    * #49 is now 17 suites on 3.8 GB, not 16.

8.26.11 EVIDENCE

    Redis: db 0 read-only throughout (`--scan`, `hget`, `dbsize`); db 13 used as
    scratch, dbsize 0 before and after. Probes: /tmp/abectorial/n27b-run.sh,
    n27b-colon.mts, n27b-probe.mts, n27b-after.txt, n27c-negative.sh, n27c-f.bak and
    n27c-a.bak (the restores), n27x.mts (the db-selector comparison). Scratch `.mts`
    and `.test.ts` files were written inside the app directories so bullmq and ioredis
    resolve, and every one of them is deleted; `git status` in abetflow and abetagent
    shows only queue.ts and README.md modified, plus shared/'s new check.

8.26.12 AFTER, ON THE LIVE BOX

    Read-only Redis after the commits, because the interesting question is whether the
    rename is actually live and what the old keyspace is doing now:

        bull:abetagent-runs:*   1 key (:meta: {version: bullmq:5.81.5, opts.maxLenEvents: 10000})
        bull:abetflow-runs:*    1 key (:meta, same shape)
        bull:runs*              26 keys, unchanged
        db 0                    253 keys (252 after the cleanup in 8.26.6)

    abetagent's dev server (:3400, pid 1161887, started Sat 2026-10-03 08:32) is the
    process holding its own new keyspace: `next dev` recompiled `src/lib/queue.ts` and
    the Queue it constructs now writes `bull:abetagent-runs:meta`. Its `/api/health`
    still reports the label `runs` (8.26.2), so nothing downstream moved.

    [CORRECTED 2026-10-05: the cause of the paragraph below is established, in §8.27.3 —
    importing `src/lib/queue.ts` constructs a Queue, and construction connects. Dev
    `REDIS_URL` defaults to db 0, so any process that merely imported the module —
    `tsx --test`, `prisma seed`, a one-off script, this round's own typecheck-adjacent
    probe — wrote exactly that key with nothing enqueued. "One `:meta`, no `:id`, no
    job hashes" is the signature of an import, not of a worker.]

    `bull:abetflow-runs:meta` in db 0 is recorded as an observation with no established
    cause. No abetflow process is running (`ss` shows nothing on :3500, `pgrep` finds no
    abetflow `next`), and the two probes this round that could have written it were
    pointed at db 13. What is known is the shape: one `:meta` key, no `:id` counter, no
    job hashes — a Queue constructed once, its version written, nothing enqueued.
    Not chased further, because it is one key of live dev state and the alternative
    explanations (a hot reload by a process that has since exited, a probe whose
    connection string silently selected db 0 per 8.26.6) are not distinguishable from
    here. Filed with the keyspace question, which is #51.

    The number that reframes #27's urgency: `OBJECT IDLETIME bull:runs:id` is 159,275s
    at 06:30Z — about 44 hours, i.e. last touched around 2026-10-03 10:10Z. The
    collision is real and its damage is stored, but on this box it has not been an
    actively bleeding thing for two days: whatever enqueued into `bull:runs` last did so
    before abetagent's server had been restarted, and the six cross-product failures in
    it are history rather than a live stream. IDLETIME counts any key-level read, so
    this round's own `hget` on that counter is a possible confound in the number — the
    direction of the error is "more recent than it was", and even so the key was not
    touched in the hours immediately before the rename.

8.27 #50 — ABETFLOW + ABETAGENT: THE QUEUE MODULE COULD NOT BE IMPORTED WITHOUT
         TOUCHING LIVE REDIS, AND NOTHING CALLED shutdownWorkers()

    Files: abetflow/src/lib/queue.ts, abetflow/src/lib/shutdown.ts (new),
    abetflow/instrumentation.ts, abetflow/tests/unit/queue.test.ts (new),
    abetflow/README.md; the same five in abetagent; shared/check-queues.sh,
    shared/README.md.

    Committed as abetflow `e460331`, abetagent `9a8fb01`, shared `02e4341`. The ledger
    and `ops-console/` are still outside version control (#42).

8.27.1 WHAT WAS ASKED

    #50 was filed as "src/lib/queue.ts is unimportable offline, and shutdownWorkers()
    is dead code". Both halves turned out to be one defect with a third underneath it,
    and the first wording was wrong about the cause: §8.26.7 recorded the import hanging
    as an interaction with the test runner's teardown. It is not.

8.27.2 THE MEASURING BUG THAT COVERED THE CAUSE UP

    §8.26.7's table said t2 and t3 exited 0 and only t1/t4 hung. Three of the four rows
    are wrong, and the reason is §8.19 — the command was

        timeout 25 npx tsx x.mts 2>&1 | grep -v 'npm warn'; echo "rc=$?"

    which reports grep's status. Re-measured with `>log 2>&1; rc=$?`, all four variants
    are rc=124. That matters beyond the table: an rc that is not the program's rc is how
    a hung process got recorded as a clean exit, and the recorded "cause not
    established" in §8.26.12 was the same event seen through the wrong exit code. The
    correction is written into §8.26.7 and §8.26.12 rather than only here.

8.27.3 CONSTRUCTION IS CONNECTION (probed)

    `new Queue(name, {connection})` is not inert, even with a client built
    `lazyConnect: true`. A script whose only act was constructing a queue was still
    alive at 30s with the client at `status=connecting` and one key —
    `bull:<name>:meta`, `{version: bullmq:5.81.5, opts.maxLenEvents: 10000}` — in the
    db it pointed at. Next's tick-scheduled connect is the whole side effect; nothing
    needs to call `add()`.

    Two consequences, one of which explains a stored observation from last round:

      * dev `REDIS_URL` defaults to `redis://localhost:6379`, i.e. db 0. So importing
        the module — in a test, a seed script, a one-off probe — writes into live dev
        state. `bull:abetflow-runs:meta` and `bull:abetagent-runs:meta`, the two keys
        §8.26.12 recorded with no established cause, are that: one `:meta`, no `:id`
        counter, no job hashes, the signature of an import rather than a worker.
      * no offline test of the queue name could be written, because the assertion and
        the write were the same act.

8.27.4 THE FIX: BUILD ON FIRST TOUCH

        const built: { runs?: Queue } = {};
        export const queues = {
          get runs(): Queue { return (built.runs ??= new Queue(RUNS_QUEUE, { connection })); },
        };

    Measured after, in abetflow and abetagent separately: `MODE=import` (import the
    module, call shutdownWorkers, nothing touched) exits rc=0 in ~2s and leaves db 13 at
    dbsize 0 — no `:meta`, because no construction. The getter is memoised, so
    `/api/health`'s `Object.entries(queues)` poll cannot leak one Queue per request, and
    the health route needed no change because it reads the *key*, not the BullMQ name.

8.27.5 TEARDOWN: THREE CRASHES, IN THE ORDER THEY WERE FOUND

    The old body was `Promise.all(Object.values(queues).map(q => q.close()))` then
    `connection.quit()`. It closed zero Workers (`startWorkers()` kept them in a local)
    and `quit()` on a never-connected client throws `Stream isn't writeable and
    enableOfflineQueue options is false`. Replacing that with
    workers → queues → `disconnect()` was not enough either, and the comment I wrote
    claiming it was is corrected below rather than quietly deleted. Measured sequence on
    a scratch db (db 13; every probe script refuses to run unless its own URL names it):

      (a) queue built and closed in the same tick → rc=1,
            Error: Connection is closed. at StandaloneConnector.js:46
            Emitted 'error' event on RedisConnection instance at redis-connection.js:87
          Same script with the connection allowed to reach `ready` first → rc=0. The
          mechanism is in bullmq: `redis-connection.js:87` attaches
          `initializing.catch(err => this.emit('error', err))` to the in-flight connect,
          and the only suppression of that rejection sits inside `close()`'s
          `if (!this.extraOptions.shared)` branch (`redis-connection.js:429`) — never
          taken here, because this app hands bullmq a shared client deliberately. An
          `error` with no listener on an EventEmitter is an exit.
      (b) fix (a) by awaiting `queue.waitUntilReady()` and racing a timeout → rc=1 on a
          *dead* Redis, `Error: Connection is closed.` at ioredis Redis.js:220.
          Abandoning a bullmq promise that never lands is itself an unhandled rejection:
          the crash the wait was meant to prevent, arriving from the other side.
          Fixed by attaching `.catch(() => undefined)` at creation, so racing it is safe.
      (c) with the client settled, SIGTERM straight after `startWorkers()` → rc=1 again,
          `Stream isn't writeable … at EventEmitter.select (event_handler.js:41)`: a
          Worker's own setup issues commands *after* the shared client reaches `ready`,
          so waiting on the client is necessary but not sufficient. Fixed by awaiting
          readiness on every started object — queues and Workers — not on the client.

    Final shape, then close Workers, close Queues, `connection.disconnect()`, bounded by
    `SHUTDOWN_SETTLE_MS = 2_000` so a Redis that answers nothing cannot hang the drain.

    After, as a 12-cell matrix per app (ports 6379 = live server, 6399 = nothing
    listening × touch none/queue/worker × settle 0/1200ms), SIGTERM delivered to the
    real process: abetflow 12/12 rc=0, abetagent 12/12 rc=0, zero error lines in any
    log. Before (a)+(b)+(c) the same matrix had six failing cells. `touch=worker` really
    runs `startWorkers()`, so `workers.push(worker)` and `worker.close()` are exercised,
    not asserted from the text.

8.27.6 WHO CALLS IT: NEXT 16.2.12 HAS NO SHUTDOWN HOOK (read from source)

    `node_modules/next/dist/server/instrumentation/types.d.ts`, in both apps' own
    installs (next 16.2.12):

        export type InstrumentationModule = {
            register?(): void;
            onRequestError?: InstrumentationOnRequestError;
        };

    That is the entire surface — `register` and `onRequestError`, no `beforeClose`. So
    the only possible caller is a handler the app registers itself: new
    `src/lib/shutdown.ts` binds SIGTERM and SIGINT, drains through
    `shutdownWorkers()`, exits *after* the drain (a handler that exits first is the same
    as no handler), leaves non-zero past the 15s deadline, exits 1 on a second signal
    while draining, and is guarded by `installed` so a double call adds no listener.
    Measured: `installShutdownHandlers()` called twice → `listenerCount` 2 (one per
    signal), not 4. `instrumentation.ts` installs it only under
    `NODE_ENV === "production"`: `next dev` owns SIGINT in development and restarts the
    server itself, and this box's abetagent dev server on :3400 is not something to
    experiment on.

8.27.7 THE TEST THAT COULD NOT BE WRITTEN, NOW CAN

    tests/unit/queue.test.ts in both apps. Ten assertions run offline (name shape and
    no colon; the Queue built inside a getter and not as a data property; `??=`
    memoisation; `workers.push`; `waitUntilReady` ordered before `.close()`;
    `disconnect()` and not `quit()`; the production-gated install; both signals) plus one
    runtime assertion that is the round's actual subject — `Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor
    (queues, "runs").get` is a function and `.value` is undefined, i.e. importing built
    nothing. A probe block runs only when `QUEUE_PROBE_URL` names a db, and pointing it
    at db 0 is a *failure*, not a skip, because a silently shorter green run is §8.20's
    defect.

        abetflow  offline          tests 10 pass 10 fail 0        (exit 0, ~550ms)
        abetflow  QUEUE_PROBE_URL=…/13   tests 12 pass 12 fail 0
        abetflow  QUEUE_PROBE_URL=…/0    rc=1, "not ok 1 - refuses db 0…"
        abetagent same three forms, same results
        abetflow  npm test         tests 131 pass 131 fail 0   (was 121)
        abetagent npm test         tests 118 pass 118 fail 0   (was 108)

    CORRECTED IN §8.28.4: the line above reading `QUEUE_PROBE_URL=…/13 → 12 pass 0
    fail` is not a scratch-db probe. The env assignment sat inside the probe test, after
    the runtime-touch test had already imported the module, so `connection` had been
    built from the unset default and the run wrote into db 0. It passed; it also proved
    nothing about the db it named. The counts themselves (12 pass, 10 pass, rc=1) are
    right — only the word "scratch" was not.

    The suites exiting is not a trivial part of this: §8.26.7's t-files were deleted
    because `tsx --test` would not exit while the queue module was imported.

    One round's worth of its own mistakes is in these files, and the gate caught it
    rather than I did: the first version imported `"../../src/lib/queue.ts"`, which tsx
    runs and `tsc` refuses — TS5097, an import path may not end in `.ts` without
    `allowImportingTsExtensions`. `npm run typecheck` had passed, because it passed
    *before the test file existed*; the extensionless form is the house style
    (`pagination.test.ts` imports `../../src/lib/pagination`) and works for both.
    Removing the extension by `sed` then broke the other half, silently: the same
    string is also a `readFileSync` path in the source-scanning helper, and the file is
    genuinely named `queue.ts`. So runtime imports take no extension and file reads
    take the real name — `tests 122 pass 121 fail 1` was the whole lesson, from the
    suite, not from reading the code back.

8.27.8 THREE DEFECTS IN THE GUARD, EACH FOUND BY IT GOING WRONG

    shared/check-queues.sh grew from 49 assertions to 66 and from 7 rules to 8.

      * rule 8 (new): importing must not connect. It scans every `new Queue(` in the
        comment-stripped source and FAILs an app whose construction is not behind a
        getter. Asserted for the two apps fixed here; leadgenius, leadworks and
        abetanalytics still build at module scope (#52), one of the reasons the rule is
        scoped rather than portfolio-wide — the same shape as rule 6's scoping.
        Negative probe: a sandbox whose abetflow uses the eager form →
        "FAIL abetflow constructs a Queue at module scope (line=2)", rc=1, 35 pass 1 fail.
      * the parser read a doc comment as a call site. abetflow's memoisation comment
        writes `new Queue()` to explain the shape; the regex saw an unresolvable
        construction and reported 46 passed / 5 failed — three failures about a queue
        that does not exist. Comments are stripped before parsing now. Rewording the
        comment was tried first and does not hold: the prose recurses.
      * `sort -u | comm` must be byte-ordered. Under this box's `en_US.UTF-8`, `sort`
        places `UNRESOLVED` last of eight names where bytewise order places it first,
        and `comm` — which compares bytewise — then emitted
        `file 1/2/input is not in sorted order` and answered `-23` with a member present
        in *both* files. A/B on identical data: `LC_ALL=C comm -23` → "send" rc=0;
        locale-ordered inputs → "send" + "UNRESOLVED" rc=1. `export LC_ALL=C` at the top.
        Also found while probing this: a module that cannot be read printed a `missing`
        row and exited 0 (`raise SystemExit` with no code), so the rc gate — which only
        sees the *last* app in the loop anyway — never fired and the row reached the
        rules as data, including as a "queue name containing a colon" because the OSError
        text has colons. The row marker is now checked directly: sandbox with all five
        modules absent → "FAIL a product's queue module could not be read (5 of 5)", rc=1.

8.27.9 STATE OF THE BOX AFTER

    Redis db 0: 253 keys, unchanged through the round; its 26 `bull:runs*` keys and the
    orphaned keyspace are still there untouched (#51); the two `bull:abet*-runs:meta`
    keys are the app's own, and now explained. db 13 (scratch) flushed to 0 at the end.
    No probe wrote to db 0 this round: the three that did in earlier rounds are the
    reason every probe in §8.27 now begins by refusing unless its own URL names db 13.
    abetagent's :3400 dev server, hub :3900, abetpdf :3910, abetconnect :3101 and the
    postgres on :5432 were not touched.

8.27.10 THE GATE

    `bash shared/check-all.sh` → 18 passed, 0 failed (six shared checks, then
    typecheck + test for the six apps). The first run of it after this round's test file
    landed was 16/2 — both FAILs were `tsc` on the new `tests/unit/queue.test.ts`, the
    TS5097 above. That is the gate doing the job §8.20.5 was written for: `npm run
    typecheck` alone had passed minutes earlier because the file it now refuses did not
    exist yet, and the suite that runs it green does not type it.

8.27.11 FILED

    #52 — the other three apps still construct Queues at import (leadgenius 6,
          leadworks 6, abetanalytics 1), so `bull:send:meta`, `bull:scrape:meta` etc.
          are written by any import. leadworks additionally has a module-scope table
          holding `queues.export`, which must move behind a function before a getter
          buys anything there. Rule 8 then widens portfolio-wide.
    #50 — closed by this section.

8.28 #52 — THE OTHER THREE APPS, AND THE DEFECT IN #50'S OWN PROBE

    Round shape held: measured the before-state against the real modules, ported the
    engine, kept every declared queue, wired the drain to a caller, added the structural
    tests, re-measured, and only then ran the gate. Three apps — abetanalytics (1 queue),
    leadgenius (6), leadworks (6, plus a table that had to move).

8.28.1 BEFORE-STATE, RE-MEASURED RATHER THAN CARRIED FORWARD

    §8.26.7's discipline applies to a round's own numbers: the figures filed when #52 was
    opened were taken from a first-pass probe, so each app's HEAD module was checked out
    to a scratch file *inside* src/lib and imported on its own, with the probe refusing
    unless REDIS_URL ended in /13.

        abetanalytics  HEAD module, import only   rc=124   db13 = 1 key  (bull:snapshots:meta)
        leadgenius     HEAD module, import only   rc=124   db13 = 6 keys (send, sequence,
                                                            enrichment, webhook, analytics, warmup)
        leadworks      HEAD module, import only   rc=124   db13 = 6 keys (scrape, enrich, export,
                                                            dedupe, validate, scheduled)

    One import. No add(), no Worker, no job. rc=124 is the 30s timeout killing a process
    that never exited, which is the same fact §8.26.7 recorded for `tsx --test`: a suite
    that imports these modules cannot finish. Dev REDIS_URL defaults to db 0, so in the
    ordinary case those :meta writes land in live state.

    A count note, because it nearly produced a false alarm: db 0 read 252 keys once during
    these runs where it reads 253 every other time. Not a loss — `bull:runs:stalled-check`
    carries PX 30000 and cycles, so dbsize on db 0 is not a stable baseline. The check
    that matters is the :meta set, and it stayed at the same 16 names throughout.

8.28.2 THE PORT, ONE SHAPE

    Same three moves as §8.27, applied to five getters' worth of queues:

      * `const built: Partial<Record<QueueKey, Queue>> = {}` plus one accessor per queue,
        `return (built.x ??= new Queue("x", { connection }))` on a single line — the name
        stays the first argument of the construction, which is exactly where
        shared/check-queues.sh reads it.
      * `const workers: Worker[] = []` and `workers.push(new Worker(...))`: before this,
        startWorkers() returned nothing that shutdown could reach. A live Worker polls
        (`bzpopmin bull:<name>:marker`, seen in MONITOR below), which is the opposite of
        a drain.
      * the settle→workers→queues→`disconnect()` teardown, and `src/lib/shutdown.ts` +
        the production-gated install in `instrumentation.ts`, so the drain has a caller.
        All five apps now carry a byte-identical shutdown.ts.

    leadgenius needed nothing else: counting its reads rather than eyeballing them, there
    are eight across `src/` — one in the queue module, seven in files outside it (four
    webhook routes, the enrollment route, sequence-engine, warmup) — and every one sits in
    a call-time frame. That count is the whole-tree classifier of §8.28.7 run over all five
    apps: 24 reads, 22 outside the queue modules, 0 at module scope. abetanalytics'
    /api/health iterates `Object.entries(queues)`, which invokes the getters — a health
    poll still builds all of them, once, memoised.

8.28.3 LEADWORKS' SECOND FAILURE MODE, WHICH A GETTER DOES NOT FIX

    The blocker the task described is real and it is not a construction:

        const jobRunners: Record<(typeof JOB_TYPES)[number], { queue: Queue; name: string }> = {
          SCRAPE: { queue: queues.scrape, name: "run-scrape" },   // … five entries

    A module-scope *read* of the getter. Converting the construction to accessors would
    have left this app connecting on import anyway, because the table is evaluated at
    import and the first read constructs. The table now holds `QueueKey`s and `enqueueJob`
    does `enqueue(queues[runner.queue], runner.name, …)`; the Record-over-JOB_TYPES
    property (a job type with no runner is a compile error, not a row stuck PENDING) is
    unchanged, and `npm run typecheck` says so.

    Proved as two negative probes on sandbox copies, not argued. Numbers are the re-run
    after §8.28.7b fixed the classifier, on a sandbox rebuilt from the live tree; the first
    attempt's hand-made copy differed in one unrelated pass (74 rather than 75) and the
    failing rules are the same:

        HEAD leadworks, all five apps otherwise current → rule 8 FAILs (six module-scope
            constructions) and rule 9 FAILs (five table reads) — 75 pass, 2 fail, rc=1
        getters kept, only the table's values put back → rule 8 PASSes and rule 9 alone
            FAILs (line=69..73) — 76 pass, 1 fail, rc=1

    The second is the case worth having: it is the one where the widened rule 8 would have
    gone green on an app that still cannot be imported offline.

8.28.4 THE DEFECT IN #50'S OWN PROBE, FOUND WHILE COPYING IT

    §8.27.7 recorded `abetflow QUEUE_PROBE_URL=…/13 → tests 12 pass 12 fail 0` as the
    scratch-db half of the new test. It was not on the scratch db. The file assigned
    `process.env.REDIS_URL = probeUrl` *inside the probe test*, and node:test runs test
    bodies after the file's top level — including the earlier "is a lazy accessor at
    runtime" test, which imports the module. By the time the assignment ran, `connection`
    had been built from the unset default for two tests.

    Measured directly this round, `redis-cli MONITOR` while running abetflow's suite with
    QUEUE_PROBE_URL=redis://localhost:6379/13:

        1791257942.524843 [0 [::1]:59140] "hset" "bull:abetflow-runs:meta" "opts.maxLenEvents"
            "10000" "version" "bullmq:5.81.5"
        (db 13 afterwards: 0 keys;  db 0: 253 keys, the meta set unchanged)

    The leading `[0 …]` is the db index MONITOR attributes the command to. So the guard
    that prints "refuses db 0" inspected the URL *string* and never looked at where the
    client went, and the run that was supposed to prove the teardown against scratch state
    proved it against live dev state instead. §8.20's defect, in the harness, in the
    round whose subject was the harness.

    Fixed in all five apps' queue.test.ts: the assignment is hoisted above every import,
    and the probe asserts the db the connection itself reports
    (`(await queue.waitUntilReady()).options.db === <the one named>`) rather than the
    string it was handed. That assertion is the one that would have failed the original.

        abetflow  QUEUE_PROBE_URL=…/13  rc=0, 12 pass — MONITOR: 1791258051.246816 [13
                  [::1]:38628] "hset" "bull:abetflow-runs:meta" …  db13=1, db0=253
        abetagent QUEUE_PROBE_URL=…/13  rc=0, 12 pass — MONITOR: 1791258117.689508 [13
                  [::1]:59064] "hset" "bull:abetagent-runs:meta" …  db13=1, db0=253
        abetflow/abetagent QUEUE_PROBE_URL=…/0  still rc=1, "not ok … refuses db 0"
                  (10 pass, 1 fail, 1 skip) — and this time nothing was written to db 0
                  to make that number, because the refusal now precedes the assignment

8.28.5 AFTER-PROBE, THREE APPS

        abetanalytics  import only, db13   rc=0   db13 = 0 keys
        leadgenius     import only, db13   rc=0   db13 = 0 keys
        leadworks      import only, db13   rc=0   db13 = 0 keys
        abetanalytics  QUEUE_PROBE_URL=…/13 rc=0  12 pass — [13 …] "hset" bull:snapshots:meta
        leadgenius     QUEUE_PROBE_URL=…/13 rc=0  13 pass — db13 = its six :meta keys
        leadworks      QUEUE_PROBE_URL=…/13 rc=0  15 pass — db13 = its six :meta keys
        all three      QUEUE_PROBE_URL=…/0  rc=1  "not ok … refuses db 0"
        db 0 through the whole round: 253 keys, the same 16 :meta names, no new keyspace

    The dead-Redis case, which is what the 2s settle bound exists for — import, touch
    every queue, drain, against a port nothing answers (redis://localhost:6399/13):

        abetanalytics  rc=0, shutdownWorkers returned in    7ms
        leadgenius     rc=0, shutdownWorkers returned in 2001ms
        leadworks      rc=0, shutdownWorkers returned in 2003ms

    Both ends of the bound, measured. Six Queues and one differ in whether the rejection
    lands before the deadline: with one the settle is never needed, with six the race is
    lost at 2000ms and the function still returns. Neither hangs, which is the property;
    the 2001/2003 pair is the deadline, not a slow Redis.

8.28.6 THE TESTS, AND WHAT THEY ADD

        abetanalytics  npm test  105 pass 105 fail 0   (queue.test.ts: 10 offline, 12 with probe)
        leadgenius     npm test  137 pass 137 fail 0   (queue.test.ts: 11 offline, 13 with probe)
        leadworks      npm test  128 pass 128 fail 0   (queue.test.ts: 13 offline, 15 with probe)
        abetflow       npm test  131 pass 131 fail 0   (unchanged: the probe fix moved lines,
        abetagent      npm test  118 pass 118 fail 0    it did not add assertions)

    leadgenius and leadworks assert per-queue rather than once: all six names visible to
    the scanner, no colon in any of them, queue↔worker pairing both directions, every
    accessor a getter and not a data property, every `built.x ??=` memoised, all six
    Workers pushed, the drain ordered and caught, `disconnect()` and never `quit()`, the
    production-gated install, both signals. leadworks adds the two assertions for its own
    failure mode — no `queue: queues.` inside the jobRunners table, and the lookup made
    with `queues[runner.queue]` at call time.

8.28.7 THE GUARD: RULE 8 WIDENED, RULE 9 NEW

    Rule 8 (importing must not connect) now runs over `"${APPS[@]}"` — all five — because
    all five pass and the rule's job is to keep the next "tidying" edit from putting a
    construction back at module scope. Rule 9 (nothing may read `queues.X` at module
    scope) is new, and it is the leadworks lesson as a gate: the checker walks the brace
    stack at each read and calls it safe only when some frame above it is a body — the `{`
    opened after `=>`, after a signature's `)`, or after a bare `try`/`else`/`do`/`finally`.
    In the five queue modules today there are two reads and both are in bodies —
    `queues.sequence` inside leadgenius' send worker (`queue.ts:140`) and
    `queues[runner.queue]` inside leadworks' enqueueJob. Those two names and that count are
    in the script's comment because they were counted, not estimated: the first draft of
    this rule matched only the dotted form, so it reported zero reads for leadworks, and the
    bracket form is where the fix put the lookup.

    66 → 72 assertions, rc=0. Rule 6 (the name must carry the product id) stays scoped to
    abetagent and abetflow on purpose: renaming `bull:send`, `bull:scrape`, `bull:snapshots`
    and the rest would orphan keyspaces that already hold jobs. That is a #51-class state
    decision, and three of the app READMEs now say so where the queue is documented.

8.28.7b THE NEW RULE'S CLASSIFIER WAS WRONG, IN BOTH DIRECTIONS, AND WAS FIXED TWICE

    Same shape as §8.27.8 — the guard going wrong, twice, each caught by running it — but
    found after §8.28.9 had already been written, so it is recorded separately rather than
    folded into the paragraph above.

    First, a classifier that could not see a named function, found by pointing it at whole
    `src/` trees instead of only `queue.ts`. The first version required the keyword `function`
    to sit *immediately* before the paren, which cannot match `function startWorkers()`: no
    character class eats the space. Every named function body in the portfolio was therefore
    read as an object literal. Trace kept from that run, on leadgenius' file: the frame at
    offset 1820 classified `literal`, with head `…rt async function startWorkers()`.

    Re-measured this round by re-running that head rule over the same trees — a
    reconstruction, labelled as one, since the script that produced the original output is
    gone; `/tmp/abectorial/n52-rescan.py` holds the fixed one. Of the 24 reads, **10** would
    have been reported as module scope: abetflow `scheduler.ts:32`; leadgenius
    `sequence-engine.ts:87`, `warmup.ts:86`; leadworks `enrichment.ts:209`,
    `exporter.ts:194`, `queue.ts:80`, `scheduler.ts:27/62/87`, `scraper.ts:236`. Not one is
    a module-scope read: every frame above each of them is a function body or a loop inside
    one. That is what makes it a false *negative* in the roundabout sense — a rule that
    reports ten correct bodies as violations gets switched off or narrowed by whoever runs
    it, and the two real violations it was written for go unguarded. Under the buggy head
    rule leadgenius' own queue-module read passed by luck: it sits inside a worker *handler*,
    whose frame is an arrow (`async (job: Job) => {`) and therefore recognised.

    The rescan itself is the number worth keeping, because the guard only ever reads the
    five queue modules and the classifier is general code: across all five `src/` trees
    there are **24** `queues.X` reads — 22 of them outside a queue module, in route handlers,
    `scheduler.ts`, `scraper.ts`, `exporter.ts`, `enrichment.ts`, `sequence-engine.ts`,
    `warmup.ts` — and with the fixed classifier **0** sit at module scope. Per app:
    abetflow 4, abetagent 1, abetanalytics 1, leadgenius 8, leadworks 10. The probe script
    is `/tmp/abectorial/n52-rescan.py`.

    Then a false positive, on correct code: with the keyword separation fixed, the live
    guard went red —

        FAIL  queue read at module scope in: leadworks:line=80 … — 71 passed, 1 failed

    — because the frame before `{` in `enqueueJob(type: string, jobId: string): Promise<void> {`
    ends with the *return annotation*, not the paren. So the annotation is stripped before
    the head is classified (`ANNOTATION_RE = r":[^:;{}()]*$"`, which cannot cross a second
    colon, a `;`, a brace or a paren and so cannot eat into the signature). `get runs(): Queue {`
    needed the same strip. The false negative this leaves is an annotation containing braces
    — `): Promise<{ a: string }> {` — which no app here writes, and the script says so.

    Re-measured with the fixed classifier, because everything below it was produced by the
    broken one: live guard **72 passed, 0 failed, rc=0**; the two negative probes again fail
    the right rules — eager HEAD module → rule 8 (six sites) *and* rule 9 (five table reads),
    **75 pass 2 fail, rc=1**; getters kept, table values only, restored → rule 8 passes and
    rule 9 alone fails (line=69..73), **76 pass 1 fail, rc=1**. §8.28.3's "74 pass" was the
    hand-made sandbox of the first attempt; the probe is now a script that rebuilds the
    sandbox from the live tree, so the pair above is the reproducible number. Full gate after
    both fixes: **18 passed, 0 failed in 64s**, queues 72/0, docs 98/0 re-run separately
    after the `shared/README.md` edit that documents this.

    The lesson is the one §8.27.8 already states and this round re-learns: a structural rule
    is only as good as the *classifier*, and the classifier has to be run against code it
    was not written for — here, the other 22 reads — before its verdict is evidence. A green
    rule 9 under the broken classifier would have said "no module-scope reads" about a
    portfolio whose six most important bodies it could not see.

8.28.8 #51 HAS NEW EVIDENCE, AND IT IS NOT WHAT THE TASK SAID

    The task reads "the orphaned bull:runs:* keyspace left in Redis db 0". It is not
    orphaned in the sense of untouched: MONITOR shows it being polled every ~5 seconds,
    and the client ports map to a live process —

        1791257941.848296 [0 [::1]:56138] "evalsha" … "bull:runs:wait" "bull:runs:active" …
        1791257941.851481 [0 [::1]:56152] "bzpopmin" "bull:runs:marker" "5"

        ss -tnp:  [::1]:56138 → :6379  users:(("next-server (v1",pid=1161887,fd=38))
                  [::1]:56152 → :6379  users:(("next-server (v1",pid=1161887,fd=40))
        ps -p 1161887: next-server (v16.2.12), ELAPSED 2-19:25  ← abetagent's :3400 dev server

    So the pre-#27 worker is still alive inside a dev server that has been up two and a
    half days and never reloaded that module. Deleting the keyspace would not stop that
    poll; restarting the server would, and it is the one process on this box that must not
    be killed. #51 is now a decision about a running process, not about stale keys.

8.28.9 THE GATE

    `bash shared/check-all.sh` → 18 passed, 0 failed in 66s of step time: six shared
    checks (check-suites 78/0, ui-drift 6/0, auth-drift 288/0, guards-drift 42/0,
    **queues 72/0**, docs 98/0) then typecheck + test for all six apps, every suite green.
    One pass, no servers needed.

    Re-run once more after §8.28.7b rewrote the classifier: **18 passed, 0 failed in 64s**,
    same six shared checks and the same six suite counts (137/128/118/131/105/127), queues
    72/0. `shared/README.md` was edited after that run, so `check-docs.sh` was executed on
    its own against the final text — 98 passed, 0 failed, same count.

8.28.10 FILED

    #53 — leadworks' `scheduled` queue has a Worker and no producer. Nothing in the app
          enqueues to it (the only `queues.scheduled` text is the getter itself) and db 0
          holds `bull:scheduled:meta` with no wait list and no jobs. Rules 4/5 pass
          because they compare names between Queue and Worker; the missing rule is the
          producer direction. Wire the schedule or delete the queue+worker, then add rule
          10.
    #51 — re-scoped by §8.28.8: the keyspace has a live poller (abetagent's :3400 dev
          server, pid 1161887) and will keep polling until that process restarts.
    #52 — closed by this section.

8.29 #53 — LEADWORKS' `scheduled` QUEUE HAD A WORKER AND NO PRODUCER, AND RULE 10 NOW KEEPS IT

    The round #52 filed when it closed §8.28.10. Same playbook, one difference worth naming:
    the defect was not on a client's input path but in a queue with only one end wired.

8.29.1 BEFORE-STATE, MEASURED

    Stored state, read from db 0 (`redis-cli -n 0 --scan --pattern 'bull:scheduled*'`):
    `bull:scheduled:meta` and nothing else — no `:id`, no `:wait`, no `:delayed`, no
    `:completed`. bullmq creates the `:id` counter on the first `add()`, so that keyset is
    its own record of a queue that has never received a job. Re-read at the end of this round,
    unchanged, because every probe this round ran on db 13.

    Source, read across leadworks' whole `src/` (not only `queue.ts`, which is the point): the
    only text naming `queues.scheduled` was the getter. `jobRunners` covers the five `Job.type`
    values a route can write — SCRAPE, ENRICH, EXPORT, DEDUPE, VALIDATE — and nothing names
    `scheduled`. `startWorkers()` pushed `new Worker("scheduled", …)` whose handler called
    `runScheduledJobs()`; that function had no other caller. So the poller was live
    (`bzpopmin bull:scheduled:marker`) and the code it was polling for was unreachable.

    What that costs is a stored-data fact, not an argument. Read off a copy of `dev.db`: three
    active sources carry `schedule = "0 9 * * *"`, three active export profiles carry
    `"0 9 * * 1"`. Six settings, no effect, and nothing said so: `GET /api/health` answers
    waiting/active/failed per queue — read from the app during the after-probe — and all three
    are zero for an idle queue and for a starved one alike. The count itself nearly went
    unmeasured: the first print was a Python `[:6]` slice of the source table and showed two
    `0 9 * * *` rows; a `GROUP BY schedule` over the same copy showed three. The comment's
    number is the latter, and the slice was the restatement this discipline exists against.

    One leg of the sweep has no data in either direction: `EnrichmentRule` rows with
    `trigger = "SCHEDULED"` number zero in that copy — all three active rules are `ON_CREATE`.
    Wired, not exercised. Recorded so nobody hunts a bug in a leg the data never uses.

8.29.2 THE SEMANTICS, MEASURED BEFORE THE CODE WAS WRITTEN

    Five questions answered against db 13 on bullmq 5.81.5 through the package entry
    (`dist/cjs/index.js`, which is what `next` and `tsx --test` resolve):

      - `upsertJobScheduler(key, {pattern}, {name, data})` with a FIXED key is idempotent, and
        across processes: two calls in one process left `getJobSchedulersCount()` at 1, and a
        second *process* upserting the same key read 1 again with the same job id,
        `repeat:leadworks-scheduled-sweep:<next-boundary>`. `bull:<q>:delayed` held exactly
        that one member. `getJobSchedulers(0, -1)` reads back
