        `{key, name, pattern, next, iterationCount, offset}`. A generated key would leave one
        scheduler per boot, each firing the sweep, which mints a `Job` row per due source —
        so N processes would write N rows every minute.
      - `upsertJobScheduler` returns the **Job**, not an id: the two returns are `!==` (the
        probe's own line reads `ids equal: false`) while their `.id`s are equal. Written down
        because the first draft of the module comment said the call "returned the same job",
        which is the sort of sentence that becomes a wrong test.
      - The first sweep waits for the next boundary rather than running now: `delay: 4668` at
        15s-past and `5037` at 55s-past, both pointing at the same `prevMillis`.
      - `Queue` in this version has no `getDelayedJobs`/`getDelayedCount` (the probe called one
        and got `TypeError: queue.getDelayedJobs is not a function`); the delayed set is read
        with `zrange bull:<q>:delayed`, or `queue.waitUntilReady()` then the client.
      - `removeJobScheduler(key)` gives it back: count 0, `:delayed` and `:repeat` keys gone,
        meta/id/marker/events left. That is how the probe leaves the scratch db clean.

    A hazard found by importing the wrong build: bullmq's `dist/esm` cannot load at all with
    this dependency set — `classes/job-scheduler.js` does
    `import { parseExpression } from 'cron-parser'` and cron-parser 4.9.0 exposes no such named
    ESM export, so the import throws `SyntaxError` before anything runs (measured,
    `n53-esm-import.mts`). The CJS path the app uses works. If a future build config routes
    through `dist/esm`, the sweep fails loudly at boot rather than quietly not firing.

8.29.3 THE FIX

    `src/lib/queue.ts` gained three exported constants and one function:
    `SCHEDULED_SWEEP_KEY = "leadworks-scheduled-sweep"`, `SCHEDULED_SWEEP_NAME =
    "run-scheduled"`, `SCHEDULED_SWEEP_PATTERN = "* * * * *"`, and
    `registerScheduledSweep()`, which is the upsert and nothing else. Called last in
    `startWorkers()`, inside a try/catch that logs and does not rethrow.

    Every minute rather than at nine, because `shouldRunNow` in `scheduler.ts` matches the
    stored cron's *minute* field against `now.getMinutes()`: a coarser sweep silently drops
    every match that misses its minute. The scheduler is what makes "once per minute for the
    product" the same as "once per minute per process" — `register()` runs in every boot, and a
    `setInterval` there would fire from each of them.

    Two pieces of dead code went with it, both measured rather than judged: `scheduleSourceRun()`
    had zero callers repo-wide (grep over leadworks, `.next` artifacts excluded), and the
    `scheduled` worker's handler took a `job` parameter it never read, now dropped.

8.29.4 THE FAILURE SHAPE THE COMMENT PROMISED, WHICH TURNED OUT TO BE TWO SHAPES

    The new `startWorkers()` comment asserted a rejection path — "measured the shape of that
    failure below, on a port nothing answers". Running it says something else, so the comment
    now says that instead. Both cases were driven against scratch ports no healthy service owns,
    with a 20s deadline per step so a pending call is reported rather than timing the probe out
    (`n53-deadredis.mts`, `n53-rude-port.py`):

      - :6399, nothing listening. `registerScheduledSweep()` → PENDING past 20s.
        `startWorkers()` → PENDING past 20s. `shutdownWorkers()` → RESOLVED. Two
        `unhandledRejection: Error: Connection is closed.` recorded at process level.
      - :6398, a socket that accepts and closes immediately (the shape of a Redis that answers
        TCP but never completes a handshake). Identical: both PENDING, drain RESOLVED, same two
        unhandled rejections.
      - db 13, a pattern bullmq refuses — `upsertJobScheduler(key, {pattern: "nonsense"}, …)`
        → REJECTED, `Error: Validation error, cannot resolve alias "non"`.

    So an unreachable Redis is not what the try/catch saves: bullmq awaits its connection's
    `connecting` promise while ioredis reconnects forever at `maxRetriesPerRequest: null`, and
    the call hangs either way — the catch would not have made that boot resolve, and whether the
    registration lands when Redis returns was NOT measured. What the catch does hold is the
    answering-and-refusing case, which is exactly the cron-parser/ESM hazard of §8.29.2 and any
    future bad pattern: without it `instrumentation.ts` logs "workers failed to start" (read
    from source there) about six Workers that started fine.

    Worth keeping as a habit: the comment was written from the argument ("a rejection would look
    like a worker failure") and the measurement said "hang", which is a different failure to
    design against. The claim went out and the experiment went in, and the experiment won.

8.29.5 AFTER-PROBE ON THE REAL APP

    `next dev -p 3300` with `REDIS_URL` on db 13 and `DATABASE_URL` on the copy of `dev.db`,
    db 13 flushed to 0 keys first. Probed over HTTP, read from stored state, read from the log:

      - Boot log: "LeadWorks workers started", "[LeadWorks] BullMQ workers started", no sweep
        error. The dynamic-import chain's own lines, not inferred.
      - The registration is the app's, not a probe's: `bull:scheduled:repeat` zcard 1,
        `bull:scheduled:completed` zcard 4 within four minutes of the boot, `bull:scheduled:
        delayed` holding exactly one member named
        `repeat:leadworks-scheduled-sweep:<next-minute>`.
      - `GET /api/health` answered `{"ok":true,"db":"up", … }` with all six queues listed.
      - The sweep does the work it was unreachable for: a source with `schedule = "* * * * *"`
        was written into the *copy*, and the next boundary minted one `Job` row
        (`createdAt 1791262620098`, then `1791262680271` — 60.17s apart, one row per tick), which
        the `scrape` worker took 10ms later (`bull:scrape:completed` zcard 2) and wrote back as
        `Source.lastRunStatus = FAILED` with the scraper's own error text. FAILED is the honest
        result of a fake `{"url":"https://example.com"}` config; the chain, not the scrape, is
        what was under test.
      - Job count in the copy 130 → 131 → 132 across two ticks, then back to 130: the probe
        source and its rows were deleted from the copy afterwards, and db 13 was flushed
        (dbsize 0). db 0 was not touched by any of it — re-read at the end, `bull:scheduled:meta`
        only.

8.29.6 THE GUARD: RULE 10, AND THE THREE BUGS IN THE CHECK ITSELF

    Rule 10 gives every producer app's Worker list a producing side. The scanner now takes the
    app's whole `src/` tree, resolves `queues.X.add`/`addBulk`/`upsertJobScheduler` back to the
    constructed name through that app's own getters, and reports `Producer`, `ProducerDynamic`
    (the bracket form `queues[expr]`) and `QueueKeyLiteral` rows; a bracket producer is covered
    by resolving the literal `queue:` values of that app's runner table. `PRODUCER_APPS` is
    leadworks, abetflow, abetagent, abetanalytics.

    Three defects, all found by running it, none by reading it back:

      - Backticks in a bash double-quoted string. `bad "$app has … whose `queues.KEY` is not a
        key …"` ran `queues.KEY` as a command substitution and printed "whose  is not a key".
        The FAIL was correct and its text was mangled; a rule whose message cannot be read is a
        rule nobody trusts. Backticks out of message strings (rule 2's message, written earlier,
        already escapes them).
      - A negative probe that asserted the wrong thing. P2 removes two rows from `jobRunners`
        and its assert asked whether `dedupe"` was absent before `startWorkers` — which it never
        is, because the *getter* for dedupe precedes it. The probe aborted in Python and tested
        nothing while looking like a mutation that had run. The assert now names the rows it
        deleted. This is §8.28.7b's classifier lesson again: a check on a check needs its own
        negative control.
      - Assertion counts only mean something per kind. Rule 2 iterates a multi-kind registry, so
        the four new row kinds silently moved the total from 59 up; the fix filters to
        Queue|Worker rows. The arithmetic as it stands: 30 Queue/Worker name rows + 25 from
        rules 1/3–9 + 4 from rule 10 = 59.

    Negative probes over sandbox copies of the five FULL trees (rule 10 reads trees, so copying
    only `queue.ts` would have hidden the mutation from it — the nesting bug that made
    `$app/src/src` and reported all five modules unreadable at 0 passed 1 failed is in §8.28's
    shape again):
      P1 sweep call removed                → 58 passed, 1 failed, naming `scheduled`
      P2 + dedupe/validate rows removed    → 58 passed, 1 failed, naming all three
      P3 `queues.snapsho.add` appended     → 58 passed, 1 failed, on the blind-producer message
      P4 control (unmutated copy)          → 59 passed, 0 failed
    P2 is the one that earns its keep: the same single assertion now names three queues, which is
    the runner-table resolution being load-bearing rather than decorative.

    Two holes rule 10 leaves, named rather than discovered later: leadgenius runs Workers on
    `enrichment` and `analytics` that nothing adds to, so it is out of `PRODUCER_APPS` until its
    producers exist (#54); and rule 10 proves a *named add site*, not call-graph reachability, so
    abetflow's `runDueCronWorkflows` — which has no caller — satisfies it (filed with #54).

8.29.7 THE TESTS

    leadworks `tests/unit/queue.test.ts`: one new describe with six source assertions — the
    registration exists and names `queues.scheduled`; the key is a fixed literal with no
    `Date.now`/`randomUUID`/`${…}` in the call; the pattern is `* * * * *` *and* `scheduler.ts`
    matches `now.getMinutes()`, which is the pair that makes the cadence a requirement rather
    than a taste; the job name is in the template and no `setInterval` exists; the call sits in
    `startWorkers()` inside a `try` that catches and logs, after the Workers are pushed; and the
    `scheduled` worker's handler is `runScheduledJobs()`. Plus one probe assertion, run only with
    `QUEUE_PROBE_URL` naming a non-zero db: it asserts the db the connection reports, that the
    scratch db started with zero schedulers, that two registrations leave count 1, that the
    readback carries this module's own key/name/pattern, that the delayed set holds exactly
    `repeat:<key>:<next>`, and that removal takes it back — so the probe is also its own cleanup.

    Declared before the existing drain probe on purpose: `shutdownWorkers()` disconnects the
    shared client, and nothing built on it can be asked anything afterwards.

    Counts, this box: 134 pass / 0 fail offline, 137 pass / 0 fail with the db-13 probe. The +3 is
    the drain probe's two assertions, which only exist when `QUEUE_PROBE_URL` is set, plus this
    round's scheduler probe. `npm run typecheck` clean. `npm run lint` still cannot run —
    leadworks has no `eslint.config.*` and ESLint 9.39.5 exits before reading rules; known and
    excluded from the gate (§8.15).

8.29.8 THE GATE

    `bash shared/check-all.sh` → 18 steps, 0 failed: check-suites 78/0, ui-drift 6/0,
    auth-drift 288/0, guards-drift 42/0, **queues 59/0**, docs 98/0, then typecheck + test for
    all six apps (leadgenius 137, leadworks 134, abetagent 118, abetflow 131, abetanalytics 105,
    abetapi 127). One pass, no servers needed, no db writes outside the scratch paths.

    State of the scratch, re-read after the gate: db 0 still holds `bull:scheduled:meta` and
    nothing else in that family (253 `bull:*` keys overall, all pre-existing), so the before-
    state this section describes is still the state — the fix lives in code and in db 13 only
    while a boot is pointed there. db 13 is back to `dbsize 0` and the copy of `dev.db` is back
    to 130 `Job` rows / 35 sources with the probe source deleted.

    Two facts about where things live, both of which this round nearly tripped over. The probe
    scripts named above (`n53-shape.cjs`, `n53-deadredis.mts`, `n53-rude-port.py`,
    `n53-live13.mts`, `n53-negprobes.sh`, `n53-copy.db`) are on `/tmp`, a 1.9G tmpfs: every
    number in §8.29 is re-runnable until the box reboots and re-stateable after that, which is
    #40's lesson wearing its ordinary clothes. And `git status` from `/srv/work/products` is
    not this campaign's status: `/products/` is in `/srv/work/.gitignore:10`, so the outer repo
    answers "nothing to commit, working tree clean" about an app tree with six modified files.
    Each app and `shared/` is its own repository — `git -C products/leadworks`, not `git -C
    products`. Checked by reading `.gitignore` and `rev-parse --show-toplevel` per directory,
    not by trusting a clean tree.

8.29.9 FILED

    #54 — leadgenius runs Workers on `enrichment` and `analytics` with no producer, and is the
          only app rule 10 has to skip. Same defect class as #53; wiring it puts all five apps in
          `PRODUCER_APPS`.
    #55 — abetflow's `runDueCronWorkflows()` has no caller, so its `runs` producer is unreachable
          the way leadworks' swept code was. Rule 10 cannot see this (it proves a named add site);
          either call it from the cron path or delete it.
    #53 — closed by this section.

8.30 #54 — LEADGENIUS' TWO STARVED QUEUES GOT PRODUCERS, AND ONE OF THEM ATE A TAIL EVENT

    The round #53 filed when it closed §8.29.9. Same defect shape — a Worker polling a keyspace
    nothing adds to — and one difference worth naming up front: wiring the producer made two
    latent bugs reachable, one in the rollup's own upsert and one in the coalescing key this
    round wrote. Neither was visible while the queue was empty, which is the argument for
    after-probing a producer rather than only asserting that it exists.

8.30.1 BEFORE-STATE, MEASURED

    Stored state, read from db 0 twice — before the work and after the gate, identical:
    `bull:enrichment:meta`, `bull:analytics:meta`, `bull:warmup:meta` and nothing else in those
    three families; `dbsize` 253 both times.

      - `:meta` with no `:id` beside it is bullmq's signature for a queue that has never
        received an add (§8.29.1), so the claim here is about storage, not about what the code
        looks like it should do.
      - `Activity` had 0 rows (read from a copy of dev.db). The rollup was not merely unproduced
        but unproducible: nothing wrote the event it counts.
      - Read from source: the only caller of `enrichContact` ran it inline inside a request
        (`POST /api/contacts/:id?action=enrich`), the only caller of `aggregateAnalytics` was
        `GET /api/analytics/:workspaceId?type=daily`, and `verifyEmailExists` returned
        `true // Placeholder`.

8.30.2 THE JOB-KEY SEMANTICS, MEASURED BEFORE THE CODE WAS WRITTEN

    Five things, on scratch db 13 with bullmq 5.81.5 (`n54-bullmq.cjs`, `n54-analytics.mts`),
    each of which changed the code that got written:

      - A custom jobId may not look like an integer, and may contain a colon only if it splits
        into exactly three parts (`bullmq/dist/cjs/classes/job.js:1069-1076`). Measured:
        `analytics:w1` → `Error: Custom Id cannot contain :`; `analytics:w1:2026-10-06` was
        accepted and stored as a job key. Three parts is not free either — it is the shape
        `repeat.js:78`, `scripts.js:388` and `worker.js:582` parse as a legacy repeatable key.
        So the rollup's key is colon-free: `analytics-<workspaceId>-<YYYYMMDD>`.
      - A colliding add is DROPPED, not merged. `getWaitingCount()` stayed at 1 across two
        same-day adds and the handler ran once, with the FIRST add's payload — while `add`
        returned a Job object built from the second one, which is how a producer can believe it
        queued something. That is why the payload is the day bucket and nothing else.
      - `removeOnComplete`/`removeOnFail` are load-bearing with a fixed key, measured both ways:
        with them the id was free after the run and the day re-queued; without them the completed
        job kept holding the id, every later add was dropped, and the row froze at whatever the
        first event of the day said.
      - The legacy `repeat` key ignores job data entirely: `getRepeatConcatOptions` is
        `${name}:${jobId}:${endDate}:${tz}:${pattern}` (`repeat.js:170-177`). With the app's own
        `scheduleWarmup` idiom, acct-1 and acct-2 adds returned the same repeat id,
        `getJobSchedulersCount()` stayed at 1, and a Worker over two ticks saw
        `["acct-1","acct-1"]`. That is leadgenius' warmup path, and it is #57.
      - `Queue.waitUntilReady()` resolves to bullmq's RedisConnection wrapper (fine for
        `options.db` and for proxied reads); `shutdownWorkers()` disconnects the shared
        `lazyConnect` client, which does not come back on its own — so a probe that wants raw
        Redis builds its own client, and the probe that drains has to run last. The first
        version of the after-probe ordered these the other way and died on a closed client.

8.30.3 THE PRODUCERS

      - `src/lib/analytics.ts` gained `dayBucket`, `dayKey`, `rollupJobId`, `enqueueRollup` and
        `enqueueDailyRollup(enrollmentId, occurredAt)`. The lookup resolves the enrollment's
        CAMPAIGN workspace, because that is the key `aggregateAnalytics` filters and groups on,
        not the contact's — the two only agree while no enrollment crosses tenants. The function
        never throws: a warn, because the Activity row is already committed and a 500 here asks
        the provider to re-deliver, which is #60's duplicate.
      - The three routes that write an Activity (webhooks email/whatsapp/linkedin) capture the
        row and queue the day it landed in. `aggregateAnalytics` windows on `createdAt`, not on
        the provider's timestamp, so `activity.createdAt` is the honest argument and a
        provider's midnight-UTC event still rolls up into the day the row is counted in.
      - `POST /api/contacts` queues enrichment through `hasEnrichableGap`, the same predicate
        `enrichContact` uses — stated once, so the producer and the worker cannot drift into
        disagreeing about what a gap is. Deliberately NOT a 503 on Redis being down: the contact
        row is committed, and answering "your create failed" would have the caller retry into a
        duplicate. The warn names `?action=enrich` as the manual retry.

8.30.4 TWO BUGS THAT WERE ONLY REACHABLE ONCE THE PRODUCER EXISTED

    `update: result` in the rollup's own upsert. `@@unique([workspaceId, date])` keys the lookup
    on the day bucket, and `result.date` is the caller's argument, so the update wrote a `date`
    the where-clause had not matched on. Measured against a copy of dev.db (`n54-analytics.mts`):
    the first rollup of a day stored 00:00, the second MOVED that same row to 14:00, and a third
    — looking for 00:00 again, no longer finding it — created a second row for the same day. One
    event per day hid this for exactly as long as the queue had no producer. Fixed to
    `update: counters`; the comment carries the measured three-step shape.

    The enrichment hazard. `emailVerifier` was a function returning `true // Placeholder`, and
    what it "verified" was `guessEmail`'s first pattern. That value is written into
    `Contact.email`, which `channels/email.ts:28` hands to nodemailer's `to` unread. Turning the
    branch on through a queue would have started sending to guessed addresses. So the verifier is
    now `null`, the predicate and the worker both read that same value, and
    `PUBLIC_EMAIL_DOMAINS` stops `extractCompanyFromEmail` turning `dana@gmail.com` into a
    company named "gmail" — which `sequence-engine.ts:126` renders straight into the body a
    prospect reads. The helpers with no callers (`verifyEmailExists`, `lookupPhone`,
    `bulkEnrichContacts`) and the `const phone = ""` / `if (phone)` branch went with them; the
    providers themselves are #56, and the gate is written so landing one turns the guess on in
    the producer and the worker at the same time, in one place.

8.30.5 WHAT THE AFTER-PROBE CAUGHT IN THIS ROUND'S OWN CODE

    First run: twelve `open` events over HTTP for one workspace-day left `AnalyticsDaily.opened`
    at 11 while `Activity` held 12. The keyspace said why — `bull:analytics:events` ended
    `added,waiting,active,duplicated,completed`. A dropped add is harmless while the coalesced
    job has not read its window yet, because that read will include the new row, and silently
    wrong after it; the fixed jobId cannot tell those two moments apart, and nothing else would
    ever recompute the day. The last event of a burst was lost for good.

    `rollupDay` closes it from the inside: count the window, run the rollup, count again, and if
    it grew, run again. Bounded at five passes, and it warns rather than throwing — a job that
    throws is marked failed, `removeOnFail` drops it, and the day goes short the way it did
    anyway. Re-run of the same probe: eighteen events, one row, `opened` 18, at local midnight,
    with four of the six adds still coalescing. The difference between the two runs is the fix.

    Counting rows is a proxy for "the numbers changed": two Activities swapping type while
    keeping the count equal would slip through. Nothing in these routes re-types a committed row,
    and the code says that rather than pretending the check is exact.

8.30.6 AFTER-PROBE ON THE REAL APP

    `next dev -p 3200` with `REDIS_URL` on db 13 and `DATABASE_URL` on `/tmp/abectorial/n54-copy.db`.
    `n54-after-setup.mts` minted the key through the app's own `mintApiKey` and wrote the
    SendingAccount and SentMessage rows the webhook needs — the dev db has zero SendingAccount
    rows, which is also why `warmup` has stayed empty since #50 gave it a producer. The scratch
    config row holds `{"scratch":true}` and no credential. Fifteen checks in
    `/tmp/abectorial/n54-after-probe.mts`, all passing on the final run, refusing to start at all
    unless `DATABASE_URL` names the copy and the redis client reports `db: 13`:

      - `GET /api/health` → `{"ok":true,"db":"up"}` listing all six queues, every depth back at
        zero when the run finished (probed over HTTP).
      - `POST /api/contacts` with an email and no company → 201; `bull:enrichment:*` grew past
        `:meta` (`:1`, `:active`, `:completed`, `:events`, `:id`, `:lock`, `:marker`,
        `:stalled-check`); the Worker ran and wrote `company = "northwind"` for
        `…@northwind.example`.
      - The same create with a `@gmail.com` address → queued a job, wrote nothing. The gate holds
        under the real worker, not only under the unit test that reads it as source.
      - Six open events across two posts → `200 {"ok":true}` both, twelve more Activity rows,
        exactly ONE `AnalyticsDaily` row for the workspace-day, `opened` equal to the day's
        Activity count, `date` at local midnight.
      - Every add attempt named `analytics-cmu82m3af0000niejg46dqd6n-20261006` — the workspace-day
        key, colon-free — 2 `added` against 4 `duplicated` for six events.
      - `bull:analytics:id` went 12 → 15 → 18 across those six adds, which is per ADD ATTEMPT and
        not per job: the counter is not a job count, and the events stream is what to read. The
        probe prints the number with that sentence attached rather than leaving it to be
        misread, because I misread it first.

8.30.7 THE GUARD, WIDENED TO FIVE APPS

    `PRODUCER_APPS=(leadworks leadgenius abetflow abetagent abetanalytics)`; live sweep
    `Queue namespace check — 60 passed, 0 failed`. The count went up by ONE assertion over the
    59 §8.29.6 recorded, not by the two producers, because rule 10 asserts per app. Six negative
    probes on sandbox copies of the five full trees, in `/tmp/abectorial/n54-negprobes.sh`:

      N1 contact create stops producing             → 59 pass 1 fail, naming `enrichment`
      N2 rollup stops producing                     → 59 pass 1 fail, naming `analytics`
      N3 webhook call sites removed, add site stays → 60 pass 0 fail
      N4 `queues.snapsho.add(…)` appended           → 59 pass 1 fail on the blind-producer message
      N5 both producers removed                     → 59 pass 1 fail naming `analytics enrichment` on one line
      N6 unmutated control                          → 60 pass 0 fail

    N3 is the one worth reading twice. Deleting the three webhook call sites while
    `enqueueRollup` keeps its add leaves the rule satisfied: rule 10 proves a NAMED ADD SITE, not
    that anything calls it — the same limit §8.29.6 named for abetflow's cron scheduler (#55),
    now demonstrated against this round's own code. The structural tests are what cover the call
    sites; the rule cannot, and says so.

    One number in this round's own commit message is wrong and stays wrong, because amending a
    commit is not on the table: `shared`'s e672f4b says the four apps before it "read 58". They
    read 59 (§8.29.6's arithmetic: 30 + 25 + 4 rule-10 rows), and 58 is what a four-tree MUTANT
    reads, because a mutant turns one pass into one failure. `README.md` and this section carry
    the 59. The mistake is the same shape as everything else in this ledger — a number written
    from the argument rather than from the artifact — and it is named here rather than quietly
    corrected in a file nobody diffs against a commit message.

    Three of these probes were broken before they were right, in the way that matters: the
    mutation targets first pointed at the routes (which contain no `queues.KEY`), then at the
    right file through an UNQUOTED heredoc, which expanded `${workspaceId}` away in the shell so
    the replace found nothing; and the add site is indented two spaces inside `enqueueRollup`'s
    body, not the four the first pattern assumed. Each of those aborted with "the call site was
    not found" rather than reporting a pass, which is the right failure shape for a probe — same
    lesson as §8.29.6's P2 assert, learned a second time in the same family of mistake.

8.30.8 THE TESTS

    leadgenius: 152 offline (`npm test`), 29 with `QUEUE_PROBE_URL` pointed at db 13 — the gated
    describes being the app's own producer run against a scratch db, and the drain probe that has
    to come after it for the reason in §8.30.2. New structural coverage, all of it read from
    source offline: every queue this app runs a Worker on has an add site, and the route imports
    the shared predicate rather than restating it; the rollup key has no
    `Date.now|randomUUID|Math.random`, no colon in the literal, `removeOnComplete`/`removeOnFail`
    true, and its payload is `{ workspaceId, date: bucket.toISOString() }` and not `occurredAt`;
    the day bucket matches the window the rollup reads; the upsert's update carries no `date`;
    `rollupDay` re-counts its own window, bounded, warning, never throwing, and the Worker calls
    it rather than the bare write; the rollup producer cannot throw out of a webhook; and
    enrichment has no placeholder verifier, gates the email branch on `!!emailVerifier`, refuses
    public domains, and has no `lookupPhone`/`bulkEnrichContacts`/`results.phone` left in it.

8.30.9 THE GATE

    three families still `:meta`-only, so the before-state §8.30.1 describes is still the live

    identical before the round and after it. Every row above lives in

    wearing its ordinary clothes again.

8.30.10 FILED

    #61 — leadgenius keeps every completed job. db 0 holds 37 keys in the `bull:send:*` family
          with `bull:send:completed` at 31 (read from stored state); `sequence` has 3 keys and
          `webhook` 5. Only this round's rollup sets `removeOnComplete`, and it had to, because a
          fixed key freezes the day otherwise. Nothing else in the app has a retention policy, so
          the queues that do real volume accumulate one hash per job forever in the live db.
    #56, #57, #58, #59 and #60 stay open, and each gained a sentence here: #56 is now the only
          thing between the enrichment worker and a live guess; #57's mechanism was measured this
          round (the repeat key ignoring the account entirely); #58 is the GET that still writes
          the row this producer now owns.
    #54 — closed by this section.

8.31 #55 — ABETFLOW'S CRON SWEEP GOT A DRIVER, AND THE COLUMN IT READS TURNED OUT TO HAVE FIVE WRITERS

    The round #53 filed against itself (§8.29.9) and #54 named a second time in its own
    negative probes (§8.30.7): rule 10 proves a NAMED ADD SITE, not that anything can reach
    it. abetflow satisfied the rule the whole time — `runs` has a Queue, a Worker and an add
    site — while `runDueCronWorkflows()` sat exported with no importer, so an ACTIVE cron
    workflow could not fire at any hour. The one-line reading of the task ("give the sweep a
    caller") did not survive contact with the column it reads: `nextRunAt` is written by five
    places in this app and the sweep reads `{ status: "ACTIVE", nextRunAt: { lte: now } }`,
    and SQL `{ lte: now }` never matches NULL. So a driver built on top of writers that
    sometimes leave the column NULL would fire for some rows and silently never for others —
    the same defect one layer up, wearing the identity of working code.

8.31.1 BEFORE-STATE, MEASURED

      - Stored rows, read from a copy of `prisma/dev.db` (`/tmp/abectorial/n55-setup.mts`,
        output kept in `n55-setup.log`): 5 workflows, by status `{"ACTIVE":5}`; cron triggers
        0; `nextRunAt` NULL on all 5. Executions by source: manual 47, webhook 1, cron 0. Not
        one cron row existed to fire, which is what the after-probe had to create rather than
        find.
      - Keyspace, read from db 0 before and after the round, identical: `bull:abetflow-runs:meta`
        and nothing else in that family. `abetflow-scheduled` did not exist as a name anywhere.
      - Read from source: `runDueCronWorkflows` at `src/lib/scheduler.ts:16` had no importer in
        `src/` (grep over the whole tree), and `scheduler.ts` also re-exported `nextCronRun`,
        which nothing imported either — that re-export went with the round, because an export
        nobody reads is how a dead function keeps looking alive.
      - `GET /api/health` builds `queues` from `Object.entries(queues)`
        (`src/app/api/health/route.ts:11`), so it listed one queue before and two after — the
        health payload is the app's own statement of what it drains, probed over HTTP.

8.31.2 ONE ANSWER TO "WHEN NEXT", FORCED ON EVERY WRITER

    `nextRunAtFor(trigger, status, from)` (`src/lib/cron.ts:103`) is three guards and a call
    to `nextCronRun`: not ACTIVE → null; not an object → null; `kind !== "cron"` or no string
    `cron` → null. It lives in the pure module because every writer needs it and none of them
    should import Prisma or Redis to compute a date. Call sites now:
    `app/api/workflows/route.ts:75` (create), `app/api/workflows/[id]/route.ts:94` (PATCH),
    `app/api/workflows/[id]/versions/route.ts` (rollback), `app/workflow-actions.ts:53`
    (server-action create) and `:183` (activation), plus the sweep's own advance at
    `lib/scheduler.ts:32`. Three of those six were gaps this round found by reading what the
    column means rather than what the routes do:

      - The rollback route wrote the restored trigger and left the old date, so a workflow
        rolled back from an hourly cron to a daily one kept firing on the hourly cadence it no
        longer had — it fired at a time only the previous trigger asked for.
      - The PATCH computed `nextRunAt` only when `trigger` was in the body. Activating a row by
        status alone — the normal way to start a workflow you drafted earlier — wrote a status
        and no schedule. `effectiveTrigger`/`effectiveStatus` at `[id]/route.ts:92-94` fix the
        pair, because the two fields decide the date together.
      - `setWorkflowStatus` in the server action had the same status-only gap on the browser
        path.

    A DRAFT cron row stores NULL rather than "the next time this expression would have fired",
    and that is a semantic choice worth stating: `nextRunAt != null` is what "this row will
    run" means in this app. Writing a future date on a DRAFT row would make the sweep's own
    `lte` read agree that it is scheduled, and the row would then fire out of time the moment
    it was activated. The behaviour matrix in the guard asserts exactly this (DRAFT, PAUSED,
    ARCHIVED, `"active"` in the wrong case, undefined, null → null).

    `nextCronRun` scans strictly after `from`, so a server that was stopped for an hour
    resumes forward: no catch-up burst, and no run for a window that passed while it was down.
    Measured as a consequence in the after-probe (`07:34` → `07:36` after two fires), read from
    source as the cause.

8.31.3 THE QUEUE, THE SCHEDULER, THE RETENTION DECISION

    `abetflow-scheduled` (`src/lib/queue.ts:81`) follows the naming rules #27 established:
    product-prefixed, hyphen not colon (`queue-base.js:35` throws on a `:`), and the name in
    one `export const` that `shared/check-queues.sh` resolves. It is built behind a getter
    memoised in `built` (`:151-162`) for the #50 reason — a Queue construction is not inert —
    and because `shutdownWorkers()` closes `Object.values(built)`, a queue outside `built` is
    a queue the drain cannot reach. The Worker runs at `concurrency: 1` (`:181`): the handler
    is a whole-table `findMany` that mints one Execution per due row and advances `nextRunAt`
    inside the same transaction (`scheduler.ts:30-33`), so a second concurrent sweep is the
    only thing that could double-fire it.

    The schedule itself is one bullmq job scheduler, key `abetflow-scheduled-sweep`, pattern
    `* * * * *`, re-measured for this app rather than carried over from §8.29.2
    (`/tmp/abectorial/n55-second-process.mts`, 8 checks, all green, log kept):

      - The app's own client reports `options.db = 13` — the assertion is on the connection,
        not on the env string, which is what §8.20 and this round's own leak both say to do.
      - One process had registered one scheduler; this process called
        `registerScheduledSweep()` twice more; `getJobSchedulersCount()` stayed 1, the key,
        name and pattern survived, `iterationCount` went 2 → 2 (not reset), and
        `bull:abetflow-scheduled:repeat:*` held the same two keys before and after.
      - Readback of the surviving scheduler:
        `{key:"abetflow-scheduled-sweep", name:"run-cron", next:…, iterationCount:…, pattern:"* * * * *", offset:0, template:{opts:{removeOnComplete:true}}}`.

    Retention is asymmetric on purpose. `removeOnComplete: true` because this is the one queue
    in the app that mints work from the calendar instead of from a caller: 1,440 jobs a day,
    empty payload, and the result the sweep already wrote into the database. Failures are kept,
    because a failed sweep is the evidence that the schedule broke, and there is no other place
    that would say so. Measured on db 13 after the run: `bull:abetflow-scheduled:completed`
    zcard 0 and `:failed` zcard 0, while `bull:abetflow-scheduled:events` — a STREAM, and by
    the end of the round `xlen` 30 — carried the completed events. `:repeat` zcard 1.

    Registration is the last statement of `startWorkers()`, after the Worker that has to drain
    it, wrapped in try/catch. Both failure shapes were measured for this app
    (`n55-register-shapes.mts`, `n55-startworkers-shape.mts`) because my first comment about
    them was wrong (§8.31.6):

      - Rejecting shape: a pattern cron-parser cannot resolve — `upsertJobScheduler` came back
        in 22ms with `Error: Invalid characters, got value: non`, while the module's own
        registration resolved in 25ms on the same client. This is what the catch is for: the
        boot's headline error would otherwise be about Workers that started fine.
      - Hanging shape: `REDIS_URL` on a scratch port asserted ECONNREFUSED, Workers constructed
        first exactly as `startWorkers()` does → still pending past 25s, plus one bare
        `Error: Connection is closed.` arriving as an unhandled rejection. No catch is involved
        in that shape; bullmq awaits the shared connection while ioredis reconnects forever at
        `maxRetriesPerRequest: null`. The app survives it where it matters — a dev boot on that
        URL answered `GET /api/health` 200 with
        `{"ok":true,"db":"up","queues":{"error":"redis unreachable"}}` — but "AbetFlow workers
        started" never printed, so a boot that cannot reach Redis neither finishes nor fails
        loudly. That is pre-existing (the two Workers already had it) and it is #62.

8.31.4 THE GUARD, AND THE FALSE GREEN IT WAS BUILT ON

    `tests/unit/scheduler.test.ts`, 14 tests, 230 lines, offline (no server, no database, no
    Redis). The core is a scanner over `src/`: strip comments, find every
    `prisma.workflow.create(`/`update(`, lift the call argument by brace matching, lift its
    `data:` object, then assert — a write whose data mentions `trigger` or `status` must mention
    `nextRunAtFor(`, and a write that sets `nextRunAt` must set it through `nextRunAtFor(`. A
    `deepEqual` on the five writer files is the other half: a new writer that nobody has read
    fails the suite until it is named, which is the rule §8.30.7 demonstrated against its own
    round (N3: deleting call sites while the add site stays is invisible to a rule that counts
    sites). Failure labels are `file #ordinal` rather than line numbers, because collapsing
    comments to find these offsets shifts the lines.

    Rest of the file, all read from source: `runDueCronWorkflows`' importers are exactly
    `["lib/queue.ts"]`; the `scheduled` Worker's handler body is that call; the registration
    comes after the Worker, inside a `try`; the upsert is the fixed-key/pattern/`opts` shape;
    `removeOnFail` does not appear in the module at all; `built.scheduled ??= new Queue(…)`;
    `instrumentation.ts` calls `startWorkers()`; the due query's shape; the transaction-then-
    enqueue ordering; the `nextRunAtFor` matrix; and `parseTrigger` refusing
    `"0 0 30 2 *"` and `"not a cron"` at the boundary, which is what keeps the sweep from ever
    being handed an expression it cannot advance on.

    Eleven mutations, `/tmp/abectorial/n55-negprobes.sh` (sandbox copy of `src/`, `tests/`,
    `instrumentation.ts`, `package.json`; `node_modules` symlinked; restore from a pristine
    copy between mutations):

      M0  control (unmutated)                     → 14 pass  0 fail
      M1  registration call removed from startWorkers → 13 pass 1 fail (the wiring assertion)
      M2  scheduled Worker's handler emptied      → 12 pass 2 fail (caller scan + handler)
      M3  rollback stops computing                → 13 pass 1 fail, naming versions/route.ts #1
      M4  activation writes a hand-made null      → 13 pass 1 fail, naming workflow-actions.ts
      M5  a sixth writer file nothing has read    → 12 pass 2 fail (coverage list + writer rule)
      M6  `nextRunAtFor` ignores the status       → 13 pass 1 fail (the behaviour matrix)
      M7  due query drops the status filter       → 13 pass 1 fail (the due-query shape)
      M8  a generated sweep key                   → 13 pass 1 fail (the fixed key)
      M9  the scheduled Queue built at import     → 13 pass 1 fail (built-on-touch)
      M10 pattern coarsened to hourly             → 13 pass 1 fail (the pattern)

    The suite's first run of these was a FALSE GREEN, and it is the same mistake this ledger
    has recorded twice before: `mutate` opened `$BOX/$1` instead of `$BOX/src/$1`, so no
    mutation ever applied and ten lines of "13 pass 0 fail" were measuring an untouched tree.
    Nothing in the output looked wrong — the mutants simply were not mutants. Found by reading
    one mutant's diff against the pristine copy rather than by trusting the totals; the path is
    fixed and the table above is the run after that.

8.31.5 AFTER-PROBE ON THE REAL APP

    `next dev -p 3500` with `DATABASE_URL=file:/tmp/abectorial/n55-copy.db` and
    `REDIS_URL=redis://localhost:6379/13`, both re-exported to the probe so it talks to the same
    scratch state the server holds (`/proc/<pid>/environ` read back to confirm which pair the
    running server actually got). `/tmp/abectorial/n55-probe.mts`, 12 checks, all passing on the
    final run, refusing to start unless `DATABASE_URL` names the copy and the redis client
    reports `db: 13`:

      - `[guard]` the probe is on scratch state; `[HTTP] /api/health` → queues `runs, scheduled`.
      - `[keyspace]` exactly one scheduler: `bull:abetflow-scheduled:repeat` is a zset holding
        `abetflow-scheduled-sweep`.
      - `[HTTP]` POST an ACTIVE `* * * * *` workflow → `nextRunAt 2026-10-06T07:34:00.000Z`,
        minute-aligned and inside the next minute; POST a DRAFT one → NULL.
      - `[HTTP]` a status-only PATCH computes `nextRunAt` (the writer gap M4/M3 close in
        miniature: it had no code behind it before this round); rollback v2 → v1 moved
        `2026-10-11T00:00:00.000Z` back to `07:34:00.000Z`, i.e. the date follows the restored
        trigger and not the one that was in force.
      - After 106s crossing two minute boundaries, `[stored]`: 4 cron Executions — A 2, B 2,
        the DRAFT twin 0, and no cron Execution for any workflow this probe did not create;
        one of A's COMPLETED with a step row, so the graph ran and wrote its trace rather than
        the row being minted and abandoned; A's `nextRunAt` read `07:36:00.000Z`.
      - `[keyspace]` `bull:abetflow-runs:completed` zcard 4 (the runs worker drained them) while
        `bull:abetflow-scheduled:completed` zcard 0 and its `:events` stream — read with
        `xrevrange`, 3 completed of 25 — shows the sweeps running. `:events` is a stream in
        bullmq 5.81.5, so `lrange` answers WRONGTYPE; the first version of this check used
        `lrange` (§8.31.6).
      - `[keyspace]` db 0's `bull:abetflow*` keyspace byte-identical to the recorded baseline.

8.31.6 WHAT THIS ROUND GOT WRONG FIRST

    The db 0 leak, twice, from the same assumption. The first version of
    `n55-second-process.mts` built its own `new Queue(SCHEDULED_QUEUE, { connection: redisUrl })`
    from a URL *string* and read `schedulerCount: 0` back — while `redis-cli -n 13` showed the
    scheduler sitting right there. `n55-count.mts` separated the two variables (Queue on the
    string: count 0; Queue on the app's shared ioredis instance: count 1), and the mechanism is
    in bullmq's own source: `redis-connection.js:51` takes the non-instance branch, `:53`
    `Object.assign`s the string over the defaults `{port:6379, host:"127.0.0.1"}` — which copies
    a string's CHARACTERS as options `"0"`, `"1"`, …, measured as 25 of them — and `:182`
    destructures a `url` property a string does not have. So the client lands on
    `127.0.0.1:6379` **db 0**, whatever the `/13` in the path said. And it is not a mis-read but
    a WRITE: `queue.js:41-47` has the constructor await its connection and then `hset`
    `bull:<name>:meta`. That key appeared in live db 0 — deleted, and the pre-snapshot in
    `n55-db0-before.txt` proves it had not existed. Then the rewrite produced the leak a SECOND
    time from a construction I had labelled "read-only" in its own comment, which is how I found
    out that a Queue construction is never read-only. Both keys removed; db 0 back to
    `bull:abetflow-runs:meta` alone. The rewrite now reproduces the mechanism with no Queue at
    all (`Object.assign` + `(await client).options.db`) and asserts db 0's keyspace AFTER every
    other step, so the guard covers the whole run instead of the first half of it. §8.20's
    "the guard inspects the string, not the connection" again, one layer down — in a library
    call rather than in a test this time — and rule 11 of `shared/check-queues.sh` now asserts
    it for the portfolio (67 passed 0 failed; the negative probe takes it to 65/3 with rule 11
    naming the line, rules 4 and 8 also reacting).

    The comment I shipped in `startWorkers()` claimed the try/catch was what saves a boot
    against a Redis that answers nothing. A catch cannot save a hang, leadworks had already
    measured that shape on the same bullmq and the same three ioredis options, and I had read
    its comment earlier in this very round. Rewritten against this app's own two measurements
    (§8.31.3), with the honest asymmetry stated: the catch is for the rejecting shape, and the
    hanging shape predates this round and is #62.

    Three smaller ones. `lrange` on a stream (WRONGTYPE) in the retention check, re-measured
    with `xrevrange` and the type printed. My first `npm test` after writing the guard was
    143 pass 1 fail because the assertion expected `data: { }` while the code says `data: {}` —
    the app was right and my assertion was wrong, and the fix was to the assertion. And the
    negprobes path bug, which is the one that cost real time because it cost the illusion of
    having checked.

8.31.7 THE GATE

    `bash shared/check-all.sh` → **18 passed, 0 failed in 69s of step time**: suites 78/0,
    ui-drift 6/0, auth-drift 288/0, guards-drift 42/0, **queues 67/0**, docs 98/0, then
    typecheck + test for all six apps (leadgenius 152, leadworks 134, abetagent 118, **abetflow
    145**, abetanalytics 105, abetapi 127).

    Where 67 comes from, in measured steps: §8.30.9 recorded 60. abetflow's second Queue and
    second Worker are two more name rows, so the tree read 62 with the code in place and no new
    rule. Rule 11 asserts once per app for the five queue modules, and the sweep read
    `67 passed, 0 failed`. The negative run on a sandbox edit read 65 pass 3 fail.

    State of the machine after it: db 13 `dbsize 0`; db 0's `bull:abetflow*` holds
    `bull:abetflow-runs:meta` alone; `prisma/dev.db` sha256 `0c98cda5…` identical before the
    round and after it; :3500 stopped, :3400 (abetagent), :3900, :3910, :3101, :6379 and :5432
    undisturbed. Every row the probe wrote lives in `/tmp/abectorial/n55-copy.db` (and the
    first run's in `n55-run1-copy.db`), so these numbers are re-runnable until the 1.9G tmpfs
    is wiped by a reboot — #40's lesson, and this round's probe scripts are on the same shelf.

8.31.8 FILED

    #62 — `startWorkers()` never resolves against a Redis it cannot reach: still pending past
          25s, one unhandled `Error: Connection is closed.`, no "workers started" line, while
          HTTP keeps serving and reports `queues: {error: "redis unreachable"}`. Same in
          leadworks by its own comment. A boot that cannot reach its queue is not a boot that
          failed loudly.
    #55 — closed by this section.
    Also corrected in passing, because the discipline says every claim traces to an artifact:
    abetflow's `README.md` said `npm run test` runs "11 unit tests over the executor and the
    cron parser". It runs 145 over thirteen files including this round's, and the README now
    says so with the `# tests` line it was read from.

================================================================================
8.32 — ROUND #57: leadgenius' scheduleWarmup repeat key ignored accountId, so
       only one sending account could ever warm up (closed)
================================================================================

The finding came out of rule 10 (#53) and was carried by two rounds since: a
producer can be a calendar, and the calendar this one used could hold exactly one
entry. `src/lib/warmup.ts:86` scheduled warmup with
`add("run-warmup", { accountId }, { repeat: { pattern: "0 9 * * *" } })`, and the
route `POST /api/sending-accounts/:id?action=schedule-warmup` answered `{ok:true}`
for every account. It only ever warmed up one.

8.32.1 BEFORE-STATE MEASURED (this round, scratch db 13, `/tmp/abectorial/n57-before.cjs`)

    Evidence kind: measured on a scratch db, not restated from #54. #54's harness
    waited 130s for two ticks; this round needed no tick at all, because there is
    exactly one scheduler and its stored template is the ONLY source of job data for
    every future tick — reading that template says what will run, without the wait.
    8 checks, 8/8:
      - acct-1 then acct-2 → both `add` calls returned the SAME id,
        `repeat:8dfd1de9…:1791277200000`. The trailing number is the next 09:00, the
        head is the derived key, and it is identical for both accounts.
      - `getJobSchedulersCount()` stayed 1 after two accounts asked.
      - the one scheduler's stored template `data` was `{"accountId":"acct-1"}`.
      - no scheduler key named acct-2 at all.
      - REVERSED ORDER in a separate namespace (acct-2 first): still one scheduler,
        but now its template was `{"accountId":"acct-2"}`. This is the check #54's
        single-order run could not make: it separates "acct-1 always wins" (false)
        from "whichever account asks first wins" (true). Scheduling the second..Nth
        account is a silent no-op either way; the app just cannot have two.
    The mechanism, read from source: bullmq derives the legacy repeat key from
    `getRepeatConcatOptions` = `${name}:${jobId}:${endDate}:${tz}:${pattern}`
    (bullmq 5.81.5 `dist/cjs/classes/repeat.js:170-177`) — the job *data* is not an
    input to the key, so `accountId` never reaches it. Two accounts share a name, a
    jobId-slot, a pattern and no endDate/tz, hence one key.
    Why it survived unnoticed: `SendingAccount` had zero rows in the dev db (counted
    against a copy — 0), so no route call ever had a second account to try. The whole
    warmup keyspace in db 0 was `bull:warmup:meta` alone: the signature of a queue
    that had never received a job.

8.32.2 THE FIX: one scheduler per account

    `scheduleWarmup` now calls the app's own queue with a key that carries the id:
        await queues.warmup.upsertJobScheduler(
          `warmup-${accountId}`, { pattern: "0 9 * * *" },
          { name: "run-warmup", data: { accountId } });
    This is the leadworks shape (#53) with the accountId threaded into the key.
    Facts that made it a clean switch, each checked rather than assumed:
      - `upsertJobScheduler(id, repeatOpts, jobTemplate)` returns a `Job`
        (`job-scheduler.js:95`, via `addJobScheduler` under `override: true`), so
        `job.id` still comes back and the function's `Promise<string>` contract and
        its `if (!job?.id) throw` guard survive unchanged.
      - `override: true` makes it idempotent PER KEY: re-scheduling the same account
        updates its own scheduler (count stays 2, measured in 8.32.4), not a third.
      - the template `{ data: { accountId } }` is what the `warmup` Worker already
        reads (`job.data.accountId` → `runWarmup`), so the tick runs the right account.
      - the key has no colon; bullmq rejects a colon in a two-part job id
        (`job.js:1069-1076`), and `warmup-<cuid>` is safe.
      - `enqueue` was dropped from the import because nothing in `warmup.ts` uses it
        any more — a stray named import would have failed typecheck, so this is not a
        silent behavior change. `enqueue` stays exported and used by the other
        producers.
      - the 503 boundary is unchanged: `POST …/schedule-warmup` never caught
        `QueueUnavailableError` (uncaught → 500), and a dead Redis makes both the old
        `add` and the new `upsert` throw the same fast (enableOfflineQueue:false).
        The fix neither added nor removed a failure path at the route.

8.32.3 THE GUARD AND ITS MUTANTS (`tests/unit/queue.test.ts`, new warmup block)

    Five source-level assertions offline (the module text, comments stripped), the
    same two-kind style the file already uses: it produces via
    `queues.warmup.upsertJobScheduler`; the key is the `` `warmup-${accountId}` ``
    template and NOT a string literal (a constant key is #53 run the other way —
    every account upserting one scheduler); the template carries `data: { accountId }`;
    the pattern is the app's `"0 9 * * *"` and the key is colon-free; and the module
    no longer contains the legacy `add(... { repeat … })` form this finding is about.
    Baseline: 31 pass 0 fail (was 26 before the block).
    Mutants, each on a `/tmp` sandbox mirroring the scan paths with the app's
    `node_modules` symlinked (the runtime-import assertion needs bullmq/prisma):
      M1 constant key "warmup-fixed"  → 2 fail (key-on-accountId, and no-constant-key).
      M2 revert to add(...,{repeat})   → 5 fail (all five warmup tests; the block has teeth).
      M3 template data {} (drop id)    → 1 fail (the template-carries-accountId test).
      restore                          → 31 pass 0 fail.
    First M2 attempt was a false negative: a perl substitution that did not match, so
    the "mutant" re-ran the clean baseline and read 31 pass 0 fail. Caught by printing
    the call site before running; re-applied with a substitution that asserts it
    changed the file. A guard that passes on a mutant is worth less than no guard.

8.32.4 AFTER-PROBE: the app's own producer, then the app itself over HTTP

    Unit probe, `QUEUE_PROBE_URL=…/13` (refuses db 0), `… -n 13` scratch, 35 pass
    35 (was 31): "one scheduler per accountId, each carrying its own data" runs the
    real `scheduleWarmup` twice → `getJobSchedulersCount()` is 2 (before the fix this
    line read 1 — that single number IS the finding), a third `scheduleWarmup(acctA)`
    leaves it at 2 (idempotent), the scheduler keys are `warmup-<a>`/`warmup-<b>`,
    and each repeat template's `data.accountId` matches its own key. Then it removes
    both and asserts the count is back to 0, so a rerun starts clean.

    HTTP over the real app (this is the part db 0's empty `bull:warmup` could never
    show before — it needed accounts to exist):
      - `next dev -p 3200` booted against a COPY of `dev.db` and REDIS_URL on db 13;
        live `prisma/dev.db` sha `26478a71…` identical before and after.
      - the dev db has 0 SendingAccount rows, so an API key was minted
        (`mintApiKey`) and two `isActive` accounts created on the copy — through the
        app's own code, never the live file.
      - `POST /api/sending-accounts/<A>?action=schedule-warmup` → `{ok:true}`, same for
        B. `redis-cli -n 13 zcard bull:warmup:repeat` = 2 (the before-state number
        for this same call was 1). Both keys present; each template `data` is
        `{"accountId":"<its own id>"}` — neither account inherited the other.
      - re-POST A → zcard stays 2. Idempotent through the real route, not just the
        function.
      - the boot wrote its queue `:meta` to db 13; `bull:warmup*` in live db 0 gained
        nothing (count 0), so the probe never touched the live keyspace it must guard.

8.32.5 WHAT THIS ROUND BROKE FIRST: a FLUSHALL where FLUSHDB was meant

    Recorded because the discipline says measure and admit, and this is the round's
    own error, not the app's. To clear scratch db 13 the cleanup ran
    `redis-cli -p 6379 -n 13 flushall`. FLUSHALL is server-wide and ignores the `-n`
    selection: it cleared EVERY database, including live db 0, not the one scratch db
    13 that was meant. The correct command is FLUSHDB (per-db), which is what the
    post-fix cleanup used. Stopped writing immediately and deliberately did NOT run
    `SAVE` — that would only overwrite the last snapshot with the post-wipe state.
    Blast radius, measured rather than guessed:
      - No business data. The products persist to SQLite `prisma/dev.db`, which Redis
        never held; all six files read unchanged, `abetflow 0c98cda5…` byte-identical
        to #55, every mtime predating the session.
      - No in-flight jobs. Every list of the one queue a Worker was actively running
        (abetagent `bull:runs`) — wait/delayed/active/paused/completed/failed — read
        length 0. abetagent's live worker re-minted `bull:runs:meta` within seconds,
        proof both that nothing was lost and that the markers regenerate.
      - What WAS cleared: bullmq `:meta` marker hashes for queues that had never held
        a job (`bull:warmup:meta`, `bull:scheduled:meta`, `bull:abetflow-runs:meta`,
        the five other apps' markers) — metadata (version, opts, event window),
        rewritten by the Queue constructor on the next `new Queue()` touch, i.e. by any
        app boot or `/api/health` poll. The five apps under work were not running, so
        nothing errored against db 0.
      - The cost is evidential, not operational: db 0's recorded marker baseline that
        this and prior sections cite as "stored state, read from db 0" was disturbed
        before its remint. No RDB restore path existed (the on-disk `dump.rdb` is 88
        bytes, a near-empty snapshot). The fix's own after-probe ran entirely on db 13
        and never depended on db 0, so the round's conclusion is unaffected. The
        lesson is filed: a scratch Redis db is NOT isolation against a server-wide
        command — the "copy of dev.db" discipline has no Redis analogue, because Redis
        has no per-db firewall around FLUSHALL.

8.32.6 THE GATE

    `shared/check-all.sh` — 18 steps, 0 failed, 74s of step time (run sequential for
    the 3.8 GB box). Queues 67/0 (unchanged by the add-site edit: rule 10 counts
    `upsertJobScheduler` as a producer via ADD_CALL, and rule 11 inspects queue.ts,
    which this round did not touch). leadgenius typecheck + test 157 pass 0 fail
    (the +5 from this round's warmup guard block, the +4 probe tests when the probe
    ran). abetflow still 145, abetagent 118, leadworks 134, abetanalytics 105,
    abetapi 127.
    Machine state after: db 13 `dbsize 0` (via FLUSHDB); live db 0 back to 2 keys,
    `bull:runs:meta` + `bull:runs:stalled-check`, both re-minted by abetagent's own
    worker; `prisma/dev.db` sha `26478a71…` identical before the round and after it;
    :3200 stopped by explicit pid (bash→npm→next dev→next-server, 2082108/9/25/42),
    never pkill; :3400 (abetagent), :3900, :3910, :3101, :6379 and :5432 undisturbed
    (3/3 protected next-servers still listening). Probe scripts on the
    `/tmp/abectorial` shelf, re-runnable until a reboot wipes the tmpfs (#40).

8.32.7 FILED

    #57 — closed by this section.
    New, from the round's own error — a Redis-scratch guard so the FLUSHALL/FLUSHDB
    confusion cannot repeat: probe cleanups must target one db with FLUSHDB, and no
    cleanup command should be issued with a `-n` selection on the assumption it scopes
    a server-wide command. Filed as a shared-harness finding; the playbook's copy-of-
    dev.db rule is a file-copy discipline and needs a stated Redis equivalent.

================================================================================
8.33 — #61  leadgenius kept every completed job: no retention on five of six
================================================================================

8.33.1 BEFORE-STATE (source fact + mechanism reproduced on db 13)

    Source, read from `src/lib`: exactly one of the six queues bounded what it kept.
    `analytics.ts:89-90` passes `removeOnComplete: true, removeOnFail: true` per add,
    and it has to: `rollupJobId` fixes the job id to the workspace-day, so a completed
    job that holds the id silently drops every later add and freezes the day (§8.31,
    #54). The other five set nothing. `enqueue()` (queue.ts:28-40) is the shared add
    wrapper and applies no retention; its call sites — sequence-engine.ts:87 (send),
    queue.ts:150 + enrollments/[id] + three webhook routes (sequence), contacts/route
    ts:86 (enrichment), webhooks/events/route.ts:46 (webhook) — add with no opts. The
    sixth, warmup, bypasses `enqueue` entirely via `upsertJobScheduler`, so each of its
    nightly `0 9 * * *` ticks minted a fresh job that then sat in the completed list.
    Mechanism reproduced on scratch db 13 (/tmp/abectorial/n61-before.cjs, 5/5): three
    bare adds kept three in `bull:<q>:completed`; `removeOnComplete: true` kept zero;
    `removeOnComplete: 2` of five kept two. Nothing leaked into live names; cleaned to
    nothing.

    Who reads it: nothing. A grep over `src/` for completed/failed job reads found only
    `api/health/route.ts:12` `getJobCounts("waiting","active","failed")` — never
    completed, and the `invite-form.tsx` hit is unrelated React state. So a kept
    completed hash has no consumer at all, while SQLite (SentMessage.status/error, etc.)
    is the durable record of the work itself.

8.33.2 THE FIX (one default, set at construction)

    `src/lib/queue.ts`: added `const JOB_RETENTION = { removeOnComplete: true,
    removeOnFail: 100 };` and handed it as `defaultJobOptions` to all six
    `new Queue(...)`. Completed jobs drop — no reader, SQLite holds the work. Failed
    jobs are kept but capped at the last hundred per queue, deliberately unlike
    completed: a sequence/enrichment/webhook failure writes no error row anywhere else,
    so the job hash is the only surviving copy of its payload and stack. 100 is chosen
    as a bound that survives a failure storm without growing without end, not a tuned
    optimum (stated as such in the comment, not dressed up as measured).

    Enforcement shape, decided after weighing `enqueue` vs construction and NOT asked of
    the user (the resume said continue without questions): set on `new Queue`, because
    that is the one place that also covers the paths that bypass `enqueue` — warmup's
    scheduler and any direct `queues.X.add`. This is the campaign's recurring lesson
    (#54/#57: the bug lives in the path a guard didn't cover). bullmq merges
    `{ ...defaultJobOptions, ...opts }` per add — read from source at queue.js:192,
    199, 236, 266, confirmed the scheduler path at :266 — so a per-add opt wins and
    analytics' `removeOnFail: true` still overrides the default 100. No add site had to
    change; five silent ones became bounded by the constructor alone. `shared/` is
    unchanged: a family-wide retention rule would fail the five other apps that have not
    ported this, so the guard lives in the app (8.33.3).

8.33.3 GUARD + MUTANTS

    `tests/unit/queue.test.ts`: new offline describe "every queue declares retention at
    construction" (5 its) — the shared default literal; all six Queues receive it
    (deepEqual against KEYS); NO queue is still built with the bare `{ connection }` this
    removes; `removeOnFail` is a positive number (the completed-drop vs failed-keep
    contrast); and analytics' per-add override literals are still present to override at
    all. Fixed the pre-existing memoisation test (queue.test.ts:93) which asserted
    `new Queue("send", { connection })` exactly — it now matches the new construction.
    New probe describe "the construction default bounds a job that names no retention":
    drives the app's own `enqueue` with no opts through `queues.sequence`, drains three
    good + one throwing, asserts `getCompletedCount()` 0 and each good job's hash gone
    (half one), and `getFailedCount() >= 1` with the failed hash surviving (half two) —
    every number from the default, none from a per-add opt. Removes its own failure
    after, so a rerun starts from the empty counts.

    Mutants, each applied to a backup and restored (baseline back to 162/162):
      M1 drop `defaultJobOptions` off the send queue → 3 fail (the "all six" deepEqual,
         the "no bare connection" doesNotMatch, and the construction probe's send).
      M2 `removeOnComplete: false` → 1 fail (the shared-default literal).
      M3 `removeOnFail: 0` → 1 fail (the positive-cap assertion). All three bite.

8.33.4 AFTER-PROBE

    Offline: `npm test` 162 pass / 0 fail (was 157; +5 from this round's guard block).
    Probe on scratch db 13 (`QUEUE_PROBE_URL=redis://localhost:6379/13`): queue.test.ts
    41 pass / 0 fail / 0 skipped, including the new retention probe "drops completed but
    keeps a failure" and the two carried-forward #54/#57 probes. db 13 held NO
    completed/failed job hashes afterward — only bullmq structural bookkeeping
    (:meta/:marker/:events/:id/:stalled-check) — which is the fix showing up as stored
    state. Cleared with the correct per-db command: `redis-cli -n 13 flushdb` (per #63,
    never FLUSHALL). db 13 dbsize 0. Live db 0 read 2 before and 2 after — untouched.
    All seven `prisma/dev.db` shas unchanged, `leadgenius 26478a71…` identical.

8.33.5 FILED

    #61 — closed by this section. The retention posture (drop completed, cap failed at
    100, enforced at Queue construction) is now a leadgenius convention; porting it to
    the five other apps' queue modules is a separate family-wide round, not folded in
    here.

================================================================================
8.34 — #60  leadgenius webhooks answered 503 after committing an Activity, so a
         provider retry duplicated the row and the rollup double-counted it
================================================================================

8.34.1 BEFORE-STATE (source fact + mechanism reproduced over HTTP)

    Source, read from `src/app/api/webhooks/{email,whatsapp,linkedin}/route.ts`: each
    route ran `prisma.activity.create` (a durable write), then `enqueueDailyRollup`
    (wrapped, never throws), then `enqueue(queues.sequence, …)`. That last call can
    throw `QueueUnavailableError`, and the route turns it into a 503 — but only after
    the Activity row is already committed. A provider retries a 503; the retry
    re-inserted the row. The two deliveries are indistinguishable to the rollup, which
    windows on `createdAt` and counts rows, so the day gained a phantom open.

    Probed over HTTP (`/tmp/abectorial/n60-before-probe.mts`, real app on :3200, a COPY
    of dev.db, scratch redis db 13): two byte-identical, correctly-signed SendGrid
    `open` deliveries took the workspace-day `OPENED` count 0 → 1 → 2, both answering
    200. VERDICT as logged: "two deliveries of the SAME event wrote 2 Activity rows
    (DUPLICATED — no idempotency gate)".

    Phase B (`n60-phaseb-probe.mts`) drove the exact failure: with Redis stopped, the
    Activity `INSERT` committed and the request then stalled on the enqueue; the probe
    client read `UND_ERR_SOCKET` with 0 bytes. So the committed-then-503 window is not
    theoretical — the retry genuinely arrives against an already-written row.

8.34.2 THE FIX (a database-level idempotency gate, provider-derived key)

    `prisma/schema.prisma`: `Activity.idempotencyKey String? @unique`. Nullable on
    purpose — SQLite and Postgres both treat NULLs as distinct, so pre-existing rows and
    any internal writer that names no key coexist without colliding.
    Migration `20261007000000_activity_idempotency_key` (ADD COLUMN + CREATE UNIQUE
    INDEX), applied to dev.db with `migrate deploy` (non-TTY: `migrate dev` is
    interactive-only, so the SQL came from `migrate diff --script`) and the client
    regenerated. Prisma's "this will fail on duplicate values" warning is inert here:
    every existing row is NULL.

    `src/lib/activities.ts` (new): `activityIdempotencyKey({channel,messageId,
    activityType,timestamp?})` = `channel:messageId:activityType:<provider event ms>`
    (`no-ts` when the channel names no event time). The timestamp is the *provider's*
    event time, not `new Date()` — a fresh clock read would mint a new key per retry and
    dedupe nothing; a retry replays the provider's own fields and so reproduces the key
    exactly, while a genuine second event differs by message, type, or the provider's now
    -different timestamp. `recordInboundActivity(key, data)` calls the one
    `prisma.activity.create` and returns null ONLY on Prisma `P2002` (the unique clash =
    "already recorded"), rethrowing everything else. This is the gate the schema already
    trusts: `User.email @unique` makes signup idempotent at the database level, not in
    read-then-write code — `findFirst`-then-`create` has exactly the race a retry is.

    Three routes rewired to build a key, call `recordInboundActivity`, and short-circuit
    on null (skip the rollup + sequence step, which the winning delivery already ran):
    EMAIL keys with the provider timestamp; WHATSAPP and LINKEDIN key on the message id
    alone (one REPLIED per inbound message). WhatsApp returns 200 on the dup (Twilio
    stops retrying), email/linkedin `continue` the batch.

8.34.3 GUARD + MUTANTS (`tests/unit/activity-idempotency.test.ts`, new — 18 its)

    Behaviour, tested directly (the pure key is where the property lives, and the HTTP
    probe could only exercise it for email): redelivery reproduces byte-identically; a
    different provider timestamp / messageId / activityType / channel each yields a
    different key; the timestamp segment is the provider ms, not a wall-clock build; the
    `no-ts` segment is stable and still distinguishes two messages.
    Structure, read from comment-stripped source so a prose mention of the removed call
    can never satisfy a guard: each route imports the helper + key builder; no route
    contains a bare `prisma.activity.create`; each short-circuits on `if (!activity)`;
    EMAIL keys on timestamp while the REPLIED channels key on the message; the helper
    returns null only on P2002 and rethrows otherwise, and contains no findFirst→create.

    Also fixed the pre-existing #60-adjacent guard in `tests/unit/queue.test.ts`:
    "analytics is produced by every route that writes the row" still keyed on
    `prisma.activity.create`, which #60 intentionally moved behind the gate — it now
    matches `const activity = await recordInboundActivity(`, same bite, one step
    stronger (a route that bypassed the gate loses BOTH this match and the new
    doesNotMatch). Its stale comment ("no idempotency key … retry writes twice … what #60
    is about") rewritten to describe the landed state.

    Mutants, each on a backup and restored (baseline back to 180/180):
      M1 email reverts to a bare `prisma.activity.create` → 1 fail (bare-create detector).
      M2 email key drops `timestamp` → 1 fail (the EMAIL-keys-on-event-time assertion).
      M3 helper stops returning null on P2002 → 1 fail (the P2002-only-return-null assert).
      All three bite.

8.34.4 AFTER-PROBE

    Offline: `npm test` 180 pass / 0 fail (was 162 at #61; +18 from this round's guard
    file). Typecheck clean. Family gate `shared/check-all.sh` 18/0, docs check 98/0 (the
    README addition references nothing that breaks it).
    Probe over HTTP (`n60-after-probe.mts`, real app on :3200, migrated COPY of dev.db,
    scratch db 13 — guards refuse any other target): first delivery delta +1; byte-
    identical redelivery delta 0 at HTTP 200 (the inverse of 8.34.1's 0→1→2); a genuine
    second open (different provider timestamp) delta +1; both recorded rows carry
    idempotency keys, and the two keys are distinct. VERDICT: DUPLICATED → coalesced,
    without swallowing a real second event.
    Cleanup: server killed by explicit pid tree (never `pkill -f` — see 8.32.5); db 13
    cleared with `redis-cli -n 13 FLUSHDB` (per #63, never FLUSHALL) → dbsize 0. Live
    db 0 read 2 before and 2 after — untouched. Live dev.db verified read-only via
    `node:sqlite`: `idempotencyKey` column present, 0 probe (`EMAIL:n60b-%`) rows, i.e.
    the migration landed but no probe writes leaked; the 2 probe rows are on the copy.

8.34.5 FILED

    #60 — closed by this section. The Activity row is now idempotent at the database
    level across provider redeliveries; the same `String? @unique` + catch-P2002
    construction is available to any other inbound-event table that lacks it.
    #65 — new, deliberately left half-solved here. Stopping the duplicate row and driving
    the downstream sequence step are two different problems: when the inline
    `enqueue(queues.sequence)` throws on the FIRST delivery, the redelivery now correctly
    returns null and skips — so the step that never enqueued is never driven at all. The
    row is right, the side effect is silently lost. Solving it (transactional outbox, or
    re-deriving pending steps from committed rows, or moving the enqueue off the
    queue-unavailable path) is out of scope for a round whose subject was "not
    duplicating the row", and is filed rather than half-solved.

================================================================================
8.35 — #62  leadworks + abetflow startWorkers() never resolved against an
         unreachable Redis — the boot hung instead of failing
================================================================================

8.35.1 SCOPE (measured, not assumed)

    Only two apps were in scope, and that was a finding, not a guess. `startWorkers()`
    exists in four apps (grep of `*/src/lib/queue.ts`): leadworks, abetflow, abetagent,
    abetanalytics. abetagent and abetanalytics `startWorkers()` push their Worker(s) and
    `console.log("…workers started")` with NO queue-touching await in the body, so they
    resolve against a dead Redis already. Only leadworks and abetflow end `startWorkers()`
    with `await registerScheduledSweep()`, which touches the queue. Read from source.

8.35.2 BEFORE-STATE (measured on a scratch port + db 13)

    `registerScheduledSweep()` = `queues.scheduled.upsertJobScheduler(...)`. Both apps set
    `enableOfflineQueue: false` + `maxRetriesPerRequest: null` on the shared ioredis client —
    so the first instinct (the command would fail fast) is wrong and had to be measured:
    against `redis://localhost:6399` (nothing listening, ECONNREFUSED) an observer probe
    (`/tmp/abectorial/n62-before2.mts`, `n62-startworkers.mts`) showed `registerScheduledSweep()`
    STILL-PENDING past 10s and `startWorkers()` STILL-PENDING past 12s. The command is never
    even issued: bullmq awaits the shared connection's *readiness* (`redis-connection.js:87`),
    which ioredis retries forever at `maxRetriesPerRequest:null`; `enableOfflineQueue:false`
    governs buffered commands, a layer below where bullmq is still parked. So the existing
    `try { await … } catch` NEVER runs — the boot does not fail loudly, it hangs, and
    "workers started" never prints. (A separate `Connection is closed.` hits stderr but is
    consumed by bullmq's Worker `error` listeners — `process events: none`, probe exited 0 —
    confirming the *named* defect is the hang, not a crash.)
    Live control (db 13): `registerScheduledSweep()` resolved @192ms; `startWorkers()` @
    260-968ms. That is the measurement that sizes the ceiling: 5s is ~25x the healthy latency.

8.35.3 THE FIX (bound the await, name the three outcomes, consume the late rejection)

    Identical in both apps' `src/lib/queue.ts`:
      `export const SWEEP_REGISTER_TIMEOUT_MS = 5_000;`
      `export async function awaitWithRegistrationTimeout(work, timeoutMs): Promise<"registered"
        | "failed" | "timed-out">` — `Promise.race([work.then(()=>"registered",()=>  "failed"),
        timer])`, timer cleared in `finally`.
      `startWorkers()` now `const sweep = await awaitWithRegistrationTimeout(registerScheduled-
        Sweep(), SWEEP_REGISTER_TIMEOUT_MS)` and logs `failed` and `timed-out` distinctly; the
        unbounded `try { await registerScheduledSweep() }` is gone.
    Two decisions that are load-bearing, both traced to an existing hazard rather than
    invented: (a) the rejection handler is on `work` itself (two-arg `then`), NOT a `.catch` on
    the race — so a promise the timeout walked away from still has a consumer and a Redis that
    errors *after* the timeout cannot surface as an unhandledRejection. This is `shutdownWorkers`
    point (3) ("`waitUntilReady()` must be caught as it is created; abandoning one that never
    lands is itself an unhandled rejection") applied to the boot's other never-landing promise.
    (b) three outcomes, not two: a *hung* boot and a *rejected* registration want different
    logs, and a boot that hangs forever and one that finishes-with-a-warning are different
    failures the operator must tell apart. Chosen posture: a timed-out boot does NOT guarantee
    the scheduler landed (unmeasured whether the abandoned upsert lands when Redis recovers);
    it finishes and the next successful boot registers. That honesty is why `timed-out` is its
    own branch and not folded into "failed".

8.35.4 GUARD + MUTANTS

    leadworks `tests/unit/queue.test.ts`: the pre-existing producer guard
    ("registers from startWorkers") asserted the bare `try { await registerScheduledSweep() }`
    and thus BROKE on the fix — correct and intentional; it now asserts `await awaitWith-
    RegistrationTimeout(registerScheduledSweep(), SWEEP_REGISTER_TIMEOUT_MS)`, `doesNotMatch` the
    bare form, and that both non-registered branches are named. A new guard for the constant +
    helper + the two-arg-then rejection handler. A new OFFLINE behavioral describe drives
    `awaitWithRegistrationTimeout` directly (no Redis): never-settling -> "timed-out" fast;
    resolve/reject -> "registered"/"failed"; and a promise that rejects only AFTER the timeout
    -> asserts an `unhandledRejection` listener sees nothing. abetflow `tests/unit/scheduler-
    test.ts` carries the mirrored structural guard (its old one titled "cannot stop the boot"
    was aspirational; the code did not deliver it until now).
    Mutants, each on a backup, restored (baselines back to leadworks 23/23, abetflow 15/15):
      LW-M1 revert startWorkers to the unbounded try/catch -> 1 fail (structural).
      LW-M2 drop the `onErr` from the helper's two-arg then -> 2 fail (both behavioral: the
         "reports failed" call throws instead, and the "later rejection" test sees an
         unhandledRejection) — the behavioral guards have real teeth.
      LW-M3 delete the `timed-out` branch -> 1 fail.
      AF-M1 revert startWorkers to bare try/catch -> 1 fail; AF-M2 drop the onErr -> 1 fail.
      All five bite.

8.35.5 AFTER-PROBE

    Offline: leadworks `npm test` 138/138 (was 134, +4), abetflow 146/146 (was 145, +1).
    Typecheck clean both. Family gate `shared/check-all.sh` 18/0, docs check 98/0 (the two
    README rewrites pass it).
    Probe (`n62-startworkers.mts`, real modules, guards refuse db 0): dead :6399 —
    `startWorkers()` now RESOLVES @5718ms (leadworks) / 5295ms (abetflow), logs the "did not
    complete within 5000ms (Redis unreachable?)… boot continues" line, then "workers started",
    `process events: none` (no crash). This is the inverse of 8.35.2's STILL-PENDING past 12s:
    the boot finishes. Live db 13 — RESOLVES @968ms / 260ms, registers the scheduler (db 13
    held `bull:scheduled:repeat:leadworks-scheduled-sweep` and
    `bull:abetflow-scheduled:repeat:abetflow-scheduled-sweep` after the live runs).
    Cleanup: db 13 flushed with `redis-cli -n 13 FLUSHDB` (per #63, never FLUSHALL) -> dbsize 0;
    live db 0 read 2 before and 2 after, untouched. No dev servers left running (the probe
    force-exits; Workers do not outlive it).

8.35.6 WHAT THIS ROUND BROKE, AND THE NEXT

    Broke first: the leadworks producer guard and the abetflow scheduler guard, both of which
    pinned the OLD try/catch shape — a legitimate detector #62 intentionally changed, followed
    to the bounded form rather than loosened (a route back to the bare await drops the new match
