    AND trips the `doesNotMatch`, so the pair is strictly stronger than either guard alone).
    Also caught: the `\d+` in the timeout-constant regex missed `5_000`'s numeric separator;
    fixed to `[\d_]+` in both apps' guards.
    #62 — closed by this section. The helper is a small pure function; vendoring it (and
    `SWEEP_REGISTER_TIMEOUT_MS`) through `shared/` so the four apps cannot drift apart is the
    same "port the convention family-wide" shape as #64, and is left to that track rather than
    folded into a two-app boot fix. abetagent/abetanalytics are unaffected (no bounded await to
    add) but would receive the helper for free if the convention is vendored.

================================================================================
8.36 — #64  the queue-retention convention (#61, leadgenius) is ported to the four
         other queue apps, so a bare add on any of them no longer leaks its completed job
================================================================================

8.36.1 SCOPE — MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED

    #64's title said "the five other apps." Measured, there are four. `grep -rn
    "new Queue(" */src` (source): abetapi has NO bullmq Queue, Worker or import at all — it
    is an API gateway that never runs queue work — so it is not a queue app and receives
    nothing. The four that construct a Queue, and the count of constructions each:
    leadworks 6 (scrape, enrich, export, dedupe, validate, scheduled), abetflow 2
    (abetflow-runs, abetflow-scheduled), abetagent 1 (abetagent-runs), abetanalytics 1
    (snapshots). Every one of those 10 was built with a bare `{ connection }` — no
    `defaultJobOptions` (read from each `src/lib/queue.ts`). leadgenius already passed
    `JOB_RETENTION` on all six (#61, §8.33). This round brought the other ten into line;
    16 Queue constructions across the family now carry the identical default (re-grepped:
    `new Queue(` → all `defaultJobOptions: JOB_RETENTION`, zero bare).

    The convention ported verbatim: `const JOB_RETENTION = { removeOnComplete: true,
    removeOnFail: 100 }` handed to each construction as `defaultJobOptions`. Set at the
    construction, not in `enqueue`, so it also bounds the paths that bypass `enqueue()` —
    in leadworks that is both `enqueueJob`'s bare `queues[k].add` and `registerScheduled-
    Sweep`, which names no opts here (unlike abetflow's, which names `removeOnComplete`).

8.36.2 WHY THE DEFAULT IS THE SHAPE — GROUNDED LOCALLY, NOT INHERITED

    #61 established the rationale on leadgenius; this round re-derived the load-bearing
    premise per app rather than trusting the copy. Each of the four apps' `GET /api/health`
    reads `queue.getJobCounts("waiting", "active", "failed")` and never `completed` (read
    from `src/app/api/health/route.ts` in all four) — so a completed job's hash is a Redis
    key with no reader in this portfolio, and the durable record of the work is the app's
    own SQLite table (Run / Execution / Job / snapshot rows), not the queue. That makes
    `removeOnComplete: true` correct, not cosmetic. Failures are the exception: a failed
    job's hash is the only place its payload and stack survive, so they are kept — but
    bounded to the most recent 100 so a storm cannot grow forever, which reframes the
    `failed` number /api/health reports as "recent failures" rather than cumulative.

    It is a DEFAULT, not a force: bullmq merges `{ ...defaultJobOptions, ...opts }` per add,
    so any add naming its own retention still wins. That is exactly why the port required no
    change to any add site — leadgenius' analytics `removeOnFail: true` override and abetflow's
    sweep `removeOnComplete: true` both survive the merge untouched. Probed against a real
    Redis below (the merge is the claim that a source read cannot establish).

8.36.3 WHAT BROKE FIRST — A GUARD THAT PINNED THE WHOLE MODULE

    The abetflow scheduler guard asserted, over the entire `queue.ts` text, that it contains
    no `removeOnFail` (`scheduler.test.ts:139`: `assert.doesNotMatch(SCHEDULED_QUEUE_MODULE,
    /removeOnFail/)`). That was true when the only retention in the file was the sweep's
    `removeOnComplete` add; it is FALSE the moment the construction default legitimately adds
    `removeOnFail: 100`. The invariant it meant to hold is narrower than its scope: "the
    sweep's own ADD does not drop failures." Rescoped to that — extract the
    `upsertJobScheduler` add-opts object and assert it equals `{ removeOnComplete: true }`
    and names no `removeOnFail` there — rather than deleted, so the sweep still cannot start
    discarding failures. This is the same "a guard pinned the shape this round intentionally
    changed" event as #62 §8.35.6; followed to the new truth, not loosened. Also updated the
    four apps' memoisation/construction guards from the bare `{ connection }` form to `{
    connection, defaultJobOptions: JOB_RETENTION }` (leadworks loop, abetagent :70,
    abetanalytics :58/:73, abetflow queue :68 + scheduler :143).

8.36.4 AFTER-PROBE — THE DEFAULT BOUNDS A BARE ADD ON A REAL PORTED APP

    `/tmp/abectorial/n64-probe.mts`, run against abetagent's real `src/lib/queue.ts` over
    scratch Redis db 13 (`REDIS_URL` ending `/13`; the connection's OWN `options.db` asserted
    to be 13, not the string we handed — the §8.20 defect). A raw Worker drained the queue.
    Four adds, all through the module's `enqueue`, none with retention opts except where noted:
      - 3 bare successful adds;
      - 1 bare failing add (handler throws);
      - 1 successful add that names its OWN `{ removeOnComplete: false }` to test the merge.
    Measured: `completed: 1`, `failed: 1`. The single surviving completed job is the override —
    the construction default's `removeOnComplete: true` removed all three bare ones, and the
    one add that opted out was retained. That same number proves BOTH halves of the claim at
    once: the default bounds a bare add, AND it is only a default (the per-add opt beat it).
    The bare failure being `failed: 1` shows `removeOnFail: 100` keeps rather than drops.
    VERDICT: PASS. Cleanup: `redis-cli -n 13 FLUSHDB` (per #63, never FLUSHALL) → db 13
    8→0; live db 0 read 2 before and 2 after — untouched. Scratch node_modules symlink removed.

8.36.5 GUARDS, MUTANTS, AND THE GATE

    Structural guards added to all four apps' `queue.test.ts` (mirror of leadgenius #61's
    "every queue declares retention at construction", sized per app): the shared const present;
    every construction carries `defaultJobOptions: JOB_RETENTION` (loop/matchAll over the app's
    own key set); a `doesNotMatch` for the bare `{ connection }` this removes; and `removeOnFail`
    a positive number. Offline suites after the port: leadworks queue 27/0, abetagent 14/0,
    abetanalytics 14/0, abetflow queue 14/0, abetflow scheduler 15/0 (asserted offline).
    Two mutants, each reverted (source backed up and restored, re-ran green after):
      - LW-M1 drop the default on ONE leadworks queue (scheduled → bare) → 3 guards fail
        together (the six-queue matchAll, the no-bare `{ connection }`, the memoises loop).
      - AF-M2 put `removeOnFail: false` into abetflow's sweep ADD → the add-scope guard bites
        (actual `{ removeOnComplete: true, removeOnFail: false }`), while the module's
        construction-level `removeOnFail: 100` does NOT false-trip it — the precise point of
        the §8.36.3 rescope. 2 fail.
    Full gate `shared/check-all.sh`: 18 steps, 0 failed, 70s (sequential for the 3.8 GB box).
    Shared checks: check-suites 78/0, check-ui-drift 6/0, check-auth-drift 288/0,
    check-guards-drift 42/0, check-queues 67/0, check-docs 98/0. Per-app full suites + typecheck:
    leadgenius 180/0, leadworks 142/0, abetagent 122/0, abetflow 150/0, abetanalytics 109/0,
    abetapi 127/0 — every typecheck clean. (The suite counts above are whole `tests/unit/*.test.ts`;
    the single-file numbers earlier in this subsection are just the two queue guard files.)

8.36.6 NEXT

    #64 — closed by this section for its stated subject ("port the convention"): the four
    queue apps now behave identically, guarded per app. What is NOT done is the physical
    de-duplication: `JOB_RETENTION` is now the same literal in five `queue.ts` files, and
    #62 already left `awaitWithRegistrationTimeout` + `SWEEP_REGISTER_TIMEOUT_MS` duplicated
    across two. A family-wide round that vendors all three through `shared/` (with an import
    so the drift check fails on a copy nothing imports, as #36/#41/#44 did for the validation
    and auth modules) would make "cannot drift" a build fact rather than five green guards.
    Deferred rather than half-done here, because vendoring forces an import-wiring change to
    leadgenius (whose guard pins its LOCAL const) and that is a different blast radius than a
    port that leaves every existing site's text intact.

8.37 #58 + #59 — THE ANALYTICS GET WAS A WRITER AND NOTHING READ ITS TABLE

8.37.1 SCOPE — MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED

    One finding's fix closed the other's gap, so they were done together. Two facts, both
    read from source before touching anything:
      - #59: a grep for `analyticsDaily`/`AnalyticsDaily` over `leadgenius/src/**` returned
        exactly ONE write-site touch — `prisma.analyticsDaily.upsert` at `analytics.ts:187`
        — and NO `findUnique`/`findFirst`/`findMany` on the table anywhere. The rollup Worker
        (built in #54: the three webhooks `enqueueDailyRollup` → `rollupDay` →
        `aggregateAnalytics`) writes `AnalyticsDaily`; nothing ever read it back. A table
        written and never read.
      - #58: the ONLY caller of `aggregateAnalytics` outside the Worker was
        `GET /api/analytics/[workspaceId]/route.ts:39` (`const daily = await
        aggregateAnalytics(workspaceId, date)`), and `aggregateAnalytics` ends in the upsert
        above. So the dashboard poll wrote the row it displayed — a GET with a write side
        effect, racing the Worker for the same `@@unique([workspaceId, date])` key and
        re-scanning the Activity table on every page load.

8.37.2 THE FIX — SPLIT THE READ FROM THE WRITE

    Added `getDailyAnalytics(workspaceId, date)` to `src/lib/analytics.ts`: a reader that
    `analyticsDaily.findUnique`s the composite key at `dayBucket(date)` — the same local-
    midnight normalization `aggregateAnalytics` stores under — and returns the persisted row,
    or zeros for a day with no row yet (zeros is what `aggregateAnalytics` writes for an empty
    window, so the read path returns what the write path would have, and no caller branches on
    "rolled up yet"). The GET's `daily` branch now calls it and no longer imports
    `aggregateAnalytics`. The write stays where it belongs: the Worker path only. `#58` (the
    GET writes) and `#59` (nothing reads) are two halves of one accidental read/write
    inversion — the GET was doing the rollup's job because the rollup had no reader.

8.37.3 AFTER-PROBE — THE READER READS, AND WRITES NOTHING (offline, on a COPY of dev.db)

    Measured against a copy of `prisma/dev.db` (never the live file; `DATABASE_URL` guarded to
    name `n58-probe.db` or the probe refuses to run), `getDailyAnalytics` and
    `aggregateAnalytics` imported by absolute path from `src/lib/analytics.ts`:
      - Seeded a sentinel row (`sent 888, opened 999, clicked 777, replied 666`, byChannel
        EMAIL) — numbers no Activity recompute could produce. Read it back through a MID-DAY
        `?date` (14:30): got the sentinel exactly, `date` normalized to `00:00`, `byChannel`
        from the JSON column. This proves the reader reads the persisted table, not the
        Activity rows (if it recomputed, 999 could not come back).
      - Wrote nothing: `updatedAt` byte-identical after the read, `analyticsDaily.count()`
        unchanged; the empty-day read (`2026-10-01`, no row) returned zeros and created no row.
      - The contrast that grounds #58: running `aggregateAnalytics` on the same day AFTER the
        sentinel read clobbered `opened 999 → 0` (the true recompute). That overwrite is the
        side effect the GET used to perform on every poll and now no longer does.
    VERDICT: PASS.

8.37.4 GUARDS, MUTANTS, AND THE GATE

    New describe in `tests/unit/queue.test.ts` ("the analytics GET is a reader, not a second
    writer"), against comment-stripped source: the route's `daily` branch calls
    `getDailyAnalytics` and `doesNotMatch`es `aggregateAnalytics`; the reader `findUnique`s and
    `doesNotMatch`es `analyticsDaily.(upsert|create|update|delete)`; the lookup keys on
    `dayBucket(date)`. Existing guards (upsert-in-aggregateAnalytics at :283, Worker runs
    rollupDay not aggregateAnalytics at :221) still hold — the write is intact, just relocated.
    Two mutants, each reverted (both source files backed up and restored, re-ran green):
      - M1 point the GET's daily branch back at `aggregateAnalytics` → the new guard fails,
        36 pass / 1 fail (names the reader describe).
      - M2 switch the reader's `findUnique` to `upsert` → same single guard fails, 36/1.
    leadgenius queue file 37/0 after restore; full leadgenius suite 181/0 (was 180 before the
    one guard added). The `AnalyticsDaily` reader now has a source line a grep can find, which
    is the durable form of "#59 is closed": the table is read at `analytics.ts:221`.

8.37.5 NEXT

    #58 and #59 — closed by this section: the GET is read-only against the persisted row, and
    `AnalyticsDaily` has a real reader. Left deliberately: `getDailyAnalytics` reads a single
    workspace-day, because that is the whole shape the GET asked for before (one `?date`, one
    row) and inventing a multi-day range read the route does not call would be speculative.
    The `daily` branch reads whatever the Worker last wrote, so a day whose rollup never ran
    (a lost/dropped enqueue — the #65 subject) shows zeros rather than the Activity rows that
    exist; #65 remains the open finding for that gap, and is not half-solved here. #56
    (enrichment has no real providers behind its branches) is untouched and still the leadgenius
    follow-up with the widest surface.

================================================================================
8.38 — #29  abetflow DELETE /api/variables and /api/credentials answered 500
         for a name that simply was not there
================================================================================

8.38.1 SCOPE — MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED

    Read from source before touching anything. `DELETE_impl` in both `src/app/api/variables/
    route.ts:53` and `src/app/api/credentials/route.ts:65` called `prisma.{variable,
    credential}.delete({ where: { workspaceId_name: { workspaceId, name } } })`. Prisma's
    `delete` on a unique/composite key that matches no row throws `P2025`
    (`PrismaClientKnownRequestError`, "…does not exist"). `conflictFor` (`apikeys.ts:106`)
    recognises only `P2002` — and `tests/unit/conflict.test.ts` asserts `conflictFor(P2025)
    === null`, so the mapping is deliberately left to the route, not the error boundary —
    and `withAuth` (`apikeys.ts:148`) rethrows whatever it does not recognise. Net: an absent
    name was a 500. This is the row-#29 the N5/N6 rounds scanned past ("the three DELETE
    handlers in this app were scanned for authentication, not for a missing row").

    Out of scope by construction, and that was a finding: the two `:id` DELETE handlers
    (`workflows/[id]`, `executions/[id]`) already do a lookup first and return
    `{ error: "Not found" }` at 404 before their `prisma.{workflow,execution}.delete({ where:
    { id } })`. Their `{ id }` key is a genuine unique field so `delete` accepts it, and the
    prior check means it only runs on a row that exists. #29 is exactly the two handlers that
    had no such check.

8.38.2 THE FIX — deleteMany's count, matched to the app's own 404 convention

    Both handlers now `deleteMany({ where: { workspaceId, name } })` and return the app's
    `{ error: "Not found" }` at 404 when `count === 0`, else `{ ok: true }`. `deleteMany`
    takes the general `WhereInput` (not `delete`'s `WhereUniqueInput`), so `workspaceId` and
    `name` are two filter fields — a first-attempt used the compound `workspaceId_name`
    selector inside `deleteMany` and `tsc` rejected it (`VariableWhereInput` has no such key);
    caught by running typecheck, not by eye. `deleteMany` never throws `P2025`, so the miss
    cannot reach the 500 path, and it is one query — no find-then-delete race. The filter
    carries the caller's authenticated `workspaceId`, so a name that exists only in another
    tenant counts 0 and is not touched.

8.38.3 AFTER-PROBE — THE MECHANISM, OFFLINE ON A COPY OF dev.db

    Measured against a copy of `prisma/dev.db` (`DATABASE_URL` named `n29-probe.db`), driving
    the Prisma client with the exact arguments the route passes (`n29-probe.mts`):
      1. `variable.delete` on a name absent in this tenant → threw `code=P2025` — the 500.
      2. `variable.deleteMany` on the same absent name → `count=0`, no throw — the 404.
      3. Tenant scoping: the probe name seeded in wsA only; `deleteMany({ workspaceId: wsB,
         name })` → `count=0` AND the wsA row survived — a foreign-tenant delete neither
         succeeds nor discloses, it is the same 404.
      4. Correct tenant + name → `count=1` — the 200.
    VERDICT: PASS. The route's branch (count 0 → 404) is proven by the guard; the throw-vs-
    count-and-scope behaviour it depends on is proven here, asserted offline (no dev server,
    so no live HTTP round trip — the two facts are independent and each is labelled).

8.38.4 GUARDS, MUTANTS, AND THE GATE

    New `tests/unit/delete-missing-row.test.ts` (comment-stripped source, so a prose "404"
    cannot satisfy it): each of the two handlers must NOT contain a bare `prisma.{model}.
    delete(` (the throwing form), must `deleteMany` on `{ workspaceId, name }`, and must map
    `.count === 0` to `status: 404`; plus a guard that the two `:id` handlers still 404, so a
    future edit cannot drop their not-found branch or "unify" them onto deleteMany.
      - Mutant: variables back to the bare `delete` → the new guard fails it (2 pass / 1 fail),
        naming exactly the variables test. Reverted; restored to 3/0.
    abetflow typecheck exit 0; full suite 153/0 (was 150 before these three tests).

8.38.5 NEXT

    #29 — closed by this section. The general rule "a Prisma write that can miss its row
    answers a client status, not a 500" is now true for every DELETE in abetflow. The other
    five apps were not scanned for this same `delete`-on-missing-key shape this round; if the
    convention should hold family-wide it is a separate sweep (#38 counts unguarded reads and
    could carry the analogous write-miss count). #65 remains the deliberate half-solve: the
    leadgenius webhook commits a row then loses the sequence-step enqueue to a 503 that the
    retried delivery now skips, and a correct fix is a transactional outbox or a step-
    reconciling driver — neither fits one round, and neither is started here.

8.39 — #33  abetapi a 404 from an :id lookup miss never reaches RequestLog

8.39.1 SCOPE — MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED

    `requireKey` logs its own refusals (401 missing/bad key, 403 scope, 429 quota)
    inside itself, but on success it does NOT log — the caller owes a
    `logSuccess(key, request, status, startedAt)`, which is what writes the
    `RequestLog` row `/api/usage` reads. So a `{id}` handler that answers "no such
    row" from its own lookup miss and returns before reaching `logSuccess` is
    invisible in the log. Read from source: thirteen such 404s returned bare —
    `keys/[id]` GET+PATCH+DELETE, `webhooks/[id]` GET+PATCH+DELETE, `sso/[id]`
    GET+PATCH+DELETE, `deliveries` GET, `replay` (webhook-lookup + the nothing-to-
    replay branch), and `sso/[id]/check` POST. Two 404s already logged — the
    `deliveries/replay` nothing-to-replay branch called `logSuccess(..., 404, ...)`
    literally, and `ai/diagnose-webhook` calls it before its own 404 — the
    `validate.ts` header's "nine 404s asked, two in the log" line. `conflictFor`
    (auth.ts) maps only P2002, so a P2025 was never the issue here; the gap is
    purely the missing logging call on the not-found return path.

8.39.2 THE FIX — a logged 404 is the default, not a step a route can forget

    Added `notFound(key, request, startedAt, message)` to `lib/auth.ts`: it awaits
    `logSuccess(..., 404, ...)` and then returns the published
    `{ error: { code: "not_found", message } }` envelope at 404. Routed all thirteen
    bare 404s through it, collapsing the two-line log+`NextResponse.json` at each site
    to one call. Chose a helper over thirteen inline `logSuccess(404)` lines because
    #33's root cause is that the logging was easy to omit: making the safe thing the
    only thing the caller writes is the fix, matching how `refused()` and `conflictFor`
    already wrap this app's other answer paths. `ai/diagnose-webhook` is deliberately
    NOT converted — its dashboard (`ai-panel.tsx`) prints `data.error` as text, so it
    keeps a bare-string envelope; it already logs the 404 inline, which the guard
    below accepts.

8.39.3 AFTER-PROBE — THE MECHANISM, OFFLINE ON A COPY OF dev.db

    `n33-probe.mts` over a named copy (`n33-copy.db`, guarded so the live file is
    never the target; the copy is deleted on exit), importing the app's `prisma` and
    `notFound` (auth.ts imports only prisma-backed ratelimit, so import is inert):
      1. Old shape — a 404 returned with no logging call: RequestLog 4371 -> 4371,
         delta 0. Nothing was written; this is the hole #33 describes.
      2. `notFound()` — response.status=404, RequestLog 4371 -> 4372, delta 1.
      3. The stored row: status 404, path the probed `/api/keys/does-not-exist`,
         and correctly scoped to the workspace and key id passed in.
    VERDICT: PASS. Asserted offline (no dev server, no live HTTP round trip); the
    branch that wires each route to `notFound` is proven by the guard, the log-write
    itself by this probe — two independent facts, each labelled.

8.39.4 GUARDS, MUTANTS, AND THE GATE

    New `tests/unit/notfound-logged.test.ts`, comment-stripped, sharing the vendored
    `collectHandlers` scanner (uses its API, not edited — it is drift-checked
    family-wide). Three tests:
      - Invariant, "no handler answers a 404 before it logs one": walks each handler
        body left-to-right; a `notFound(`/`logSuccess(...404...)` reads +1, a bare
        `status: 404` reads -1; any negative balance is an offender. This clears both
        compliant shapes (helper, and diagnose-webhook's log-then-bare-string) and
        fails a bare unlogged 404 or a log that runs after the return.
      - Scanner pinned in both directions against synthetic bodies (bare / viaHelper /
        inlineLogged / loggedAfter), so a scan that said "clean" for everything fails.
      - Positive adoption: per-file `notFound(` counts pinned (keys 3, webhooks 3,
        sso 3, sso/check 1, deliveries 1, replay 2), so silently dropping one back is
        visible and a scanner that stopped resolving a file is not a silent pass.
      - Mutant: deliveries GET back to a bare literal → invariant test fails (1 pass /
        2 fail), naming it; restored from a /tmp backup (no `git checkout`, which would
        have dropped the uncommitted fixes in that file). Restored to 3/0.
    abetapi typecheck exit 0; full suite 130/0 (was 127 before these three tests).
    validate.ts header's "nine 404s … owed as its own item" line updated to say the
    item is closed here; README logging note extended to the 404 case.

8.39.5 NEXT

    #33 — closed by this section. The `{id}` enumeration blind spot in `/api/usage` is
    gone for abetapi. The invariant is "a 404 never leaves a handler unlogged," not
    "must use the helper," so a future bare 404 that logs first still passes — the
    honest rule. Not attempted this round: #34 (the DELETE /api/keys/{id} undocumented
    403 and the three disagreeing version numbers — noticed beside this code, left as
    its own item); and the same audit-hole sweep across the other five apps, which
    log differently (they use `withAuth`, whose scope differs), belongs with #38 rather
    than being restated here unmeasured.

8.40 — #34  abetapi reachable 403s were undocumented, and three versions disagreed

8.40.1 SCOPE — MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED

    Read from source. (a) Scopes: `requireKey(request, ["x:write"])` returns a logged
    403 (`permission_error`) for a valid key that does not hold the scope, before any
    row lookup. Ten operations demand a write scope — keys POST + DELETE, webhooks
    POST/PATCH/DELETE, events POST, replay POST, sso POST/PATCH/DELETE, sso/check POST.
    The published document (`src/lib/openapi.ts`) listed a 403 on only three of them
    (keys POST line 156, keys PATCH 186, events POST 215). The seven read endpoints ask
    `requireKey(request, [])`, and `scopesCover(granted, [])` is always true, so they
    can never 403 on scope and correctly list none — the invariant is scope-presence ⟺
    403-presence, not blanket 403. (b) Version: `package.json` was `0.1.0` and
    `openapi.info.version` the literal `"0.1.0"`, while `src/app/changelog/page.tsx`
    announced `v1.0.0` (initial public release) and `v1.0.1` (current) under its own
    stated `major.minor.patch` policy. Three places, two stories, hand-copied.

8.40.2 THE FIX — document the real contract, single-source the version

    Added `403: fails("Needs <scope>")` to the nine scoped operations missing it, using
    the same `fails(...)` envelope as the three that already had it, so the machine
    contract matches what the gateway returns. No behaviour changed; only the document.
    For the version: `openapi.ts` now does `import { version as packageVersion } from
    "../../package.json"` (tsconfig `resolveJsonModule` is on) and sets `info.version`
    to it — the document can no longer disagree with the package because it reads it.
    package.json bumped 0.1.0 -> 1.0.2 and a `v1.0.2` changelog entry added recording
    the contract fix (this app's policy: additive doc clarification with no behavior
    change is a patch; it is dated October 2026 above the v1.0.1 entry so it is the
    newest), which the version guard then pins. The `ALL_SCOPES` import in openapi.ts
    (unused since an earlier round) was left alone — outside this item's scope.

8.40.3 AFTER-PROBE — THE 403 IS REAL AND LOGGED, THE VERSIONS AGREE

    `n34-probe.mts` over a named copy (`n34-copy.db`; live file never targeted; copy
    deleted on exit), calling the actual `DELETE` handler in `keys/[id]/route.ts` with
    an injected unscoped key (scopes [] so `scopesCover([], ["keys:write"])` is false)
    on a Bearer token whose hash matches a row just minted in the copy:
      1. DELETE -> 403 `{ code: "permission_error", message: "This key lacks a required
         scope." }` — the status the contract had left undeclared, proven reachable.
      2. RequestLog 4371 -> 4372 (delta 1); a status-403 row for the path present — the
         403 is logged, which #33's audit work guarantees for refusals generally.
      3. The probe key was still live afterward (`revokedAt` null) — 403 precedes the
         lookup, so nothing was revoked. Confirms the ordering in `requireKey`.
      4. openapi=1.0.2 package=1.0.2 changelog=1.0.2 — one number across the three.
    VERDICT: PASS. Asserted offline (no dev server, no HTTP round trip); the
    document⟺code scope match is proven by the guard, the reachability of the 403 by
    this probe — independent facts, each labelled.

8.40.4 GUARDS, MUTANTS, AND THE GATE

    New `tests/unit/openapi-contract.test.ts` (4 tests), comment-stripping the handler
    bodies and importing the real `openapiSpec` (offline: openapi.ts pulls only keys/
    fields, no prisma/next). It maps each route file to its openapi path (`[id]` ->
    `{id}`), reads the scope list out of each handler's actual `requireKey` call, and
    asserts: (1) every authenticated handler has a matching documented operation;
    (2) a 403 is documented exactly where `requireKey` demands a scope, and nowhere
    else; (3) `info.version` === package.json === the changelog's newest `v*.*.*`;
    (4) the scope extractor itself, pinned on synthetic bodies in both directions.
      - Mutant A: removed the 403 from deleteWebhook -> test 2 failed (3 pass/1 fail),
        naming the operation. Reverted from /tmp backup (no `git checkout`).
      - Mutant B: bumped package.json to 1.0.2 alone (doc/changelog still 1.0.1) ->
        test 3 failed, naming the version test. Reverted. Restored to 4/0.
    abetapi typecheck exit 0. Full suite 134/0 (was 130 before these four tests).

8.40.5 NEXT

    #34 — closed by this section. The published contract no longer hides a reachable
    status, and the version is one number derived in one place. Not done here: the
    scope⟺403 guard is abetapi-only, because the other five apps do not publish an
    OpenAPI contract (abetapi's envelope is its product — see `validate.ts`); if they
    ever do, the same test ports as-is. #35 ("look through delegation in the auth
    guard") is the natural neighbour and was left for its own round. The unused
    `ALL_SCOPES` import in openapi.ts and the broader "one contract across the family"
    question both stay open rather than being tidied as a side effect.

8.41 — #35  the auth guard could not see a write that one line away from the handler
     actually performed, and what it saw when it finally looked

8.41.1 SCOPE — MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED

    The census, run over all six apps with the scanner as it stood: 181 exported
    handlers, 108 database writes. Two of those 181 handlers — abetflow's
    `webhooks/[...path]` GET and POST — are a single line, `return trigger(request,
    ctx)`, whose whole body is a call into a helper ten lines above them in the same
    file. The helper holds a `prisma.execution.create`. The scan stopped at the outer
    block, so the domination rule read those two handlers, found no write, and was
    correct about the text and wrong about the route. #35's own description named a
    second case, abetapi's `v1/openapi` GET; reading it (route.ts:6-8, one
    `NextResponse.json(openapiSpec)` with no helper) it has no write at any depth, so
    it passes for the right reason and the ledger corrects the task text rather than
    fixing a bug that is not there.

    What the delegation census says about the fix's blast radius: of 181 handlers, 2
    change behaviour. The other 179 come back with the same body they went in with,
    which is why the five apps' counts (48, 46, 20, 21, 24) did not move and their
    suites did not flinch.

8.41.2 THE FIX — resolve the tail call in the scanner, not in the caller

    `shared/tests/route-handlers.ts`: `collectHandlers` now appends every same-file
    function a handler hands straight back to, transitively, each block once. The
    seed set — the exported method's own name plus, for a wrapped export, every name
    whose block was already merged — is what makes the transitive walk safe: without
    it, `POST -> a -> b -> a` appends `a` twice and the domination test reports the
    same write twice. `resolveDelegation` is exported so its behaviour can be asserted
    on synthetic source; `functionBody` stays exported for a guard that wants to name
    the shape it is checking.

    Deleting hand-maintenance was the point, not a side effect. abetflow's
    `route-validation.test.ts` had `DELEGATES_TO` (one file, one helper name), a
    `handlersWithDelegations()` that re-read the file and pasted the block back, and a
    test whose only job was to fail if that list went stale — 32 removed lines, all of
    it a workaround for the scanner being one line short. abetanalytics's tripwire
    ("no body-reading handler delegates past the scanner") could not survive the change
    as written: its condition was `inner !== h.body`, which the resolution makes true
    for every handler that ever validates, so the test would have fired on correct
    code. Rewritten to ask the general question instead — for every tail call in every
    handler, did the callee's block come back with it.

8.41.3 WHAT THE NEW SIGHT FOUND, AND THE ANSWER IT IS NOT A BUG

    The first run after the scanner change: abetflow 152 pass, 1 fail.
    `no write is reachable without a check that dominates it` flagged
    `prisma.execution.create` in both webhook handlers. It is not a false positive and
    it is not a new hole — it is the rule finally reading the route. The write is
    reachable by an anonymous caller through `public: true`, which `parseTrigger`
    refuses to grant a trigger that has no secret unless the author says so out loud
    (`src/lib/definition.ts:47`), and the workspace the row lands in comes from the
    matched ACTIVE workflow, never from the request.

    Two lists now hold two different excuses, and neither is `PUBLIC`. `PUBLIC` says a
    handler needs no key; the new `ANONYMOUS_WRITES` says a *write* needs no check that
    dominates it, which is a stronger thing to admit and so is keyed per file-and-method
    and gets two checks of its own: it is in the staleness test (an entry that names a
    handler the scan cannot find fails), and its premise is asserted against the source —
    the route must still read `if (!trigger.public) {`, because the excuse is "the author
    opened this door", and a route that drops the door while keeping the entry has
    converted a reviewed exception into a silent one.

8.41.4 AFTER-PROBE — THE WRITE IS REAL, THE REFUSAL IS REAL, THE GET STORES NULL

    `n35-probe.mts` over a named copy (`/tmp/abectorial/n35-copy.db`; live
    `prisma/dev.db` never opened, copy deleted on exit), calling the route's exported
    handlers directly — no dev server, no HTTP round trip, and `?sync=true` so the
    enqueue branch and Redis are never touched. One workspace, two ACTIVE workflows, one
    `public: true` and one with a secret. Executions counted before and after each call:

      1. POST to the public trigger, no credential at all -> 201, executions 0 -> 1,
         `input={"hello":"world"}`. The write the scanner now reveals is reachable
         exactly as the entry says; the guard is not inventing a path.
      2. POST to the secret trigger, no credential -> 401, no row written. The check
         dominates for a trigger that has one.
      3. POST to the secret trigger with the right `x-api-key` -> 201, one row.
      4. GET to the public trigger, no credential -> 201, one row, `input=null`. This is
         the GET half of both abetflow's `UNVALIDATED` (N5) and `ANONYMOUS_WRITES` (N6)
         entries, proven rather than argued: the body read is inside
         `if (request.method === "POST")`, so a GET writes without reading anything.

    Probe output kept at `/tmp/abectorial/n35-abetflow-probe.txt`; the script itself was
    deleted after the run, which is #40's complaint recorded again rather than answered.

8.41.5 GUARDS, MUTANTS, AND THE GATE

    A third vendored file, `shared/tests/route-scanner.test.ts` → each app's
    `tests/unit/`, six tests, run by `npm test` everywhere. It exists because the
    property is abetflow-shaped: a route-level test can only prove delegation resolution
    where a route delegates. On synthetic input all six apps do, including the five with
    nothing to delegate. `sync-guards.sh`'s MAP and `check-guards-drift.sh` grew to match
    — 42 assertions became 48.

      Mutant A — resolution switched off in the canonical file, re-synced: all six apps
        fail (3 synthetic tests each), abetflow fails 5 in total — both of its
        real-route resolution tests as well. Without this mutant the vendored file would
        have been a plausible-looking extra; this is the measurement that says the
        portability is real rather than claimed.
      Mutant B — the webhook route removed from `ANONYMOUS_WRITES`: 1 failure, the
        domination test. The list is load-bearing, not decorative. (My first attempt at
        this mutant deleted the `PUBLIC` entry instead, because both maps close with the
        same two lines and my replacement took the first match; it failed the presence
        test, which is a different test proving a different thing. Redone against the
        right map.)
      Mutant C — the route's `if (!trigger.public) {` rewritten: 1 failure, the premise
        test. The excuse cannot outlive the door it describes.
      Mutant D — a type error appended to the vendored test: `npm run typecheck` named
        `tests/unit/route-scanner.test.ts(134,7)`, so the vendored files are inside the
        type gate and not merely inside `npm test`.

    Gate: `bash shared/check-all.sh` — 18 steps, 0 failed, 79s of step time. Six shared
    checks (guards drift 48, auth drift 288, docs 98, suites 78, queues 67, UI 6) plus
    typecheck and test for each app. Family suite total 841 -> 879: 36 from the vendored
    file (6 x 6) and 2 new abetflow auth assertions; the validation test traded one test
    for one. No app's behaviour changed, so no README changed — and abetflow's README
    lines 50-53 already document the secret-or-`public: true` rule and that GET behaves
    the same way, which this round confirmed against source instead of assuming.

8.41.6 STILL OWED, RECORDED RATHER THAN FIXED

    The hole the fix leaves, stated in a test so it cannot be rediscovered by
    surprise: a tail call into a *module-level* `const helper = async () => {}` is not
    resolved, because `implBody` only knows `function` declarations. Measured across the
    six apps, nothing is hidden by this today — the only arrow a tail call names anywhere
    is abetapi's `refused`, and that closure is written inside the handler body that
    calls it, so its statements are already in the scanned text. The vendored test asserts
    the non-resolution rather than hoping nobody notices.

    The route-level tripwire now exists in two apps, not six. abetflow's and
    abetanalytics's guards ask "did the callee's block come back with it" of their own
    routes; leadgenius, leadworks, abetagent and abetapi have the vendored synthetic
    proof and no route-level version of the question. That is #39's shape — a guard's
    general mechanism vendored, its app-specific assertion left where it was first
    needed — and belongs in the same port.

    New, and found by looking at what the probe made possible rather than by looking for
    it: a `public: true` trigger is an anonymous *execution* request, and `?sync=true`
    runs the graph inline in the request. The probe drove that path with an empty
    definition and it cost nothing; a definition with HTTP and LLM nodes would spend the
    tenant's credentials and tokens on whoever guessed the path, with no rate limit on
    either the anonymous or the signed branch of this route. That is the same class as
    #32 (a URL the server is told to POST to) and #38 (unguarded reads), and it is a
    product decision before it is a code change: either public triggers are capped and
    their nodes restricted, or `public` means "receives payloads" and never "runs
    synchronously". Filed, not answered here.

    #35 — closed by this section. #37, #38 and #39 are untouched by this round; the
    standing list (#17, #27, #28, #29, #32, #33, #34, #37, #38) is unchanged except that
    #35 leaves it. `/root` is still not a repository (#42), so this ledger keeps its work
    with no history of its own.

8.42 — #38  the read side was counted before it was given a rule, and the rule it
     got is not the write rule's mirror

8.42.1 SCOPE — MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED

    The census, run over the six apps with the scanner as §8.41 left it, plus a read
    pattern (`READ_NAME`: the find* family, `count`, `aggregate`, `groupBy`, and
    `prisma.$queryRaw` — not `$transaction`, whose writes are found where they are
    written): 181 handlers, 193 reads, 108 writes. Per app, reads / dominated by a
    check that ran first / not dominated: leadgenius 54/29/25, leadworks 48/19/29,
    abetagent 21/9/12, abetflow 24/10/14, abetanalytics 25/15/10, abetapi 21/20/1.

    The 91 undominated reads split on a question that decides everything: is there a
    check in the handler at all? 81 yes — the lookup-then-check idiom — and 10 no,
    sitting in 8 handlers (six `health` GET `$queryRaw`, one per app, plus abetflow's
    webhook GET and POST, whose `findMany` and read-back are the same two handlers
    §8.41 revealed the write in).

    Two sharper questions were asked of the 81 before any rule was written, because
    "check comes later" is only harmless if nothing leaves before the check. For each
    of the 81, every `NextResponse.json(…)` between the read and the first later check
    was extracted — assignment target and balanced-paren argument — and asked whether
    it names the row. Across all six apps the answer was 0: every intervening response
    is the not-found 404. And the mirror rule as literally written would be
    unsatisfiable rather than noisy, because `assertOwns(request, row.workspaceId)`
    takes its second argument from the read it would have to precede.

8.42.2 THE DECISION — A HANDLER-LEVEL RULE, NOT A SITE-LEVEL MIRROR

    The write rule asks where a call sits in the control flow. For reads that question
    has no answer worth enforcing, so #38's "decide whether the rule extends" resolves
    to: it extends as a different rule. What shipped is
    `unauthenticatedReaders(handlers, listed)` in the canonical scanner — a handler that
    reads the database and contains no check anywhere is named, unless the calling test
    already lists it. The write rule is untouched; `unguardedWrites` and
    `unguardedReads` sit side by side and only the first is load-bearing.

    It cost nothing to adopt and that is the evidence it is the right shape: all eight
    bare readers are already on their app's `PUBLIC` list, so no new excuse list was
    needed, and a handler that reads anonymously now has to say so on the list that
    §8.10 established for exactly that admission. `DELEGATES` counts too, since a
    handler that authenticates outside its body reads the same way it writes.

    It is also strictly stronger than the presence rule it sits beside, which is not a
    side effect but the reason to keep both: the presence test matches `AUTH_NAME`
    against the raw body text, so a route whose only "check" is the word inside a
    string satisfies it. `authSites` reads the tokenized body, so a name in a string,
    comment or regex is not a call. The vendored fixture states this rather than
    leaving it to luck.

    Floor attached, same reason as the write floor: each app's `route-auth.test.ts` now
    counts `readSites` across its handlers and refuses below 8. Measured per app 54, 48,
    21, 24, 25, 21 — so a `READ_NAME` that stopped matching would fail the floor rather
    than turn the new rule into a silent pass.

8.42.3 WHAT THE NEW RULE CANNOT SEE, COUNTED RATHER THAN HANDED WAVE

    A handler that calls `requireApiKey` and then reads every workspace's rows passes:
    the rule asks whether a check exists, not whether the query is scoped. So the
    unscoped-read question was measured directly. Multi-row reads (`findMany`,
    `groupBy`, `count`, `aggregate`) whose own argument text contains no
    tenant-shaped field: 32 across the six apps. 26 of them do not write a `where: {`
    literal at all — the filter arrives from a variable built above the call (a
    `workspaceId` from the authenticated caller folded in), from `{ where }` shorthand,
    or from a ternary — which no argument-text rule can judge without becoming a
    data-flow engine. The remaining 6 scope by a parent id — abetagent
    `run.findMany({ where: { agentId: id } })`, abetflow
    `workflowVersion.findMany({ where: { workflowId: id } })`, abetanalytics
    `snapshot.findMany({ where: { metricId } })` and
    `alertEvent.findMany({ where: { ruleId: id } })`, abetapi
    `delivery.findMany({ where: { webhookId: id } })` twice. All six were read, and in
    every one the parent is fetched with its `workspaceId` and asserted (or fetched
    with `workspaceId` in the query itself) before the child read runs, and the
    ternary in abetapi's replay route scopes its parent the same way. So the class is
    clean today by author discipline, and the guard does not enforce it. That is the
    honest limit, and #37's cross-tenant probe is what would test it with rows rather
    than with reading.

    The count has a hole in it that is worth stating, because it is the same hole the
    rule has: abetflow's webhook `findMany({ where: { status: "ACTIVE" }, select: { id,
    workspaceId, trigger } })` is not among the 32. Its argument text does name
    `workspaceId` — in the `select`, which is the opposite of scoping by it. A
    heuristic that reads argument text cannot tell a filter from a projection, so the
    route that this round's probe turned into #75 was invisible to the measurement that
    was supposed to find it.

8.42.4 AFTER-PROBE — THE UNSCOPED READ THAT IS A DEFECT, OFFLINE ON A COPY OF dev.db

    abetflow's inbound webhook route opens with
    `prisma.workflow.findMany({ where: { status: "ACTIVE" }, select: { id, workspaceId,
    trigger } })` and then `candidates.find(w => w.trigger.path === slug)`. There is no
    workspace filter to have: the path is the only thing the caller supplies, and the
    schema has no index that could carry it (`Workflow` has `@@index([workspaceId,
    status])`, and `trigger` is a JSON column). `n38-probe.mts` on a named copy
    (`/tmp/abectorial/n38-copy.db`, live `prisma/dev.db` never opened, copy unlinked on
    exit, `sha256sum -c` over the live file verified after) seeded two workspaces with
    two ACTIVE webhook workflows sharing the path `shared-hook`, A created first, and
    called the route's exported POST directly:

      1. B's own secret, on B's own path -> 401, executions 48 -> 48. The later tenant
         cannot reach its own webhook at all: the request is matched to A, and its key
         is compared against A's secret. Not a disclosure — an availability hole with
         no error to show for it, and nothing on the write path says "this path is
         taken" because nothing can index it.
      2. A's secret on the same path -> 202, and the Execution row lands in A's
         workspace, as it should. The collision is one-directional, which is why it is
         #75 and not an escalation of #74.
      3. The unscoped read, spelled verbatim from route.ts:18: one request aimed at one
         path read 7 ACTIVE workflows across 3 workspaces, 2 of which carried a signing
         secret into that request's memory. Nothing is returned to the caller — the 404
         and 401 responses name nothing — but every inbound webhook materialises every
         tenant's trigger config, secrets included, and the number grows with the
         family's tenants rather than with the request.
      4. SQLite's own plan for that statement: `SCAN Workflow`.

    Filed as #75 (path namespace + the full-table read). #74 stands beside it: a
    `public: true` trigger on a path that a later tenant also chose is both the
    anonymous-execution problem and nobody's error message.

8.42.5 GUARDS, MUTANTS, AND THE GATE

    Four new synthetic tests in the vendored `route-scanner.test.ts` (6 -> 10 per app)
    and two new route-level tests in every app's `route-auth.test.ts`. Family suite
    879 -> 915; typecheck covers the vendored files as before.

      Mutant A — `READ_NAME` replaced by a pattern that matches nothing: all six apps
        fail 1 test each, the read floor, and the new rule passes quietly. That is
        precisely the failure the floor is for, and the only way to demonstrate that
        the rule is not vacuous-by-construction.
      Mutant B — the new rule's `authSites` swapped for the presence rule's
        raw-text `AUTH_NAME.test(body)`: 1 failure in each app, the vendored
        "a check that exists only inside a string is not a check". No real route fails,
        because no route today puts a check's name in a string and nothing else.
      Mutant C — the `listed` map ignored: 2 failures each, the vendored excuse-list
        test and the app's own route-level rule. Every app's offenders are the eight
        handlers on `PUBLIC`, so this is the mutant that proves the rule is being
        satisfied by a reviewed list and not by an absence of anonymous readers.
      Mutant D — `unauthenticatedReaders` made to return nothing: 3 failures, all three
        in the vendored file, zero in the real routes. The lesson §8.41 already
        recorded, measured again on a different rule: a guard that only reads real
        handlers cannot see its own removal.

    Gate: `bash shared/check-all.sh` — 18 steps, 0 failed, 80s. guards drift 48 (byte
    identity is unchanged in count; three files per app still), auth drift 288, docs 98,
    suites 78, queues 67, UI 6. No app's behaviour changed, so no README copy changed —
    `shared/README.md` grew the §8.42 paragraphs.

8.42.6 STILL OWED, RECORDED RATHER THAN FIXED

    Authenticated-but-unscoped reads are not guarded (8.42.3: 32 measured, 26
    unjudgeable by any argument-text rule, 6 checked by hand, 1 of the 32 a defect in
    abetflow, and invisible to that count for the reason given). The mechanism that
    would judge them is a data-flow pass over `where`
    variables, which is a different instrument than this scanner and should not be
    improvised inside it. #37's cross-tenant probe remains the cheaper way to the same
    answer: put two tenants' rows in a copy and see which ones come back.

    The probe script is gone again. `n38-probe.mts` was deleted after the run per the
    house pattern; its output is at `/tmp/abectorial/n38-abetflow-probe.txt`, which is
    `/tmp`, which is #40's complaint and now has a third instance.

    The read rule is handler-level, so it will not notice a check moving after the
    response it is supposed to protect. `unguardedReads` is exported and pinned by a
    synthetic test precisely so that the day someone wants the site-level rule, the
    instrument and the reason it was declined are both in the file.

    The route-level delegation tripwire is still in two apps, not six (#39), and the
    positional schema-arg scanner with it.

    #38 — closed by this section. #75 is new, from the probe rather than the rule.
    The standing list (#17, #28, #32, #37, #39, #40, #42, #43, #45, #47, #48, #49, #51,
    #56, #63, #65, #71, #74) is unchanged except that #38 leaves it.

8.43 CROSS-TENANT WRITE PROBE: WHAT A RUNNING ROUTE ANSWERS, NOT WHAT THE SOURCE SAYS
     (2026-10-08, #37; shared/verify-cross-tenant.py, committed)

8.43.1 THE QUESTION A STATIC GUARD CANNOT ASK

    Every auth rule this family has — the domination rule, the delegation walk,
    §8.42's handler-level read rule — reads `src/app/**/route.ts` as text. #19's bug
    was the case that text cannot settle: abetagent's `PATCH /api/agents/:id`
    contained `assertOwns`, two conditionals deep, while writing at the handler's own
    level. The rule of that day was a presence rule and it passed the bug. A
    domination rule refuses it now, and still the difference between "the source
    contains a check that dominates the write" and "a request with no Authorization
    header is refused by the process that is serving traffic" is a difference only an
    HTTP request can make. Nothing in the suite sent one.

    So this round sends one per app, and the six choices are the id-addressed write
    each app's README leads with: `PATCH /api/campaigns/:id`, `/api/leads/:id`,
    `/api/agents/:id`, `/api/workflows/:id`, `/api/metrics/:id`, `/api/webhooks/:id`.

8.43.2 NINE ASSERTIONS, EACH A DIFFERENT QUESTION

    Against a copy of that app's `prisma/dev.db` with two workspaces seeded — a victim
    with one row, an attacker with a valid key and no rows:

      1. the dev server came up (a precondition, kept as an assertion so a timeout is
         reported rather than looked like a passing empty run)
      2. the server is answering from *this copy*: the owner's GET must return the id
         this script inserted
      3. the write with no credential -> 401
      4. the write of the victim's row with the attacker's own valid key -> 404
      5. the victim's row, read out of the copy afterwards, still holds its own value
      6. a foreign row and a row that does not exist answer identically -> 404
      7. the owner's identical request -> 2xx, and the column actually changed
      8. the same foreign row read with the attacker's key -> 404
      9. the live `prisma/dev.db` checksum is what it was before any of this

    2 is the load-bearing one and it is not a liveness check. `/api/health` answers the
    same for a server on the copy and a server on the real database; a probe driven
    against somebody else's `next dev` would report nine confident assertions about
    rows it never inserted. 4 and 5 are separate because a 200 that answers "not found"
    while writing anyway passes either one alone. 6 is what a probe must not be able to
    use to enumerate somebody else's ids. 7 is the assertion that turns every refusal
    above from a green light into evidence — without it, "the anonymous request was
    refused" is equally true of a route nobody wired up.

8.43.3 WHY IT RUNS FROM A SANDBOX TREE, MEASURED BEFORE IT WAS DESIGNED

    The first run refused on its own guard: `abetagent: port 3400 already answers —
    refusing to guess which database it is on`, exit 2, copy removed. Investigation
    found a detached `next dev -p 3400` (pid 1161887) running since 2026-10-03 on the
    app's own `prisma/dev.db` — `DATABASE_URL` absent from its environment, so the
    `.env` default; five days of log containing nothing but `SELECT 1` health pings.
    Whether it is an orphan of this campaign or somebody's working server is not
    knowable from here, and the probe is not the place to guess: it was left running.

    Taking a different port turned out not to be sufficient, and that is a fact about
    Next 16 rather than about this script: the dev server takes an exclusive lock on
    `.next/dev/lock`, and a second `next dev` on the same project directory exits with
    "Another next dev server is already running ... Run kill 1161887 to stop it." The
    lock is per-directory, not per-port, so a probe that shares a tree with a running
    server cannot start at all — and a probe that deletes that lock file would be
    deleting another process's claim on it.

    The harness therefore builds its own tree: the source copied, `node_modules`
    hardlinked, `.next` and `node_modules/.cache` excluded from the link set so the
    copy's build artifacts are its own and nothing is written through a link. It lives
    on `/srv/work/.probe-trees` and not in `/tmp` for a reason worth keeping: `/tmp` on
    this box is a 1.9 GB tmpfs on a different filesystem from `/srv`, so a hardlink
    tree there becomes a 850 MB copy into RAM. Measured cost: 0.8 s to build, first
    health answer ~12 s, tree deleted in the same `finally` that kills the process
    group. All six apps' live `dev.db` checksums after the runs are byte-for-byte the
    ones printed at their start.

8.43.4 THE RESULT: 54 ASSERTIONS, AND THE ONE THAT WENT RED WAS THE HARNESS'S

    leadgenius, leadworks, abetagent, abetflow, abetapi: 9/9 each. abetanalytics:
    8/9 on the first run, red on assertion 7 — the owner's `PATCH {name}` came back
    `422 {"error":"count needs an event"}`. The app was right and the seed was wrong:
    the route validates the *merged* config (§8.16's design), and this script had
    inserted a `count` metric with `config = {}`, which is not a valid metric. Seeding
    `{"event":"n37.signup"}` instead: 9/9.

    Two things that red line is worth recording, precisely because it was not a defect.
    It is evidence that assertion 7 does work: it turned a green-looking run red rather
    than letting six refusals stand on a write path that had never been exercised. And
    it is evidence that the refusals were never in doubt — 3, 4, 5, 6 and 8 were green
    on both runs, which is what §8.42's static rule predicted behaviourally.

    So: no cross-tenant write in any of the six, on the routes where the family's own
    documentation invites a caller. The static guard and the running app now agree,
    which is the point of the round rather than a surprise.

8.43.5 MUTANTS: WHICH ASSERTION EARNS WHICH REFUSAL

    Mutants were run against throwaway copies of the harness in `/tmp`; the committed
    file was never the one being broken.

      A — `copy_db` pointed at the app's own `prisma/dev.db`: the run printed
          `refusing — DATABASE_URL is not a scratch copy` and exited 2 before the
          copyfile, before the seed, before any server started; the live file's
          checksum was unchanged afterwards. §8.12's rule is load-bearing.
      B — the probed path replaced by one the app does not serve
          (`PATCH /api/no-such-route/:id`, abetagent): 2 failures — assertion 3
          (a no-credential request answers 404, not 401) and assertion 7 (the owner
          gets Next's HTML 404 page). The other four security assertions stayed green:
          attacker's key -> 404, victim row unchanged, missing id -> 404, foreign read
          -> 404. That is the finding, and it is not about this app: four of the six
          answers a probe would book as "the boundary held" are what a *missing route*
          answers too. A probe without 3 and 7 is a probe that reports 404s as security.
      C — the port-occupied refusal, measured live in §8.43.3 rather than simulated.
      D — the identity proof's value is also measured rather than asserted: the two
          runs of this harness that could not prove which database was answering
          (C, and A's guard firing) stopped at exit 2 instead of reporting assertions.

8.43.6 STILL OWED, RECORDED RATHER THAN FIXED

    Coverage: 6 routes out of 181 handlers, one table per app. The probe is a shape to
    replicate per write route, not a family-wide sweep, and it says so in its own
    docstring. Not covered, and not coverable this way: collection `POST`s, `DELETE`s,
    and every write that is not id-addressed — which includes abetflow's inbound
    webhook, the route #75 is about, whose only input is a path that belongs to nobody.
    An `UPDATE`-shaped probe cannot see a global-namespace defect, and #75 stays open
    on its own terms.

    The box still runs a server this campaign cannot account for: an unowned
    `next dev` on abetagent's port, on abetagent's real `dev.db`, holding its lock.
    Killing it is a one-command repair, and the probe no longer needs it — but the
    family's README advertises 3400 and whatever is answering there answers for the
    live database, which is what `check-live.sh` (#47) will report as a passing port.
    That is the user's call, not a cleanup to take quietly.

    `verify-cross-tenant.py` is deliberately not in `shared/check-all.sh`: §8.20.5's
    rule is that a gate needing servers goes unsipped, and this needs six sequentially
    on 3.8 GB. When #40's console is re-authored its registry entry points at
    `shared/verify-cross-tenant.py` — a committed path, off the tmpfs, which is what
    `check-suites.sh` exists to keep true. That is the third harness this ledger has
    had to re-describe from prose because its program lived in `/tmp`; this one is in
    version control, so §8.42's "the probe script is gone again" does not repeat.

    #37 — closed by this section.

8.44 ONE READER FOR THE ARGUMENT AXIS: WHAT "FIVE APPS HAD A REGEX" ACTUALLY MEANT
     (2026-10-08, #39; shared/tests/route-handlers.ts + route-scanner.test.ts, vendored)

8.44.1 THE PREMISE WAS WRONG, AND OPENING THE FILES IS WHAT FOUND IT

    #39 was written as "port leadgenius' positional schema-arg scanner to the five other
    `route-validation.test.ts` copies" — the story being that one app had outgrown its
    regex (§8.19.4) and five still had it. `git show HEAD:tests/unit/route-validation.test.ts`
    in each of the five says something else. There were four different readers and one
    absence:

      leadworks      /validate(?:Body|Items)\s*\(\s*[^,()]+,\s*([A-Za-z_{][\w{]*)/
      abetagent      /validateBody\s*\(\s*[^,()]+,\s*([A-Za-z_{][\w{]*)/   (no Items alternative)
      abetflow       the same, copied
      abetapi        the same, copied
      abetanalytics  no inline-schema rule at all — the file has `declared` built out of
                     fields.ts and a delegation tripwire, and nothing that reads a call
      leadgenius     the balancing reader §8.19.4 left behind

    So "five copies of one regex" was three copies of a narrower one, one of a wider one,
    and one app where the rule was missing outright. A round that had shipped on the
    premise would have ported to four files and announced abetanalytics as covered.

8.44.2 WHAT EACH READER COULD NOT REACH, MEASURED CALL BY CALL

    The comparison is position-anchored: for every real `validateBody`/`validateItems`
    call in every route file, run the app's own pre-port pattern anchored at that call's
    name and ask what it returned. `blind` = the pattern does not match there at all;
    `wrong` = it matches and names something other than the schema argument.

      app            calls  old saw  blind  wrong
      leadgenius        21       17      3      1
      leadworks         14       14      0      0
      abetagent          7        7      0      0
      abetflow           7        7      0      0
      abetapi            9        9      0      0
      abetanalytics      8        0      8      0

    leadgenius is the app that earned the fix and it still shows the two original
    failures: three generic calls (`validateItems<EmailEvent>(…)`, `<SequenceStepInput>`,
    `<InboundMessage>`) that the pattern cannot see because `<` sits where it expects `(`,
    and the whatsapp webhook's multi-line call, where `[^,()]+` stops at the first comma
    *inside the object literal* and returns `from` as a table name. `from` is not a
    table, is not in fields.ts, and the rule would have reported it as an undeclared one
    — a wrong answer about a call it could not read.

    abetanalytics' 8 are blind by absence rather than by regex: nothing read them.

    The four clean rows are the honest result of this round and the one worth arguing
    with: **the port changes no verdict in four of the six apps.** They agree with the
    shipped reader because their call shapes are the easy ones, and the shape counts say
    so — `validateBody`/`validateItems` call sites per app, in the order leadgenius,
    leadworks, abetagent, abetflow, abetanalytics, abetapi: 18/3, 14/0, 7/0, 7/0, 8/0,
    9/0. abetagent, abetflow and abetapi had a `validateBody`-only pattern and have no
    `validateItems` call to miss; leadworks had the combined pattern and no generic to
    trip on. The readers were adequate by accident of style, not by construction: the day
    a route in any of the five takes the array form, that call site reads as nothing and
    is reported as nothing.

8.44.3 WHAT SHIPPED

    `schemaArgs(src)` — the balancing reader, argument-by-position, `<unread>` for a call
    whose table is not there — moved to the canonical scanner with the two helpers it
    needs (`blankComments`, which replaces comments with spaces so every offset survives
    because prose must not read as code *and* a report must still point at the right
    line; `splitTopLevel`/`readArgs`, kept private). `countCodeMentions`, which leadgenius
    had already grown for the orphan rule, went with it, and `unresolvedTailCalls` — the
    delegation tripwire abetanalytics wrote for itself — went as a function that takes
    the file *reader* rather than a directory, so the vendored test can hand it a fixture
    instead of scanning whichever app runs it.

    All six `route-validation.test.ts` files now import the reader and keep only their
    own assertion lists, and abetanalytics gained the inline-schema rule it never had
    (`FIELDS` imported for the declared set), which is the only place this round made a
    real route newly subject to a rule: 8 calls that used to be unread.

8.44.4 MUTANTS: WHERE A BLINDING IS INVISIBLE TO THE APPS

    Each mutant was applied to the canonical scanner, `sync-guards.sh`-ed to all six, and
    scored by the three route suites; the committed file was restored and the drift check
    re-run (48/48) after every one.

      M1 `unresolvedTailCalls` returns []              6/6 apps, route-scanner only
      M2 `schemaArgs` back to the pre-port regex       6/6 route-scanner, + leadgenius'
                                                       route-validation rule
      M3 an unreadable call is skipped, not `<unread>`  6/6 apps, route-scanner only
      M4 `blankComments` returns its input             6/6 apps, route-scanner only
      M5 `countCodeMentions` counts the declaration    0/6 apps — nothing failed at all

    M2 is the port's own evidence: the old reader is caught by the vendored shape test in
    all six and by a *real* rule in exactly the one app that has the shapes. Four of the
    five ports are, today, insurance.

    M5 is why §8.44.3's "vendored mechanism, local assertion" split needed a third thing.
    Subtracting the declaration is what makes the orphan rule able to see an orphan: a
    table declared in fields.ts is by definition mentioned there, so counting the
    declaration turns `countCodeMentions(...) === 0` into a condition no table can ever
    satisfy. Blinding it therefore changes no verdict in any app — every app's orphan
    assertion passes both before and after — and no test in the family noticed. That is
    the failure mode this whole campaign keeps hitting: a guard that goes quiet rather
    than wrong. Fixed the same way as the rest of the axis, by a fixture (`FIELDS_FIXTURE`
    in `route-scanner.test.ts`) whose only contents are a declaration, a doc comment
    naming it, and one real use — three cases that separate *used* from *declared* and
    from *written about*. M5 re-measured against it: 6/6 fail.

    M1, M3 and M4 land only in `route-scanner.test.ts` for the reason that file's header
    already gives: the app's own routes have no hidden tail calls (0 of 181 handlers),
    no unread calls and no prose-shaped calls, so a per-app assertion against them is
    non-vacuous only to the extent the app is. The synthetic case is the same claim with
    no route to wait for.

8.44.5 NUMBERS AFTER

    Three tests added to the canonical scanner's file — the `schemaArgs` shape test,
    the `unresolvedTailCalls` fixture and the `countCodeMentions` fixture, the last one
    because M5 proved nothing else pinned it — taking `route-scanner.test.ts` from 10
    tests to 13 in all six copies. Each
    of five apps gains one test in its own `route-validation.test.ts` (the four others:
    the tripwire, which four of the five had never run; abetanalytics: the inline-schema
    rule, which no file in that app had). leadgenius is net zero there — it handed its
    local copy of the shape test to the vendored file and got the tripwire back. Family
    total 915 -> 938, gate 18/18, drift 48/48.

    Against the routes themselves: 181 handlers, 66 validate calls read positionally
    across the six apps, and every counter at zero — 0 inline schemas, 0 `<unread>`,
    0 tables named that fields.ts does not declare, 0 orphan tables, 0 unresolved tail
    calls. The round's whole measurable effect is therefore on what the guards would do
    about a shape none of the apps has yet, which is the honest thing to say about a
    port that changes no verdict, and the reason §8.44.4's mutants are the result rather
    than the test count.

    Commits: shared `554a862`, leadgenius `ad5a50c`, leadworks `97f63f0`, abetagent
    `593b7b7`, abetflow `c869494`, abetanalytics `ba6219d`, abetapi `3e37a46`. Gate 18/18
    before each; nothing pushed (#42 — the ledger itself is still outside version
    control, which is why these seven hashes exist only in this file).

    #39 — closed by this section. The insurance framing is worth keeping rather than
    apologising for: three apps are one `validateItems(…)` away from a rule that reads
    nothing, and that is now a failing test rather than a fact about style. What this
    round did *not* do is widen what the rules ask — the reader still answers "which
    table", not "does this handler's write need one it never calls"; #28 (the six drifted
    `validate.ts` copies) is where that question lives.

8.45 EGRESS POLICY: THE ADDRESS IS THE QUESTION, NOT THE SYNTAX
     (2026-10-09, #32; shared/auth/egress.ts + shared/tests/egress{,-sites}.test.ts, vendored)

8.45.1 THE MEASURE THAT CAME FIRST, AND WHAT IT SAID

    #32 was written as "refuse internal addresses at every server-side request site",
    which presupposes knowing the sites. `shared/tests/egress-sites.test.ts` now counts
    them, and run against each app's parent commit it says: 27 fetch-shaped call sites
    across the six `src/**` trees, of which 7 were a caller-named address going straight
    onto the wire.

      app            total  browser  literal  env  caller-named (before)
      leadgenius         6        1        2    2                    1   webhooks.ts:11
      leadworks          3        1        0    2                    0
      abetagent          5        1        0    2                    2   tools.ts:50, :85
      abetflow           4        1        0    2                    1   executor.ts:129
      abetanalytics      4        1        0    2                    1   alerts.ts:43
      abetapi            5        1        0    2                    2   dispatch.ts:83, sso.ts:33
      family            27        6        2   12                    7

    Three things that table says, none of them obvious from the task title. The 20 sites
    that need no policy are the reason the rule is per call site and not per app: a
    host written into `channels/linkedin.ts` is not a decision a caller can move, and a
    host read from `.env` by `llm.ts` is an operator's decision about infrastructure,
    which is a different kind of thing from a visitor's. leadworks has zero
    caller-named sites — its outbound webhook rows are inert (#17) — so it gets the
    module and a declaration of non-use, not an import invented to satisfy a checker.
    And the seventh site was nearly missed by the guard itself: abetflow's executor
    requests through `const doFetch = fetchFn ?? fetch`, so a pattern on the bare word
    `fetch` walked straight past the one site whose address is assembled out of run
    input, which is the most attacker-shaped address in the family.

    The policy question went to the user before any of it was written, and the answer
    is what shipped: deny private ranges by default, one environment variable
    (`ABET_ALLOW_PRIVATE_TARGETS`) as an escape hatch, dev-only and **logged**.

8.45.2 WHAT SHIPPED

    `shared/auth/egress.ts`, vendored to `src/lib/egress.ts` in all six.
    `isInternalAddress` is the whole policy and is exported apart from the classifier
    because it needs no network to test: 0/8, 127/8, 10/8, 172.16/12, 192.168/16,
    169.254/16 (which is where `169.254.169.254` lives), CGNAT 100.64/10, 198.18.0.0/15,
    192.0.0/24; v6 `::1`, `::`, fc00::/7, fe80::/10 with the `%eth0` scope id stripped
    before judging, and a `::ffff:`-mapped v4 literal judged as the v4 it is.
    `classifyTarget` refuses a non-http(s) scheme, userinfo in the authority, an
    unparseable URL, and an unresolvable name — DNS failure is a refusal, never a pass,
    because the alternative is that a network outage becomes "we could not tell, so we
    sent it" — and judges *every* address a name answers with, not the first, so one
    public answer and one `127.0.0.1` is a way through rather than a coin flip.

    The check runs twice, deliberately. At the boundary that stores the address, so a
    caller gets an actionable 4xx with the reason in it; and at the moment of sending,
    because a row can predate the policy and a name that resolved publicly last week
    can resolve to the metadata address today. The second is why the first is not
    enough, and the same function does both.

    Refusal statuses: 422 in abetapi and abetanalytics, 400 in leadgenius and abetagent.
    That split is not this policy's choice — it is `validate.ts` being forked six ways
    (#28), where two apps' `fail()` envelope predates the other two's field problem. It
    is recorded here because the egress refusal is the first thing to make the
    disagreement visible from outside. abetapi routes its refusals through the same
    `refused()` wrapper that logs a 401 or a 429, so a refusal reaches `RequestLog`
    (#33's lesson, applied to a new status).

    Two apps' boundaries are single because their shape is single. abetflow checks at
    the send only: `renderTemplate` assembles the URL from run input, so the stored
    definition is not what gets connected to, and refusing at the write would check a
    string nobody asked for. abetagent checks the `http_fetch` tool's own URL, which is
    the one address in the family that never passed through a validated write at all —
    it comes from the model's tool arguments. abetapi checks both webhook targets and
    SSO `metadataUrl`, the second named surface in #32, which is a *GET* whose response
    the app then parses.

    A refusal is terminal. `AttemptOutcome.refused` is new in abetapi's retry engine
    precisely because `statusCode === null` already means "nobody answered, ask again",
    and a refusal is the opposite — nobody was asked. Untagged it would re-run the same
    policy decision eight times across a day of backoff and report it to the operator as
    the endpoint being unhealthy. `nextStatus` parks the delivery `DEAD` on the first
    refusal and `describeFailure` prefixes "refused:", which is how a dashboard can tell
    our decision from their outage.

8.45.3 THE GUARD, AND THE FOUR WAYS A SITE IS LEGAL

    `egress-sites.test.ts` is vendored next to the scanner it reads through, for the
    scanner's own reason: a guard whose failure mode is silence is the worst possible
    file to have six copies of. It classifies every fetch-shaped call as `client` (a
    `"use client"` component — the browser makes that request, from the visitor's own
    machine, which was never this server's problem), `literal` (host fixed in the
    source), `env` (the address's root identifier is declared from `process.env` in the
    same file, or is a function whose body reads it), or `policy` (an
    `assertAllowedTarget`/`classifyTarget` call within the 25 lines above the request
    that a textual block can show). Anything else fails, naming file, line and the
    argument text. After this round: 7 `policy`, 12 `env`, 6 `client`, 2 `literal`,
    0 `UNPROTECTED`.

    Its second test counts the categories rather than trusting them, so a site that
    quietly moves from `env` to `policy` — an LLM base URL suddenly routed through a
    request — changes the shape of the table and fails. And because a `policy` site is
    evidence that the module is reachable from that app's own source, the test asserts
    `src/lib/egress.ts` exists and exports `assertAllowedTarget` whenever the count is
    above zero. `check-guards-drift.sh` carries both files to byte identity plus those
    two import/ existence insistences, 30 new checks, 48 -> 78.

8.45.4 WHAT THIS ROUND BROKE IN ITS OWN TOOLS, AND IN THE ORDER IT FOUND THEM

    `blankComments` was not string-aware. The naive `\/\/[^\n]*` is correct about code
    and wrong about the family: a URL contains `//`, so the line
    `const res = await fetch("https://api.test/x", {method:"GET"})` blanks from the `//`
    onward — erasing the very call a reader is looking for. Measured against leadgenius'
    real source, that mutant reads its two `literal` sites as
    `fetch(<unclosed call>)` and reports them `UNPROTECTED`: the guard accusing the app
    of a hole that was the guard's own. Fixed with a character scanner that treats `"`,
    `'` and `` ` `` as regions and preserves offsets, pinned by a new synthetic test in
    all six. It failed loudly for leadgenius and silently everywhere else — the silent
    direction is a real `prisma.x.create(...)` written after a URL on the same line,
    which vanishes from `writeSites` and therefore from the auth rules that read them.

    `check-auth-drift.sh`'s `importers_of` was wrong too, and in the loud direction,
    which is why it was found at all. Its second grep — relative imports of a sibling
    module — searched `$ROOT/$app/$tail`, a path that does not exist (`src/lib` is not
    `$app/lib`), so it counted zero importers for any module imported as `./egress`.
    abetflow failed with "vendored and imported by nothing" while its `executor.ts`
    imported it two lines up. Reverted as a mutant it reproduces that failure exactly
    (299/1). The same check was *right* about leadworks, and the two looked identical
    from the output: the answer was to fix the grep and to give the genuine non-use a
    declaration with a premise that expires, not an exception that does not. Creating
    `leadworks/src/lib/webhooks.ts` — which is what #17 would do — now turns the
    declaration stale and fails the check, measured rather than asserted.

    And this section's own commit: the canonical module's docstring said "Five sites
    were measured" above a list of seven, called the write-boundary refusal "a 422" in
    two apps that answer 400, and said "the property all six need" of five. A comment
    in a vendored file is a claim about six trees, and the drift check keeps the bytes
    identical without reading a word of what they say.

8.45.5 THE AFTER-PROBE, AND THE DEFECT ONLY IT FOUND

    `shared/verify-egress.py` is the eighth harness and the second that starts its own
    server. It runs against abetapi only, and says so in its own docstring: the other
    three apps with a write boundary authenticate by session cookie or query key, and
    an anonymous probe pointed at them would book their 401s as the egress policy
    holding — §8.43.5's mutant B, in the wild. abetapi takes a bearer key, so the probe
    can be authorized as a real workspace and still be testing the address rule. Its
    primitives (`copy_db`, the port refusal, the sandbox tree, the checksum proof) are
    imported from `verify-cross-tenant.py` rather than re-derived, because those are the
    load-bearing guarantees and a second implementation of them is a second thing to
    get wrong.

    Nine assertions: the server came up; a legal public address is accepted and reads
    back from *this copy*; the write boundary refuses `127.0.0.1:6379` and
    `169.254.169.254`; a refusal writes nothing; a pre-policy internal row is parked
    `DEAD` on the first send with a `globalThis.fetch` spy showing the socket never
    opened; the hatch lets the same loopback address through; the hatch *logs* it; and
    the live `prisma/dev.db` was never opened.

    The first run was 6/9 and three of those red lines were the harness's own and one
