  #65" in 8.59.4; the ordinary failure mode (Redis briefly unavailable,
  provider retries) is what this round solves.

8.59.3 THE GUARD

  `tests/unit/webhook-undo.test.ts`, thirteen assertions across five suites.
  Source-reading, same style as route-auth, route-validation, ai-claim,
  webhook-sync and webhook-namespace.

    - module (4): export shape, activity.delete by id, allSettled wrapping,
      rejected arm logs.
    - per route × 3 (9): import pair; the undo awaited *inside* the
      QueueUnavailableError catch, not outside it; activity.id passed as the
      first argument.
    - email ordering (2): priorEnrollment read before the enrollment flip;
      priorSentMessageStatus captured before the sentMessage flip; both
      reverts pushed onto the array handed to the undo; the reverts array is
      initialised outside the branch so OPENED/CLICKED still undo the row.

  Non-vacuous proof, two mutants on the linkedin route (restored between
  each): drop the `await undoInboundSideEffect(activity.id, […])` block, the
  "calls undo on the catch" and "passes activity.id" assertions both fire —
  the two arms reading the same shape; drop the `priorEnrollment` read from
  email and the "read before flipping" ordering assertion fires alone.
  Suite returned to 238/0 on restore.

8.59.4 NUMBERS AFTER

  leadgenius test count — 221 → 238 (thirteen new assertions in one new
  file). Rest of the family unchanged; the compensation shape is
  leadgenius-only (only leadgenius has Activity + `recordInboundActivity`).
  Gate 20/0 in ~82s.

8.59.5 STILL OWED

  A transactional outbox — one Prisma transaction writes the Activity row and
  an `OutboxJob` row, and a poller drains OutboxJob into queues — would close
  both the concurrent-delivery race and the "compensation itself failed"
  partial-state above. #65 is filed closed against the ordinary failure; the
  outbox shape stays open for a heavier round.

8.60 ENRICHMENT DROPS THE LINKEDIN STUB (#56)

  #56 was filed as "leadgenius enrichment has no real providers behind its
  branches" and closed the same way the phone branch was closed in §8.34's
  follow-up work — not by inventing a provider, but by removing the promise of
  one. `findLinkedIn` returned "" unconditionally, `enrichContact` wrote it
  into `results.linkedinUrl` and then into `Contact.linkedinUrl`, and the
  `if (results.linkedinUrl)` arm in the update meant an empty string never
  actually landed — but the function ran on every linkedin-URL-less contact
  and the branch was a shape the family had already ruled against for phone.

8.60.1 WHAT CHANGED

  `linkedinUrl` is gone from `EnrichmentResult`, from the branch in
  `enrichContact`, from the spread in the `prisma.contact.update`, and the
  `findLinkedIn` function itself is deleted. `emailVerifier` still lives
  behind a null gate (§8.34's design), so `guessEmail` and its
  `getCompanyDomain` clearbit call stay as pre-wired scaffolding for a
  provider that has not landed — same shape as before this round. Only the
  dead LinkedIn arm was removed, and the docstring on `hasEnrichableGap` now
  points at the phone precedent instead of writing a sentence about a stub.

  `Contact.linkedinUrl` is still a real column. Nothing about the storage
  contract changed: the URL can be set from the manual-edit route or from a
  bulk import, and enrichment is simply not one of the things that fills it.
  The `hasEnrichableGap` predicate never named the column, so removing the
  branch costs no reachability — an "enrich me" call for a contact with a
  gap in the two shapes the predicate still considers (no company + email,
  or no email + verifier present) works exactly as before.

8.60.2 THE GUARD

  `queue.test.ts` already had the arm:
  `assert.doesNotMatch(enrichmentModule, /lookupPhone|bulkEnrichContacts|results\.phone/)`.
  #56 extends the same regex to forbid the linkedin helpers too:
  `findLinkedIn | results\.linkedinUrl | linkedinUrl:\s*results`.

  The choice to widen the existing assertion rather than write a new file is
  deliberate — the rule is the same ("a helper that returns "" and the
  branch that calls it are both gone") and splitting it across two files
  would only make a future reader ask which arm covers which provider. One
  assertion, one rule.

  Non-vacuous proof: re-adding `findLinkedIn` or the `results.linkedinUrl =
  await findLinkedIn(…)` branch to enrichment.ts is the only mutation that
  trips this guard, and the assertion fires on the first one. Adding the
  function while leaving the branch out still trips on the function name.

8.60.3 NUMBERS AFTER

  Test count unchanged (238), because the assertion was widened in place
  rather than added. leadgenius typecheck clean. Gate 20/0 in ~76s.

8.60.4 STILL OWED

  A real provider (a Clearbit-style lookup for linkedinUrl, an SMTP verifier
  for email) is still owed. The docstring on `emailVerifier` says the gate
  is the right place for it, and `hasEnrichableGap` names the shape. When
  #56 grows that half, both the verifier and the LinkedIn provider turn on
  through the same construction.

8.61 LEADWORKS OUTBOUND WEBHOOKS, BUILT (#17)

  #17 was filed as "leadworks outbound webhooks are inert — build or
  remove". The row existed (`Webhook` at `prisma/schema.prisma:296`),
  `api/webhooks/route.ts` let a workspace write one with a `crypto.random
  Bytes(32)` secret, and nothing in the app ever read the table back. The
  drift check declared leadworks' egress module as present-but-unused
  under the explicit premise "there is no sender" (§8.44's NON_USE list);
  that sentence is now the file's own reason for existing.

  The choice was "build," not "remove". A Webhook table whose only
  mutator is a UI that offers no sender is a lie to the operator: the
  row looks configured, the events string reads as if the workspace
  opted in, and nothing happens. Removing the table is a schema
  migration and a UI deletion; wiring a sender is one module and one
  call site, and the sender is exactly what #32's egress policy has been
  waiting for leadworks to need.

8.61.1 WHAT CHANGED

  `src/lib/webhooks.ts` is a new module. `dispatchWebhookEvent
  (workspaceId, event, data)` reads the workspace's ACTIVE hooks, filters
  by the comma-separated `events` column, and hands each match to an
  inner `deliver(hookId, rawUrl, secret, payload)`. `deliver` builds the
  JSON body once, computes an HMAC-SHA256 hex signature over those exact
  bytes, and then — critically — calls `assertAllowedTarget(rawUrl,
  "url")` from the vendored `./egress` module immediately above the
  `fetch`, in the same block. That is the shape the egress-sites guard
  reads as "policy" (§8.44's four legal arms), and the ordering matters:
  a check hoisted into the loop head, or one that runs only at the write
  boundary, is what §8.44 measured as present-but-not-covering. A row
  that predates the egress policy still hits the check when the
  dispatcher reads it back, and DNS-rebinding between the two does not
  help an attacker, because both checks resolve.

  The signature is delivered as `X-Leadworks-Signature`, the event name
  as `X-Leadworks-Event`, and the User-Agent as `Leadworks-Webhook/1.0`.
  A `signal: AbortSignal.timeout(5000)` bounds every fetch, because the
  dispatcher is awaited by an API route and an unbounded delivery is an
  unbounded request — same reason §8.34's `emailVerifier` null gate
  refuses to hand a hung verifier the request.

  Failure handling is `Promise.allSettled` over the per-hook promises,
  each of which swallows its own errors into a `console.error`. A
  webhook outage is not a 500 the API caller has to reason about. That
  is a *choice*, not an oversight, and §8.61.4 is the follow-up it owes.

  `POST /api/leads` is the first and only wired caller so far. After
  `prisma.lead.create` returns the row, the route `await`s
  `dispatchWebhookEvent(workspaceId, "lead.created", { lead })` and
  then responds 201 with the lead unchanged. Every other row-mutating
  route in leadworks is untouched; the dispatcher is available and no
  other event name is emitted yet.

  `shared/check-auth-drift.sh` NON_USE loses the
  `leadworks|egress.ts|absent|src/lib/webhooks.ts` entry. The comment
  that used to explain why the file was declared is rewritten to say the
  premise has closed: `webhooks.ts` now imports `assertAllowedTarget`
  from `./egress`, so `egress.ts` has a real src/* importer in leadworks
  and #44's byte check would fail the "declared non-use" for claiming
  the module is unused. The list is empty again, and the comment says
  what a fresh exception has to look like so this pattern repeats
  honestly.

8.61.2 THE GUARD

  `tests/unit/webhook-dispatch.test.ts` is new. Nine assertions, two
  suites. The sender suite pins the module's shape — the import pair,
  the isActive + events filter, the ordering of the egress check
  against the fetch, the HMAC signature construction and its header
  name, the timeout, and the allSettled + catch that keeps a bad hook
  from failing the API caller. The wire-up suite pins that
  POST /api/leads actually imports and calls the dispatcher with the
  `"lead.created"` string, and that the dispatch sits at a byte offset
  after `prisma.lead.create` (a caller that sees `lead.created` must be
  seeing a row that exists).

  Ordering is asserted with byte offsets inside `deliver`, not line
  numbers, for the same reason §8.26's route-scanner asserts offsets
  rather than shapes: a reformat is not a re-ordering, and the rule is
  about which of two statements runs first.

8.61.3 NUMBERS AFTER

  Non-vacuous proof, three mutants, each trips a distinct arm:

    Mutant 1 — remove `await assertAllowedTarget(rawUrl, "url");` from
      webhooks.ts. The sender suite's "egress check above fetch" arm
      fails (guardAt is -1); 8 pass / 1 fail.
    Mutant 2 — remove the `await dispatchWebhookEvent(...)` line from
      POST /api/leads. The wire-up suite's "dispatches lead.created
      after row commits" arm fails (dispatchAt is -1); 8 pass / 1 fail.
    Mutant 3 — move `await assertAllowedTarget` to *below* the fetch.
      The ordering arm's `guardAt < fetchAt` assertion fails; 8 pass /
      1 fail.

  Restored, the guard reports 9/9. Full family gate: 20 steps, 20
  passed, 0 failed in 78s. Leadworks test count moves from 183 to 192
  (the nine new arms), and auth drift stays at 300 pass because the
  retired NON_USE entry is now the empty list.

8.61.4 STILL OWED

  Delivery is inline and best-effort. A receiver that is down at the
  moment a lead is created gets nothing, and there is no retry table,
  no dead-letter, and no visibility into "did the operator's hook fire
  for this lead" beyond the console.error that never reaches them. The
  row's `events` string is still what the operator sees; the wire now
  reads that string instead of ignoring it. When the family wants a
  `WebhookDelivery` table (or a `webhook` queue alongside
  scrape/enrich/export/dedupe/validate/scheduled in `src/lib/queue.ts`),
  the seam is `dispatchWebhookEvent`'s `Promise.allSettled` — swap the
  map for an enqueue and the sender becomes a producer. That is
  deliberately not built here: #17's gap was "no sender exists", and a
  retry system built on top of nothing is the same class of premature
  abstraction §8.60 refused to add back to `enrichment.ts`.

  Only `lead.created` is dispatched. `lead.updated`, `lead.converted`,
  `list.reassigned` are the obvious candidates but each is a design
  question about who owns the transition (route? worker? a change
  trigger?); a second event name should be a decision, not a copy.

  HMAC is one scheme. Some receivers want a timestamp in the signed
  string to make replays bounded, and a small number want an asymmetric
  signature. The header names and the hex encoding are the current
  stable contract; a scheme negotiation belongs with the receiver, not
  with the sender.

8.62 DB 0 ORPHAN DECISION, AND THE README THAT HAD NOT CAUGHT UP (#51)

  #51 was filed as "decide what happens to the orphaned bull:runs:*
  keyspace left in Redis db 0", with the round's shape being (a) copy the
  six failed job hashes and their failedReasons into an evidence file
  and then DEL the keyspace, or (b) leave the keyspace and note in
  README.md that db 0 holds a retired keyspace. It was written when #27
  separated the two products' `runs` queues and the payloads were the
  campaign's best artefact for the defect.

  Measure first. The 26-key, 7-failed-job inventory the task named is
  not the state on disk today:

    redis-cli -n 0 --scan --pattern 'bull:runs*'  → 2 keys
    bull:runs:meta            a hash with one field: opts.maxLenEvents=10000
    bull:runs:stalled-check   a string, the timestamp of the last sweep

  No id counter, no run-execution payloads, no run-agent payloads, no
  failed jobs. The retention default #64 gave every construction
  (`removeOnComplete: 0`, `removeOnFail: <bounded>`) is what reaped them,
  not a delete anyone ran. Bullmq wrote `meta` when the last Worker
  touched the queue and rewrites `stalled-check` on its sweep cadence;
  neither key names the thief or says anything the ledger does not
  already record better.

  That flips the choice. Option (a)'s motive was "preserve the evidence
  before the DEL", and the evidence is already gone — so the DEL would
  be a live-state change on db 0 for two signature keys, on the same rule
  #27 originally invoked ("deleting stored state from the developer's
  live db 0 needs an explicit ask"). The right call is (b), not because
  (b)'s reasoning is stronger on its own merits, but because (b) is what
  the state has already decided and the round's job is to say so.

8.62.1 WHAT CHANGED

  No code. `shared/README.md`'s "The queue namespace check" section
  claimed, in present tense, that "db 0 holds one bull:runs id counter
  at 20 with both products' job payloads under it, and its failed set
  carries run-execution jobs whose failedReason is …". That was true
  when §8.26.10 was written and false today, and the drift is exactly the
  kind this campaign rules against: a README that reads as current but
  describes a state that has moved becomes the file a future reader
  trusts over their own redis-cli. The section now says what was there
  when #27 filed, and what is there now, and names #64's retention
  defaults as what drained it. The two signature keys are explicitly
  left alone for the same rule the payloads were left alone for
  originally — an explicit-ask DEL on live db 0 — and the sentence says
  that those two keys are not the campaign's evidence, §8.26.10 is.

  Rule 6 in `shared/check-queues.sh` was already correct about its own
  narrowness ("Asserted only for the two apps this round separated,
  because the other three still use bare names … filed, not silently
  widened here"). README.md now says the same thing in prose so the file
  does not overclaim rule 6's coverage. That half of #51 — widening the
  rule to leadgenius, leadworks and abetanalytics by renaming 13 queues
  and re-seating their repeat jobs — is a separate round with its own
  measurement and is filed as task #84. Doing it here would have meant
  renaming `bull:scheduled:repeat:leadworks-scheduled-sweep` in one
  commit while asserting nothing about the new name's sweep, which is
  the kind of half-answer §8.60.4 says not to write.

8.62.2 NUMBERS AFTER

  Docs check 110 pass / 0 fail — the changed paragraph stays inside the
  line-length budget and introduces no live-reference to a URL, port or
  command the docs check would flag.

  Full family gate: 20 steps, 20 passed, 0 failed in 78s. No code
  changed; nothing else moves.

8.62.3 WHAT IS STILL OWED IN THIS NEIGHBOURHOOD

  Task #84 — widen rule 6 to all five products by renaming the three
  apps' bare queue names to product-prefixed ones. The measurement this
  round does not do: leadworks' `scheduled` queue is the only place in
  db 0 with a live repeat key
  (`bull:scheduled:repeat:leadworks-scheduled-sweep:1791444060000`), and
  renaming `scheduled` → `leadworks-scheduled` re-seats it. Whoever
  picks up #84 reads that repeat state before they touch the queue.ts
  literals, so the sweep does not skip an interval.

8.63 SWEEP CLOSES WITH #40 STILL PENDING — MEASURED, NOT INVENTED

  The "fix all" sweep ran #74, #75, #65, #56, #17, #51 to a commit pair
  each. Two tasks stay open at the end: #40 (ops-console/simulate.py)
  and #84 (widen rule 6 across the three bare-name apps). Both are filed
  with what they need; neither is closed with a fabrication. #40's own
  description said as much — "a round against seven backends, not
  something to invent to match a recorded number" — and this round's
  job was to measure that precondition and say it plainly.

8.63.1 WHAT A LIVE STACK LOOKS LIKE ON THIS BOX TODAY

  Responding:
    3101 abetConnect        Docker, healthy, up 8 days
    3400 abetagent          next dev
  Not responding:
    3200 leadgenius         —
    3300 leadworks          —
    3500 abetflow           —
    3600 abetanalytics      —
    3700 abetapi            —
    3999 (the sim's own HMAC receiver)

  The buyer simulation is eight suites — S0 tenant isolation, S1
  abetConnect, S2 leadgenius, S3 leadworks, S4 abetagent, S5 abetflow,
  S6 abetanalytics, S7 abetapi — so six of seven product journeys have
  no live backend right now. `Mem: 3861 MB total, 1568 MB available` on
  a box already holding abetagent + the Docker stack, which is what
  §8.24 measured as the ceiling on how much more can be booted before
  the threads start paying for swap.

  The 46-assertion PASS-line list is preserved. `ops-console/history
  .json` holds fifteen green entries; the newest, at 2026-09-25T14:46:53,
  finishes with `RESULT: 46 passed, 0 failed`. Its `output` field prints
  every one of the 46 names, grouped under the eight suite headers.
  That record tells us *what* was asserted ("seed mints an API key",
  "HMAC verifies with hashed secret", "double enrollment refused"). It
  does not tell us the HTTP method, URL, request body or expected
  status for any of them, and re-authoring means deciding all four, per
  line, against the current routes of seven apps whose contracts this
  campaign has been changing all sweep long.

  Some of what the sim depends on is not measurable from source in one
  sitting either. The suite names concrete rows — the
  "launch-announcement campaign" (S2), "Meridian Corp gets triaged"
  (S5), "demo support agent" (S4) — which implies each app's `dev.db`
  carried demo seed data at the last green. This session has not run
  `prisma db seed` on any of the five down apps, and I do not know
  whether their demo seed is idempotent against the schema changes in
  §8.26 through §8.61. Booting the stack without re-verifying seed is
  a way to fail assertions that have nothing to do with the routes.

8.63.2 WHY #40 DOES NOT GET A FAKE HERE

  There is a version of this round that lands a stub `simulate.py`,
  prints `RESULT: 46 passed, 0 failed`, and turns the Simulate button
  green. That is not a fix, that is the exact failure §8.20 was about —
  a green that measures the report rather than the code. #40's
  detection half (§8.25's `shared/check-suites.sh`) exists specifically
  to say "this suite cannot run because its precondition is missing",
  and it currently reports the missing script as owed. Writing a stub
  that passes the check without passing the journeys inverts the check's
  purpose. The whole reason `TEST-REPORT.md:29` still reads "46 passed,
  0 failed on 2026-09-25" instead of a fresher date is that nothing
  between 2026-09-25 and today produced a green run against the same
  assertion list; a fabricated green would overwrite that honest stale
  record with a lie.

8.63.3 WHAT #40 WILL ACTUALLY TAKE WHEN IT IS PICKED UP AGAIN

  Recorded on the task itself so the next round starts from this
  measurement rather than re-doing it:

    (a) Boot the five missing Next.js dev servers in a way that does
        not push the box into swap. §8.24's thread-count ceiling and
        the RSS budget above are the constraint; a single-process
        `next dev` for each app, in sequence with each other's warm-up
        settled, is what last worked when the suite was green.
    (b) Verify each app's `dev.db` carries the demo rows the assertion
        names depend on. This is a per-app read of `prisma/seed.ts` and
        the current schema state after §8.26-§8.61's writes — not a
        blind `db push` run.
    (c) Write the 46 assertions against the current routes. Each of the
        PASS-line names in the newest history.json output is the shape;
        the request bodies and expected statuses are the round's
        judgement.
    (d) Bind port 3999 for abetapi's HMAC receiver, and check for an
        existing listener before the bind (the last failed run in
        history.json died on `OSError: [Errno 98] Address already in
        use` at `/tmp/simulate.py:316`, so this is a known shape, not a
        hypothetical).

  None of (a)-(d) is a one-command fix. #40 stays pending with this
  measurement attached.

8.63.4 AND #84

  #84 is the widening of `check-queues.sh` rule 6 to the three bare-name
  apps. It is a rename round: 13 queue names across leadgenius,
  leadworks and abetanalytics, plus each app's `queue.ts` literals and
  `tests/unit/queue.test.ts` KEYS array, plus a careful re-seat of
  `bull:scheduled:repeat:leadworks-scheduled-sweep` in Redis so the
  sweep does not skip an interval when the queue is renamed under it.
  README.md's queue-namespace section now says the same thing in prose
  (§8.62.1), so a future reader of the console knows rule 6 is narrow
  and that narrowness is deliberate.
